ABOUT THIS VOLUME

This is volume 2 of five, and it continues the catalogue at Chapter 60.

The front matter and Part One, the general account of the neuron, are given in Volume 1 and are not repeated here.

The tags and flags used throughout are explained in Volume 1, and are summarised again in one line each below.

• (PUC) marks a Previously Unnamed Construct: a neuron type that is real, functionally necessary and relied upon in the literature, but which had no accepted proper name until this work gave it one.

• (COG) marks a type involved in cognition, and (NON-COG) marks a type that is not.


PART THREE: HIPPOCAMPAL FORMATION - MEMORY AND NAVIGATION (CONTINUED FROM VOLUME 1)

CHAPTER 57. BACK-PROJECTION CELLS (Upstream-Feedback Muting Neuron) (COG)

Information in the hippocampus is supposed to flow one way, from the dentate gyrus to CA3 to CA1 and out; this cell drives the wrong way up that one-way street, on purpose, every time you remember something.

Back-projection names a direction on a map, not a purpose.

Upstream names where the axon goes, against the normal flow of the hippocampal circuit, from area CA1 back into several layers of area CA3 and the dentate gyrus.

Feedback names the loop it closes, letting the final stage of the hippocampal chain report back to the earlier stages once an output has been produced.

Muting names the payload, since the cell is inhibitory, so what travels backwards is a suppression signal rather than a copy of the message.

Neuron marks it as a long-range inhibitory cell of the hippocampal formation.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The back-projection cell is a GABA-releasing inhibitory neuron whose cell body sits in stratum oriens or the alveus of CA1, the final stage of the hippocampal circuit, and whose axon runs backwards into CA3 and into the hilus of the dentate gyrus, the earlier stages.

It was identified by intracellular labelling in the living rat brain, where two cells with somata in the alveus were found to innervate CA3 in addition to arborising richly within CA1.

Its job is to close a long inhibitory feedback loop.

CA1 is the hippocampus's answer; the back-projection cell takes a copy of that answer and uses it to quieten the parts of the circuit that produced the question.

This does three things: it prevents the recurrent, self-exciting network of CA3 from running away into seizure, it helps terminate bursts of activity once an output has been produced, and it imposes a timing relationship between the output stage and the input stage that matters for sharp wave ripples, the 150 to 200 hertz bursts during which memories are replayed.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is horizontally elongated and lies in stratum oriens or within the alveus itself.

Dendrites run horizontally within stratum oriens, which means, as with the trilaminar cell, that its input is CA1's own pyramidal output rather than incoming information.

The axon leaves through stratum oriens and the alveus, travels along the length of the hippocampus toward CA3, and there branches in strata oriens, radiatum and lacunosum-moleculare, then continues into the hilus and the dentate molecular layer, while also leaving a rich local arbour behind in CA1.

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly 15 to 25 micrometres along its long axis.

Total axonal length is in the tens of millimetres in the rat, and would be substantially greater in the much larger human hippocampus, plausibly 50 to 200 millimetres, though no human reconstruction exists.

SIZE figures for human are extrapolations and should be labelled as such in any model.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Somatostatin-positive back-projecting cells whose axon reaches CA3 and the dentate gyrus.

• Double-projecting cells, which send collaterals to CA3, the dentate, the subiculum, the medial entorhinal cortex and the contralateral hippocampus as well as to the medial septum.

• Oriens-retrohippocampal projecting cells, whose long branch reaches the subiculum rather than CA3.

• Somatostatin-positive cells projecting across the midline to the opposite hippocampus.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies are in CA1 stratum oriens and in the alveus, with some in CA3 stratum oriens.

The interfaces that define it are three.

The stratum oriens and alveus border, where it sits.

The CA1 and CA3 border, which its axon crosses in the retrograde direction.

The hilus, which is itself the interface between CA3's proximal territory and the dentate gyrus, and which its terminal branches invade.

NUMBER:

Back-projection cells are allocated about 30,000 per hemisphere, so about 60,000 across both hemispheres, which is about 0.04 percent of the 150 million neuron budget for the hippocampal formation.

Confidence is low.

The anchor is that human hippocampus contains an estimated 141,500 somatostatin-positive interneurons per hemisphere, and back-projecting cells are a minority subset of the somatostatin-positive population in stratum oriens.

A defensible bound is 10,000 to 60,000 per hemisphere, and the overlap with double-projecting cells means any model must avoid double counting them with the hippocampo-septal type.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

As somatostatin-positive oriens neurons they have high input resistance, roughly 200 to 400 megaohms, so they are easily driven.

They show a prominent sag when hyperpolarised, caused by the hyperpolarisation-activated current known as Ih, which gives them a natural rebound and a resonant preference near theta frequency.

They have low firing thresholds, broad spikes and strong spike frequency accommodation.

In the behaving animal, somatostatin-positive long-range oriens cells projecting to the dentate fire at about 4 hertz during theta and increase to about 23 hertz during sharp wave ripples.

That ripple increase is important: it separates them from oriens-lacunosum-moleculare cells, which go quiet during ripples.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The input synapse is the celebrated one.

CA1 pyramidal axon collaterals contact these cells through synapses with an extremely low initial release probability that facilitates dramatically during a train, so the first pyramidal spike is nearly ignored and the tenth is not.

This target-specific facilitation depends on the presynaptic adhesion protein Elfn1 and on the metabotropic glutamate receptor mGluR1 alpha on the interneuron.

The practical consequence is that back-projection cells are burst detectors: they respond to sustained pyramidal activity and ignore isolated spikes.

They also receive septal GABAergic and cholinergic input and inhibition from vasoactive intestinal peptide expressing interneurons.

Outputs go to CA3 pyramidal cell dendrites, hilar interneurons and mossy cells, dentate granule cell dendrites, and locally within CA1.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Long-range GABAergic axons are among the few inhibitory axons in the brain that acquire myelin, and this axon is patchily myelinated along its inter-regional stretch in stratum oriens and the alveus, then unmyelinated at its terminal arbours.

Conduction is therefore heterogeneous, perhaps 0.5 to 2 metres per second along the myelinated stretch and a few tenths of a metre per second at the terminals.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA, released fast and synaptically.

Somatostatin, a peptide released more slowly and requiring bursts, acting on somatostatin receptors that suppress calcium channels and dampen transmitter release over hundreds of milliseconds.

Many also carry neuropeptide Y, and some carry calbindin, a calcium buffering protein.

Metabotropic glutamate receptor mGluR1 alpha is a reliable marker on the dendrites.

MYELINATION:

Patchy and partial, present along the long-range stretch and absent at terminals.

This matters for simulation, because the conduction delay from CA1 to the hilus is not a single number.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High, driven by axon length rather than firing rate.

Maintaining ionic gradients along tens to hundreds of millimetres of thin axon is the dominant cost, and these cells depend heavily on lactate shuttled from astrocytes during sustained activity.

PLASTICITY:

The pyramidal-to-somatostatin-interneuron synapse undergoes long-term potentiation that depends on mGluR1 alpha, and blocking that plasticity impairs hippocampal memory in rodents.

Oriens somatostatin cells also express the enzymes that manufacture endocannabinoids and show CB1-dependent long-term potentiation, which is unusual for an interneuron.

So this is not a fixed piece of wiring; the strength of the back-projection is itself learned.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Oligodendrocytes in stratum oriens and the alveus myelinate a GABAergic axon, which is a specialised job most oligodendrocytes never do, and the myelination pattern is activity-dependent, so this cell instructs its own conduction delay.

Microglia cluster around these neurons early in experimental epilepsy, and somatostatin-positive hilar and oriens cells are among the first to die in that disease, which makes their glial environment a site of unusually active inflammatory signalling.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the back-projection cell and CA3 loses its report card.

CA3 is an auto-associative network, a web of neurons wired back onto themselves so that a fragment of a memory can complete the whole pattern, and such a network is intrinsically explosive.

Without a downstream inhibitory signal telling CA3 that CA1 has already produced an answer, pattern completion would not know when to stop.

The dentate hilus would also lose a top-down brake, and the whole loop would drift toward the runaway synchrony that underlies temporal lobe epilepsy.

The flavour lost is closure: the sense that a recollection has finished.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Target-specific presynaptic facilitation makes this cell a temporal integrator implemented in the synapse rather than the membrane, and any simulation that omits it will get the cell qualitatively wrong.

Somatostatin peptide release requires burst firing and then acts by volume transmission over tens of micrometres for hundreds of milliseconds, a chemical shadow rather than a synapse.

Neuropeptide Y release from the same terminals is anticonvulsant and suppresses glutamate release presynaptically.

Endocannabinoids released from pyramidal dendrites act back onto these cells, and the cells themselves make endocannabinoids.

Back-propagating action potentials invade their dendrites and trigger local calcium signalling that gates the mGluR1-dependent plasticity.

Their myelination is instructed by their own activity, and their long axons make them unusually dependent on axonal transport and on astrocytic metabolic support.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ CA1 pyramidal collateral glutamate with facilitating release probability rising from about 0.03 to about 0.6 over a train.

◦ Medial septal GABA and acetylcholine, 0 to 1 scalar tone.

◦ Vasoactive intestinal peptide interneuron GABA input, 0 to 3 nanosiemens.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ GABA-A conductance onto CA3 pyramidal dendrites, hilar neurons and dentate granule cell dendrites, 0.3 to 1.5 nanosiemens per spike.

◦ Local GABA-A conductance within CA1.

◦ Somatostatin and neuropeptide Y as diffusible fields, released only above a burst threshold.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, facilitation variable, Ih activation, recent spike list for burst detection, peptide store fraction, per-target axonal delay.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Initial release probability 0.03, maximum 0.6, input resistance 200 to 400 megaohms, threshold about minus 50 millivolts, theta rate 4 hertz, ripple rate 23 hertz.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Facilitation decay about 200 milliseconds, membrane about 25 milliseconds, Ih 50 to 300 milliseconds, peptide action 200 to 1000 milliseconds, CA1 to CA3 conduction delay 2 to 10 milliseconds in rodent.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In temporal lobe epilepsy these somatostatin-positive cells are selectively lost, dentate and CA3 inhibition falls, and the loop that limits recurrent excitation fails, which is one of the best supported cellular accounts of how the disease becomes self-sustaining.

◦ Silencing hippocampal somatostatin interneurons in healthy mice is by itself sufficient to induce recurrent spontaneous limbic seizures.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the burst-detecting feedback output of a back-projection cell whose input synapse facilitates.
Create a number variable called release_probability with initial value 0.03.
Create a number variable called maximum_probability with initial value 0.6.
Create a number variable called facilitation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called membrane_voltage with initial value -62.
Create a number variable called output_spikes with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called peptide_store with initial value 1.
Create a list variable called recent_spikes with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called step_count with initial value 0.
While step_count is less than 400, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the remainder of step_count divided by 20.
    Check if that remainder is equal to 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of maximum_probability and 0.03.
        Calculate the product of that difference and facilitation.
        Calculate the sum of 0.03 and that product.
        Set current_probability to that result.
        Calculate the product of current_probability and 12.
        Calculate the sum of membrane_voltage and that product.
        Set membrane_voltage to that result.
        Calculate the difference of 1 and facilitation.
        Calculate the product of that difference and 0.25.
        Calculate the sum of facilitation and that product.
        Set facilitation to that result.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if membrane_voltage is greater than or equal to -50.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane_voltage to -62.
        Calculate the sum of output_spikes and 1.
        Set output_spikes to that result.
        Add step_count to the end of recent_spikes.
        Get the total number of items in recent_spikes.
        Check if that total is greater than or equal to 4.
        If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
            Calculate the product of peptide_store and 0.05.
            Calculate the difference of peptide_store and that product.
            Set peptide_store to that result.
        End the conditional block.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of step_count and 1.
    Set step_count to that result.
End the loop.
Display output_spikes to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: short-term facilitation plus a peptide pool is a handful of exponentials,
# and readability beats raw speed for a single cell.
DT = 0.5   # milliseconds per step
class BackProjectionCell:
    """CA1 pyramidal collateral -> back-projection cell -> CA3 and hilus."""
    def __init__(self):
        self.p0 = 0.03        # release probability at the first spike: almost silent
        self.p_max = 0.60     # ceiling after Elfn1-dependent facilitation
        self.fac = 0.0        # facilitation state, 0 to 1
        self.tau_fac = 200.0  # milliseconds
        self.v = -62.0        # millivolts
        self.tau_m = 25.0     # milliseconds, high input resistance means a slow membrane
        self.thresh = -50.0
        self.gain = 12.0      # millivolts of depolarisation at release probability 1
        self.recent = []      # spike times, for burst detection
        self.sst_pool = 1.0   # somatostatin peptide store, fraction remaining
        self.sst_released = 0.0
        self.out_spikes = 0
    def pyramidal_spike(self):
        p = self.p0 + (self.p_max - self.p0) * self.fac   # this spike uses the old state
        self.v += self.gain * p
        self.fac += 0.25 * (1.0 - self.fac)               # then the terminal facilitates
        return p
    def step(self, t):
        self.fac -= self.fac / self.tau_fac * DT
        self.v += (-62.0 - self.v) / self.tau_m * DT
        if self.v >= self.thresh:
            self.v = -62.0
            self.out_spikes += 1
            self.recent = [s for s in self.recent if t - s < 100.0] + [t]
            # Peptide release needs a burst, not a single spike.
            if len(self.recent) >= 4 and self.sst_pool > 0.0:
                give = 0.05 * self.sst_pool
                self.sst_pool -= give
                self.sst_released += give
cell = BackProjectionCell()
probs = []
for k in range(400):                       # 200 milliseconds
    t = k * DT
    if t % 10.0 == 0.0 and t < 200.0:      # 100 Hz feedback train from CA1 pyramids
        probs.append(cell.pyramidal_spike())
    cell.step(t)
print("release probability, spike 1 -> spike 20: %.3f -> %.3f" % (probs[0], probs[-1]))
print("back-projection spikes sent to CA3 and hilus:", cell.out_spikes)
print("somatostatin released: %.3f of the store" % cell.sst_released)
# Output: 0.030 -> 0.502, 5 output spikes, 0.050 of the peptide store released.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

An inhibitory axon travelling upstream, from the hippocampus's output stage into its input stages, reaching CA3 and the hilus.

Nothing else in the hippocampus points that way.

CHAPTER 58. HIPPOCAMPO-SEPTAL PROJECTING INHIBITORY CELLS (Exploration-Curbing Long-Range Neuron) (COG)

The hippocampus keeps time to a clock that sits two centimetres away in the middle of the brain, and this single cell type is the wire that lets the hippocampus argue with its own clock.

The old label is a route joined to a chemistry note, naming where the axon runs and what it releases without saying what either achieves.

Exploration-Curbing names the measured behavioural consequence, because switching these cells on, or switching on their terminals in the medial septum, reduces locomotion and exploratory behaviour in freely moving animals.

Long-Range names the anatomy that makes this unusual, since inhibitory cells almost always speak only to their immediate neighbourhood, whereas this one sends an axon right out of the hippocampus into the septum and preferentially contacts inhibitory cells there.

Neuron marks it as the hippocampus reaching forward to slow the animal down and to bind the septal rhythm generator to its own timing.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is a GABA-releasing inhibitory neuron of the hippocampus whose axon leaves the hippocampus entirely, travels in the fimbria and fornix, the great arching output cable, and ends in the medial septum and the diagonal band of Broca in the basal forebrain.

That is remarkable in itself, because inhibition is normally a local business.

What makes it more remarkable is what it lands on.

Its targets in the septum are mostly the septal GABAergic neurons that themselves project back into the hippocampus and drive hippocampal interneurons.

Inhibiting an inhibitor of an inhibitor produces a precisely timed release, so this cell does not simply quieten the septum; it sculpts the septum's rhythmic output.

The septo-hippocampal circuit is therefore a genuine closed loop with a measurable delay, and closed loops with delays are how brains build oscillations.

The practical result is the theta rhythm, the roughly 8 hertz sweep that organises hippocampal activity during exploration and rapid eye movement sleep, and the suppression of that rhythm during sharp wave ripples.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is fusiform and horizontally oriented, lying in stratum oriens of CA1 and CA3 or in the hilus of the dentate gyrus.

Dendrites run horizontally through stratum oriens or spread through the hilus, so once again the cell samples local principal cell output rather than incoming cortical information.

There are two axonal domains: a local arbour that preferentially contacts the dendrites of other interneurons, and a long main axon that gathers into the fimbria and heads for the septum.

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly 15 to 25 micrometres by 10 to 15 micrometres.

The projecting axon is a few millimetres long in a mouse, perhaps 5 to 10 millimetres in a rat, and in the human brain the fornix route from hippocampus to septum is on the order of 3 to 6 centimetres, making this one of the longest inhibitory axons in the human nervous system.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• CA1 stratum oriens hippocampo-septal cells, of which about 94 percent are somatostatin positive and about 68 percent carry mGluR1 alpha.

• CA3 hippocampo-septal cells, about 93 percent somatostatin positive and about 84 percent mGluR1 alpha positive.

• Hilar hippocampo-septal cells, of which essentially 100 percent are somatostatin positive and about 92 percent carry neuropeptide Y.

• Double-projecting cells that innervate the septum and also the subiculum, entorhinal cortex or contralateral hippocampus.

• Theta-off, ripple-on cells, sometimes abbreviated TORO cells, which are nearly silent during theta and fire vigorously during ripples.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies sit in CA1 stratum oriens, across the layers of CA3, and in the hilus, also called the polymorph layer, of the dentate gyrus.

The interfaces are explicit and sequential.

First the alveus and fimbria junction, where the axon leaves the hippocampus.

Then the fimbria and fornix white matter tract.

Then the medial septum and the vertical limb of the diagonal band of Broca, where the axon terminates in a structure outside the hippocampal formation altogether.

NUMBER:

Hippocampo-septal cells are allocated about 50,000 per hemisphere, so about 100,000 across both hemispheres, about 0.067 percent of the 150 million neuron budget for the hippocampal formation.

Confidence is moderate for the anchor and low for the fraction.

The anchor is a human stereological estimate of about 141,500 somatostatin-positive interneurons per hippocampal hemisphere.

The fraction comes from mouse retrograde labelling, in which at least 40.6 percent of hippocampal somatostatin neurons were labelled from the medial septum and 38 percent of hippocampo-septal cells were calbindin positive.

Applying that fraction gives about 57,000 per hemisphere, so a defensible bound is 30,000 to 60,000 per hemisphere.

Any model must subtract the double-projecting cells shared with the back-projection type to avoid counting them twice.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Most are low-threshold spiking cells with high input resistance and a strong Ih sag, giving them a natural rebound at theta intervals.

Behaviour splits the population sharply.

Theta-on cells fire in phase-locked fashion during exploration.

Double-projecting cells with septal targets fire at only 0.7 plus or minus 0.6 hertz during theta and then jump to 40.2 plus or minus 20.5 hertz during sharp wave ripples, measured in six identified cells.

The theta-off, ripple-on subset is virtually silent during locomotion and rapid eye movement sleep and begins firing before the ripple even starts, peaking before the ripple peak.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are local pyramidal cell collaterals through strongly facilitating synapses, CA3 glutamatergic input, and both GABAergic and cholinergic input from the septum itself, closing the loop from the other side.

Outputs in the septum land preferentially on parvalbumin-expressing septal GABAergic pacemaker neurons, with a smaller share onto cholinergic neurons.

Local outputs within the hippocampus contact interneuron dendrites, including parvalbumin and cholecystokinin basket cells, rather than pyramidal cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The projecting axon is myelinated within the fimbria and fornix and conducts at roughly 1 to 4 metres per second.

In the rat this gives a hippocampus-to-septum delay of a few milliseconds; scaling the human fornix length suggests something on the order of 10 to 20 milliseconds, which is a substantial fraction of a theta cycle and therefore a functionally important number for any simulation.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA as the fast transmitter.

Somatostatin as the near-universal co-transmitter.

Neuropeptide Y in the hilar members, and calbindin as a calcium buffer in about 38 percent.

Most, about 75 percent, lack the M2 muscarinic receptor, which distinguishes them chemically from trilaminar cells.

MYELINATION:

Yes, and this is one of the defining features.

A myelinated GABAergic axon is rare, and it exists here because the loop delay must be short and reliable enough to sustain an oscillation.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high for an interneuron.

A centimetres-long myelinated axon plus 40 hertz burst firing during every ripple, thousands of times a night, is a large sustained energy commitment, and these cells depend on oligodendrocyte-supplied lactate delivered through the myelin sheath.

PLASTICITY:

The facilitating pyramidal input is potentiated through mGluR1 alpha, so the strength of the hippocampal voice in the septum is itself learned.

Cholinergic and GABAergic septal feedback modulates the cell moment to moment.

These cells are among the most vulnerable in epilepsy, and their loss is not compensated.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The oligodendrocytes of the fimbria and fornix are doing something unusual: myelinating an inhibitory axon and thereby setting the delay of a brain-wide oscillator.

Their myelin thickness and internode length on these axons are functionally load-bearing, not incidental.

Within the hippocampus, astrocytic support is broadly typical, but in the septum the terminal fields sit among cholinergic neurons with distinctive astrocytic and microglial populations that are themselves early casualties in Alzheimer's disease.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the septo-hippocampal circuit becomes an open loop.

The septum would still send its rhythmic drive into the hippocampus, but the hippocampus could no longer answer, so theta would become a metronome imposed from outside rather than a negotiated rhythm that can be reset by what the hippocampus is actually processing.

Sharp wave ripples would lose their ability to suppress septal theta drive, so the two great hippocampal states, exploration and replay, would stop being cleanly exclusive.

The flavour lost is conversation: the hippocampus would be spoken to and would no longer speak back.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The most important non-classical property here is that the cell is a delay line, and the delay is a computational parameter of the whole brain, not a nuisance.

Somatostatin and neuropeptide Y are co-released into the septum by volume transmission, producing slow modulation lasting hundreds of milliseconds on top of the fast GABA signal.

The input synapse facilitates, so the cell is a burst detector like the back-projection cell.

Its myelination is activity-dependent, so the cell tunes its own loop delay over days, which is a genuinely astonishing idea: the oscillator adjusts its own period by rebuilding its wiring.

Back-propagating action potentials and mGluR1-dependent dendritic calcium signalling gate its plasticity, and its long axon makes it exceptionally dependent on mitochondrial transport.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Local pyramidal collateral glutamate, facilitating, release probability 0.03 rising toward 0.6.

◦ CA3 Schaffer and associational glutamate, 0 to 20 nanosiemens.

◦ Septal GABA and acetylcholine returning through the fornix, 0 to 1 scalar tone each.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ GABA-A conductance onto medial septal parvalbumin pacemaker neurons, delivered after an axonal delay.

◦ GABA-A conductance onto local hippocampal parvalbumin and cholecystokinin basket cell dendrites.

◦ Somatostatin and neuropeptide Y volume signals in the septum, burst-gated.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, facilitation variable, Ih activation, brain state flag for theta or ripple, axonal delay ring buffer, peptide store.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Theta firing 0.7 plus or minus 0.6 hertz, ripple firing 40.2 plus or minus 20.5 hertz, conduction velocity 1 to 4 metres per second, human axon length 30 to 60 millimetres, somatostatin positive fraction above 0.9.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Axonal delay about 12 milliseconds in human, theta period 125 milliseconds, ripple episode 40 to 100 milliseconds, facilitation decay 200 milliseconds, peptide action 200 to 1000 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Selective loss of somatostatin hippocampo-septal cells in temporal lobe epilepsy breaks the feedback limb of the theta generator, which is a plausible cellular basis for the disrupted theta and impaired memory that accompany the disease.

◦ Fornix damage, whether surgical, vascular or from head injury, severs this axon and produces a dense anterograde amnesia out of proportion to the tissue lost.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the delayed inhibition that a hippocampo-septal cell delivers to the septal pacemaker across the fornix.
Create a list variable called axon_buffer with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called buffer_slots with initial value 25.
Create a number variable called fill_count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called write_index with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called septal_rate_total with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step_count with initial value 0.
While fill_count is less than buffer_slots, repeat the following steps.
    Add 0 to the end of axon_buffer.
    Calculate the sum of fill_count and 1.
    Set fill_count to that result.
End the loop.
While step_count is less than 20000, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the remainder of step_count divided by 600.
    Check if that remainder is less than 120.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set hippocampo_septal_rate to 40.2.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set hippocampo_septal_rate to 0.7.
    End the conditional block.
    Remove the item at position write_index from axon_buffer.
    Add hippocampo_septal_rate to the end of axon_buffer.
    Access the item at position 0 in axon_buffer.
    Set arriving_rate to that item.
    Calculate the product of arriving_rate and 0.65.
    Calculate the difference of 35 and that product.
    Set septal_rate to that result.
    Check if septal_rate is less than 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set septal_rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of septal_rate_total and septal_rate.
    Set septal_rate_total to that result.
    Calculate the sum of step_count and 1.
    Set step_count to that result.
End the loop.
Calculate the quotient of septal_rate_total divided by 20000.
Display that quotient to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C: an axonal conduction delay is exactly a ring buffer, and C makes the
   fixed-size buffer and the per-step indexing explicit and fast enough to
   scale to the whole septo-hippocampal loop. */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <string.h>
#define DT_MS     0.5                       /* step size, milliseconds        */
#define STEPS     20000                     /* 10 seconds of simulated time   */
#define DELAY_MS  12.0                      /* hippocampus -> septum, human   */
#define NBUF      ((int)(DELAY_MS / DT_MS) + 1)
int main(void) {
    double axon[NBUF];                      /* the fornix, as a delay line    */
    int head = 0, i;
    double hs, septal, sum_septal = 0.0, sum_ripple_septal = 0.0;
    int ripple_steps = 0;
    memset(axon, 0, sizeof axon);
    for (i = 0; i < STEPS; i++) {
        double t_ms = i * DT_MS;
        /* Sharp wave ripple packets: 60 ms of ripple every 300 ms at rest. */
        int in_ripple = (((int)t_ms) % 300) < 60;
        /* Hippocampo-septal cell: near silent in theta, vigorous in ripples. */
        hs = in_ripple ? 40.2 : 0.7;        /* spikes per second, measured    */
        /* Push today's output into the axon, read out what left 12 ms ago.   */
        axon[head] = hs;
        head = (head + 1) % NBUF;
        {
            double arriving = axon[head];   /* head now points at the oldest  */
            /* Septal parvalbumin cell: 35 Hz baseline, inhibited by arrivals.*/
            septal = 35.0 - 0.65 * arriving;
            if (septal < 0.0) septal = 0.0;
        }
        sum_septal += septal;
        if (in_ripple) { sum_ripple_septal += septal; ripple_steps++; }
    }
    printf("mean septal pacemaker rate            : %5.2f Hz\n", sum_septal / STEPS);
    printf("mean septal rate during ripple packets: %5.2f Hz\n", sum_ripple_septal / ripple_steps);
    printf("loop delay carried by the fornix      : %5.1f ms (%d slots)\n", DELAY_MS, NBUF);
    return 0;
}
/* Output: 29.31 Hz mean, 14.01 Hz during ripples, 12.0 ms delay over 25 slots. */

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A GABA cell whose axon leaves the hippocampus through the fornix and ends in the medial septum, carrying somatostatin, wrapped in myelin, and targeting the pacemaker neurons that drive the hippocampus in return.

CHAPTER 59. RADIATUM-RETROHIPPOCAMPAL CELLS (Early-Arriving Calming Neuron) (COG)

Most inhibitory cells whisper to the neighbours; this one shouts across the brain to the patch of cortex that keeps track of where you are.

The old label is an address joined to a destination list, since stratum radiatum is where the cell body sits and retrohippocampal merely names where the axon ends.

Early-Arriving names the cell's timing advantage, because its axon is thicker and more heavily myelinated than a CA1 pyramidal cell axon, so its signal reaches the subiculum, presubiculum, retrosplenial cortex and indusium griseum before the excitatory volley from pyramidal cells arrives.

Calming names the work, since the cell releases gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main inhibitory messenger, landing preferentially on the dendrites of interneurons and on the layer one apical tufts of retrosplenial pyramidal cells.

Neuron marks it as a long-range inhibitory cell that sets the receiving cortex up rather than reacting to it, firing hard on the descending phase of the theta rhythm yet staying out of sharp-wave ripples.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The radiatum-retrohippocampal cell is a GABA-releasing long-range projection neuron of hippocampal area CA1, named for where its cell body sits and where its axon goes.

Its body sits at the border between stratum radiatum and stratum lacunosum-moleculare, and its axon leaves the hippocampus for the retrohippocampal region and beyond: the molecular layer of the subiculum, the presubiculum, the retrosplenial cortex, and the indusium griseum, a vestigial ribbon of hippocampal tissue lying on top of the corpus callosum.

Critically, it does not innervate the medial septum, which is what separates it from the hippocampo-septal cells.

Its function is a piece of long-distance veto.

In the retrosplenial cortex, a region essential for spatial orientation and for translating between the view from your eyes and a map of the world, its axon terminates in layer one and inhibits the apical tuft dendrites of layer five pyramidal neurons.

Layer one is also where the anterior thalamic nuclei deliver their excitation.

So this cell intercepts a thalamic input at the exact dendritic branch where it lands, and by doing so it decides whether that input will trigger a dendritic plateau potential, the long regenerative depolarisation that turns a cortical neuron from a spike emitter into a bursting, plasticity-inducing unit.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is at the radiatum and lacunosum-moleculare border, a boundary that is also an information boundary.

Below it, stratum radiatum receives Schaffer collaterals from CA3, the hippocampus's own internal memory store.

Above it, stratum lacunosum-moleculare receives the temporoammonic path directly from entorhinal cortex layer three, and input from nucleus reuniens of the thalamus.

Its dendrites span both, so this cell reads the internal recollection and the external report at the same time.

Its axon gathers, leaves the hippocampus, and travels long distances, in part through the cingulum bundle to reach the retrosplenial cortex.

SIZE:

The cell body is roughly 12 to 20 micrometres across.

The projecting axon in a rodent runs several millimetres; in a human brain the route from CA1 to the retrosplenial cortex through the cingulum is several centimetres, so 30 to 70 millimetres is a reasonable modelling range, and it should be flagged as extrapolated.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Subiculum-projecting cells, terminating in the subicular molecular layer.

• Retrosplenial-projecting cells, terminating in layer one of granular retrosplenial cortex; these are the ones likely to express netrin-G1, the gene Ntng1.

• Presubiculum-projecting and indusium griseum-projecting cells.

• Partly overlapping long-range inhibitory nNOS-expressing cells, or LINCs, which are somatostatin-negative, about 47 percent neuropeptide Y positive, and project widely.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Hippocampus CA1, at the stratum radiatum and stratum lacunosum-moleculare border.

That lamina boundary is the interface that defines the cell, because it is the anatomical seam between CA3-derived and entorhinal-derived information.

Its terminal fields are outside the hippocampus proper: subiculum molecular layer, presubiculum, granular retrosplenial cortex layer one, and indusium griseum.

Its axon passes through the alveus and the cingulum bundle, another named interface between grey and white matter.

NUMBER:

Radiatum-retrohippocampal cells are allocated about 15,000 per hemisphere, so about 30,000 across both hemispheres, about 0.02 percent of the 150 million neuron budget for the hippocampal formation.

This is the least certain figure in the batch.

In the study that named the type, only two such cells were physiologically identified and recovered in a large in vivo survey, so the estimate rests on rarity of encounter rather than on any count.

A defensible bound is 5,000 to 40,000 per hemisphere, and it should be widened if the overlapping nNOS long-range population is counted here rather than separately.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

During theta these cells fired at a mean of 5.2 hertz, preferentially on the descending phase of the local theta wave.

During sharp wave ripples they did not increase their firing, averaging about 0.7 hertz, which is the opposite of what trilaminar and hippocampo-septal cells do.

Both figures come from only two identified cells, so they must be treated as provisional, and any simulation should expose them as parameters rather than constants.

The ripple result is nonetheless informative: this projection is a theta-time channel, not a replay channel.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are Schaffer collateral glutamate from CA3 onto the radiatum portion of its dendrites, temporoammonic glutamate from entorhinal cortex layer three onto the lacunosum-moleculare portion, and thalamic input from nucleus reuniens onto the same distal region.

Local inhibitory input arrives from CA1 interneurons.

Outputs are GABA-A synapses onto the apical tuft dendrites of retrosplenial layer five pyramidal neurons in layer one, onto subicular and presubicular neurons in their molecular layers, and onto local CA1 elements.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Long-range, at least partly myelinated once it enters white matter, conducting at roughly 1 to 2 metres per second.

Terminal branches in layer one of the retrosplenial cortex are fine and unmyelinated, and the synapses there are described as potent.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA.

The two cells originally characterised were immunonegative for every standard marker tested apart from glutamic acid decarboxylase, the enzyme that makes GABA, which is itself a useful negative fingerprint.

Related long-range populations projecting from CA1 to retrosplenial cortex express netrin-G1, and the overlapping LINC population expresses neuronal nitric oxide synthase and often neuropeptide Y.

MYELINATION:

Yes along the long central stretch, absent at the terminals.

The myelinated stretch is what makes a cortical veto arrive fast enough to matter within a theta cycle.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High, and dominated by axon maintenance rather than by firing rate, since 5 hertz is modest.

Long thin axons through white matter make these cells dependent on axonal transport and on oligodendrocyte metabolic support, and they are correspondingly vulnerable to white matter disease.

PLASTICITY:

Honestly, this is largely unknown.

No long-term potentiation or depression protocol has been characterised at either the input or the output synapses of identified radiatum-retrohippocampal cells.

What can be said is that the cell sits on two input streams that are themselves highly plastic, and that its output targets, cortical apical tufts, are the classic site of plasticity gating, so it is well placed to be a plasticity controller even if its own plasticity is uncharacterised.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes at the radiatum and lacunosum-moleculare border have a specific role, because that border is where two glutamatergic pathways with different release statistics abut, and astrocytic glutamate uptake there prevents cross-talk between the CA3 and entorhinal streams onto the same dendrite.

Oligodendrocytes of the cingulum bundle carry this axon across the brain, which places it in a white matter environment quite unlike the rest of the hippocampus.

Otherwise its microglial environment appears unremarkable.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and two regions change flavour.

CA1 loses very little, because its local arbour is modest.

The retrosplenial cortex loses its hippocampal veto: the anterior thalamic input to layer one would drive apical tuft plateau potentials unopposed, so retrosplenial layer five neurons would burst and undergo plasticity whenever the thalamus said so, regardless of what the hippocampus knew.

The concrete loss is arbitration between two accounts of where you are, the thalamic head-direction and landmark account and the hippocampal memory account, with the hippocampus no longer able to say "not that one".

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This is a dendritic computer at both ends.

At its own input end, its dendrites cross two laminae carrying different information, so it multiplies rather than merely sums the CA3 recollection stream and the entorhinal present-tense stream.

At its output end, it does not merely reduce firing in its targets: by inhibiting the apical tuft it blocks NMDA-receptor-dependent plateau potentials and calcium spikes, which is a categorical veto on burst firing and on the plasticity that bursts induce.

That is control over learning in a distant cortex, exercised by a single inhibitory hippocampal neuron.

Because parts of this population express neuronal nitric oxide synthase, they can also release nitric oxide as a diffusible signal that dilates local vessels and modulates transmitter release in the target cortex.

Their long white matter axons place them in continuous metabolic dialogue with oligodendrocytes, and their conduction delay is therefore adjustable.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Schaffer collateral glutamate from CA3 onto proximal dendrites, 0 to 20 nanosiemens.

◦ Temporoammonic glutamate from entorhinal cortex layer three and nucleus reuniens onto distal dendrites, 0 to 15 nanosiemens.

◦ Local CA1 GABA input, 0 to 5 nanosiemens.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ GABA-A conductance onto retrosplenial layer five apical tufts in layer one, sufficient to raise the plateau threshold by roughly 10 to 20 millivolts.

◦ GABA-A conductance onto subicular and presubicular molecular layers.

◦ Nitric oxide as a diffusible field in the nNOS-expressing subset.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential in two dendritic compartments plus soma, theta phase estimate, axonal delay buffer, target tuft plateau timer.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Theta firing rate 5.2 hertz, preferred theta phase on the descending slope, ripple firing rate about 0.7 hertz, conduction velocity 1 to 2 metres per second, human axon length 30 to 70 millimetres, tuft inhibition strength 10 to 20 millivolts equivalent.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds, GABA-A decay at the cortical tuft 8 to 20 milliseconds, target plateau duration 30 to 100 milliseconds, conduction delay 15 to 40 milliseconds in human.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Retrosplenial cortex is one of the earliest regions to show reduced glucose metabolism in Alzheimer's disease, and loss of this hippocampal input is one candidate contributor to that early disconnection.

◦ Damage to the cingulum or to this projection would leave retrosplenial plasticity ungated by hippocampal state, which maps onto the topographical disorientation seen after retrosplenial lesions, in which patients recognise landmarks but cannot use them to navigate.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether a retrosplenial apical tuft fires a plateau potential given thalamic drive and hippocampal long-range inhibition.
Create a number variable called tuft_voltage with initial value -70.
Create a number variable called soma_voltage with initial value -70.
Create a number variable called plateau_timer with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called plateau_count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called somatic_spikes with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step_count with initial value 0.
While step_count is less than 1200, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the remainder of step_count divided by 400.
    Check if that remainder is less than 100.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set thalamic_drive to 30.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set thalamic_drive to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set hippocampal_inhibition to the current long-range GABA input in millivolts.
    Calculate the difference of thalamic_drive and hippocampal_inhibition.
    Set net_drive to that result.
    Calculate the difference of -70 and tuft_voltage.
    Calculate the sum of that difference and net_drive.
    Calculate the quotient of that sum divided by 24.
    Calculate the sum of tuft_voltage and that quotient.
    Set tuft_voltage to that result.
    Check if both plateau_timer is less than or equal to 0 and tuft_voltage is greater than or equal to -45 are true.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set plateau_timer to 80.
        Calculate the sum of plateau_count and 1.
        Set plateau_count to that result.
    End the conditional block.
    Set plateau_boost to 0.
    Check if plateau_timer is greater than 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of plateau_timer and 1.
        Set plateau_timer to that result.
        Set plateau_boost to 22.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of tuft_voltage and plateau_boost.
    Calculate the sum of that sum and 70.
    Calculate the product of that sum and 0.45.
    Calculate the difference of -70 and soma_voltage.
    Calculate the sum of that difference and that product.
    Calculate the quotient of that sum divided by 30.
    Calculate the sum of soma_voltage and that quotient.
    Set soma_voltage to that result.
    Check if soma_voltage is greater than or equal to -52.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set soma_voltage to -70.
        Calculate the sum of somatic_spikes and 1.
        Set somatic_spikes to that result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of step_count and 1.
    Set step_count to that result.
End the loop.
Display plateau_count to the console.
Display somatic_spikes to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: a two-compartment neuron with a regenerative dendritic event is a
# tiny differential-equation problem, and Python states it in the fewest lines.
DT = 0.5   # milliseconds per step
class RetrosplenialTuft:
    """Layer 5 retrosplenial neuron: apical tuft in layer 1, soma below."""
    def __init__(self):
        self.tuft = -70.0        # tuft membrane potential, millivolts
        self.soma = -70.0
        self.tau_t, self.tau_s = 12.0, 15.0
        self.plateau = 0.0       # milliseconds of plateau left to run
        self.plateau_thresh = -45.0
        self.coupling = 0.45     # fraction of tuft voltage reaching the soma
        self.spike_thresh = -52.0
        self.somatic_spikes = 0
        self.plateaus = 0
    def step(self, thalamic_mv, hippocampal_gaba_mv):
        # Anterior thalamic axons excite the tuft in layer 1; the hippocampal
        # GABAergic axon inhibits the same tuft in the same layer.
        drive = thalamic_mv - hippocampal_gaba_mv
        self.tuft += ((-70.0 - self.tuft) + drive) / self.tau_t * DT
        if self.plateau <= 0.0 and self.tuft >= self.plateau_thresh:
            self.plateau = 40.0          # NMDA plateau, tens of milliseconds
            self.plateaus += 1
        boost = 0.0
        if self.plateau > 0.0:
            self.plateau -= DT
            boost = 22.0                 # the plateau clamps the tuft depolarised
        self.soma += ((-70.0 - self.soma)
                      + self.coupling * (self.tuft + boost + 70.0)) / self.tau_s * DT
        if self.soma >= self.spike_thresh:
            self.soma = -70.0
            self.somatic_spikes += 1
def run(gaba_mv):
    cell = RetrosplenialTuft()
    for k in range(1200):                        # 600 milliseconds
        t = k * DT
        thal = 30.0 if (int(t) % 200) < 50 else 0.0   # thalamic bursts
        cell.step(thal, gaba_mv)
    return cell.plateaus, cell.somatic_spikes
for label, g in (("hippocampal axon silent", 0.0), ("hippocampal axon active", 14.0)):
    p, s = run(g)
    print("%-26s plateaus %d, somatic spikes %d" % (label, p, s))
# Output: silent -> 3 plateaus and 3 somatic spikes; active -> 0 and 0.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A GABA cell body sitting precisely on the stratum radiatum and stratum lacunosum-moleculare border whose axon is found, centimetres away, in layer one of the retrosplenial cortex, and never in the medial septum.


PART FOUR: AMYGDALA, OLFACTORY CORTEX, AND CLAUSTRUM

CHAPTER 60. AMYGDALA PRINCIPAL NEURONS (Threat-And-Reward Valuing Neuron) (COG)

The flinch you feel when a shadow moves the wrong way is written first into a few million spiny cells buried an inch behind your temple.

Principal is a committee word, telling a student only that the cell is numerous rather than what it does.

Threat-And-Reward records the discovery that this population is not a fear centre alone but two intermingled opposed groups, one answering to bad outcomes and one to good, which suppress each other through shared inhibitory partners.

Valuing names the work itself, the stamping of an emotional worth onto an event before that value is shipped out to the prefrontal cortex, the striatum and the amygdala's own output nucleus.

Neuron marks it as the spiny excitatory cell that makes up roughly eighty to ninety percent of the basolateral amygdala.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These excitatory workhorses of the basolateral amygdala bind a sensory pattern arriving from thalamus and cortex to whatever good or bad thing happened next, so that afterwards the cue alone drives them.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A pyramid-shaped body sends three to seven thick dendrites, the branching antennae that gather input, out in all directions rather than in a layer, crusted with spines.

SIZE:

The body is 15 to 25 micrometres across, a quarter the width of a hair, and the dendritic tree spans 300 to 600 micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Anatomy gives lateral, basal and accessory basal cells, while mouse genetics gives Rspo2 cells biased to bad outcomes and Ppp1r1b cells biased to good ones.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They fill the lateral, basal and accessory basal nuclei, plus the paralaminar nucleus, a thin shelf at the interface with entorhinal cortex and hippocampus.

Laterally they stop at the external capsule, a sheet of passing white matter, and medially at the intercalated clusters guarding the central nucleus.

NUMBER:

Roughly 8 to 10 million per hemisphere, so 16 to 20 million in both, from counts of 12.2 and 15.3 million neurons per amygdala, about 80 percent of them basolateral and 80 percent of those principal.

Confidence is moderate, and the total is near two hundredths of one percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

They rest 65 to 75 thousandths of a volt negative inside, show an input resistance of 100 to 250 megohms, fire under one spike per second, and adapt strongly.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Ten to twenty thousand excitatory contacts arrive from thalamus, temporal and prefrontal cortex, ventral hippocampus, insula and the pain-carrying parabrachial nucleus, checked by fewer but stronger contacts from parvalbumin and somatostatin interneurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is 0.3 to 0.8 micrometres wide, branches early, and leaves by the stria terminalis and the ventral amygdalofugal pathway at one to ten metres per second.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

They release glutamate, the main excitatory chemical, onto fast AMPA and slow NMDA receptors, named after alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid and N-methyl-D-aspartate, while dopamine, noradrenaline, serotonin and acetylcholine set the mood.

MYELINATION:

Long axons wear thin myelin, the fatty insulation that speeds conduction, and human amygdala myelination is unusually slow, still thickening into the third decade of life.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Energy use sits near the whole-brain average of about 30 micromoles of glucose per 100 grams each minute, spent mostly on holding the tree ready.

PLASTICITY:

This is the textbook site of associative learning, where pairing a sound with a shock strengthens the sound pathway by NMDA-receptor-dependent long-term potentiation.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Perineuronal nets, the lattice cages that lock synapses in place, wrap the local inhibitory cells densely and leave principal cells uncaged, keeping the excitatory population editable for life.

The neighbouring paralaminar nucleus holds immature neurons that keep maturing into principal cells through adolescence, a growth pool almost unknown in the adult forebrain.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the amygdala becomes a wire rather than a memory, still passing sights and sounds but unable to make anything neutral frightening or attractive.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Thin dendrites fire local NMDA spikes, small self-amplifying events that let one branch decide alone, and each somatic spike travels backwards up the tree to license active synapses to strengthen.

They build protein at the synapse and send endocannabinoids backwards to mute their own inhibition.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: sensory glutamate 0 to 20 nanosiemens, teaching signal 0 to 10 nanosiemens, local GABA 0 to 40 nanosiemens.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate to central amygdala, intercalated clusters, accumbens, prefrontal cortex and hippocampus at 0 to 40 hertz.

• STATE: membrane voltage, adaptation current, synaptic weights, calcium tags, branch voltages, slow excitability variable.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 0.070 volts, threshold minus 0.050 volts, resistance 100 to 250 megohms, 10000 to 20000 spines.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 to 40 milliseconds, adaptation 100 to 300 milliseconds, tag 1 to 10 seconds, consolidation 1 to 6 hours.

• FAILURE MODE: over-strengthened cue pathways give the intrusive threat memories of post-traumatic stress disorder.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether a neutral cue should be treated as a warning and to strengthen that warning when harm follows.
Create a number variable called membrane with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called cueWeight with initial value 0.005.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
For each timeStep in trialTimeline, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the product of cueWeight and the cue level.
    Set drive to that result.
    Calculate the sum of drive and the harm level.
    Set drive to that result.
    Calculate the sum of membrane and drive.
    Set membrane to that result.
    Check if membrane is greater than or equal to 0.020.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane to 0.
        Calculate the difference of 0.030 and cueWeight.
        Calculate the product of that result and 0.05.
        Calculate the sum of cueWeight and that result.
        Set cueWeight to that result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display cueWeight to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because plain loops with no libraries port directly into any simulator.

# Threat association in one basolateral amygdala principal neuron.
dt, tauM, tauW = 0.001, 0.030, 0.150
vRest, vThresh, bAdapt = -0.070, -0.050, 0.003
learnRate, wMax = 0.05, 0.030
def runTrial(cue, shock, weight):
    v, adapt, spikes = vRest, 0.0, 0
    for t in range(len(cue)):
        drive = weight * cue[t] + 0.050 * shock[t]   # learned cue plus innate harm
        v = v + dt * (vRest - v - adapt + drive) / tauM
        adapt = adapt - dt * adapt / tauW
        if v >= vThresh:
            v, spikes = vRest, spikes + 1
            adapt = adapt + bAdapt
            weight = weight + learnRate * cue[t] * (wMax - weight)   # Hebbian
    return spikes, weight
cue, silent = [1.0] * 600, [0.0] * 600
shock = [0.0] * 300 + [1.0] * 300
w = 0.005
before, w = runTrial(cue, silent, w)
for trial in range(3):
    paired, w = runTrial(cue, shock, w)
after, w = runTrial(cue, silent, w)
print(before, after, round(w, 4))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A spiny pyramid-like cell in a nucleus with no layers, firing rarely, adapting hard, and changing what it answers to only after the world teaches it that a sound means pain.

CHAPTER 61. AMYGDALA INTERCALATED CELLS (Safety-Learning Brake Neuron) (COG)

Wedged into the thin white seams between the amygdala's big nuclei sit clusters of tiny cells that decide whether fear is expressed or suppressed.

Intercalated means only inserted between, an address describing where the cells sit rather than what they achieve.

Safety-Learning names the occasion they own, extinction, the process by which an animal learns that a once-frightening cue is now harmless, since the ventromedial cluster is required for forming and recalling that safety memory while a second cluster pulls the other way during fear expression.

Brake names the mechanism, because their single output is inhibition, laid onto the amygdala's fear output nucleus so that a learned alarm is held back.

Neuron marks it as one of the tight clusters of small inhibitory cells wrapped around the basolateral amygdala, about ninety-five percent of them medium spiny cells with somas of nine to eighteen micrometres.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Small inhibitory neurons packed into islands, inserted between the larger nuclei rather than belonging to any of them, they release GABA, short for gamma-aminobutyric acid, across the path from the learning part of the amygdala to its output part.

They are the machinery of extinction, by which an outdated fear stops producing a reaction.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small round body carries three to five spiny dendrites that stay inside the island, and the cell develops like a striatal medium spiny neuron with the same Foxp2 transcription factor.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are only 10 to 15 micrometres across and the islands are 50 to 300 micrometres wide, small enough that one electrode pass can miss them entirely.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There is a main intercalated nucleus plus dorsal medial and lateral paracapsular clusters, and the dorsal and ventral medial clusters inhibit each other, flipping the amygdala between fear and extinction.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They live inside white matter, in the external capsule outside the basolateral amygdala and in the intermediate capsule between the basolateral amygdala and the central nucleus.

The main nucleus sits exactly at the ventral interface where the basal nucleus meets the central nucleus, and these are the only amygdala neurons whose job is defined by a boundary.

NUMBER:

The defensible bound is 0.1 to 0.4 million per hemisphere, from rodent island counts scaled to human volume and capped at a few percent of the 12 to 15 million amygdala neurons, well under one thousandth of one percent of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Input resistance is high at 200 to 500 megohms, adaptation is slight, firing holds at 40 to 80 hertz, and a constant background chloride current through delta-subunit GABA-A receptors sets their gain.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

They take glutamate from basolateral principal cells and the safety-signalling infralimbic cortex, plus dense dopamine and enkephalin fibres, and send GABA onto central amygdala output cells and onto each other.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are very thin and under a millimetre long, making dense baskets so inhibition lands within a few milliseconds of the excitation it must cancel.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA is their only transmitter, but they carry dense dopamine D1 and mu-opioid receptors, and activating either silences them, which is how opioids release fear circuits from restraint.

MYELINATION:

Their own axons are bare, and the oddity is the address rather than the wiring, since these unmyelinated cells live inside heavily myelinated fibre bundles.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Demand is modest because the cells are small, but the background chloride current is a standing cost, since every ion that leaks in must be pumped out using adenosine triphosphate.

PLASTICITY:

Extinction training strengthens the basolateral input onto these cells, and blocking that change leaves an animal able to learn fear but unable to shed it.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here are white matter glia, dominated by oligodendrocytes servicing passing axons rather than the astrocyte-rich neuropil of a normal nucleus.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the amygdala can still learn to be afraid but never to stop, so every therapy that works by safe repeated exposure is an attempt to talk to these cells.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Mutual inhibition between the medial clusters turns a graded input into a two-state switch rather than a dimmer, with the background chloride conductance as the volume knob.

Dopamine and opioids reach them by volume transmission, spilling into the tissue rather than acting at one synapse, and coupling lets a few cells pull in the rest.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from basolateral amygdala and infralimbic cortex 0 to 15 nanosiemens, cross-cluster GABA 0 to 30 nanosiemens.

• OUTPUTS: GABA onto central amygdala output cells, basolateral principal cells and the opposing cluster, 0 to 80 hertz.

• STATE: membrane voltage, background chloride conductance, one rate variable per cluster, basolateral input weight.

• PARAMETERS: resistance 200 to 500 megohms, rest minus 0.075 volts, cross-inhibition 1.4 normalised, background conductance 0.5 to 2 nanosiemens.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 25 milliseconds, inhibitory decay 5 to 15 milliseconds, cluster switching 50 to 200 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: weakened cells give fear that cannot be extinguished, the core of post-traumatic stress disorder and of relapse after exposure therapy.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether the fear brake is engaged by letting two intercalated clusters compete through mutual inhibition.
Create a number variable called dorsalRate with initial value 0.1.
Create a number variable called ventralRate with initial value 0.1.
Create a number variable called dorsalNet with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called ventralNet with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 2000 times.
    Calculate the product of 1.4 and ventralRate.
    Calculate the difference of the safety input and that result.
    Set dorsalNet to that result.
    Calculate the product of 1.4 and dorsalRate.
    Calculate the difference of the threat input and that result.
    Set ventralNet to that result.
    Check if dorsalNet is less than 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set dorsalNet to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if ventralNet is less than 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set ventralNet to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set dorsalRate to dorsalNet.
    Set ventralRate to ventralNet.
End the loop.
Display dorsalRate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because a two-variable rate model is the clearest statement of a mutual inhibition switch.

# Two intercalated clusters compete; the winner sets the fear state.
dt, tau, cross = 0.001, 0.020, 1.4
def clamp(x):
    return 0.0 if x < 0.0 else x
def step(dorsal, ventral, safety, threat, opioid):
    gain = 1.0 - opioid                      # opioids silence both clusters
    dorsal = dorsal + dt * (-dorsal + clamp(gain * safety - cross * ventral)) / tau
    ventral = ventral + dt * (-ventral + clamp(gain * threat - cross * dorsal)) / tau
    return clamp(dorsal), clamp(ventral)
dorsal, ventral = 0.1, 0.1
for i in range(2000):
    safety = 2.0 if i > 1000 else 0.2        # extinction training starts halfway
    dorsal, ventral = step(dorsal, ventral, safety, 0.8, 0.0)
    if i == 999:
        print("before", round(dorsal, 3), round(ventral, 3))
print("after ", round(dorsal, 3), round(ventral, 3))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A striatum-like cell smaller than its neighbours, living inside a white matter seam, drenched in opioid and dopamine receptors, whose only output is a veto.

CHAPTER 62. CENTRAL AMYGDALA NEURONS (Defence-Response Selecting Neuron) (COG)

If the basolateral amygdala is where fear is learned, the central amygdala is where fear is spoken, in the language of a stopped breath and a hammering heart.

Central Amygdala is a coordinate, not a job description.

Defence-Response names what its targets produce, the whole coordinated package of freezing, blood pressure change and even the breath-holding that accompanies a frozen animal assessing danger.

Selecting names the computation, because the lateral part of the nucleus does not simply pass fear along but chooses among competing reactions by disinhibiting one output line while holding the others shut.

Neuron marks it as the amygdala's inhibitory exit door, projecting to the hypothalamus and to hindbrain centres including the periaqueductal grey and the pre-Botzinger complex, the brainstem's breathing rhythm generator.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These inhibitory output neurons turn a learned emotional judgement into bodily action, releasing freezing, a jump in blood pressure, a surge of stress hormone, or an escape run.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

They resemble striatal medium spiny neurons, with a compact body, three to six moderately spiny dendrites and a dense local axon web, and medial division cells add one long descending axon.

SIZE:

Bodies are 12 to 20 micrometres across with fields of 200 to 400 micrometres, and the whole human central nucleus is a few cubic millimetres, smaller than a grain of rice.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The capsular, lateral and medial parts differ, and the lateral part holds opposing populations marked by protein kinase C delta, by somatostatin driving freezing, and by corticotropin releasing factor driving flight.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They occupy the capsular, lateral and medial divisions of the central nucleus, tucked dorsomedially just beneath the globus pallidus and substantia innominata.

Medially they do not stop at a border but blend into the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis through the sublenticular extended amygdala, while laterally they meet the intercalated clusters.

NUMBER:

About 0.36 million per hemisphere by direct stereological counting, so roughly 0.7 million in both, which is three percent of amygdala neurons and under one thousandth of one percent of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Three patterns dominate, late-firing cells that delay their first spike 100 to 300 milliseconds through a transient potassium current, regular spiking cells, and low-threshold bursting cells, all near 200 to 500 megohms.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from basolateral cells, intercalated clusters, the pain-carrying parabrachial nucleus, insula, paraventricular thalamus and the nucleus of the solitary tract, while outputs reach the periaqueductal grey for freezing, the lateral hypothalamus for blood pressure and the paraventricular hypothalamus for cortisol.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Descending axons are 0.5 to 1 micrometre wide and run by the ventral amygdalofugal pathway to the medulla, while dense short collaterals inside the lateral division carry the internal competition.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA is the fast transmitter, layered with peptides including somatostatin, corticotropin releasing factor, enkephalin, dynorphin and neurotensin, which need high firing rates, so the same cell says different things when calm and when alarmed.

MYELINATION:

Descending axons carry thin myelin, enough to reach the brainstem in tens of milliseconds, while local collaterals stay bare because a millisecond over a hundred micrometres costs nothing.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Peptide release is expensive in a particular way, since the large vesicles holding peptides can only be built in the cell body and shipped out, and chronic stress raises that bill for weeks.

PLASTICITY:

Parabrachial synapses onto lateral cells undergo long-term potentiation in models of persistent pain, a leading account of how an injury becomes chronic pain.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here are not passive, since their calcium signals are recruited during fear learning and shift the excitation reaching output cells, glial gatekeeping shown more clearly here than in most nuclei.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and the amygdala becomes a philosopher with no voice, still judging a thing dangerous while producing no freezing, no faster heart, no cortisol and no startle.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Mutual inhibition between subtypes turns a graded threat estimate into a discrete choice between freezing and fleeing, and peptides spread through tissue rather than crossing one synapse, so one busy cell shifts a neighbourhood for minutes.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from basolateral, parabrachial, insular and thalamic sources 0 to 20 nanosiemens, GABA from intercalated clusters and neighbours 0 to 40 nanosiemens.

• OUTPUTS: GABA and peptides to periaqueductal grey, lateral hypothalamus, paraventricular hypothalamus, vagal motor nucleus and locus coeruleus at 0 to 60 hertz.

• STATE: membrane voltage, potassium current availability, releasable peptide pool, subtype competition variable, slow expression level.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 0.072 volts, threshold minus 0.048 volts, delaying conductance 1 to 2 normalised, peptide release above about 10 hertz sustained.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 to 30 milliseconds, potassium recovery 100 to 300 milliseconds, peptide action seconds to minutes, autonomic response 0.5 to 5 seconds.

• FAILURE MODE: runaway parabrachial potentiation gives chronic pain, overactive corticotropin releasing factor cells give sustained anxiety and drug withdrawal states.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to let a central amygdala output cell fire only when a threat signal outlasts the delay set by its slow potassium current and beats the intercalated brake.
Create a number variable called membrane with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called availability with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spikeCount with initial value 0.
For each timeStep in stimulusWindow, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the difference of the basolateral input and 0.035.
    Set drive to that result.
    Calculate the product of availability and membrane.
    Calculate the difference of drive and that result.
    Calculate the quotient of that result divided by 50.
    Calculate the sum of membrane and that result.
    Set membrane to that result.
    Calculate the product of availability and 0.996.
    Set availability to that result.
    Check if membrane is greater than or equal to 0.024.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane to 0.
        Calculate the sum of spikeCount and 1.
        Set spikeCount to that result.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spikeCount to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the delaying potassium current becomes one explicit variable a reader can watch decay.

# Late-firing central amygdala output cell, gated by intercalated inhibition.
dt, tauM, tauA = 0.0005, 0.025, 0.120
vRest, vThresh = -0.072, -0.048
gA = 1.6
def output(blaDrive, itcBrake, steps):
    v, a, spikes = vRest, 1.0, 0             # a is delaying channel availability
    for i in range(steps):
        delaying = gA * a * (v - vRest)      # opposes depolarisation early on
        v = v + dt * ((vRest - v) + (blaDrive - itcBrake) - delaying) / tauM
        a = a - dt * a / tauA                # availability decays, cell escapes
        if v >= vThresh:
            v, spikes = vRest, spikes + 1
    return spikes
print("brake off", output(0.040, 0.000, 2000))
print("brake on ", output(0.040, 0.035, 2000))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

An inhibitory striatum-shaped cell that waits a fifth of a second before its first spike, carries a bag of peptides, and can stop your breath from inside the temporal lobe.

CHAPTER 63. PIRIFORM CORTEX SEMILUNAR CELLS AND SUPERFICIAL PYRAMIDAL CELLS (COG)

Smell is the one sense that reaches the cortex directly without being relayed through the thalamus, and these are the cells it lands on.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are the two excitatory principal classes of layer 2 of the piriform cortex, the largest piece of primary smell cortex, where the frontal and temporal lobes meet, and together they turn a chemical fragment into a recognised thing such as coffee, or smoke.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Semilunar cells have a half-moon body and, unusually for a cortical neuron, no basal dendrites, so they listen only upward, while superficial pyramidal cells keep basal dendrites too.

SIZE:

Bodies are 10 to 15 micrometres across, the apical dendrite runs 300 to 500 micrometres, and a superficial pyramidal cell carries 3000 to 5000 micrometres of dendrite.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The two named classes are the main subtypes, split by depth into layer 2a and 2b and by target, semilunar cells favouring the olfactory tubercle, superficial pyramidal cells favouring orbitofrontal cortex and amygdala.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They occupy layer 2 of anterior and posterior piriform cortex, with equivalents in periamygdaloid cortex and olfactory tubercle, and in humans the band breaks into islands.

The key interface is the limen insulae, bridging frontal and temporal lobes, and a second lies under layer 3 where this cortex becomes endopiriform nucleus and then claustrum.

NUMBER:

About 2 to 3 million layer 2 principal cells per hemisphere, so 4 to 6 million in both, perhaps a quarter of them semilunar, which is about six thousandths of one percent of the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Semilunar cells have higher input resistance and a weaker afterhyperpolarisation, the dip after a spike, so they sum inputs longer and adapt less than superficial pyramidal cells.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Bulb fibres in the lateral olfactory tract land on both classes in layer 1a, more strongly on semilunar cells, each cortical cell sampling a scattered subset of bulb channels rather than a map.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Associational axons run many millimetres, so the region behaves as one network with no map, while projection axons leave for orbitofrontal cortex, amygdala and entorhinal cortex.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Both release glutamate and both are modulated by acetylcholine from the diagonal band, which suppresses associational fibres while sparing incoming ones, switching the cortex between learning a smell and recognising one.

MYELINATION:

The lateral olfactory tract is heavily myelinated and delivers the bulb's message in milliseconds, while thin associational axons run the recurrent computation slower than its own input.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Energy use is high because the recurrent network idles even without an odour, and only sparse coding keeps the bill down.

PLASTICITY:

Associational synapses show strong long-term potentiation, and it is these, not the incoming fibres, that store what a smell means.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

What is special is a reserve of dormant immature neurons around layer 2, marked by doublecortin, persisting into adulthood in many mammals including primates as a slow structural reserve.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and smell becomes a detector without a dictionary, so you would know the air had changed but could not tell coffee from smoke.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Distal synapses force dendritic amplification so far contacts count as much as near ones, recurrent excitation completes an odour from a fragment, and global inhibition holds the active count steady.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: lateral olfactory tract glutamate 0 to 10 nanosiemens per active bulb channel, associational glutamate 0 to 20 nanosiemens, global GABA feedback 0 to 50 nanosiemens.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate to other piriform cells, orbitofrontal cortex, amygdala, entorhinal cortex and mediodorsal thalamus at 0 to 50 hertz, sparseness 3 to 15 percent.

• STATE: membrane voltage, adaptation current, associational weight matrix, global inhibition level, running population activity.
