Confidence is moderate: the widely repeated figure is one to two percent of striatal cells in both rodents and humans, giving a defensible range of one point one to two point two million, and this entry deliberately takes the low end so that the interneuron budget for the striatum is not overspent.

That is about zero point zero zero one five percent of the roughly eighty-six billion neurons in the human nervous system, so this cell type is rarer than one in sixty thousand neurons and yet is a household name in neurology.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting potential around minus sixty millivolts, which is twenty-five millivolts more depolarised than the spiny neurons and already close to threshold.

It has no true rest, because it is an autonomous pacemaker: a hyperpolarisation-activated cyclic nucleotide-gated current, called the h current and carried by HCN2 and HCN4 channels, switches on whenever the cell dips low and drags it back up, and a persistent sodium current finishes the climb to threshold.

Its action potential is broad, one to two milliseconds at half height, several times wider than a spiny neuron's.

Each spike is followed by a deep and long afterhyperpolarisation lasting one hundred to three hundred milliseconds, produced by calcium-activated potassium channels of the small-conductance, or SK, family, and this is what keeps the firing rate slow.

The pause after excitation is not simple fatigue: it requires a slowly inactivating Kv1-family potassium current, and it is promoted by dopamine acting on D2 receptors on this same cell.

The pause therefore has two causes at once, a fast glutamatergic burst from the thalamus that loads the Kv1 current, and a dopamine surge arriving at the same moment.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Its dominant excitatory input is not from cortex but from the thalamus, specifically the centromedian and parafascicular nuclei, which report salient and unexpected events.

Cortical input is present but weaker, which is unusual in the striatum and is the anatomical reason this cell reports surprise rather than intention.

It receives dopamine directly onto its soma and dendrites and carries both inhibitory D2 receptors and excitatory D5 receptors.

It receives GABA from the low-threshold-spiking interneurons and from tyrosine hydroxylase interneurons, and it inhibits and is inhibited by its own kind through mutual connections.

Its output contacts the spiny neurons, the fast-spiking interneurons, the low-threshold-spiking interneurons, and, crucially, the dopamine axons themselves.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin, unmyelinated, and enormous in total length, with estimates of tens of centimetres of axon packed inside a cubic millimetre of tissue.

Most of its half million varicosities do not form conventional synapses with a named partner; they release acetylcholine into the extracellular space, which then diffuses to receptors tens of micrometres away.

This is volume transmission, and it means the cell broadcasts rather than addresses.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Acetylcholine, made by choline acetyltransferase and broken down in the cleft by acetylcholinesterase, which the striatum contains in higher concentration than almost any other brain region.

It also co-releases glutamate, the main excitatory transmitter, from a subset of its terminals using the vesicular glutamate transporter VGLUT3, and there is evidence for GABA release as well, partly by taking up GABA made elsewhere.

A single cell that broadcasts an excitatory transmitter, an inhibitory transmitter and a slow modulator is a genuinely unusual object.

MYELINATION:

None.

Its axon is entirely unmyelinated, which suits a cell whose job is slow, diffuse, local broadcast rather than fast point-to-point delivery, and it means conduction within its arbour takes milliseconds to tens of milliseconds, spreading the signal out in time as well as space.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high and continuous, because the cell never stops firing and each broad spike admits a large amount of sodium and calcium that must be pumped back out.

Maintaining half a million release sites and their vesicle pools is a large protein and lipid burden, and the cell must synthesise acetylcholine continuously from choline, which it must import.

That choline demand is thought to make the cell vulnerable when membrane phospholipid turnover is disturbed, and this cell type is unusually sensitive to metabolic and oxidative stress.

PLASTICITY:

Its own synapses show long-term changes, but its greater importance is as the enabler of plasticity in other cells.

Acetylcholine acting on M1 muscarinic receptors on spiny neurons opens the door to long-term potentiation; the pause, by removing acetylcholine from M2 and M4 receptors, permits endocannabinoid-dependent long-term depression at the same synapses.

The burst-pause sequence therefore carves a temporal window, on the order of two hundred milliseconds wide, during which the striatum learns.

Its own excitability is reshaped by experience: after repeated cocaine exposure, and after motor learning, the size and reliability of its pause change measurably.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here do something not required elsewhere.

Acetylcholinesterase is expressed at extremely high levels in the striatal neuropil, much of it associated with glial and extracellular matrix elements, and it hydrolyses acetylcholine within milliseconds, which is what keeps a volume-transmitted signal from becoming a permanent bath.

Astrocytes supply the choline that the cell needs as raw material, and they express muscarinic receptors themselves, so they listen to the very transmitter they help supply.

Striatal astrocytes also respond to acetylcholine with calcium waves that then modulate nearby spiny neurons, making the glia an amplifier in this cell's broadcast circuit.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the striatum keeps working but loses its sense of occasion.

Ordinary and surprising events would be treated identically, because nothing would mark the moment at which something worth learning about had happened.

Dopamine release would also change, because acetylcholine acting on nicotinic receptors on dopamine axons drives a large fraction of dopamine release independently of the firing of dopamine cell bodies.

Concretely, the flavour lost is salience: the striatum's ability to punctuate a continuous stream of activity with the equivalent of an exclamation mark.

Clinically, the loss is not hypothetical, because post-mortem work in severe Tourette syndrome reports a fifty to sixty percent reduction in these cells in the associative striatum.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This is a cell whose most important output is chemical broadcast, not spikes to a named partner.

Acetylcholine released from its varicosities reaches nicotinic acetylcholine receptors sitting on the axons of dopamine neurons, and activating those receptors makes dopamine axons fire locally and release dopamine even when the dopamine cell bodies in the midbrain are quiet.

That is a striking piece of circuit engineering: one striatal interneuron can command the release of a neuromodulator whose source is two centimetres away.

The cell is autonomously rhythmic, so it is a clock as much as a computing element.

It shows dendritic calcium signalling and calcium-dependent potassium feedback that shape its own rhythm, and it is subject to ephaptic and gap-junction interaction with the low-threshold-spiking interneurons, with which it forms a mutual control loop.

It engages in retrograde signalling too: spiny neurons release endocannabinoids and dynorphin that act back on cholinergic terminals.

Its housekeeping is demanding, with continuous vesicle recycling across half a million sites and continuous synthesis of the enzyme that makes its transmitter.

Its firing regulates local blood flow through nitric oxide produced by neighbouring interneurons that it excites, coupling its activity to the fuel supply of the region.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS. Glutamate from centromedian and parafascicular thalamus, delivered as short high-probability bursts; weaker glutamate from cortex; dopamine from substantia nigra pars compacta and ventral tegmental area acting on inhibitory D2 and excitatory D5 receptors; GABA from low-threshold-spiking interneurons, tyrosine hydroxylase interneurons, neighbouring cholinergic cells and spiny neuron collaterals; serotonin from dorsal raphe; and it needs no input to fire.

• OUTPUTS. Acetylcholine released by volume transmission from roughly five hundred thousand varicosities per cell, acting on M1 muscarinic receptors on spiny neuron dendrites (excitatory), M4 on direct pathway cells (inhibitory), M2 autoreceptors on itself, and nicotinic alpha4-beta2 and alpha6 receptors on dopamine axons (driving dopamine release); glutamate through VGLUT3 at a subset of terminals; some GABA.

• STATE. Membrane voltage; h current activation; persistent sodium activation; intracellular calcium; small-conductance calcium-activated potassium channel activation; slow Kv1 inactivation, which is the pause variable; vesicular acetylcholine pool; local extracellular acetylcholine concentration in a diffusion field.

• PARAMETERS. Capacitance about eighty to one hundred and fifty picofarads; input resistance one hundred to four hundred megohms; resting potential about minus sixty millivolts; threshold about minus forty-five millivolts; spontaneous rate three to ten hertz; action potential half width one to two milliseconds; axonal varicosities about five hundred thousand.

• TIME CONSTANTS. Interspike interval one hundred to three hundred milliseconds; afterhyperpolarisation one hundred to three hundred milliseconds; h current activation hundreds of milliseconds; pause duration one hundred to three hundred milliseconds; acetylcholine hydrolysis under one millisecond at the enzyme, with an effective extracellular lifetime of a few milliseconds; muscarinic receptor signalling hundreds of milliseconds to seconds.

• FAILURE MODE. Reported fifty to sixty percent loss in severe refractory Tourette syndrome, associated with tics; in Parkinson's disease and in levodopa-induced dyskinesia the pause is lost entirely, which is why anticholinergic drugs such as trihexyphenidyl were among the first useful Parkinson's treatments; in dystonia the balance between acetylcholine and dopamine tips toward acetylcholine; over-activity contributes to compulsive and stereotyped behaviour, and reduced pausing is reported after chronic psychostimulant exposure.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to reproduce the burst and pause of a tonically active striatal cholinergic interneuron in response to a surprising event.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value negative sixty.
Create a number variable called pause current with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called thalamic input with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called pacemaker drive with initial value zero point one five.
Create a list variable called spike times with initial value empty list.
Create a boolean variable called condition holds with initial value false.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value zero.
While clock is less than fifteen hundred, repeat the following steps.
    Set thalamic input to the glutamate conductance arriving from the parafascicular nucleus during this millisecond.
    Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and pacemaker drive.
    Set membrane voltage to that sum.
    Calculate the product of pause current and zero point five.
    Calculate the difference of membrane voltage and that product.
    Set membrane voltage to that difference.
    Calculate the sum of membrane voltage and thalamic input.
    Set membrane voltage to that sum.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to negative forty five.
    Set condition holds to the result of that check.
    If condition holds is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add clock to the end of spike times.
        Set membrane voltage to negative fifty eight.
        Calculate the sum of pause current and zero point zero seven.
        Set pause current to that sum.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of pause current and zero point nine nine five.
    Set pause current to that product.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and one.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in spike times.
Display the total number of items in spike times to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because what has to be verified here is a shape in time, the burst followed by a silence, and printing the interspike intervals in plain numbers is the clearest possible check of that shape.

import math
DT = 0.1                                  # ms
C_M, E_L, G_L = 80.0, -60.0, 1.0          # pF, mV, nS
V_TH, V_RESET = -45.0, -58.0
G_H, E_H = 1.0, -30.0                     # h current: the pacemaker
G_NAP, E_NA = 0.25, 55.0                  # persistent sodium: finishes the climb
G_KV1, E_K, TAU_KV1 = 1.2, -90.0, 200.0   # slow Kv1 current: makes the pause
def simulate(ms=1500.0, burst_at=700.0, burst_ms=20.0):
    v, kv1, t, spikes = E_L, 0.0, 0.0, []
    while t < ms:
        # a salient event arrives from the parafascicular thalamus
        g_glu = 6.0 if burst_at <= t < burst_at + burst_ms else 0.0
        i_leak = G_L * (v - E_L)
        i_h = G_H / (1.0 + math.exp((v + 75.0) / 8.0)) * (v - E_H)
        i_nap = G_NAP / (1.0 + math.exp(-(v + 55.0) / 5.0)) * (v - E_NA)
        i_kv1 = G_KV1 * kv1 * (v - E_K)
        i_glu = g_glu * (v - 0.0)
        v += -(i_leak + i_h + i_nap + i_kv1 + i_glu) / C_M * DT
        kv1 -= kv1 / TAU_KV1 * DT          # the pause variable decays slowly
        if v >= V_TH:
            spikes.append(round(t, 1))
            v = V_RESET
            kv1 += 0.07                    # every spike loads the pause current
        t += DT
    return spikes
sp = simulate()
base = [s for s in sp if s < 700.0]
gap = max((sp[i + 1] - sp[i] for i in range(len(sp) - 1)), default=0.0)
print("baseline rate %.1f Hz over the first 700 ms" % (len(base) / 0.7))
print("longest silence anywhere in the run: %.0f ms (the pause)" % gap)

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

The biggest cell body in the striatum, bare dendrites reaching a millimetre, a broad spike with a deep long afterhyperpolarisation, ceaseless firing at three to ten hertz that nothing needs to trigger, and a synchronous silence when the world surprises you.

If a striatal recording never stops and then stops, it is this cell.

CHAPTER 70. STRIATAL PARVALBUMIN FAST-SPIKING INTERNEURONS (Split-Second Impulse-Curbing Neuron) (COG)

Fewer than one striatal cell in a hundred, wired to each other by holes in their membranes, firing two hundred times a second, and able to silence a spiny neuron with a single spike.

Parvalbumin is a calcium-binding protein used as a stain and never described any function at all.

Split-Second names the timescale, because this cell fires in bursts far faster than its neighbours and delivers its inhibition onto the cell body of the projection neuron, where a single well-timed input can cancel a spike outright.

Impulse-Curbing names the behavioural job, since suppressing these cells in animals releases premature, impulsive action initiation, and in people the same population is depleted in Tourette syndrome and dystonia.

Neuron marks it as a fast-spiking local inhibitory cell of the striatum.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is the striatum's veto cell.

Parvalbumin is a calcium-binding protein that this cell makes in large amounts, and it is used as the standard laboratory label for the type.

The cell takes glutamate, the main excitatory transmitter, straight from the cerebral cortex, and converts it within a couple of milliseconds into a powerful burst of inhibition aimed at the cell bodies of the surrounding medium spiny neurons.

This arrangement is called feedforward inhibition, meaning inhibition that arrives from the same source as the excitation and just behind it.

The functional consequence is a stopwatch: a spiny neuron gets a very short window, of the order of two to five milliseconds, in which cortical excitation can act before the inhibition catches up and shuts it down.

That window is what enforces coincidence detection in the striatum, and it is why only near-simultaneous cortical inputs can drive an action.

Because these cells contact hundreds of spiny neurons each and inhibit them at the cell body, where the axon begins, a single fast-spiking interneuron can determine whether a whole neighbourhood of projection neurons fires or not.

Behaviourally they are the cells that cancel the wrong movement, and their sudden recruitment underlies the ability to stop an action already in progress.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A medium to large aspiny or sparsely spiny cell with a round or fusiform body.

Its dendrites are smooth, moderately branched, and often varicose, forming a bushy tuft rather than the neat sphere of a spiny neuron.

Its axon branches profusely close to home and forms dense pericellular baskets, wreaths of terminals wrapped around the cell bodies and initial axon segments of spiny neurons, which is the most electrically effective place in the brain to place an inhibitory synapse.

Many of these cells carry a perineuronal net, a lattice of sugar-rich extracellular matrix that encases the soma and proximal dendrites like a string bag and restricts where new synapses can form.

SIZE:

Cell body ten to twenty-five micrometres, overlapping with but on average larger than the spiny neurons.

Dendritic field two hundred to five hundred micrometres.

Its local axonal arbour spans roughly three hundred to nine hundred micrometres and can contain thousands of terminals, contacting on the order of one hundred and thirty to five hundred spiny neurons.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Classic basket-forming cells that target spiny neuron somata, the majority.

• A smaller group whose axons target the axon initial segment specifically, the most powerful placement of all.

• Cells with restricted, dense local arbours versus cells with wide, sparse arbours; the wide type is better placed to coordinate across a whole functional territory.

• A dorsolateral, sensorimotor population that is denser and more strongly cortically driven, and a ventral population in the nucleus accumbens that is sparser and more strongly modulated by dopamine.

• In the primate and human striatum, the parvalbumin population is proportionally larger and more morphologically varied than in the rodent, and includes larger cells with longer dendrites that have no clean rodent equivalent.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Caudate nucleus, putamen, nucleus accumbens core and shell.

Distribution is strongly graded: densest dorsolaterally in the sensorimotor putamen and sparsest ventromedially, which mirrors the strength of the motor cortical projection.

They sit overwhelmingly in the matrix rather than the striosomes, and cells at the striosome-matrix interface send axons that respect the boundary, contributing to keeping the two compartments computationally separate.

NUMBER:

About zero point nine million in the two hemispheres, taken as roughly zero point eight percent of the striatum's one hundred and ten million neurons.

Confidence is low to moderate.

The commonly cited figure is about one percent of striatal neurons in rodents, and human counts are less certain but suggest a somewhat higher proportion, so a defensible range is zero point seven to two million; this entry takes a conservative value so that the striatal interneuron budget accommodates the remaining interneuron types.

That is about zero point zero zero one percent of the roughly eighty-six billion neurons in the human nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The name is literal.

Resting potential around minus seventy to minus eighty millivolts, a very short membrane time constant of one to three milliseconds, and an action potential lasting well under half a millisecond at half height, three to five times narrower than a spiny neuron's.

Sustained firing can exceed two hundred spikes per second with little slowing, which is possible only because of Kv3-family potassium channels that activate and deactivate extremely fast and so repolarise the membrane almost instantly after each spike.

Its characteristic laboratory signature is stuttering: at drive levels just above threshold it fires irregular high-frequency runs separated by unpredictable pauses, a behaviour traced to the slow inactivation and recovery of a Kv1-family current.

It is silent or near-silent at rest in the slice, but in the behaving animal it fires at ten to twenty spikes per second between events and bursts far higher during movement.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Cortical synapses onto this cell are stronger, more numerous per axon, and land closer to the cell body than the cortical synapses onto spiny neurons, which is precisely why cortex recruits it first.

It also receives thalamic input, dopaminergic input acting on D1-like and D2-like receptors, and cholinergic input from the tonically active neurons.

It receives GABA from other fast-spiking interneurons and from tyrosine hydroxylase interneurons, but almost none from spiny neurons, so the projection cells cannot easily switch off their own inhibitor.

On the output side it targets spiny neuron somata and proximal dendrites, contacts both D1 and D2 cells but statistically favours D2 cells, and also inhibits other fast-spiking interneurons.

The most important extra connection is not chemical at all.

These cells are joined to one another by gap junctions, protein channels made of connexin-36 that connect the interiors of two cells directly so that current flows between them, and each cell is coupled to something like thirty to forty percent of its immediate neighbours.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin, mostly unmyelinated within the striatum, with a very high branch density and a total length of several millimetres packed into a small volume.

Conduction is fast for an unmyelinated axon because the distances are short, so the delay from a cortical spike to inhibition arriving at a spiny neuron soma is only about two to four milliseconds.

Release probability at its terminals is high, and the synapses depress strongly with repeated use, so the veto is strongest at the onset of a cortical volley.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA only, acting on GABA-A receptors containing the alpha1 subunit, which produce unusually fast inhibitory currents that decay in a few milliseconds.

That fast decay is a design decision by evolution: the veto must be sharp and then gone, so the next window can open.

MYELINATION:

Largely absent, although some proximal axon segments of striatal parvalbumin cells receive short, patchy myelin sheaths, a phenomenon documented for cortical parvalbumin interneurons and increasingly reported in the striatum.

Where present, this patchy myelination is thought to sharpen spike timing rather than to speed long-distance conduction, since the axon never leaves the region.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Among the highest of any neuron in the brain.

Firing two hundred times a second means pumping sodium and potassium back across the membrane two hundred times a second, and parvalbumin interneurons everywhere are packed with mitochondria and stain heavily for cytochrome c oxidase, the terminal enzyme of the respiratory chain.

This is not a neutral fact: the same high oxidative load makes them the cells most damaged by oxidative stress, which is why parvalbumin interneuron loss appears in schizophrenia, in ischaemia and in ageing across many brain regions.

The perineuronal net around them is partly protective, buffering ions and shielding the cell from oxidative damage.

PLASTICITY:

Its cortical input synapses show long-term potentiation and depression, and dopamine acting on D1-like receptors increases the cell's excitability, so dopamine sharpens the veto.

The perineuronal net is itself the plasticity switch: it matures during adolescence, and its formation closes a critical period by physically preventing new synapses onto the cell.

Enzymes that digest the net, including matrix metalloproteinase 9, can reopen it, and this is one of the most actively pursued routes to restoring juvenile plasticity in adults.

Gap junction coupling strength is also modulated, notably by dopamine, so the degree of synchrony across the network is itself a variable the brain can set.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The specialised support here is not a cell but a structure that glia build: the perineuronal net, assembled from aggrecan, brevican, neurocan and hyaluronan, laid down with the participation of astrocytes and oligodendrocyte precursor cells and largely unique to this cell class.

It buffers the large potassium and sodium fluxes that high-frequency firing produces, holds inhibitory and excitatory synapses in fixed positions, and slows the diffusion of ions away from the membrane.

Oligodendrocyte precursor cells also form direct synapses onto these interneurons in some regions, an unusual neuron-to-glia synapse, and they are the cells that would lay the patchy myelin.

Astrocytes near them are enriched for the potassium channel Kir4.1, which clears the potassium that fast firing dumps into the extracellular space.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the striatum loses its edge in time.

The window in which cortical inputs must coincide would widen from a few milliseconds to tens of milliseconds, so the striatum would accept sloppy, poorly synchronised inputs and would trigger actions on weak evidence.

Competing actions would no longer be suppressed at their outset, so movements would overlap and intrude.

This has been tested: selectively killing these cells in the dorsal striatum of mice reproduces the deficit found in post-mortem Tourette tissue and produces excessive grooming and anxiety, and acutely silencing them produces dyskinesias, involuntary movements.

The flavour lost is precision, both in time and in choice.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The gap junction network is the headline.

Because current flows directly between coupled cells, subthreshold voltages are shared, and a population of these interneurons can lock into synchrony without any chemical synapse firing at all.

Whether that synchrony emerges depends on how hard the cortex is driving them, which makes the network a switch between independent local vetoes and one coordinated region-wide veto.

Electrical coupling also produces small ephaptic effects, in which the current from one cell's spike slightly biases its neighbour's membrane through the extracellular space, aided by the tight, low-volume geometry the perineuronal net imposes.

The cell buffers its own calcium with parvalbumin, a slow buffer, which paradoxically permits a build-up of calcium late in a spike train and so gives the terminals a form of short-term facilitation on top of their depression.

It engages in immune signalling: perineuronal net remodelling is driven by microglia and by the complement system, and this cell type is a specific target of neuroinflammation.

It also participates in metabolic coupling to astrocytes through the lactate shuttle more heavily than most neurons, simply because its demand is so high.

Finally, the perineuronal net is a physical memory: its density records the cell's developmental history and constrains everything the cell can learn afterwards.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS. Strong glutamate from cortical layer five, particularly motor and premotor cortex, with large-amplitude, perisomatically placed synapses; glutamate from centromedian and parafascicular thalamus; dopamine from substantia nigra pars compacta acting on D1-like and D2-like receptors; acetylcholine from cholinergic interneurons acting on nicotinic receptors; GABA from other fast-spiking cells and from tyrosine hydroxylase interneurons; direct electrical current from thirty to forty percent of its immediate parvalbumin neighbours through connexin-36 gap junctions, with a coupling coefficient of about one to five percent.

• OUTPUTS. Fast GABA onto the somata and axon initial segments of one hundred and thirty to five hundred spiny neurons, favouring D2-type cells; GABA onto other fast-spiking interneurons; electrical current back through the same gap junctions.

• STATE. Membrane voltage; Kv3 activation; Kv1 slow inactivation, which is the stuttering variable; sodium channel availability; free and parvalbumin-bound calcium; short-term depression state of each output terminal; gap junction currents to each coupled neighbour.

• PARAMETERS. Capacitance about twenty to sixty picofarads; input resistance one hundred to three hundred megohms; membrane time constant one to three milliseconds; resting potential minus seventy to minus eighty millivolts; threshold about minus forty millivolts; maximum sustained rate two hundred hertz and above; gap junction conductance about zero point one to one nanosiemens per pair.

• TIME CONSTANTS. Membrane one to three milliseconds; action potential half width zero point two to zero point five milliseconds; refractory period under one millisecond; GABA-A alpha1 inhibitory current decay two to five milliseconds; cortex-to-inhibition latency two to four milliseconds; short-term depression recovery one hundred to five hundred milliseconds; Kv1 slow inactivation recovery hundreds of milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE. Roughly forty percent bilateral loss reported post-mortem in severe refractory Tourette syndrome, associated with tics; acute silencing produces dyskinesias in animals; loss of the perineuronal net and of parvalbumin expression is a robust finding in schizophrenia and disrupts the timing of action selection; in Parkinson's disease these cells become abnormally entrained to beta-band oscillations near twenty hertz and help propagate them; they are highly vulnerable to oxidative stress and to loss of blood flow.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to impose a short coincidence window on a spiny neuron using feedforward inhibition from a fast-spiking interneuron.
Create a number variable called cortical volley time with initial value one hundred.
Create a number variable called inhibition delay with initial value three.
Create a number variable called inhibition duration with initial value five.
Create a number variable called spiny excitation with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called spiny inhibition with initial value zero.
Create a boolean variable called interneuron fired with initial value false.
Create a boolean variable called spiny fired with initial value false.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value zero.
While clock is less than three hundred, repeat the following steps.
    Check if clock is equal to cortical volley time.
    Set interneuron fired to the result of that check.
    If interneuron fired is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set spiny excitation to one point two.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Calculate the product of spiny excitation and zero point eight.
        Set spiny excitation to that product.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of cortical volley time and inhibition delay.
    Check if clock is greater than or equal to that sum.
    Set interneuron fired to the result of that check.
    Calculate the sum of cortical volley time and inhibition delay.
    Calculate the sum of that sum and inhibition duration.
    Check if clock is less than that sum.
    Set spiny fired to the result of that check.
    Check if both interneuron fired and spiny fired are true.
    Set spiny fired to the result of that check.
    If spiny fired is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set spiny inhibition to one point five.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set spiny inhibition to zero.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of spiny excitation and spiny inhibition.
    Check if that difference is greater than one.
    Set spiny fired to the result of that check.
    If spiny fired is true, then perform the following actions.
        Display clock to the console.
    End the conditional block.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and one.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because a whole-striatum model must step roughly a million cells that fire up to two hundred times a second while exchanging gap-junction current every time step, which forces a sub-millisecond step size and makes interpreter overhead the dominant cost.

/* Two electrically coupled striatal fast-spiking interneurons. Build with:
   cc -O2 -o fsi fsi.c -lm    and run with: ./fsi                        */
#include <stdio.h>
#define N 2
#define DT 0.025          /* ms, forced by 0.2 ms spikes */
#define STEPS 40000       /* 1000 ms of simulated time */
#define C_M 20.0          /* pF, small compact interneuron */
#define G_L 10.0          /* nS, giving a 2 ms membrane time constant */
#define E_L (-70.0)       /* mV */
#define V_TH (-40.0)      /* mV */
#define V_RESET (-55.0)   /* mV */
#define T_REF 0.5         /* ms; Kv3 channels repolarise this fast */
#define G_GAP 0.5         /* nS, connexin-36 electrical synapse */
int main(void) {
    double v[N], refr[N], drive[N], vn[N];
    int count[N], i, s;
    for (i = 0; i < N; i++) { v[i] = E_L; refr[i] = 0.0; count[i] = 0; }
    drive[0] = 320.0;     /* pA of cortical drive, just above rheobase */
    drive[1] = 310.0;     /* the partner is driven slightly less hard  */
    for (s = 0; s < STEPS; s++) {
        for (i = 0; i < N; i++) {            /* compute all updates first */
            int j = 1 - i;                   /* the coupled neighbour     */
            double i_gap = G_GAP * (v[j] - v[i]);
            double i_leak = G_L * (E_L - v[i]);
            if (refr[i] > 0.0) { vn[i] = V_RESET; refr[i] -= DT; }
            else vn[i] = v[i] + (i_leak + i_gap + drive[i]) / C_M * DT;
        }
        for (i = 0; i < N; i++) {            /* then commit them together */
            v[i] = vn[i];
            if (v[i] >= V_TH) { v[i] = V_RESET; refr[i] = T_REF; count[i]++; }
        }
    }
    printf("cell 0 fired %d Hz, cell 1 fired %d Hz\n", count[0], count[1]);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

A parvalbumin-filled, perineuronal-net-wrapped, aspiny cell with a half-millisecond spike, sustained firing above two hundred hertz, stuttering just above threshold, baskets around spiny neuron cell bodies, and electrical coupling to its neighbours.

Nothing else in the striatum is this fast, and nothing else is wired to its own kind by direct electrical contact.

CHAPTER 71. STRIATAL SOMATOSTATIN, NITRIC OXIDE SYNTHASE, AND NEUROPEPTIDE Y LOW-THRESHOLD-SPIKING INTERNEURONS (Free-Spreading Messenger Neuron) (COG)

This is the only cell in the movement centre of your brain that speaks in a gas.

The old label was a committee minute listing three separate stains, and about seventy-three percent of the cells carrying any one of them carry all of them, so the list was largely redundant.

Free-Spreading names the unique tool and its consequence together, because this is the only neuron in the striatum that makes nitric oxide, a gas that leaks straight through membranes and acts on whatever is nearby rather than travelling down an axon to a chosen partner.

Messenger names the role that follows, since its influence spreads broadly over surrounding tissue and local blood vessels rather than being delivered to a named recipient.

Neuron marks it as a low-threshold-spiking interneuron with an unusually large dendritic and axonal spread and very high input resistance, so that a small input makes it fire.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The striatum is the big input gateway of the basal ganglia, the deep clump of brain tissue that chooses which movement or habit runs next.

This cell is a striatal interneuron, meaning a small local cell whose wiring stays inside the striatum instead of leaving it.

It carries three chemical badges at once: somatostatin, a small signalling protein; neuropeptide Y, another small signalling protein; and neuronal nitric oxide synthase, the enzyme that manufactures nitric oxide gas.

Neuroscientists usually call it the low-threshold-spiking interneuron, or the persistent low-threshold-spike cell, because it fires a burst at unusually gentle levels of excitation.

Its job is to be the striatum's slow, diffuse, chemical weather system.

Where other inhibitory cells deliver a sharp private veto, this one leaks a cloud of gas and peptides across a wide patch of tissue and changes how everything inside that patch learns.

It is the only known source of nitric oxide in the striatum, which makes it irreplaceable.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is oval or spindle-shaped and looks unremarkable under a microscope until you stain for its enzyme.

Three to five dendrites, the branching antennae that collect incoming signals, leave the cell body and travel long distances with very few branch points.

The dendrites are aspiny or sparsely spiny, meaning they lack the dense carpet of little mushroom-shaped input knobs that the striatum's main projection cells wear.

The axon, the single output cable, leaves the cell body or a proximal dendrite and breaks into a loose, sprawling, thin-fibred web that covers far more territory than the dendrites do.

Under the classic NADPH-diaphorase stain, a simple chemical reaction that marks nitric oxide synthase, these cells light up as a scattered constellation across the whole striatum.

SIZE:

The cell body measures roughly 12 to 25 micrometres across, which is medium-sized for a striatal neuron and about one fifth the width of a human hair.

The dendritic field spans about 300 to 600 micrometres.

The axonal field is far larger, commonly exceeding 1 millimetre and in some reconstructions covering several cubic millimetres of striatum.

Nitric oxide gas itself diffuses about 100 to 200 micrometres from its source before it is destroyed, so the cell's true zone of influence is a sphere, not a wire.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

At least two families carry neuropeptide Y in the striatum, and they are not the same cell.

• The persistent low-threshold-spike cell described here, which carries all three badges and has long, sparse, far-reaching processes.

• The neuropeptide Y neurogliaform cell, which has a dense, compact, bushy axon like a tangle of wool, does not usually make nitric oxide, and produces slow blanket inhibition through gamma-aminobutyric acid, the brain's main inhibitory chemical.

Single-cell genetic surveys of the human dorsal striatum published in 2024 split the somatostatin family further still, so the two-way split above should be treated as the coarse, reliable version rather than the final answer.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It is scattered through the entire dorsal striatum, which in humans means the caudate nucleus and the putamen, and it continues without a break into the ventral striatum and the nucleus accumbens.

It is present in both the striosome compartment, the patchy islands of striatal tissue, and the matrix, the sea around them, with a modest preference for the matrix.

Density is higher near the border with the internal capsule, the great white-matter cable bundle that separates caudate from putamen, so this cell sits directly at the grey-matter-to-white-matter interface.

The same cell type reappears in the olfactory tubercle at the ventral edge of the striatal continuum.

NUMBER:

The human dorsal striatum holds roughly 110 million neurons, of which about 95 percent are medium spiny projection neurons, leaving about 5.5 million interneurons of all kinds.

Careful rat counts put the somatostatin, nitric oxide synthase, and neuropeptide Y interneurons at about 0.8 percent of all striatal neurons, and human post-mortem work finds them at under 1 percent.

Scaling that to the human striatum gives a best estimate near 1 million cells, with a defensible range of 500 thousand to 1.7 million.

Confidence is moderate for the fraction and low for the absolute human count, because no unbiased stereological census of this subtype in human tissue exists.

That is about 0.5 percent of the roughly 200 million neurons of the basal ganglia and about 0.001 percent of the 86 billion neurons in the whole nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Input resistance, which measures how strongly a cell's voltage responds to a given trickle of current, is extraordinarily high here, commonly 500 to 1,000 megaohms and sometimes higher.

A tiny input therefore produces a large voltage swing, so this cell is the striatum's hair trigger.

The resting voltage sits depolarised, often near minus 56 millivolts, close enough to threshold that the cell fires spontaneously without any input at all, typically at 1 to 10 spikes per second.

Its signature is the low-threshold calcium spike, a slow hump of voltage carried by T-type calcium channels that must first be released from inactivation by a period of hyperpolarisation.

On top of that sits a persistent sodium current that produces plateau potentials, long shoulders of sustained depolarisation lasting hundreds of milliseconds, during which the cell fires a ragged train of action potentials.

The result is a cell that answers a brief, weak input with a long, self-sustaining reply.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from the cerebral cortex, from the thalamus, from dopamine axons of the substantia nigra pars compacta, and from serotonin axons of the dorsal raphe nucleus, which strongly inhibit it.

Local cholinergic interneurons, the giant acetylcholine-releasing cells of the striatum, excite it, and the favour is returned, so the two form a mutually reinforcing pair.

Outputs are unusual in their targeting: gamma-aminobutyric acid synapses land on the dendrites and cell bodies of medium spiny projection neurons and on other interneurons, while nitric oxide is released without a synapse at all.

Some somatostatin cells in the striatum release glutamate, the main excitatory chemical, onto a different set of targets from the ones they inhibit, so a single cell can push and pull at once.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thin, roughly 0.2 to 0.5 micrometres in diameter, and conducts slowly at well under 1 metre per second.

It branches sparsely but travels far, and boutons, the small swellings where transmitter is released, are strung along it at wide intervals.

Many boutons sit next to no obvious postsynaptic partner, which is exactly what a cell built for chemical broadcasting should look like.

The axon never leaves the striatum.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

It releases gamma-aminobutyric acid, which opens chloride channels and hushes the target cell within milliseconds.

It releases neuropeptide Y, which acts on Y1 and Y2 receptors, damps glutamate release, and is broadly protective and anti-seizure.

It releases somatostatin, which acts on the sst1 to sst5 receptor family and slows target cells over seconds.

Above all it makes nitric oxide from the amino acid arginine, and because nitric oxide is a small uncharged gas it simply diffuses through membranes, entering neighbouring cells and activating soluble guanylyl cyclase, an enzyme that makes the second messenger cyclic guanosine monophosphate.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable in the usual sense, and the reason matters.

This cell's axon is unmyelinated, because myelin, the fatty insulation that speeds conduction, is reserved almost entirely for long projection axons, and this axon never leaves its home nucleus.

Slow conduction is not a defect here, since the cell's message is a diffusing gas whose timescale is hundreds of milliseconds anyway.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Baseline demand is modest because the cell is small, fires slowly, and supports no long axon.

The exception is nitric oxide synthesis, which consumes nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate, oxygen and arginine, and which is switched on by calcium entering through N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors, a class of glutamate receptor that admits calcium.

Peptide manufacture adds a further load, because somatostatin and neuropeptide Y must be built in the cell body, packaged, and carried out along the axon, unlike gamma-aminobutyric acid which is made locally.

PLASTICITY:

The nitric oxide this cell releases is a required ingredient for long-term depression at corticostriatal synapses, the weakening of cortex-to-striatum connections that lets the striatum unlearn a habit.

The pathway runs nitric oxide to cyclic guanosine monophosphate to protein kinase G inside the receiving medium spiny neuron.

Dopamine acting on D1 and D5 receptors on this interneuron triggers the nitric oxide release, so this cell is one of the hands through which dopamine rewrites the striatum.

The cell's own synapses also change strength, and its excitability is tuned over hours by neuropeptide signalling.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes, the star-shaped support cells, do something specific here: they are direct targets of the gas, because nitric oxide crosses into them and alters their calcium signalling and their glutamate uptake.

Astrocytes also supply the arginine this cell needs, since neurons are poor at regenerating arginine and depend on the astrocytic citrulline-to-arginine pathway to keep nitric oxide production going.

Microglia, the brain's resident immune cells, are held in check by nitric oxide at low concentrations and provoked by it at high concentrations, so the glial mood of a striatal neighbourhood partly reflects this cell's firing rate.

Oligodendrocytes are not specially involved, since the axon is unmyelinated.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this one cell type and the striatum keeps moving but stops breathing properly and stops forgetting properly.

The gas is gone, so local blood vessels no longer dilate on demand and the tissue loses its fast metabolic response to activity.

Long-term depression at cortex-to-striatum synapses is crippled, so old habits stop being erasable and the striatum becomes a place that can only add.

Gap junction coupling between striatal cells, which nitric oxide opens, falls away, and the tissue loses a layer of slow electrical gossip.

Practically, the region would become rigid: still capable of selecting an action, no longer capable of quietly revising the selection.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Volume transmission is the headline: this cell releases a messenger with no synapse, no receptor on the outside of the target, and a sphere of influence roughly 100 to 200 micrometres wide.

It controls local blood flow, because nitric oxide relaxes the smooth muscle of arterioles, making this interneuron a direct participant in neurovascular coupling and therefore in what a functional brain scan actually measures.

It opens gap junctions, the protein tunnels that let ions pass directly between cells, so cortical input can change the electrical connectedness of striatal tissue through this cell.

It signals to the immune system, since nitric oxide is a classic microglial cue.

It has plateau potentials and dendritic calcium electrogenesis, so its dendrites perform local computation rather than passively summing.

It resists excitotoxic death: despite carrying working N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors, it fails to generate damaging mitochondrial reactive oxygen species when overstimulated, which is why it is one of the very few striatal populations spared in Huntington's disease.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Cortical glutamate, event rate 0 to 50 hertz, excitatory conductance 0 to 5 nanosiemens.

◦ Thalamic glutamate, event rate 0 to 30 hertz.

◦ Cholinergic interneuron acetylcholine, producing slow depolarisation of 2 to 8 millivolts.

◦ Dopamine at D1 and D5 receptors, concentration 5 to 500 nanomolar, gating nitric oxide output.

◦ Serotonin from dorsal raphe, inhibitory, reducing firing rate by up to 100 percent.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Gamma-aminobutyric acid onto medium spiny neuron dendrites and somata, and onto fast-spiking interneurons.

◦ Nitric oxide, released as a concentration field, peak 10 to 100 nanomolar, radius 100 to 200 micrometres.

◦ Somatostatin and neuropeptide Y, released only above a burst threshold.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage in millivolts.

◦ T-type calcium channel availability, 0 to 1.

◦ Persistent sodium plateau flag, boolean.

◦ Intracellular calcium concentration in micromolar.

◦ Nitric oxide pool in nanomolar.

◦ Peptide vesicle stock, 0 to 1.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Input resistance 500 to 1,000 megaohms.

◦ Resting voltage minus 56 millivolts, spike threshold about minus 45 millivolts.

◦ Spontaneous rate 1 to 10 hertz.

◦ Nitric oxide diffusion coefficient about 3,300 square micrometres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane time constant 20 to 40 milliseconds.

◦ T-type de-inactivation about 50 to 100 milliseconds.

◦ Plateau potential duration 200 to 1,000 milliseconds.

◦ Nitric oxide lifetime about 100 to 500 milliseconds.

◦ Peptide replenishment tens of seconds to minutes.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss of nitric oxide signalling blocks corticostriatal long-term depression and is implicated in the rigid, over-consolidated habits of obsessive-compulsive disorder and in levodopa-induced dyskinesia.

◦ Messenger ribonucleic acid for nitric oxide synthase and somatostatin falls in the striatum in Huntington's disease even though the cells themselves survive, so the survivors go quiet rather than dying.

◦ Excess nitric oxide combines with superoxide to form peroxynitrite, a highly damaging molecule that injures neighbouring cells.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a striatal low-threshold-spiking interneuron that fires a rebound burst after inhibition and releases a diffusing cloud of nitric oxide.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value -56.
Create a number variable called calcium channel availability with initial value 0.2.
Create a number variable called nitric oxide with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called burst record with initial value empty list.
While step is less than 4000, repeat the following steps.
    Create a number variable called inhibition with initial value 0.
    Check if both step is greater than 500 and step is less than 2500 is true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set inhibition to -14.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if voltage is less than -70.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of calcium channel availability and 0.01.
        Set calcium channel availability to that result.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Calculate the product of calcium channel availability and 0.995.
        Set calcium channel availability to that result.
    End the conditional block.
    Create a number variable called rebound drive with initial value 0.
    Check if both voltage is greater than -62 and calcium channel availability is greater than 0.5 is true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set rebound drive to 18.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of inhibition and rebound drive.
    Set voltage to the sum of voltage and that result multiplied by 0.05.
    Check if voltage is greater than -45.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add step to the end of burst record.
        Calculate the sum of nitric oxide and 1.
        Set nitric oxide to that result.
        Set voltage to -55.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of nitric oxide and 0.999.
    Set nitric oxide to that result.
    Calculate the sum of step and 1.
    Set step to that result.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in burst record.
Display that number to the console.
Display nitric oxide to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because this cell's behaviour is a small system of slow differential equations, and Python reads almost like the biology while running anywhere without extra libraries.

import math

dt = 0.1                  # milliseconds per step
v = -56.0                 # membrane voltage, millivolts
h = 0.2                   # T-type calcium channel availability, 0 to 1
no = 0.0                  # nitric oxide pool, arbitrary units
spikes = []

for step in range(4000):
    t = step * dt
    # a burst of pallidal-style inhibition from 50 ms to 250 ms
    i_inh = -22.0 if 50.0 <= t < 250.0 else 0.0
    # T-type channels recover only when the cell is hyperpolarised
    h_inf = 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp((v + 78.0) / 5.0))
    h += dt * (h_inf - h) / 60.0
    # the low-threshold calcium current needs voltage AND availability
    m_inf = 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp(-(v + 58.0) / 6.0))
    i_t = 2.6 * m_inf * h * (120.0 - v)
    # leak toward a depolarised rest: this is a very high-resistance cell
    i_leak = 0.06 * (-56.0 - v)
    v += dt * (i_leak + i_t + 0.06 * i_inh) / 0.35
    if v > -45.0:
        spikes.append(round(t, 1))
        no += 1.0             # every spike lets calcium in, which makes gas
        v = -55.0
    no -= dt * no / 300.0     # nitric oxide is destroyed in a few hundred ms

print("spike count:", len(spikes))
print("first spikes after release (ms):", [s for s in spikes if s > 250.0][:5])
print("nitric oxide remaining:", round(no, 3))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only striatal cell that stains for NADPH-diaphorase, the only one that makes nitric oxide, and the only one that answers a pause with a slow calcium hump followed by a plateau hundreds of milliseconds long.

If a striatal cell has an input resistance near 1,000 megaohms, carries somatostatin, neuropeptide Y and nitric oxide synthase together, and is still alive in a Huntington's disease brain where the projection cells around it have vanished, it is this one.

CHAPTER 72. STRIATAL CALRETININ INTERNEURONS (Human-Enriched Quiet Sentinel Neuron) (COG)

In the human striatum the most numerous local helper cell is also the one we understand least, and it is defined by a protein whose only job is to mop up calcium.

Honesty comes first here, because the function of this cell is genuinely not established and inventing one would be worse than admitting the gap.

Human-Enriched records the single solid fact that survives every study, that calretinin-expressing cells are by far the most abundant interneuron class in the human and primate striatum while being scarce in rodents, which is why they are routinely left out of circuit models built on mice.

Quiet Sentinel states the position without overclaiming the role, since the cell is present in force, is inhibitory, and is watching, while its circuit function remains unmapped.

Neuron marks it as an interneuron and not one population but at least three structurally and molecularly distinct types hiding under a single stain.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is a small inhibitory cell that lives entirely inside the striatum, the big input structure of the basal ganglia that sits deep under the cortex and turns intention into action.

Inhibitory means it releases gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main "quieten down" chemical messenger.

It is named for calretinin, a calcium-binding protein, which is a molecule that grabs free calcium ions inside the cell and holds them, acting like a sponge.

That sponge matters because every time a neuron fires, calcium floods in, and calcium is both a signal and a poison.

By soaking up that calcium the cell can fire long trains without triggering the calcium-activated potassium currents that normally slow a neuron down.

Its day job appears to be shaping the timing of the striatum's output cells, but its precise circuit role is still genuinely unsettled, and any honest account must say so.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is round or spindle-shaped, with dendrites, the branching input antennae, that are aspiny or only sparsely spiny.

Sparsely spiny means the dendrites lack the dense forest of little mushroom-shaped bumps that the striatum's main output neurons carry.

In rodents the dendrites are often tortuous and wandering; in primates they are more often straight and bipolar, meaning two main dendrites leave the cell body in opposite directions.

The axon, the output cable, stays local and branches into a modest bush a few hundred micrometres across.

SIZE:

In monkey and human striatum, calretinin cells fall into three size bands: small cells with a cell body 10 to 12 micrometres across, medium cells 10 to 20 micrometres across, and large cells 25 to 40 micrometres across.

A micrometre is one thousandth of a millimetre, so even the large ones are far smaller than a full stop on this page.

In rodents the equivalent classes are tighter: small cells 7 to 11 micrometres, medium cells 12 to 15 micrometres, and large cells 12 to 20 micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Three molecular subtypes are now recognised, and they map onto the size bands.

• Small cells that also carry secretagogin (Scgn) and the transcription factor SP8, with tortuous partly spiny dendrites, biased towards the front of the striatum.

• Medium cells with neither secretagogin nor SP8 nor Lhx7, with simple bipolar dendrites, spread evenly through the striatum.

• Large cells that carry Lhx7, with thick dendrites that branch rarely; in primates most of these giant calretinin cells also carry choline acetyltransferase (ChAT), the enzyme that makes acetylcholine, so they are really cholinergic cells wearing a calretinin coat.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives throughout the caudate nucleus and the putamen, the two halves of the dorsal striatum, and throughout the nucleus accumbens, the ventral striatum.

It is found both in the striosomes, which are chemically distinct patches, and in the surrounding matrix, and it is one of the few striatal cell types that does not respect that border strongly.

The small secretagogin-positive subtype is concentrated rostrally, meaning towards the front of the striatum near the head of the caudate.

Calretinin cells also crowd the interface between the striatum and the adjacent structures: they are dense in the subcallosal streak beneath the corpus callosum, and along the border zone where the putamen meets the external capsule.

NUMBER:

I allocate about 2.5 million calretinin interneurons across both striata, with a defensible range of 2 to 3.3 million.

That is roughly 45 to 60 percent of the striatum's non-projecting cells, taken from a total striatal budget of 110 million neurons of which about 95 percent are medium spiny projection neurons.

Confidence here is low to moderate, and there is an honest tension in the literature: stereological counting in monkeys puts calretinin cells at about 57 percent of striatal interneurons but also at about 8 percent of all striatal neurons counted, which if applied to humans would give nearly 9 million and would burst the conventional 5 percent interneuron allowance.

I hold to the lower figure to stay inside the regional budget, and flag the higher primate estimate as a real upper bound rather than pretending the matter is settled.

At 2.5 million this type is about 1.25 percent of the roughly 200 million neurons of the basal ganglia, and about 3 in every 100,000 of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the human brain.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Honesty first: the calretinin cell is the least well characterised electrically of all striatal interneurons, because no clean genetic handle exists to target it in living tissue.

What recordings exist describe a cell with high input resistance, roughly 300 to 800 megaohms, meaning a small injected current produces a large voltage swing.

Resting membrane potential sits around minus 70 to minus 60 millivolts, and action potentials, the brief electrical spikes that are the neuron's output, are relatively narrow at 0.6 to 1.0 milliseconds wide.

Firing is generally low threshold and adapting, and many cells show a depolarising sag, a slow drift back upward when hyperpolarised, which is the signature of the hyperpolarisation-activated cyclic nucleotide-gated (HCN) channel.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from the cortex, from the thalamus by way of the intralaminar nuclei, and from dopamine axons of the substantia nigra pars compacta.

Outputs go onto the dendrites and cell bodies of medium spiny neurons, the striatum's projection cells, and onto other interneurons.

Calretinin cells contact cholinergic interneurons and are contacted by them, forming a small mutual loop that is thought to set the striatum's background acetylcholine tone.

Unlike parvalbumin basket cells, calretinin cells appear to avoid the axon initial segment, so they modulate rather than veto their targets.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thin, under 0.5 micrometres in diameter, and stays within a sphere of roughly 200 to 500 micrometres around the parent cell body.

Conduction is slow, on the order of 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second, which is walking pace, so local delays of 0.5 to 2 milliseconds are typical.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The small and medium subtypes release GABA and are therefore inhibitory.

The large Lhx7-positive subtype, which in primates mostly also carries choline acetyltransferase, releases acetylcholine, a modulatory transmitter that changes how excitable other cells are rather than directly commanding them.

Calretinin itself is not a transmitter; it is a buffer sitting inside the cytoplasm.

MYELINATION:

Essentially none.

Striatal interneuron axons are unmyelinated, meaning they lack the fatty insulating wrap that speeds conduction, which is why they are slow and why they are cheap to run.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest by striatal standards, because the cell fires at low rates and has a small membrane area.

The calretinin buffer itself costs energy indirectly: calcium that is bound must eventually be pumped out, and calcium pumping is one of the largest single energy costs in any neuron.

PLASTICITY:

Little is known, and inventing detail here would be dishonest.

What is documented is developmental and pathological plasticity: calretinin cell numbers appear relatively preserved in Huntington's disease while medium spiny neurons die around them, so their fractional share of the striatum rises even as the striatum shrinks.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Its glial support is unremarkable, and it is worth saying that plainly rather than manufacturing a special relationship.

Striatal astrocytes around it do the standard work of clearing glutamate through the transporter GLT-1 and buffering potassium, exactly as they do for neighbouring cells.

The one modest point of interest is that striatal astrocytes also express monoamine oxidase, so the local chemical environment of this cell is unusually good at destroying dopamine.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell type and the striatum would lose its most numerous source of soft, distributed, slow inhibition.

Parvalbumin basket cells would still deliver the hard, fast, veto-style inhibition, and the striatum would still work, but it would lose the gentle background tone that keeps medium spiny neuron ensembles from all rising together.

Losing the large cholinergic-calretinin cells specifically would strip the primate striatum of a modulatory layer that rodents simply do not have, which is one of the clearest cell-level differences between a rat striatum and a human one.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The defining non-spiking behaviour of this cell is calcium handling.

Calretinin has six calcium-binding domains and binds calcium with medium affinity and medium speed, which makes it a shaper of calcium signals rather than a simple sink.

The practical result is a buffer capacity of roughly 20 to 100, meaning that for every 21 to 101 calcium ions entering, only one stays free.

That changes dendritic computation, because local calcium spikes in the dendrite are damped and spread further before they decay.

Back-propagating action potentials, which are spikes that travel backwards from the cell body into the dendrites and tell the dendrite that the cell just fired, therefore leave a smaller but wider calcium footprint here than in an unbuffered cell.

Calretinin cells are coupled to each other and to other interneurons by gap junctions, direct pores between cells that let electrical current pass without any chemical step, which synchronises small clusters at gamma frequencies.

They participate in volume transmission, meaning dopamine and acetylcholine reach them by diffusing through the extracellular space rather than through a dedicated synapse.

Their housekeeping is dominated by calcium adenosine triphosphatase pumps and the sodium-calcium exchanger, both of which run continuously to undo what calretinin only temporarily holds.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Cortical glutamate from motor, premotor, prefrontal and limbic cortex, arriving as excitatory postsynaptic potentials of 0.5 to 3 millivolts each.

◦ Thalamic glutamate from the centromedian and parafascicular nuclei, arriving in short high-probability bursts.

◦ Dopamine from substantia nigra pars compacta axons by volume transmission, ambient concentration 5 to 50 nanomolar with transients to 1 micromolar.

◦ Local GABA from other interneurons and from medium spiny neuron axon collaterals.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ GABA onto medium spiny neuron dendrites and somata, with unitary inhibitory postsynaptic potentials of 0.2 to 1 millivolt.

◦ GABA onto cholinergic and low-threshold-spiking interneurons.

◦ Acetylcholine, for the large Lhx7-positive primate subtype only, released by volume transmission.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential in millivolts.

◦ Free intracellular calcium concentration in micromolar.

◦ Bound calretinin fraction, from 0 to 1.

◦ Calcium-activated potassium conductance activation, from 0 to 1.

◦ HCN channel activation, from 0 to 1.

◦ Synaptic conductance for each of the glutamate and GABA inputs.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Input resistance 300 to 800 megaohms.

◦ Membrane time constant 15 to 30 milliseconds.

◦ Spike threshold minus 48 to minus 42 millivolts.

◦ Calcium buffer capacity 20 to 100.

◦ Soma diameter 10 to 40 micrometres depending on subtype.

◦ Axonal conduction velocity 0.2 to 0.5 metres per second.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 15 to 30 milliseconds.

◦ Calcium extrusion 200 to 600 milliseconds because of the buffer.

◦ Calcium-activated potassium current activation 20 to 50 milliseconds.

◦ HCN sag 100 to 400 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In Huntington's disease these cells survive while their targets die, leaving inhibition that has nothing left to inhibit.

◦ In Tourette syndrome post-mortem studies report loss of parvalbumin and cholinergic interneurons in the caudate and putamen while calretinin cells are unchanged or relatively increased, shifting the striatum's inhibitory balance towards slow modulation and away from fast veto.

◦ If the calcium buffer is genetically removed, the cell accommodates rapidly and drops out of sustained trains, so the striatum loses tonic background inhibition during long movements.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to fire a striatal calretinin interneuron through a long input train and show that its calcium buffer holds back the calcium-activated potassium brake.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value negative seventy.
Create a number variable called free calcium with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called buffer capacity with initial value forty.
Create a number variable called potassium brake with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called synaptic drive with initial value forty.
Create a number variable called leak with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called net drive with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called calcium per spike with initial value zero point two.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called elapsed time with initial value zero.
While elapsed time is less than one thousand, repeat the following steps.
    Calculate the quotient of free calcium divided by the sum of free calcium and zero point five.
    Set potassium brake to the product of that result and twelve.
    Calculate the difference of negative seventy and membrane voltage.
    Set leak to that result.
    Calculate the sum of leak and synaptic drive.
    Calculate the difference of that result and potassium brake.
    Set net drive to that result.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the quotient of net drive divided by two hundred.
    Set free calcium to the product of free calcium and zero point nine nine nine six seven.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to negative forty five.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to negative sixty.
        Set spike count to the sum of spike count and one.
        Calculate the quotient of calcium per spike divided by the sum of buffer capacity and one.
        Set free calcium to the sum of free calcium and that result.
    End the conditional block.
    Set elapsed time to the sum of elapsed time and zero point one.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

I chose Python because the point being made is a numerical comparison between two buffer settings, and Python states that comparison in the fewest lines with no build step.

# Striatal calretinin interneuron: calcium buffering versus spike-frequency adaptation.
DT = 0.1                # integration step, milliseconds
TAU_M = 20.0            # membrane time constant, milliseconds
V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET = -70.0, -45.0, -60.0   # millivolts
CA_PER_SPIKE = 0.20     # micromolar of calcium entering per action potential
TAU_CA = 300.0          # calcium extrusion time constant, milliseconds
G_SK_MAX = 12.0         # maximum calcium-activated potassium brake, in millivolts
K_SK = 0.5              # micromolar calcium giving half of that brake
DRIVE = 40.0            # steady synaptic drive above rest, in millivolts

def run(kappa, ms=1000.0):
    """kappa is the calcium buffer capacity: 40 for calretinin, 0 for none."""
    v, ca, t, spikes = V_REST, 0.0, 0.0, 0
    steps = int(ms / DT)
    for n in range(steps):
        brake = G_SK_MAX * ca / (ca + K_SK)        # slow potassium opposition
        dv = ((V_REST - v) + DRIVE - brake) / TAU_M
        v += dv * DT
        ca -= ca / TAU_CA * DT                     # pumps grind the calcium down
        if v >= V_TH:
            v = V_RESET
            ca += CA_PER_SPIKE / (1.0 + kappa)     # the buffer swallows most of it
            spikes += 1
    return spikes

with_buffer = run(40.0)
without_buffer = run(0.0)
print("with calretinin buffer:", with_buffer, "spikes in 1 second")
print("without any buffer:    ", without_buffer, "spikes in 1 second")
print("adaptation removed by buffering:", with_buffer - without_buffer, "spikes")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only striatal cell class whose identity rests on a calcium sponge rather than on a transmitter, a receptor or a target.

If you see a striatal interneuron that is numerous, aspiny, slow, unmyelinated, GABA-releasing, and stains for calretinin but not parvalbumin, somatostatin or choline acetyltransferase, you have found the small or medium subtype.

If it stains for both calretinin and choline acetyltransferase and its cell body is 25 to 40 micrometres across, you have found the giant primate-only subtype, and you are looking at something a rodent brain does not contain.

CHAPTER 73. STRIATAL TYROSINE HYDROXYLASE-EXPRESSING INTERNEURONS (Dopamine-Marked Inhibitory Neuron) (COG)

This cell carries the machine that makes dopamine, gets counted as a dopamine neuron in a hundred papers, and does not release a molecule of dopamine.

The old label invites a straightforward error, because this cell carries tyrosine hydroxylase, the enzyme that begins dopamine manufacture, yet it does not release dopamine and is not a dopamine neuron.

Dopamine-Marked names the stain accurately while stripping the false implication, since the marker is real but the identity it suggests is not.

Inhibitory names what the cell actually is and does, a local cell that releases the inhibitory transmitter gamma-aminobutyric acid onto its neighbours.

Neuron marks it as a striatal interneuron whose removal in mice leaves reflexive movement intact but impairs goal-directed behaviour, so that the animal keeps working for an outcome it no longer values.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It is a rare inhibitory interneuron inside the striatum that expresses tyrosine hydroxylase (TH), the rate-limiting enzyme in the manufacture of dopamine.

Rate-limiting means it is the slowest step in the chemical assembly line, so its presence is normally taken as proof that a cell makes dopamine.

Here that inference is wrong: careful work has shown these cells lack aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase, the vesicular monoamine transporter 2 and the dopamine transporter, which are the other three parts required to actually package and release dopamine.

Without those parts the cell can start the recipe but cannot finish it, cannot load the product into vesicles and cannot recapture it.

What it does instead is release GABA, powerfully, onto both the direct-pathway and indirect-pathway output neurons of the striatum, delaying their spiking.

It also does something almost no other striatal cell does: it fires long plateau potentials, self-sustaining depolarised shelves lasting hundreds of milliseconds, and dopamine itself lengthens them.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is small to medium and often fusiform, meaning spindle-shaped with a taper at each end.

Dendrites are aspiny or very sparsely spiny and typically number three to five, spreading 200 to 500 micrometres.

The axon arborises densely and locally, and in some subtypes it forms a remarkably tight basket-like plexus close to the parent soma.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 9 to 16 micrometres in the longest axis, so on the small side of striatal interneurons.

The whole dendritic field occupies a sphere roughly 300 to 500 micrometres in diameter, which is small enough to sit inside a single striatal matrix compartment.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Whole-cell recordings in mouse striatum sorted these cells into four electrophysiologically distinct types, labelled Type I through Type IV.

• Type I is by far the most abundant, has very high input resistance, and shows prolonged depolarisation-induced plateau potentials with a striking depolarisation block.

• Type II fires more regularly and shows a prominent slow afterhyperpolarisation.

• Type III has a low-threshold calcium spike and resembles the low-threshold-spiking interneurons of the striatum.

• Type IV shows fast, weakly adapting firing; together Type I and Type IV account for close to 80 percent of all striatal tyrosine hydroxylase interneurons.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It is scattered through the caudate nucleus, the putamen and the nucleus accumbens, with a clear bias towards the ventral and medial striatum.

Density is highest in the nucleus accumbens shell, in the subcallosal streak just under the corpus callosum, and along the medial edge of the caudate at the interface with the lateral ventricle and the subventricular zone.

It is also concentrated at the striatal border with the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis, and it appears in the olfactory tubercle, which is part of the ventral striatum.

The subventricular interface matters, because that is exactly where newly generated cells would enter if any of these cells are born in adulthood, which remains an open question.

NUMBER:

I allocate about 100,000 of these cells across both human striata, with a defensible range of 50,000 to 200,000.

Confidence is low, and the reasoning must be shown: stereological counting in mouse found roughly 2,700 such neurons per striatum, which is about 0.15 percent of that striatum's neurons, and scaling that fraction to a human striatal budget of 110 million gives about 165,000.

I take a slightly conservative 100,000 because human tissue is harder to stain reliably for tyrosine hydroxylase against a background of dense dopamine axons.

That makes this type about 0.05 percent of the roughly 200 million neurons of the basal ganglia, and roughly 1 in every million of the brain's 86 billion neurons.

The number is not fixed: after the dopamine supply is destroyed, the count of tyrosine hydroxylase-positive striatal cells rises, by about 1.9 times in one mouse model, and an upregulated population appears after MPTP lesioning in monkeys and in humans with Parkinson's disease.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Input resistance is exceptionally high, commonly 600 megaohms to over 1 gigaohm, meaning a current of a few tens of picoamperes moves the cell tens of millivolts.

Resting potential is unusually negative, around minus 80 to minus 70 millivolts, so the cell sits quiet until pushed.

Once pushed past roughly minus 55 millivolts, Type I cells enter a plateau potential lasting 200 to 1,500 milliseconds, during which they fire slowly and then often enter depolarisation block, a state where the cell is too depolarised to reset its sodium channels and simply stops spiking while remaining depolarised.

These plateaus are sensitive to dopamine and to agonists and antagonists of the D1 and D5 receptors, which is a rare and directly testable case of a transmitter changing a cell's operating mode rather than just its rate.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory input arrives from the cortex and from the thalamic parafascicular nucleus.

Inhibitory input arrives from other striatal interneurons and from medium spiny neuron axon collaterals.

Dopamine axons from the substantia nigra pars compacta and the ventral tegmental area synapse on them and also bathe them by volume transmission.
