Outputs are GABAergic and go onto both direct-pathway and indirect-pathway medium spiny neurons; paired recordings show unitary inhibitory potentials strong enough to measurably delay an evoked spike in the target cell.

They also inhibit each other and inhibit cholinergic interneurons.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thin, under 0.4 micrometres, unmyelinated, and confined within roughly 400 micrometres of the soma.

Conduction velocity is low, about 0.2 to 0.4 metres per second, so all of its influence is local and delayed by well under 2 milliseconds.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA is the transmitter, full stop.

Tyrosine hydroxylase is present and functional as an enzyme, so the cell makes L-DOPA, the immediate chemical precursor of dopamine, but it cannot convert, store or release dopamine itself.

Some of these cells co-express neuropeptide Y or somatostatin, peptides that act slowly and over longer distances than GABA.

MYELINATION:

None.

This is an unmyelinated local circuit cell, and its speed is irrelevant to its function because plateau potentials operate on a timescale a thousand times slower than conduction delay.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low at rest, because the cell is quiet and small.

During a plateau potential the demand rises sharply, because the cell holds itself depolarised for up to a second and the sodium-potassium pump must work continuously to undo the sodium load.

Running tyrosine hydroxylase also costs tetrahydrobiopterin and molecular oxygen, and produces hydrogen peroxide as a by-product, so the cell carries a small permanent oxidative burden that a plain GABA interneuron does not.

PLASTICITY:

The headline plasticity here is phenotypic, meaning the cell changes what proteins it expresses rather than changing a synapse.

After dopamine denervation the striatum shows a marked increase in tyrosine hydroxylase-positive cells, most likely because existing interneurons switch the gene on rather than because new cells are born.

This looks like an attempted compensation for lost dopamine, and it is a failed one: stimulating these cells does not elicit striatal dopamine release in either control or lesioned tissue.

Their GABAergic synapses onto medium spiny neurons also show ordinary short-term depression during repetitive firing.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Its glial support is largely unremarkable, but there is one genuinely specific point.

Striatal astrocytes contain aromatic L-amino acid decarboxylase, the very enzyme this neuron lacks, so L-DOPA made by this cell can in principle be converted to dopamine outside it.

That makes the neuron and the astrocyte a potential two-part factory, which is a real and testable hypothesis rather than an established fact, and any simulation should mark it as such.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell type and the ventral striatum would lose a slow, latching, dopamine-tunable brake.

Most striatal inhibition is fast and event-locked; this one commits to a decision and holds it for up to a second, which is roughly the timescale of a whole action rather than a single movement.

The striatum would also lose the false-alarm signal that has misled dopamine researchers for decades, and, more seriously, it would lose whatever compensation the parkinsonian striatum is attempting when it multiplies these cells.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The plateau potential is the whole story, and it is not integrate and fire in any sense.

It is a bistable membrane, meaning the cell has two stable operating states, a quiet one near minus 75 millivolts and a depolarised one near minus 40 millivolts, and a brief input flips it between them.

Bistability of this kind is generated by a persistent sodium current and a calcium-activated non-selective cation current, and it turns a millisecond input into a second-long output.

D1 and D5 receptor activation raises cyclic adenosine monophosphate inside the cell and lengthens the plateau, so dopamine sets how long the latch holds.

The cell releases GABA from conventional axon terminals and, through its peptide co-transmitters, by volume transmission that does not need a synapse at all.

It carries the oxidative chemistry of catecholamine synthesis without the protective vesicular packaging that dopamine neurons use, which is a plausible reason these cells are stress-sensitive.

Back-propagating action potentials into its dendrites are unusually effective because the dendrites are short and the cell is electrically compact.

Its housekeeping includes continuous degradation of tyrosine hydroxylase by the ubiquitin-proteasome system, the cell's protein shredder, which is one lever by which enzyme levels change so quickly after denervation.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Cortical glutamate, chiefly from limbic and prefrontal cortex, excitatory postsynaptic potentials 1 to 5 millivolts given the high input resistance.

◦ Thalamic glutamate from the parafascicular nucleus.

◦ Dopamine at D1 and D5 receptors, ambient 5 to 50 nanomolar, transients to 1 micromolar, acting as a plateau-duration control rather than a drive.

◦ Local GABA from other interneurons and from medium spiny neuron collaterals.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ GABA onto direct-pathway and indirect-pathway medium spiny neurons, unitary inhibitory postsynaptic potentials 0.5 to 2 millivolts.

◦ GABA onto cholinergic interneurons and onto other tyrosine hydroxylase interneurons.

◦ Neuropeptide Y or somatostatin by volume transmission, for the subset that carries them.

◦ L-DOPA released or leaked locally, with no dopamine output.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential in millivolts.

◦ Plateau flag, true or false.

◦ Plateau time remaining in milliseconds.

◦ Depolarisation block flag, true or false.

◦ Cyclic adenosine monophosphate level as a proxy for D1 and D5 occupancy, 0 to 1.

◦ Synaptic conductances for glutamate and GABA.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Input resistance 600 to 1,200 megaohms.

◦ Resting potential minus 80 to minus 70 millivolts.

◦ Plateau trigger threshold minus 58 to minus 52 millivolts.

◦ Baseline plateau duration 200 to 600 milliseconds, extended up to about 1,500 milliseconds by full D1 and D5 occupancy.

◦ Soma diameter 9 to 16 micrometres.

◦ Population fraction 0.05 to 0.2 percent of striatal neurons in health, up to about twice that after dopamine loss.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 30 to 60 milliseconds, long because of the high input resistance.

◦ Plateau 200 to 1,500 milliseconds.

◦ GABA synaptic decay 5 to 12 milliseconds.

◦ Phenotypic upregulation of tyrosine hydroxylase after denervation, days to weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In Parkinson's disease the population expands but cannot supply dopamine, so the striatum gains extra GABAergic braking at exactly the moment it needs less of it, which may worsen rather than relieve slowness of movement.

◦ Runaway plateaus, if the persistent sodium current is not repolarised, lock the cell in depolarisation block and silence its output entirely despite it being maximally depolarised.

◦ Because this cell is routinely miscounted as dopaminergic, any model that treats striatal tyrosine hydroxylase staining as dopamine supply will overestimate striatal dopamine after nigral loss.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to make a striatal tyrosine hydroxylase interneuron latch into a plateau potential and to let dopamine set how long the latch holds.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value negative seventy five.
Create a number variable called dopamine occupancy with initial value one.
Create a number variable called base plateau duration with initial value four hundred.
Create a number variable called plateau remaining with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called plateau duration with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called refractory remaining with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called elapsed time with initial value zero.
Calculate the product of dopamine occupancy and one point five.
Calculate the sum of one and that result.
Set plateau duration to the product of base plateau duration and that result.
While elapsed time is less than one thousand five hundred, repeat the following steps.
    Set drive to zero.
    Check if elapsed time is less than sixty.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set drive to forty five.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if plateau remaining is greater than zero.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set drive to the sum of drive and thirty four.
        Set plateau remaining to the difference of plateau remaining and zero point five.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if refractory remaining is greater than zero.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set refractory remaining to the difference of refractory remaining and zero point five.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Calculate the difference of negative seventy five and membrane voltage.
        Calculate the sum of that result and drive.
        Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the quotient of that result divided by eighty.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if both membrane voltage is greater than negative fifty five and plateau remaining is less than or equal to zero is true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set plateau remaining to plateau duration.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to negative forty five.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set spike count to the sum of spike count and one.
        Set membrane voltage to negative sixty.
        Set refractory remaining to four.
    End the conditional block.
    Set elapsed time to the sum of elapsed time and zero point five.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

I chose Python because a plateau potential is a small state machine and Python keeps the states visible instead of burying them in numerical machinery.

# Striatal TH interneuron: a dopamine-tunable plateau potential.
DT = 0.5                # milliseconds per step
V_REST = -75.0          # this cell rests unusually negative
V_TH, V_RESET = -45.0, -60.0
TAU_M = 40.0            # long, because input resistance is about 1 gigaohm
TRIGGER = -55.0         # voltage that lights the plateau
PLATEAU_DRIVE = 34.0    # millivolts of self-sustaining depolarisation
PLATEAU_BASE_MS = 400.0 # plateau length with no dopamine on board
REFRACTORY_MS = 4.0

def simulate(d1_occupancy, stim_mv=45.0, stim_ms=60.0, total_ms=1500.0):
    """d1_occupancy runs 0.0 to 1.0 and only changes plateau DURATION."""
    duration = PLATEAU_BASE_MS * (1.0 + 1.5 * d1_occupancy)
    v, plateau_left, refr, spikes = V_REST, 0.0, 0.0, 0
    for n in range(int(total_ms / DT)):
        t = n * DT
        drive = stim_mv if t < stim_ms else 0.0
        if plateau_left > 0.0:                 # the latch is closed
            drive += PLATEAU_DRIVE
            plateau_left -= DT
        if refr > 0.0:
            refr -= DT
        else:
            v += ((V_REST - v) + drive) / TAU_M * DT
        if t < stim_ms and v > TRIGGER and plateau_left <= 0.0:
            plateau_left = duration            # latch closes once, on the input
        if v >= V_TH:
            spikes, v, refr = spikes + 1, V_RESET, REFRACTORY_MS
    return duration, spikes

for occupancy in (0.0, 0.5, 1.0):
    dur, n = simulate(occupancy)
    print(f"D1/D5 occupancy {occupancy:.1f}: plateau {dur:.0f} ms, {n} spikes")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the cell that stains positive for the dopamine-making enzyme and is not a dopamine cell.

Find a striatal interneuron with input resistance approaching a gigaohm, a resting potential near minus 75 millivolts, a plateau potential lasting most of a second that dopamine makes longer, GABA as its only output, and tyrosine hydroxylase but no dopamine transporter, and there is nothing else it can be.

Its population size is also a diagnostic in itself: if the striatal count of these cells has roughly doubled, the nigrostriatal dopamine system has already been damaged.

CHAPTER 74. GLOBUS PALLIDUS PROTOTYPIC NEURONS (Nonstop Braking Relay Neuron) (COG)

This neuron has never once been silent in your entire life; it fires about sixty times a second, awake, asleep and under anaesthetic, and the message is carried in when it stops.

The old name was an address joined to the empty word prototypic, which said only that this type was described first.

Nonstop names its defining electrical habit, a high and unusually regular background firing rate that never lets up, so downstream targets sit under permanent inhibition.

Braking names what that constant output achieves, holding movement in check until something lifts the pressure.

Relay names its position, since these cells make up roughly two-thirds of the external pallidum and are the main population feeding the subthalamic nucleus, passing the indirect route onward.

Neuron marks it as a projection cell rather than a local one.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It is the dominant cell of the external globus pallidus (GPe), a pale, iron-rich slab of tissue wedged between the putamen and the internal segment of the pallidum.

Pale is literal: pallidus is Latin for pale, and the structure looks light because it is criss-crossed by bundles of myelinated fibres, myelin being the fatty white insulation wrapped around axons.

The prototypic neuron is inhibitory, releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main quieten-down messenger.

Its defining trick is autonomous pacemaking, meaning it generates its own action potentials, the brief electrical spikes that are a neuron's output, with no input at all.

Cut every connection to it in a slice of living tissue and it keeps firing at 20 to 60 spikes per second forever.

Its job is therefore not to fire but to pause, and its main target is the subthalamic nucleus, which it holds down continuously; a pause in this cell is a release of the subthalamic nucleus.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is large and either oval or polygonal, and only three to five thick primary dendrites leave it.

The dendrites are long, straight, and branch rarely, and they are aspiny or carry only a scattering of spines.

The most striking feature is the shape of the dendritic field: it is disc-like, a flat pancake rather than a sphere, and the plane of the disc is oriented perpendicular to the incoming striatopallidal fibres.

That geometry is functionally deliberate: a flat sheet held across the grain of the incoming cables intercepts the maximum number of different striatal axons, which is why one pallidal neuron can sample hundreds of striatal cells.

The axon leaves the cell body or a proximal dendrite, gives off local collaterals inside the pallidum, and then runs out as a thick myelinated cable.

SIZE:

Cell bodies measure roughly 40 by 23 micrometres in the largest class and 29 by 17 micrometres in a smaller class, so 20 to 40 micrometres across is the working range.

A micrometre is one thousandth of a millimetre.

The dendritic disc is enormous relative to the cell body: roughly 1,000 by 1,000 micrometres across the face of the disc and only about 200 to 400 micrometres thick.

Total dendritic length per cell runs to several millimetres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Prototypic neurons are themselves heterogeneous, and the useful divisions are molecular and by target.

• Parvalbumin-positive prototypic cells, the largest group, carrying the calcium-binding protein parvalbumin and the transcription factor Nkx2-1, fast-firing, projecting mainly to the subthalamic nucleus.

• Lhx6-positive, parvalbumin-negative prototypic cells, firing somewhat more slowly, biased towards the substantia nigra and the striatum.

• Npas1-positive prototypic cells, a minority within the Npas1 family, which is otherwise dominated by the arkypallidal type described next.

• A small population of cholinergic pallidal neurons that belong developmentally to the basal forebrain and project to the cortex, usually counted separately.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the external segment of the globus pallidus, on both sides of the brain.

The external globus pallidus is bounded laterally by the external medullary lamina and the putamen, and medially by the internal medullary lamina and the internal globus pallidus.

Prototypic cells are found throughout, but are relatively enriched in the medial and ventral portions, whereas arkypallidal cells favour the lateral border.

The lateral border with the putamen is a genuine interface and should be modelled as one: it is where the striatopallidal axon bundles enter, and pallidal dendrites lie flat across them.

Equivalent cells occupy the ventral pallidum beneath the anterior commissure, at the interface between the pallidum and the nucleus accumbens.

NUMBER:

I allocate about 650,000 prototypic neurons across both external pallidal segments, with a defensible range of 550,000 to 750,000.

The reasoning is explicit: the regional budget gives the whole globus pallidus about 1.5 million neurons, human stereology puts the pallidum at roughly 688,000 to 711,000 neurons per side which is consistent with that budget, the external segment holds roughly two thirds of pallidal neurons, and prototypic cells are about two thirds of the external segment.

Confidence is moderate for the total and lower for the prototypic fraction, because the prototypic and arkypallidal split has been established mainly in rodents and assumed to carry over to humans.

I am counting only external segment prototypic cells here; internal globus pallidus output neurons are a separate type and are not counted in my share.

That makes this type about 0.33 percent of the roughly 200 million neurons of the basal ganglia, and about 8 in every million of the brain's 86 billion neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting potential is unusually depolarised at around minus 55 millivolts, and the cell never truly rests there because it is always climbing towards threshold.

Autonomous firing is driven by a persistent sodium current, a sodium leak that does not switch off, together with the hyperpolarisation-activated cyclic nucleotide-gated (HCN) current, which turns on when the cell is pushed downward and pulls it back up.

In an isolated slice the rate is 20 to 60 spikes per second; in the intact awake brain, prototypic cells discharge at 40 to 70 spikes per second in primates, with high regularity, interrupted by characteristic pauses.

Action potentials are narrow, 0.4 to 0.7 milliseconds, and are followed by a fast afterhyperpolarisation driven by large-conductance and small-conductance calcium-activated potassium channels.

Input resistance is modest, 60 to 200 megaohms, and the membrane time constant is short, 5 to 15 milliseconds, so this cell is fast and hard to push around.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The dominant input is GABA from indirect-pathway medium spiny neurons of the striatum, arriving on the soma and proximal dendrites through large boutons packed with vesicles.

Convergence is extreme: several hundred striatal neurons contact each pallidal cell, and the striatopallidal synapse is strong, reliable and strongly depressing during repetitive use.

Excitatory glutamate arrives from the subthalamic nucleus onto distal dendrites, closing a reciprocal loop, and from the thalamic parafascicular nucleus.

Local GABA arrives from neighbouring pallidal cells through axon collaterals, and from arkypallidal cells.

Outputs go to the subthalamic nucleus above all, and also to the internal globus pallidus, the substantia nigra pars reticulata, the thalamic reticular nucleus, the pedunculopontine nucleus, and back to striatal interneurons, especially parvalbumin basket cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The main axon is 1 to 3 micrometres in diameter and myelinated.

Conduction velocity is high for a subcortical inhibitory cell, roughly 2 to 8 metres per second, so a pallidal pause reaches the subthalamic nucleus within 1 to 3 milliseconds.

A single prototypic axon gives rise to several hundred to a few thousand terminals in the subthalamic nucleus, and it targets subthalamic cell bodies and proximal dendrites, the positions of maximum control.

Local collaterals within the pallidum are extensive and give each cell a strong say over its neighbours.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA, released at conventional fast synapses acting on GABA-A receptors, which are chloride channels that open in about a millisecond.

Parvalbumin-positive prototypic cells co-express the vesicular GABA transporter at high levels and sustain release at frequencies above 100 hertz without running out.

Some prototypic neurons co-release the peptide enkephalin, and a small distinct pallidal population releases acetylcholine, though that population is usually treated as basal forebrain rather than pallidal proper.

MYELINATION:

Heavy, and it is the reason the structure looks pale.

Prototypic axons are myelinated by oligodendrocytes with internodes, the insulated segments between gaps, of roughly 100 to 200 micrometres.

The myelin here is functionally essential rather than incidental: the whole point of this cell is that a pause arrives at the subthalamic nucleus fast enough to matter within a single beta-frequency cycle of about 50 milliseconds.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high, and among the highest per cell in the entire basal ganglia.

Firing 60 times a second continuously for a lifetime means the sodium-potassium pump runs flat out, and the pump is the single biggest consumer of adenosine triphosphate in the brain.

Mitochondria are dense in the soma and along the proximal dendrites, and the pallidum has a high capillary density to match.

The pallidum also accumulates more iron than any other brain region, and iron is needed for mitochondrial enzymes but catalyses damaging free-radical chemistry when it accumulates.

PLASTICITY:

Striatopallidal synapses show robust short-term depression and also long-term potentiation and depression that depend on dopamine D2 receptors.

The cell's own pacemaking is plastic: after dopamine is lost, prototypic neurons lose reliable autonomous firing, partly through maladaptive changes involving N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors and adenosine-triphosphate-sensitive potassium channels, and they begin to fire in bursts locked to beta rhythms.

Homeostatic scaling of the persistent sodium current lets these cells claw back a target firing rate over days after a perturbation.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here are genuinely unusual, in three specific ways.

First, oligodendrocyte density is exceptionally high because the pallidum is threaded with myelinated bundles, so a prototypic cell body sits in a sea of white matter rather than grey.

Second, pallidal microglia and astrocytes carry more ferritin, the iron storage protein, than glia almost anywhere else, and this is why the pallidum lights up on iron-sensitive magnetic resonance imaging and why the diseases called neurodegeneration with brain iron accumulation strike here first.

Third, the pallidum is a preferential site of manganese deposition, so pallidal astrocytes are the first cells in the brain to swell in manganese poisoning and in liver failure, producing a parkinsonism that starts in this exact cell's neighbourhood.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the subthalamic nucleus loses its leash.

The external pallidum would stop being a brake and would become an empty relay, and the entire indirect pathway, the route by which the basal ganglia suppress unwanted movements, would have no output stage.

Concretely the region would lose its continuous, high-frequency, regular tonic tone, the steady hum against which every striatal pause is read; without that hum a striatal pause would carry no information, because there would be nothing to interrupt.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This cell is a clock, not an integrator, and the distinction is the point.

Its computation is phase advance and phase delay: an input does not decide whether it fires but when in the ongoing cycle it fires, so the correct model is an oscillator with a phase response curve rather than a leaky bucket filling towards threshold.

Dendritic computation is shaped by the disc geometry, which makes the cell approximately a two-dimensional integrator sampling across, rather than along, the striatal fibre bundles.

Back-propagating action potentials invade the long straight dendrites efficiently because those dendrites are thick and unbranched, so every spike floods the whole dendritic disc with calcium.

Prototypic cells are electrically coupled to each other by gap junctions in early life, and the coupling persists at low levels in adults, contributing to synchronisation.

They are also strongly subject to ephaptic effects, meaning the extracellular electric field generated by thousands of neighbours firing together nudges each cell's membrane directly through the extracellular fluid; in the tightly packed, myelin-bounded pallidum these fields are large enough to matter, and they are one plausible amplifier of parkinsonian beta synchrony.

Local axon collaterals give the population lateral inhibition and mutual desynchronisation, and losing that mutual inhibition is one route to pathological oscillation.

The cell instructs its own myelination through activity-dependent signalling to oligodendrocyte precursors, so its high firing rate is partly why the region is so heavily myelinated.

Housekeeping is dominated by mitochondrial turnover and by iron handling, and both are stressed by the sheer rate of firing.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ GABA from indirect-pathway striatal medium spiny neurons, 300 to 1,000 converging cells, unitary inhibitory postsynaptic currents 50 to 500 picoamperes at the soma, strongly depressing.

◦ Glutamate from subthalamic nucleus neurons onto distal dendrites, unitary excitatory postsynaptic potentials 0.2 to 1 millivolt, facilitating.

◦ Glutamate from the thalamic parafascicular nucleus.

◦ GABA from local pallidal collaterals and from arkypallidal neurons.

◦ Dopamine from substantia nigra pars compacta collaterals acting at D2 receptors, ambient 5 to 50 nanomolar.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ GABA to subthalamic nucleus somata and proximal dendrites, the principal target.

◦ GABA to internal globus pallidus and substantia nigra pars reticulata.

◦ GABA to thalamic reticular nucleus and pedunculopontine nucleus.

◦ GABA to striatal parvalbumin interneurons.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential in millivolts.

◦ Persistent sodium current activation, 0 to 1.

◦ HCN current activation, 0 to 1.

◦ Calcium-activated potassium activation, 0 to 1.

◦ Intracellular calcium in micromolar.

◦ Striatopallidal synaptic depression variable, 0 to 1.

◦ Instantaneous oscillation phase, 0 to 2 pi, for network synchrony models.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Autonomous firing rate 40 to 70 spikes per second in vivo, 20 to 60 in isolated slice.

◦ Resting potential minus 58 to minus 52 millivolts.

◦ Spike threshold minus 47 to minus 43 millivolts.

◦ Input resistance 60 to 200 megaohms.

◦ Soma 20 to 40 micrometres; dendritic disc about 1,000 micrometres across and 200 to 400 micrometres thick.

◦ Axon diameter 1 to 3 micrometres, conduction velocity 2 to 8 metres per second.

◦ Population share about two thirds of external pallidal neurons.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 5 to 15 milliseconds.

◦ Interspike interval 14 to 25 milliseconds.

◦ GABA-A synaptic decay 5 to 10 milliseconds.

◦ Striatopallidal short-term depression recovery 200 to 800 milliseconds.

◦ HCN activation 100 to 500 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In Parkinson's disease these cells lose regular pacemaking and burst in synchrony at beta frequencies of 13 to 30 hertz, and the strength of that beta rhythm tracks how rigid and slow the patient is; suppressing it with deep brain stimulation improves movement.

◦ In Huntington's disease the external pallidum degenerates earlier and more severely than the internal segment, removing the brake on the subthalamic nucleus and contributing to involuntary writhing movements.

◦ In neurodegeneration with brain iron accumulation and in manganese poisoning, iron and manganese loading of this exact neighbourhood kills these cells and produces parkinsonism plus dystonia.

◦ If the persistent sodium current fails, the cell falls silent, the subthalamic nucleus is released, and basal ganglia output rises indiscriminately.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to run a prototypic pallidal neuron as an autonomous pacemaker and to make it pause when a barrage of striatal inhibition arrives.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value negative fifty five.
Create a number variable called gaba conductance with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called pacemaker drive with initial value sixteen.
Create a number variable called synaptic current with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called leak with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called net drive with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called pause window spikes with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called elapsed time with initial value zero.
While elapsed time is less than one thousand, repeat the following steps.
    Check if both elapsed time is greater than or equal to five hundred and elapsed time is less than five hundred and sixty is true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the remainder of elapsed time divided by ten.
        Check if that result is less than zero point zero two five.
        If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
            Set gaba conductance to the sum of gaba conductance and zero point nine.
        End the conditional block.
    End the conditional block.
    Set gaba conductance to the product of gaba conductance and zero point nine nine five eight.
    Calculate the difference of negative eighty and membrane voltage.
    Set synaptic current to the product of gaba conductance and that result.
    Calculate the difference of negative fifty five and membrane voltage.
    Set leak to that result.
    Calculate the sum of leak and pacemaker drive.
    Calculate the sum of that result and synaptic current.
    Set net drive to that result.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the quotient of net drive divided by four hundred and eighty.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to negative forty five.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to negative sixty.
        Set spike count to the sum of spike count and one.
        Check if both elapsed time is greater than or equal to five hundred and elapsed time is less than six hundred is true.
        If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
            Set pause window spikes to the sum of pause window spikes and one.
        End the conditional block.
    End the conditional block.
    Set elapsed time to the sum of elapsed time and zero point zero two five.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
Display pause window spikes to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

I chose C because a pallidal network model must step thousands of these cells at 25 microsecond resolution to resolve spike timing inside a beta cycle, and a tight compiled loop is the honest tool for that job.

/* Prototypic GPe neuron: autonomous pacemaker interrupted by a striatal barrage. */
#include <stdio.h>
#define DT        0.025   /* integration step, milliseconds */
#define TAU_M     12.0    /* membrane time constant, milliseconds */
#define V_REST   -55.0    /* depolarised rest: this cell is never at rest */
#define V_TH     -45.0
#define V_RESET  -60.0
#define I_AUTO     1.3    /* persistent sodium plus HCN drive, millivolts per ms */
#define E_GABA   -80.0    /* chloride reversal potential, millivolts */
#define TAU_GABA   6.0    /* striatal GABA decay, milliseconds */
int main(void) {
    double v = V_REST, g = 0.0;
    int spikes = 0, in_pause = 0;
    const long total = (long)(1000.0 / DT);
    const long every  = (long)(10.0 / DT);   /* 100 Hz striatal barrage */
    for (long n = 0; n < total; ++n) {
        double t = n * DT;
        if (t >= 500.0 && t < 560.0 && (n % every) == 0) g += 0.9;  /* MSN input */
        g -= g / TAU_GABA * DT;
        double dv = ((V_REST - v) + I_AUTO * TAU_M + g * (E_GABA - v)) / TAU_M;
        v += dv * DT;
        if (v >= V_TH) {
            v = V_RESET;
            ++spikes;
            if (t >= 500.0 && t < 600.0) ++in_pause;   /* 100 ms pause window */
        }
    }
    printf("tonic rate over the second: %d spikes/s\n", spikes);
    printf("spikes inside the 100 ms striatal pause window: %d\n", in_pause);
    printf("expected without a pause: about %d\n", (int)(spikes / 10.0));
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Nothing else in the basal ganglia is a large, parvalbumin-positive, myelinated, GABA-releasing cell with a flat disc of dendrites held perpendicular to the incoming striatal fibres, firing 40 to 70 times a second on its own with no input.

Sever every connection and it keeps going; that single test separates it from every striatal cell, all of which fall silent.

And it is the only cell whose signal is defined by absence: to read a prototypic neuron you do not watch its spikes, you watch its pauses.

CHAPTER 75. GLOBUS PALLIDUS ARKYPALLIDAL NEURONS (Backward-Reaching Cancel Neuron) (COG)

This is the cell that shouts stop, and it is the only neuron in the pallidum that sends its entire output backwards, into the structure that fed it.

Arkypallidal is a coined Latin compound that tells a student nothing at all.

Backward-Reaching names the anatomy that makes this cell remarkable, because alone among pallidal cells it sends its axon back upstream into the striatum and sends nothing to the subthalamic nucleus, reversing the expected direction of flow.

Cancel names the job, since in rats performing a stop-signal task these cells respond earlier and more strongly to the stop cue than their neighbours, and their firing can shut down striatal activity already underway.

Neuron marks it as just over a quarter of all external pallidal cells, firing more slowly and less regularly than the braking relay cells beside it.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It lives in the external globus pallidus alongside the prototypic neuron, and it is inhibitory, releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA).

Its name comes from the Greek arkhe, meaning beginning, because it projects back to the beginning of the circuit: the striatum.

That is its whole anatomy in one sentence, and it is remarkable, because the pallidum is supposed to be a downstream station and this cell reverses the arrow.

Functionally it delivers a broadcast stop signal.

When an animal is told to cancel a movement it has already begun to prepare, arkypallidal cells fire a burst about 20 milliseconds after the stop cue, at exactly the right moment to suppress the striatal activity that would otherwise have launched the action.

It is not an accelerator or a brake on a movement in progress; it is a cancel key pressed before the action commits.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is smaller and rounder than the prototypic neuron's, and the dendritic tree is bushier, with more branch points and a less flattened shape.

The dendrites are aspiny and spread more isotropically, meaning roughly equally in all directions rather than in a disc.

The axon is the astonishing part: it leaves the pallidum travelling laterally, enters the striatum, and there arborises into an enormous, sparse, wide-field plexus.

A single arkypallidal axon can carry tens of thousands of terminals spread across a large fraction of the striatum, which is why a few of these cells can inhibit an enormous number of targets at once.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are roughly 15 to 25 micrometres across, distinctly smaller than the 20 to 40 micrometre prototypic cells.

Total dendritic length is around 3 to 5 millimetres, contained within a field of 500 to 800 micrometres.

The axonal field is by far the biggest thing about this cell: its striatal arborisation covers a volume of several cubic millimetres, and total axonal length per cell runs to tens of centimetres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Arkypallidal cells are more homogeneous than prototypic cells, but two useful divisions exist.

• Dorsal arkypallidal cells projecting to the dorsal striatum, the caudate and putamen, involved in motor and habit stopping.

• Ventral arkypallidal cells projecting to the nucleus accumbens, involved in stopping approach and reward-seeking behaviour.

• Molecularly they are defined by the transcription factors Npas1 and FoxP2, together with Lhx6 and the absence of parvalbumin, and FoxP2 is the single most reliable marker.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lives in the external globus pallidus, preferentially towards its lateral border, that is, the side facing the putamen.

That lateral position is not accidental and should be modelled as an interface: these cells sit right at the pallidostriatal border, closest to the structure they innervate.

They are relatively sparse in the medial and ventral external pallidum, where prototypic cells dominate.

Developmentally they come from the lateral ganglionic eminence, the same embryonic bulge that produces striatal neurons, whereas prototypic cells come largely from the medial ganglionic eminence, so this cell is in a real sense a striatal cell that migrated into the pallidum.

NUMBER:

I allocate about 250,000 arkypallidal neurons across both external pallidal segments, with a defensible range of 200,000 to 300,000.

The reasoning follows the same chain as before: the regional budget gives the whole pallidum about 1.5 million neurons, the external segment holds roughly two thirds of them, and arkypallidal cells are reported as just over one quarter of external pallidal neurons in rodent work.

Confidence is low to moderate, because the prototypic and arkypallidal distinction has been demonstrated principally in mice and rats and only inferred in humans, and no human stereological count of FoxP2-positive pallidal cells exists that I would rely on.

That makes this type about 0.125 percent of the roughly 200 million neurons of the basal ganglia, and about 3 in every million of the brain's 86 billion neurons.

Together my prototypic and arkypallidal allocations come to about 900,000, leaving room within the 1.5 million pallidal budget for the internal segment output neurons and the small cholinergic population, which are counted elsewhere.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Everything about this cell is slower and looser than its prototypic neighbour, and the contrast is the diagnostic.

Resting potential is more negative, around minus 65 to minus 58 millivolts, and autonomous firing is weaker.

In the intact brain arkypallidal cells discharge at roughly 3 to 15 spikes per second, against 40 to 70 for prototypic cells, and they fire irregularly rather than like a metronome.

Action potentials are broader, typically 0.8 to 1.2 milliseconds against 0.4 to 0.7, and are followed by a deep, slow afterhyperpolarisation.

Input resistance is higher, 150 to 400 megaohms, so these cells are easier to drive.

During the slow cortical oscillation of deep sleep and anaesthesia the two types are in antiphase: arkypallidal cells fire during the active component of the cortical wave, prototypic cells during the inactive component, and that single measurement separates them in a recording without any staining at all.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from striatal medium spiny neurons, from the subthalamic nucleus, from the cortex indirectly, and from local prototypic collaterals.

Crucially the arkypallidal cell receives stronger striatal drive and weaker subthalamic drive than the prototypic cell, which is part of why the two fire in antiphase.

Outputs go exclusively to the striatum, and this is the defining fact: arkypallidal cells make essentially no projection to the subthalamic nucleus, the internal pallidum or the substantia nigra.

Within the striatum they contact both direct-pathway and indirect-pathway medium spiny neurons, and they also contact striatal interneurons, so they suppress both the output cells and the local circuitry that would otherwise compensate.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thinner than the prototypic axon, roughly 0.5 to 1 micrometre, and lightly myelinated.

Conduction velocity is accordingly slower, about 1 to 3 metres per second, and the distance from pallidum to striatum is short, so the pallidostriatal delay is around 1 to 4 milliseconds.

Terminal density is low per unit volume but the total field is vast, so each individual striatal neuron receives only a few arkypallidal contacts while nearly every striatal neuron receives some.

That is the architecture of a broadcast: weak everywhere, simultaneous everywhere.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA, acting on GABA-A receptors.

Because the terminals are sparse and spread out, arkypallidal inhibition acts partly through spillover onto extrasynaptic GABA-A receptors, producing a tonic conductance that shifts the whole striatum's excitability rather than vetoing individual cells.

Many arkypallidal cells also express preproenkephalin, and the peptide enkephalin is co-released during high-frequency bursts.

MYELINATION:

Light and patchy.

The axon is myelinated in its proximal course through the pallidum and the internal capsule fibres, and largely loses myelin as it breaks up into its striatal plexus.

That mixed myelination means the conduction delay is dominated by the unmyelinated terminal branches, which is why the stop signal takes tens of milliseconds to become effective rather than a couple.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate at the cell body, because the firing rate is low.

Very high in the axon, because maintaining tens of centimetres of thin, largely unmyelinated axon with tens of thousands of terminals is expensive: axonal transport, mitochondrial supply and vesicle recycling all scale with terminal number.

This is a cell whose energy budget is dominated by its output cable rather than by its spiking, which is unusual and should be modelled that way.

PLASTICITY:

Striatal input onto arkypallidal cells shows short-term depression, and cortical training that improves stopping ability strengthens the effective stop signal.

In dopamine-depleted animals arkypallidal cells increase their firing and become more strongly entrained to cortical rhythms, which may contribute to the difficulty parkinsonian patients have in initiating movement: the cancel key is being pressed too often.

Structural remodelling of the vast striatal axon over weeks has been observed after dopamine loss, meaning this cell rewires the geometry of its broadcast.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

At the cell body the glial environment is the same iron-rich, oligodendrocyte-dense pallidal environment described for the prototypic neuron, and in that respect it is not special.

What is specific is the axon: this single cell has more of its membrane inside the striatum than inside the pallidum, so most of its glial support is provided by striatal astrocytes, not pallidal ones.

Those striatal astrocytes take up the GABA it spills through the transporters GAT-1 and GAT-3, and because arkypallidal transmission relies on spillover, astrocytic GABA uptake in the striatum directly sets the strength and duration of the stop signal.

That is a genuine, testable case of a glial cell being part of the computation rather than a bystander.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the striatum loses its emergency brake.

The basal ganglia would still be able to select actions and still be able to suppress the wrong ones slowly through the indirect pathway, but they would lose the ability to abort an action already being prepared.

Concretely the region would lose its only fast, global, simultaneous, all-of-striatum inhibition: every other inhibitory input to the striatum is local, but this one arrives everywhere at once.

The pallidum itself would lose its only backward-pointing output and would become, correctly and boringly, a pure relay.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The arkypallidal cell computes by broadcast timing, which no integrate-and-fire model captures.

Its influence depends on the coincidence between when its burst lands in the striatum and how far the developing motor plan has already progressed, so it implements a race, and the outcome is decided by a deadline rather than by a sum.

Because its terminals are sparse and it relies on GABA spillover, it performs volume transmission rather than point-to-point synaptic transmission, and the spatial spread of that transmission is set by astrocytic uptake and by the tortuosity of the extracellular space.

Extrasynaptic GABA-A receptors containing the delta subunit mediate a tonic chloride conductance in striatal medium spiny neurons, and shifting that tonic conductance changes the gain of the whole striatum, not the state of any one cell.

The cell also co-releases enkephalin, an opioid peptide that acts on mu and delta opioid receptors over seconds and micrometres, adding a slow second channel on top of the fast one.

Its enormous axon requires continuous local protein synthesis in the axon and terminals, because there is far too much membrane to supply from the soma alone, and axonal messenger ribonucleic acid transport is therefore part of its normal operation.

FoxP2, its defining transcription factor, is itself activity-regulated, so this cell's identity gene responds to how much it has been firing.

Gap-junction coupling among arkypallidal cells is weak, which fits their irregular, unsynchronised discharge.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ GABA from striatal medium spiny neurons, strong, the dominant input, with short-term depression.

◦ Glutamate from the subthalamic nucleus, weaker than onto prototypic cells.

◦ GABA from local prototypic collaterals.

◦ Cortical stop-related drive arriving indirectly by way of the subthalamic nucleus and the striatum, latency 10 to 20 milliseconds from cue.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ GABA onto direct-pathway and indirect-pathway striatal medium spiny neurons across a wide field, few contacts per target.

◦ GABA onto striatal interneurons including parvalbumin and cholinergic cells.

◦ Tonic extrasynaptic GABA-A conductance in the striatum by spillover.

◦ Enkephalin by volume transmission during bursts.

◦ No projection to subthalamic nucleus, internal pallidum or substantia nigra.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential in millivolts.

◦ Slow afterhyperpolarisation conductance, 0 to 1.

◦ Burst flag and burst time remaining in milliseconds.

◦ Accumulated striatal tonic GABA conductance, in nanosiemens per target cell.

◦ Short-term depression variable on the striatal input, 0 to 1.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Baseline firing rate 3 to 15 spikes per second, irregular.

◦ Stop-evoked burst rate 60 to 120 spikes per second.

◦ Stop-cue to burst-onset latency 15 to 25 milliseconds.

◦ Burst duration 40 to 100 milliseconds.

◦ Resting potential minus 65 to minus 58 millivolts.

◦ Input resistance 150 to 400 megaohms.

◦ Spike width 0.8 to 1.2 milliseconds.

◦ Population share about one quarter of external pallidal neurons.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 15 to 30 milliseconds.

◦ Pallidostriatal conduction delay 1 to 4 milliseconds.

◦ Synaptic GABA-A decay 6 to 12 milliseconds; spillover tonic component 50 to 200 milliseconds.

◦ Slow afterhyperpolarisation 100 to 300 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ If this cell fails, stopping fails: the behavioural signature is a lengthened stop-signal reaction time, meaning the person needs more warning to abort an action, which is the laboratory measure of impulsivity.

◦ Weak arkypallidal signalling is a leading circuit hypothesis for impulse control disorders, including the gambling and compulsive behaviour that dopamine agonist drugs sometimes trigger in Parkinson's disease.

◦ Overactive arkypallidal signalling produces the opposite problem, a striatum that is constantly being told to cancel, which is one proposed contributor to freezing of gait and to difficulty initiating movement in parkinsonism.

◦ Because its effect depends on astrocytic GABA uptake in the striatum, anything that impairs striatal GAT-1 and GAT-3 will smear the stop signal in time and blunt it.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to race a rising striatal go signal against an arkypallidal stop burst and to report whether the action was launched or cancelled.
Create a number variable called go accumulator with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called go ramp per millisecond with initial value zero point zero zero eight five.
Create a number variable called threshold with initial value one.
Create a number variable called stop cue time with initial value forty.
Create a number variable called stop latency with initial value eighteen.
Create a number variable called burst duration with initial value eighty.
Create a number variable called burst start with initial value zero.
Create a number variable called inhibition per millisecond with initial value zero point zero one eight.
Create a number variable called baseline inhibition with initial value zero point zero zero one eight.
Create a boolean variable called plan started with initial value false.
Create a text variable called outcome with initial value undecided.
Create a number variable called elapsed time with initial value zero.
Set burst start to the sum of stop cue time and stop latency.
While elapsed time is less than four hundred, repeat the following steps.
    Set go accumulator to the sum of go accumulator and go ramp per millisecond.
    Set go accumulator to the difference of go accumulator and baseline inhibition.
    Calculate the sum of burst start and burst duration.
    Check if both elapsed time is greater than or equal to burst start and elapsed time is less than that result is true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set go accumulator to the difference of go accumulator and inhibition per millisecond.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if go accumulator is greater than zero point one five.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set plan started to true.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if both plan started is true and go accumulator is less than or equal to zero is true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set outcome to cancelled.
        Break out of the current loop immediately.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if go accumulator is greater than or equal to threshold.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set outcome to moved.
        Break out of the current loop immediately.
    End the conditional block.
    Set elapsed time to the sum of elapsed time and one.
End the loop.
Display outcome to the console.
Display elapsed time to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

I chose Python because this is a timing race between two rates, not a stiff differential equation, and Python shows the race logic and the deadline plainly.

# Arkypallidal stop signal racing a striatal go ramp.
DT = 1.0                 # milliseconds per step
GO_RAMP = 0.0085         # striatal evidence accumulated per millisecond
THRESHOLD = 1.0          # go accumulator level that launches the movement
BASELINE_INHIB = 0.0018  # tonic arkypallidal drag, per millisecond
BURST_INHIB = 0.018      # drag during the stop burst, per millisecond
STOP_LATENCY = 18.0      # milliseconds from stop cue to burst onset
BURST_MS = 80.0          # milliseconds the arkypallidal burst lasts

def trial(stop_cue_ms, total_ms=400.0):
    """Returns the outcome and the time at which it was settled."""
    go, started = 0.0, False
    burst_start = None if stop_cue_ms is None else stop_cue_ms + STOP_LATENCY
    for n in range(int(total_ms / DT)):
        t = n * DT
        go += (GO_RAMP - BASELINE_INHIB) * DT
        if burst_start is not None and burst_start <= t < burst_start + BURST_MS:
            go -= BURST_INHIB * DT          # the broadcast stop signal lands
        if go > 0.15:
            started = True                  # a motor plan is now committed to
        if started and go <= 0.0:
            return ("cancelled", t)         # the plan collapsed before launch
        if go >= THRESHOLD:
            return ("moved", t)
    return ("timed out", total_ms)

print("no stop cue:        ", trial(None))
print("stop cue at 40 ms:  ", trial(40.0))
print("stop cue at 140 ms: ", trial(140.0))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron in the globus pallidus whose axon goes backwards, and it sends that axon nowhere except the striatum.

Stain a pallidal cell for FoxP2 and Npas1 and find no parvalbumin, record it firing at under 15 spikes per second irregularly, watch it fire on the active component of the cortical slow oscillation while its neighbours fire on the inactive one, and you have it.

And its behavioural fingerprint is unique in the entire basal ganglia: a burst about 20 milliseconds after a cue that means cancel, arriving across the whole striatum at once.

CHAPTER 76. SUBTHALAMIC NUCLEUS NEURONS (Sudden-Halt Excitatory Neuron) (COG)

Half a million cells the size of a lentil hold the only brake pedal in the basal ganglia, and cutting them makes a limb fly out of control.

The old label was purely an address, a nucleus name, and taught nothing.

Sudden-Halt names the moment it owns, since it receives a direct shortcut from frontal cortex that bypasses the striatum entirely and can therefore raise system-wide suppression faster than any other route, aborting a movement already in flight.

Excitatory marks the fact that makes this cell unique in its neighbourhood, because it is the only excitatory node in a system otherwise built entirely from inhibition, and its glutamate projection is the sole excitatory connection reaching the basal ganglia output stations.

Neuron marks it as the cell of the nucleus that is the standard target of deep brain stimulation for Parkinson's disease.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The subthalamic nucleus is a small lens-shaped structure sitting just under the thalamus, the brain's great relay station, at the top of the midbrain.

Almost all of its cells are projection neurons that release glutamate, the brain's main excitatory chemical, which makes it the one excitatory nucleus inside a system otherwise built from inhibition.

Its job is to raise the brain's threshold for acting.

When the cortex is torn between two possible movements, or when a planned movement must be cancelled at the last instant, the subthalamic nucleus receives a direct message from the frontal cortex, floods the output nuclei of the basal ganglia with excitation, and those output nuclei clamp down on the thalamus so that nothing moves.

Neuroscientists call this the hyperdirect pathway, and it is the fastest route from cortex to basal ganglia output, arriving before the striatum has finished thinking.

It is also the nucleus that surgeons target with deep brain stimulation for Parkinson's disease, which makes it the most electrically interrogated cell population in the human brain.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The typical cell has a polygonal or spindle-shaped body with five to eight thick primary dendrites that radiate outward and then branch sparingly.

The dendrites are long, straight and sparsely spined, and they lie flattened along the long axis of the nucleus, so a single cell samples a slab of tissue shaped like the nucleus itself.

The dendritic tree is deliberately oversized relative to the nucleus, so each cell reads a large fraction of everything arriving.

The axon leaves the cell body, and in most cells it splits early into branches heading for several different targets at once.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 20 to 35 micrometres across in humans, making these medium-to-large neurons.

The dendritic field spans roughly 500 to 1,200 micrometres along the long axis of the nucleus and less across it.

The whole human subthalamic nucleus is only about 150 cubic millimetres, roughly the size of a lentil, measuring about 9 by 4 by 3 millimetres.

That tiny volume is why deep brain stimulation demands sub-millimetre surgical accuracy.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There is no clean neurochemical split of the kind seen in the striatum, but three overlapping divisions are well supported.

• A dorsolateral sensorimotor territory, which receives input from primary motor and premotor cortex and is the standard deep brain stimulation target.

• A ventromedial associative territory, connected to the dorsolateral prefrontal cortex and involved in choosing between options.

• A medial limbic territory, connected to the anterior cingulate and orbitofrontal cortex and to the ventral pallidum, involved in motivation and impulse control.

A small minority of cells, perhaps 5 to 10 percent, are local interneurons releasing gamma-aminobutyric acid, the brain's main inhibitory chemical, although in primates this population is smaller than in rodents and some workers doubt it exists at all.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It sits in the subthalamus, wedged between the internal capsule laterally, the zona incerta and thalamus dorsally, and the substantia nigra ventrally.

Its ventral border is a direct interface with the substantia nigra pars reticulata and its lateral border is a direct interface with the great white-matter cable of the internal capsule, which is why stimulation that spreads a millimetre too far causes muscle pulling of the face and hand.

Fibres of the lenticular fasciculus and the ansa lenticularis, the output cables of the globus pallidus, pass immediately above it.

The nucleus is crossed by the medial forebrain bundle and lies close to the red nucleus and the third nerve fibres, which is why misplaced stimulation can cause double vision.

NUMBER:

The human subthalamic nucleus contains roughly 500 thousand neurons in total across both sides, which is the figure this book budgets.

Published stereological counts, which estimate cell numbers by systematic sampling of tissue sections, cluster around 240 thousand to 300 thousand per side, giving 480 thousand to 600 thousand in total.

Confidence is good, because the nucleus has sharp borders and has been counted repeatedly.

That is about 0.25 percent of the roughly 200 million neurons of the basal ganglia and about 0.0006 percent of the 86 billion neurons of the whole nervous system.

For scale, the subthalamic nucleus has roughly one neuron for every 220 neurons in the striatum that it must help override.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These are autonomous pacemakers, meaning they fire regularly with no input at all, at about 5 to 20 spikes per second in an isolated slice and 10 to 30 in the living brain.

Pacemaking is driven by a persistent sodium current and a slow calcium-activated potassium current that together produce a steady sawtooth of depolarisation.

Cutting off all synaptic input does not silence them, and that is the single most important fact about this nucleus for anyone building a simulation.

They also produce rebound bursts: a period of inhibition from the globus pallidus de-inactivates T-type calcium channels, and when the inhibition lifts the cell fires a rapid volley far above its baseline rate.

This rebound property, combined with the reciprocal wiring between the subthalamic nucleus and the external globus pallidus, is the engine that generates pathological rhythms in Parkinson's disease.

Human recordings during surgery show that subthalamic cells are multistable, able to sit in more than one persistent firing state for the same input.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory input arrives directly from the frontal cortex, chiefly primary motor, supplementary motor and prefrontal areas, and from the intralaminar nuclei of the thalamus, especially the centromedian and parafascicular nuclei.

Inhibitory input arrives overwhelmingly from the external globus pallidus, and these gamma-aminobutyric acid synapses land close to the cell body where they have maximum authority.

Modulatory input arrives from the pedunculopontine nucleus, which supplies acetylcholine and glutamate, and from dopamine axons of the substantia nigra pars compacta.

Outputs excite the internal globus pallidus, the external globus pallidus, the substantia nigra pars reticulata, the pedunculopontine nucleus and, more weakly, the striatum.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thin, about 0.5 to 1.5 micrometres in diameter, and branches early so that a single cell contacts several targets at once.

Most subthalamic cells send collaterals to both segments of the globus pallidus and to the substantia nigra pars reticulata simultaneously, so one spike is broadcast to the whole output stage of the basal ganglia.

Conduction distances are short, a few millimetres, and conduction times from cortex to subthalamic nucleus are around 3 to 5 milliseconds, which is what makes the hyperdirect pathway fast.

Terminal boutons are small and are placed on the dendrites of pallidal and nigral cells.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The projection cells release glutamate, packaged by the vesicular glutamate transporter VGLUT2, and act on both AMPA receptors for fast excitation and N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors for slower calcium-permeable excitation.

They also drive metabotropic glutamate receptors on their targets, producing effects lasting seconds.

The cells themselves carry gamma-aminobutyric acid type A receptors for pallidal inhibition, gamma-aminobutyric acid type B receptors for slow inhibition, dopamine D1, D2 and D5 receptors, and serotonin and acetylcholine receptors.

There is no peptide co-transmitter of established importance here, which is unusual and makes this cell chemically simple compared with its neighbours.

MYELINATION:

Subthalamic axons are thinly myelinated, with oligodendrocytes wrapping the projection fibres to the pallidum and nigra.

Because the distances are short, myelination buys only a modest speed gain, and conduction velocities are on the order of 1 to 5 metres per second.

The clinically important myelin is not the cell's own but the heavily myelinated internal capsule immediately lateral to it, whose fibres are far easier to excite with an implanted electrode than the subthalamic cells themselves.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Continuous pacemaking at 10 to 30 spikes per second with no rest makes this an expensive cell.

Every spike must be paid for by the sodium-potassium pump, and the persistent sodium current leaks sodium in even between spikes, so the pump never stops.

The nucleus has a high density of mitochondria and high cytochrome oxidase activity, a standard marker of sustained energy use.

In Parkinson's disease the nucleus becomes overactive, and its metabolic rate rises further, which is one reason its overactivity is thought to be self-damaging over decades.

PLASTICITY:

Cortex-to-subthalamic synapses show long-term potentiation and long-term depression, the strengthening and weakening of connections, and both depend on dopamine.

Loss of dopamine in Parkinson's disease shifts the balance toward strengthened cortical drive, which helps lock the nucleus into pathological beta-band rhythms of 13 to 35 cycles per second.

Deep brain stimulation itself induces lasting change: patients often keep some benefit for minutes to hours after the stimulator is switched off, which cannot be explained by instantaneous electrical effects alone.

The pallidal-to-subthalamic synapse is also plastic and is remodelled after dopamine loss, with a documented proliferation of inhibitory terminals.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here do something unusual and clinically important: they respond directly to deep brain stimulation with waves of calcium and release adenosine triphosphate, which is broken down to adenosine, which then damps excitatory transmission.

Part of the therapeutic effect of high-frequency stimulation is therefore glial rather than neuronal.

The nucleus is rich in iron, and its glia handle a heavy iron load, which makes it visible on susceptibility-weighted magnetic resonance imaging and gives surgeons a direct target.

Microglia in the nucleus become activated in parkinsonian states, and the chronically overactive nucleus sits in a mildly inflamed environment.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the subthalamic nucleus and the basal ganglia lose the ability to say no.

The clinical proof is hemiballismus, in which a stroke destroying one subthalamic nucleus causes violent, uncontrollable flinging movements of the opposite arm and leg, because the output nuclei are no longer excited enough to keep unwanted movements suppressed.

Without it the system also loses its pause during conflict, so decisions are made too fast and impulsively, which is exactly the side effect seen in some patients after subthalamic deep brain stimulation.

Flavour lost: urgency, restraint and the capacity to hold everything still while the brain makes up its mind.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Autonomous pacemaking means the cell is a self-running oscillator whose baseline must be modelled explicitly rather than emerging from input.

Rebound bursting after inhibition gives the pallido-subthalamic loop a natural resonance, and the loop's delay of about 5 to 10 milliseconds each way sets the beta frequency at which parkinsonian brains lock up.

Dendritic calcium electrogenesis lets distal dendrites generate their own regenerative events, so cortical inputs far from the cell body are not simply attenuated.

Multistability, documented in human recordings during surgery, means the same synaptic input can leave the cell in two different persistent firing states depending on history.

Gap-junction coupling has been reported between subthalamic cells and may help synchronise the nucleus.

Ephaptic effects, in which the electric field of one cell influences its neighbours without any synapse, are plausible in such a small dense nucleus and are the presumed mechanism by which an implanted electrode entrains thousands of cells at once.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Cortical glutamate via the hyperdirect pathway, latency 3 to 5 milliseconds, event rate 0 to 100 hertz.

◦ Thalamic glutamate from centromedian and parafascicular nuclei, 0 to 40 hertz.

◦ External globus pallidus gamma-aminobutyric acid, 30 to 80 hertz baseline, perisomatic, conductance 1 to 10 nanosiemens.

◦ Dopamine, 5 to 100 nanomolar, acting mainly to reduce excitability.

◦ Externally applied electrical stimulation, 60 to 185 hertz, 1 to 5 milliamperes, pulse width 60 to 90 microseconds.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Glutamate onto internal globus pallidus, external globus pallidus, substantia nigra pars reticulata and pedunculopontine nucleus.

◦ Firing rate 10 to 30 hertz at baseline, 100 to 400 hertz transiently during a rebound burst.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage in millivolts.

◦ Adaptation variable representing calcium-activated potassium drive.

◦ T-type calcium channel availability, 0 to 1.

◦ Recent inhibition history over the last 200 milliseconds.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Membrane capacitance about 40 picofarads.

◦ Leak conductance about 2 nanosiemens, resting voltage about minus 55 to minus 60 millivolts.

◦ Spike threshold about minus 45 millivolts.

◦ Intrinsic pacemaker drive sufficient to fire at 10 to 30 hertz with no input.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane time constant about 15 to 20 milliseconds.

◦ Adaptation time constant about 60 milliseconds.

◦ T-type de-inactivation about 100 milliseconds.

◦ Pallido-subthalamic loop delay about 5 to 10 milliseconds each way, setting a resonance near 20 hertz.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Destruction of the nucleus causes hemiballismus, violent involuntary flinging of the opposite limbs.

◦ Overactivity plus beta-band synchrony at 13 to 35 hertz produces the rigidity and slowness of Parkinson's disease, and the strength of that rhythm tracks symptom severity closely enough to be used as a feedback signal for closed-loop stimulation.

◦ Stimulation of the limbic territory can produce impulsivity, hypomania or depression.

◦ Current spread into the neighbouring internal capsule causes muscle contraction and slurred speech.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a subthalamic neuron that paces itself, is silenced by pallidal inhibition, and fires a rebound burst when that inhibition is released.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value -60.
Create a number variable called adaptation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called rebound count with initial value 0.
While step is less than 40000, repeat the following steps.
    Create a number variable called drive with initial value 90.
    Check if both step is greater than 10000 and step is less than 14000 is true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set drive to -100.
    End the conditional block.
    Create a number variable called leak with initial value 0.
    Calculate the difference of voltage and -55.
    Set leak to the product of that result and -2.
    Calculate the sum of leak and drive.
    Create a number variable called net with initial value 0.
    Set net to the difference of that result and adaptation.
    Set voltage to the sum of voltage and the quotient of the product of net and 0.05 divided by 40.
    Check if voltage is greater than or equal to 20.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set voltage to -55.
        Calculate the sum of adaptation and 20.
        Set adaptation to that result.
        Calculate the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set spike count to that result.
        Check if both step is greater than 14000 and step is less than 15200 is true.
        If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
            Calculate the sum of rebound count and 1.
            Set rebound count to that result.
        End the conditional block.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of voltage and -55.
    Create a number variable called adapt target with initial value 0.
    Set adapt target to the product of that result and 4.
    Set adaptation to the sum of adaptation and the quotient of the product of the difference of adapt target and adaptation and 0.05 divided by 60.
    Calculate the sum of step and 1.
    Set step to that result.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
Display rebound count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

C is chosen because realistic subthalamic models are run inside pallido-subthalamic network loops with tens of thousands of cells and millions of time steps, where raw speed decides whether the experiment finishes.

#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>

int main(void) {
    const double dt = 0.05;      /* ms */
    const double C = 40.0;       /* pF  */
    const double gL = 2.0;       /* nS  */
    const double EL = -55.0;     /* mV  */
    const double VT = -45.0;     /* mV, spike-initiation voltage */
    const double DT = 2.0;       /* mV, spike sharpness */
    const double a = 4.0;        /* nS, subthreshold adaptation */
    const double b = 20.0;       /* pA, spike-triggered adaptation */
    const double tw = 60.0;      /* ms, adaptation time constant */
    double v = -60.0, w = 0.0;
    int spikes = 0, rebound = 0;

    for (int i = 0; i < 40000; i++) {      /* 2000 ms */
        double t = i * dt;
        double I = 90.0;                   /* intrinsic pacemaker drive, pA */
        if (t > 500.0 && t < 700.0) I = -100.0;  /* pallidal GABA brake */
        double dv = (-gL * (v - EL)
                     + gL * DT * exp((v - VT) / DT)
                     - w + I) / C;
        v += dt * dv;
        if (v >= 20.0) {                   /* spike */
            v = EL; w += b; spikes++;
            if (t > 700.0 && t < 760.0) rebound++;  /* post-inhibition volley */
        }
        w += dt * (a * (v - EL) - w) / tw;
    }
    printf("total spikes in 2 s: %d\n", spikes);
    printf("spikes in the 60 ms rebound window: %d\n", rebound);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only large excitatory nucleus inside the basal ganglia, it never stops firing even when every input is cut, and it answers a pause with a volley instead of silence.

If a cell in the depths of the human brain fires steadily at 10 to 30 hertz, is quieted by pallidal inhibition and then bursts on release, sits in a lentil-sized iron-rich nucleus above the substantia nigra, and its destruction throws the opposite arm into violent flinging, it is a subthalamic neuron.

CHAPTER 77. SUBSTANTIA NIGRA PARS RETICULATA NEURONS (Standing-Brake Release Neuron) (COG)

Right now, as you read this line, a quarter of a million cells are firing about seventy times a second purely to stop your eyes from jumping somewhere else.

The old name is a Latin address, meaning the netlike part of the black substance, and it describes pigment rather than purpose.

Standing names the resting condition of this cell, because it is not silent at rest but fires continuously at roughly sixty spikes per second in the awake primate, driven by its own intrinsic sodium and Kv3-type potassium currents rather than by incoming excitation.

Brake names what that constant firing does, since the cell is inhibitory and its targets, chiefly the superior colliculus and the motor thalamus, are held clamped shut by it by default.

Release names the moment the cell earns its keep, because when a movement is finally permitted these neurons pause, and the sudden removal of inhibition lets the collicular burst cells fire and the saccade or limb movement go.

Neuron marks it as the output cell that teaches the counterintuitive central fact of the basal ganglia, that movement is produced by stopping a cell rather than by starting one.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The substantia nigra, Latin for black substance, is a dark stripe in the midbrain with two very different halves.

The pars compacta is the dark half full of dopamine cells, and the pars reticulata, meaning the netlike part, is the pale half beneath it, threaded with fibres.

Pars reticulata cells are the exit door of the basal ganglia.

They release gamma-aminobutyric acid, the brain's main inhibitory chemical, and they do it continuously and at high speed, so their targets are held under a permanent brake.

Movement, in this system, is not produced by switching something on: it is produced by these cells briefly falling silent, which lifts the brake and lets a target circuit run.

Neuroscientists call this disinhibition, and it is the central trick of the basal ganglia.

The clearest example is the eye: every time you flick your gaze to a new object, a group of pars reticulata cells pauses for about 100 milliseconds, and only then can the superior colliculus, the midbrain's eye-movement map, fire the saccade.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is medium-to-large, fusiform or polygonal, with three to five long, thick primary dendrites.

The dendrites are smooth and almost spine-free, and they radiate in a flattened disc lying in the plane of the nucleus.

Crucially, many dendrites climb upward into the pars compacta, where they are bathed in dopamine released from the dendrites of the dopamine cells above.

The axon leaves the cell body and usually splits into two or three main branches within a few hundred micrometres, each heading for a different target.

The cell contains parvalbumin, a calcium-binding protein typical of fast-firing neurons, and it lacks the pigment neuromelanin, which is why the pars reticulata looks pale next to the pars compacta.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are about 20 to 35 micrometres across in humans.

Dendrites extend 500 to 1,000 micrometres and are among the longest smooth dendrites in the midbrain.

The whole human pars reticulata occupies roughly 200 to 400 cubic millimetres per side, a thin curved sheet a few millimetres thick.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The population is more uniform than most, but it divides usefully by target and by position.

• Cells projecting to the motor thalamus, chiefly the ventral anterior and ventral lateral nuclei, controlling limb and trunk movement.

• Cells projecting to the superior colliculus, controlling eye movements and orienting.

• Cells projecting to the mediodorsal thalamus, feeding prefrontal cortex and therefore cognition rather than movement.

• Cells projecting to the pedunculopontine nucleus and the medullary reticular formation, controlling posture, gait and licking or chewing.

• A lateral group receiving direct glutamate input, shown in monkeys to serve reactive inhibition, the emergency stop.

Most cells send branches to more than one of these, so the subtypes are tendencies rather than sealed classes.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It occupies the ventral two thirds of the substantia nigra in the midbrain tegmentum, directly beneath the pars compacta and directly above the cerebral peduncle, the massive fibre bundle carrying cortex to spinal cord.

Its dorsal boundary with the pars compacta is a genuine interface, because reticulata dendrites and compacta dendrites intermingle there and exchange signals without synapses.

Laterally it abuts the cerebral peduncle and medially it approaches the red nucleus and the third nerve fibres.

The pars lateralis, a small lateral extension, projects preferentially to the superior colliculus.
