Functionally it is the twin of the internal segment of the globus pallidus, and the two together are described as one output stage split across two places.

NUMBER:

Stereological counting gives about 288 thousand neurons in the human substantia nigra pars reticulata, with an uncertainty of roughly plus or minus 32 thousand.

Confidence is moderate, resting largely on one careful comparative study, and it is not always clear in the literature whether such figures refer to one side or both, so treat 250 thousand to 350 thousand as the working range.

That is about 0.15 percent of the roughly 200 million neurons of the basal ganglia and about 0.0003 percent of the 86 billion neurons of the whole nervous system.

Notably, humans have proportionally fewer of these cells than rats do, so the human basal ganglia lean more heavily on the internal globus pallidus for output.

Roughly 380 striatal neurons converge on every one of these cells, which is the funnel that makes action selection possible.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These are among the fastest sustained firers in the brain, discharging spontaneously at a median of about 68 spikes per second in primates and commonly quoted at 30 to 100 hertz.

They are autonomous pacemakers, driven by a persistent sodium current, and they keep firing when every synapse is blocked.

Action potentials are narrow, under 1 millisecond wide, and the afterhyperpolarisation that follows each spike is brief, which is what permits such high rates without exhaustion.

They show almost no spike-frequency adaptation, meaning they do not slow down during a long train, because a brake that fades would be useless.

Their firing rate is modulated by hydrogen peroxide acting on ATP-sensitive potassium channels, a direct link between the tissue's metabolic state and the strength of the brake.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inhibitory input arrives from the direct-pathway medium spiny neurons of the striatum, which land on distal dendrites in large numbers, and from the external globus pallidus, which lands closer to the cell body.

Excitatory input arrives from the subthalamic nucleus, from the pedunculopontine nucleus, and in the lateral region directly from cortex.

Dopamine reaches these cells from the dendrites of the pars compacta above, acting on D1 receptors on striatal terminals and D1 or D5 receptors on the cells themselves.

Outputs are inhibitory synapses onto the ventral anterior, ventral lateral and mediodorsal thalamic nuclei, the intermediate layers of the superior colliculus, the pedunculopontine nucleus and the reticular formation.

One important subtlety: nigral input to the thalamus is not always purely inhibitory in its effect, because in some thalamic targets it produces rebound excitation, so the sign of the output depends on the target.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are thin to medium, roughly 0.5 to 2 micrometres, and they branch to multiple targets from a single cell.

The projection to the thalamus travels through the fields of Forel and joins the thalamic fasciculus; the projection to the superior colliculus runs a short course dorsally.

Terminals are large, form multiple release sites, and cluster around the cell bodies and proximal dendrites of their targets, giving them powerful, reliable control.

Conduction is fast enough that a pause in the nigra appears in the colliculus within a few milliseconds.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Gamma-aminobutyric acid is the transmitter, loaded into vesicles by the vesicular GABA transporter and acting on gamma-aminobutyric acid type A receptors for fast chloride-mediated inhibition.

Because the release is tonic and heavy, target cells also experience a standing background of receptor activation rather than isolated events.

The cells carry gamma-aminobutyric acid type A and type B receptors, AMPA and N-methyl-D-aspartate glutamate receptors, dopamine D1 and D5 receptors, and ATP-sensitive potassium channels.

There is no significant peptide co-transmitter.

MYELINATION:

Axons are myelinated, thinly to moderately, by oligodendrocytes, and the nucleus sits among heavily myelinated fibre bundles which is partly why it appears netlike.

Myelination matters here because the brake must be lifted and reapplied with millisecond precision across a distance of one to two centimetres.

Conduction velocities are on the order of 2 to 10 metres per second.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Firing 70 times a second, every second, for a lifetime is a very large standing energy bill.

These cells sit near the top of the brain's per-cell energy consumption, and they are packed with mitochondria.

Their sensitivity to hydrogen peroxide and ATP-sensitive potassium channels means their firing rate is directly reported to, and directly modulated by, their own energy state.

Unlike their dopamine neighbours, they do not carry the extra oxidative burden of dopamine metabolism, which is part of why they survive in Parkinson's disease while the cells above them die.

PLASTICITY:

Striatal-to-nigral synapses show long-term potentiation and depression, and their strength is set by dopamine.

After dopamine loss, the number of inhibitory terminals onto pars reticulata cells increases, a documented structural proliferation that changes the gain of the whole output stage.

Short-term plasticity is prominent: striatal terminals depress strongly during a burst, so the brake is lifted most effectively by a fast, brief striatal volley rather than a slow steady one.

The cells' intrinsic excitability is also plastic and shifts in models of epilepsy and dystonia.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here manage an unusually heavy glutamate and gamma-aminobutyric acid recycling load because of the sheer traffic, and they buffer the extracellular potassium that 70 hertz firing pours out.

Astrocytic calcium responses in the nigra are directly triggered by subthalamic stimulation, so the glia participate in the loop rather than merely servicing it.

Astrocyte-derived hydrogen peroxide is a genuine signal here, not just a waste product, tuning the firing rate through ATP-sensitive potassium channels.

Microglia are relatively quiescent in the healthy pars reticulata but become strongly activated in parkinsonian states, partly because of debris from the dying cells above.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and the midbrain loses its default of stillness.

Thalamic and collicular circuits, no longer held down, would fire whenever any input arrived, so the eyes would drift and jump uncontrollably and posture would collapse into continuous involuntary movement.

Experimentally, injecting a drug that silences the pars reticulata produces exactly this: uncontrollable eye movements and involuntary limb movements.

The flavour lost is restraint with a hair trigger, the ability to be absolutely still and then release in a single tenth of a second.

The pars reticulata is also the brain's seizure gate, and without it the midbrain loses a powerful natural anticonvulsant circuit.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Tonic pacemaking means the informative signal is the pause, not the spike, so any simulation must treat rate decreases as the carrier of information.

Ephaptic and field effects are plausible given the dense, synchronously firing population.

Gap-junction coupling among reticulata cells has been reported and supports local synchrony.

Metabolic sensing through hydrogen peroxide and ATP-sensitive potassium channels means firing rate is coupled directly to the cell's own energy status, an unusual and computationally important feedback.

Dendrites reaching up into the pars compacta receive dopamine released from dendrites rather than axon terminals, which is a form of volume transmission between two cell types across a boundary.

Target-dependent output sign, in which the same inhibitory synapse produces suppression in one thalamic nucleus and rebound excitation in another, means the cell's effective output cannot be computed without knowing the receiving cell.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Striatal direct-pathway gamma-aminobutyric acid on distal dendrites, event rate 0 to 40 hertz per input, thousands of inputs converging.

◦ External globus pallidus gamma-aminobutyric acid, perisomatic, 30 to 80 hertz.

◦ Subthalamic glutamate, 10 to 30 hertz baseline, rising sharply during a stop signal.

◦ Dendritic dopamine from the pars compacta above, 5 to 500 nanomolar.

◦ Hydrogen peroxide from local metabolism, modulating ATP-sensitive potassium channels.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Gamma-aminobutyric acid onto ventral anterior, ventral lateral and mediodorsal thalamus, superior colliculus intermediate layers, pedunculopontine nucleus and reticular formation.

◦ Baseline rate 60 to 80 hertz; the behaviourally relevant signal is a pause to near zero lasting 50 to 200 milliseconds.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage in millivolts.

◦ Synaptic conductance from striatum and from subthalamic nucleus.

◦ Short-term depression level of striatal terminals, 0 to 1.

◦ Local hydrogen peroxide concentration.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Intrinsic pacemaker rate about 65 hertz with all input removed.

◦ Membrane time constant about 10 milliseconds.

◦ Spike width under 1 millisecond, refractory period about 2 milliseconds.

◦ Adaptation close to zero, deliberately.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Interspike interval about 15 milliseconds.

◦ Pause duration 50 to 200 milliseconds.

◦ Short-term depression recovery about 300 to 800 milliseconds.

◦ Dopamine modulation seconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In Parkinson's disease these cells become overactive and their firing becomes bursty and synchronised, so the brake is jammed on and movement becomes slow and small.

◦ Silencing them experimentally causes involuntary movements and uncontrollable saccades.

◦ Loss of their tonic output removes a natural seizure gate and lowers the threshold for generalised seizures.

◦ Abnormal patterning here contributes to dystonia, in which opposing muscles contract at once.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a substantia nigra pars reticulata neuron whose steady high-rate firing holds a target circuit shut, and whose brief pause opens it.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value -60.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called pause spikes with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called gate open steps with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called steps since last spike with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
While step is less than 20000, repeat the following steps.
    Create a number variable called striatal inhibition with initial value 0.
    Check if both step is greater than or equal to 6000 and step is less than 7000 is true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set striatal inhibition to -40.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of voltage and -60.
    Create a number variable called restoring with initial value 0.
    Set restoring to the product of that result and -1.
    Calculate the sum of restoring and 18.5.
    Create a number variable called net with initial value 0.
    Set net to the sum of that result and striatal inhibition.
    Set voltage to the sum of voltage and the quotient of the product of net and 0.1 divided by 10.
    Calculate the sum of steps since last spike and 1.
    Set steps since last spike to that result.
    Check if voltage is greater than or equal to -45.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set voltage to -58.
        Calculate the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set spike count to that result.
        Set steps since last spike to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if steps since last spike is greater than 300.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of gate open steps and 1.
        Set gate open steps to that result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of step and 1.
    Set step to that result.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
Display gate open steps to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Julia is chosen because this cell is a high-rate integrator whose science lives in the exact timing of a pause, and Julia gives compiled speed for long fine-grained runs while still reading like arithmetic.

using Printf

function nigral_gate()
    dt      = 0.1      # ms per step
    tau     = 10.0     # ms membrane time constant
    v_rest  = -60.0    # mV
    v_th    = -45.0    # mV spike threshold
    v_reset = -58.0    # mV reset after a spike
    drive   = 18.5     # mV of intrinsic pacemaker drive, tuned for about 65 Hz
    v = v_rest
    spikes = 0
    pause_spikes = 0
    for i in 1:20000                       # 2000 ms of simulated time
        t = i * dt
        # a 100 ms burst from direct-pathway striatal neurons
        inhib = (600.0 <= t < 700.0) ? -40.0 : 0.0
        v += dt * (-(v - v_rest) + drive + inhib) / tau
        if v >= v_th                       # spike, then reset
            v = v_reset
            spikes += 1
            if 600.0 <= t < 700.0
                pause_spikes += 1
            end
        end
    end
    @printf("mean firing rate: %.1f Hz\n", spikes / 2.0)
    @printf("spikes during the 100 ms striatal input: %d\n", pause_spikes)
    @printf("gate state during that window: %s\n", pause_spikes == 0 ? "OPEN" : "shut")
end

nigral_gate()

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is a pale, parvalbumin-rich, neuromelanin-free cell in the midbrain that fires around 70 times a second without pause, without adaptation, and without needing any input at all.

If a cell's meaningful message is a silence rather than a spike, if its dendrites climb into a layer of dopamine cells and drink dopamine that was never released from an axon, and if switching it off makes the eyes jump uncontrollably, it is a pars reticulata neuron.

CHAPTER 78. SUBSTANTIA NIGRA PARS COMPACTA NEURONS (Movement-Vigor Supplying Neuron) (COG)

Each of these cells grows an axon four and a half metres long, keeps it naked, and pays for that extravagance with its life.

The old name is a Latin address meaning the compact part of the black substance, describing the pigment neuromelanin rather than any function.

Movement-Vigor names the specific quantity this cell carries, since its transient activity precedes movement and scales with how fast, how large and how long the coming action will be, so it sets the amplitude of behaviour rather than choosing which behaviour occurs.

Supplying names the mode of delivery, a slow pacemaker discharge of a few spikes per second that keeps a background level of dopamine present, punctuated by brief high-frequency bursts.

Neuron marks it as one of only about three hundred and eighty-two thousand such cells per human brain, roughly half of which must die before the classic motor signs of Parkinson disease appear.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are the dopamine cells of the midbrain, the ones whose death causes Parkinson's disease.

Dopamine is a chemical messenger, and these cells manufacture it, carry it enormous distances, and spray it over the striatum, the input gateway of the basal ganglia.

Their job is not to tell the striatum what to do; it is to tell the striatum how surprised to be.

When something better than expected happens, they fire a brief burst; when something worse than expected happens, they fall silent for a moment.

This difference between what happened and what was expected is called a reward prediction error, and it is the teaching signal that makes habits form and actions get chosen.

They do a second job at the same time: a background level of dopamine sets how vigorously and how quickly a movement is executed, which is why losing them makes movement small and slow rather than impossible.

They are dark to the naked eye because they accumulate neuromelanin, a black-brown pigment made from oxidised dopamine, which is why the nucleus is called the black substance.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is large and fusiform or polygonal, packed with pigment granules that grow more numerous with every decade of life.

Three to six long dendrites leave the cell body, and one or two of them plunge downward into the pars reticulata below, sometimes travelling more than half a millimetre into it.

Those descending dendrites are not just receivers: they release dopamine themselves, from vesicles in the dendrite, which is a very unusual arrangement.

The axon is the extraordinary part.

It emerges, often from a dendrite rather than the cell body, travels up through the medial forebrain bundle, and then explodes into an arbour of astonishing size within the striatum.

SIZE:

The cell body is 25 to 45 micrometres across, among the larger neurons in the midbrain.

Dendrites span 400 to 1,000 micrometres.

The axon is the record holder: reconstructions and scaling estimates put the total axonal length of a single human pars compacta dopamine neuron at about 4.5 metres, all of it packed inside a striatum a few centimetres wide.

Each cell forms an estimated 1 million to 2.4 million release sites.

To picture it, imagine one cell whose body is a fifth of a millimetre wide growing a wire long enough to cross a room, then folding that wire into a space the size of a walnut.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Ventral tier cells, which sit lower in the pars compacta, are calbindin-poor, project to the sensorimotor putamen, and are the population that dies first and hardest in Parkinson's disease.

• Dorsal tier cells, which are calbindin-rich, project more to associative and limbic striatum, and are relatively spared.

• Lateral cells, in which the calcium channel subtype Cav1.3 acts as a linear amplifier of firing rate across the cell's entire working range.

• A minority co-express the vesicular glutamate transporter VGLUT2 and release glutamate as well as dopamine, and glutamate-releasing neurons are genuinely intermixed with dopamine neurons in the human midbrain.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They form the dorsal band of the substantia nigra in the midbrain, corresponding to cell group A9, sitting directly above the pars reticulata and below the medial lemniscus and red nucleus.

Their descending dendrites cross into the pars reticulata, making the compacta-to-reticulata border an active chemical interface rather than a wall.

Medially they merge without a sharp border into the ventral tegmental area, cell group A10, and dorsolaterally into the retrorubral field, cell group A8.

Their axons run in the medial forebrain bundle and terminate mainly in the putamen and caudate nucleus, with sparser projections to the subthalamic nucleus, globus pallidus and thalamus.

NUMBER:

This book budgets about 600 thousand pars compacta dopamine neurons at birth, counting both sides.

Published human stereological counts vary widely, from roughly 200 thousand to 400 thousand per side in adults, with one careful estimate of about 382 thousand plus or minus 20 thousand per hemisphere.

Confidence is moderate: the counts are real but they disagree, partly because the nucleus has fuzzy borders and partly because the number falls throughout life at roughly 5 to 10 percent per decade.

That is about 0.3 percent of the roughly 200 million neurons of the basal ganglia and about 0.0007 percent of the 86 billion neurons of the whole nervous system.

It is the most consequential rounding error in neuroscience: fewer than one cell in a hundred thousand, and their loss defines one of the most common neurodegenerative diseases in the world.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

They are autonomous pacemakers, firing slowly and regularly at 1 to 5 spikes per second with no input at all.

Pacemaking is unusual because it is driven substantially by L-type calcium channels of the Cav1.3 subtype, which open at unusually negative voltages and let calcium in on every cycle.

Action potentials are broad, over 2 milliseconds, with a long afterhyperpolarisation, and they are followed by a slow ramp back to threshold.

On top of the pacemaker sits burst firing, in which glutamate acting on N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors switches the cell into a volley of 2 to 6 spikes at 15 to 30 hertz.

They also show pauses, when inhibitory input silences them for 100 to 400 milliseconds, and the pause is the signal for a worse-than-expected outcome.

A distinctive sag in voltage during hyperpolarisation, produced by the hyperpolarisation-activated current, is used by electrophysiologists as a fingerprint.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory input arrives from the subthalamic nucleus, the pedunculopontine and laterodorsal tegmental nuclei, and the superior colliculus, which delivers the fast visual surprise signal.

Inhibitory input arrives from the striatum, the globus pallidus, the substantia nigra pars reticulata and, importantly, from the rostromedial tegmental nucleus, a small structure that acts as the brake for disappointment.

Outputs are dense axonal release sites throughout the striatum, only a minority of which are classical synapses; most dopamine is released into the extracellular space.

Dendritic release into the pars reticulata provides local feedback within the nigra itself.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is exceptionally thin, roughly 0.1 to 0.5 micrometres, and largely unmyelinated once it enters the striatum.

Its total length of about 4.5 metres per cell means a single neuron must maintain a membrane surface area and a transport system out of all proportion to its cell body.

Conduction is slow, around 0.5 metres per second, so dopamine arrives tens of milliseconds after the spike.

Because release sites are mostly non-synaptic, dopamine acts by volume transmission, diffusing several micrometres and reaching thousands of targets from one release point.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Dopamine is made from tyrosine by tyrosine hydroxylase, the rate-limiting enzyme, then by aromatic amino acid decarboxylase, and is loaded into vesicles by the vesicular monoamine transporter VMAT2.

It acts on D1-family receptors, which raise cyclic adenosine monophosphate and excite, and D2-family receptors, which lower it and inhibit.

The cell carries D2 autoreceptors on itself, so dopamine shuts down its own producer, a negative feedback loop that limits bursting.

Dopamine is cleared by the dopamine transporter DAT and broken down by monoamine oxidase and catechol-O-methyltransferase.

A subset co-releases glutamate, and many co-release gamma-aminobutyric acid that they take up from outside rather than synthesise conventionally.

MYELINATION:

The axon is unmyelinated or only very thinly myelinated near its origin, and it is definitively unmyelinated once it arborises in the striatum.

This is not an oversight but a consequence of geometry: an arbour of 4.5 metres with over a million branch points could not physically be myelinated.

The cost is severe, because an unmyelinated axon must run sodium and potassium currents along its entire length, and the energy bill is thought to be the single largest contributor to this cell's vulnerability.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

This is plausibly the most metabolically stressed neuron in the human brain.

It pays for continuous pacemaking, for calcium entry on every cycle through Cav1.3 channels, for a 4.5-metre unmyelinated axon, for over a million release sites, and for the manufacture and containment of dopamine, which oxidises spontaneously into damaging quinones.

Its mitochondria run near capacity, its basal oxidative stress is high, and the resulting oxidised dopamine is packed away as neuromelanin, which chelates iron and is protective until the load exceeds its buffering capacity.

Blocking Cav1.3 channels reduces the calcium burden, which is why the blood-pressure drug isradipine was tested as a neuroprotective agent in Parkinson's disease.

PLASTICITY:

Their own inputs are plastic: glutamate synapses onto these cells undergo long-term potentiation, and a single dose of a drug of abuse can strengthen them measurably for days.

Their output is the plasticity signal for everyone else, because dopamine gates long-term potentiation and depression at cortex-to-striatum synapses.

Firing pattern itself is plastic, and surviving cells after partial lesion recover their full firing range through changes in Kv4.3 potassium channels, a compensation that helps explain why symptoms appear only after most cells are gone.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes in the substantia nigra are relatively sparse compared with the striatum, and they carry unusually low levels of the antioxidant glutathione, leaving these cells poorly defended against the oxidative stress they generate.

Microglia, by contrast, are denser in the substantia nigra than almost anywhere else in the brain, which makes this a hair-trigger inflammatory environment and helps explain why the region degenerates when microglia are provoked.

Neuromelanin released from dying cells is itself a potent microglial activator, creating a self-amplifying loop of degeneration.

Oligodendrocyte support is minimal in the striatal arbour, because there is no myelin there to maintain.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and the striatum keeps its wiring but loses its opinion about outcomes.

Movements still occur, but they shrink, slow and lose vigour, because the background dopamine that sets movement gain is gone.

Learning from reward stops, so nothing new becomes a habit and nothing old gets revised.

The direct and indirect pathways of the striatum, normally held in balance by D1 excitation and D2 inhibition, fall out of balance, and the whole basal ganglia loop begins to oscillate at beta frequencies of 13 to 35 hertz.

The flavour lost is motivation itself: not the ability to move, but the reason to.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Volume transmission dominates, since most release sites are not synapses and dopamine acts as a diffusing field over micrometres and seconds.

Dendritic release means the cell secretes transmitter from its input structures into the pars reticulata below, feeding back on the output stage of the basal ganglia directly.

Autoreceptor self-inhibition through D2 receptors gives the cell a built-in governor.

Back-propagating action potentials invade the dendrites and trigger the calcium entry needed for dendritic release.

Its calcium-driven pacemaker creates a permanent oxidative load, coupling electrical activity directly to cellular ageing.

Neuromelanin accumulation is a lifelong structural change that converts a metabolic by-product into an iron-chelating store, and eventually into an immune trigger.

Alpha-synuclein, a protein that these cells express heavily, misfolds and spreads from cell to cell, so this neuron's failure mode is transmissible along its own connections.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Subthalamic and pedunculopontine glutamate, driving bursts through N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors, 0 to 60 hertz.

◦ Superior colliculus glutamate, latency about 70 to 100 milliseconds after a visual event.

◦ Rostromedial tegmental and striatal gamma-aminobutyric acid, producing pauses.

◦ Dopamine acting on the cell's own D2 autoreceptors, 5 to 500 nanomolar.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Dopamine into the caudate nucleus and putamen, tonic level 5 to 50 nanomolar, phasic transients 100 to 1,000 nanomolar lasting a few hundred milliseconds.

◦ Dendritic dopamine into the substantia nigra pars reticulata.

◦ Glutamate from the VGLUT2-positive minority.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage in millivolts.

◦ Intracellular calcium concentration in micromolar.

◦ Vesicular dopamine stock, 0 to 1.

◦ Learned value estimate for the current cue, if modelling reward prediction error.

◦ Cumulative oxidative damage, for long-timescale models.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Baseline firing 1 to 5 hertz, burst rate 15 to 30 hertz, pause floor 0 hertz.

◦ Total axon length about 4.5 metres, release sites 1 to 2.4 million.

◦ Learning rate for value updating, typically 0.05 to 0.3 in models.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Interspike interval 200 to 1,000 milliseconds.

◦ Burst duration 100 to 200 milliseconds; pause duration 100 to 400 milliseconds.

◦ Dopamine clearance by the transporter, 100 to 500 milliseconds in dorsal striatum.

◦ Neuromelanin accumulation, decades.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Progressive death produces Parkinson's disease, with motor symptoms appearing only after roughly 40 to 60 percent of these cells and a larger fraction of their striatal terminals are lost.

◦ Alpha-synuclein misfolds into Lewy bodies inside surviving cells and spreads between them.

◦ Excess dopamine replacement therapy overshoots and causes levodopa-induced dyskinesia, involuntary writhing movements.

◦ Iron overload and neuromelanin loss are both measurable in the living human nigra with 7 tesla magnetic resonance imaging.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a substantia nigra pars compacta dopamine neuron that keeps a running prediction of reward, bursts when the outcome is better than predicted, and pauses when it is worse.
Create a number variable called predicted value with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called learning rate with initial value 0.15.
Create a number variable called baseline rate with initial value 4.
Create a number variable called burst gain with initial value 16.
Create a number variable called trial with initial value 1.
Create a list variable called rate history with initial value empty list.
While trial is less than or equal to 20, repeat the following steps.
    Create a number variable called reward with initial value 0.
    Check if trial is less than or equal to 15.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set reward to 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Create a number variable called prediction error with initial value 0.
    Calculate the difference of reward and predicted value.
    Set prediction error to that result.
    Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value 0.
    Calculate the product of burst gain and prediction error.
    Set firing rate to the sum of baseline rate and that result.
    Check if firing rate is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Add firing rate to the end of rate history.
    Calculate the product of learning rate and prediction error.
    Set predicted value to the sum of predicted value and that result.
    Display firing rate to the console.
    Calculate the sum of trial and 1.
    Set trial to that result.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in rate history.
Display that number to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the reward prediction error rule and the spiking output are both short, the arithmetic is exactly what the biology does, and every reinforcement-learning toolkit a simulation team will reach for is already written in it.

import random

random.seed(7)

BASE_RATE  = 4.0      # spikes per second when nothing surprising happens
BURST_GAIN = 16.0     # extra spikes per second per unit of positive surprise
ALPHA      = 0.15     # how fast the cell learns to expect the reward
WINDOW_MS  = 200      # the response window after the cue

value = 0.0           # the cell's running prediction of reward

for trial in range(1, 21):
    reward = 1.0 if trial <= 15 else 0.0     # reward is withdrawn after trial 15
    prediction = value
    rpe = reward - prediction                # the reward prediction error
    rate = BASE_RATE + BURST_GAIN * rpe
    if rate < 0.0:
        rate = 0.0                           # a cell cannot fire less than silence
    # Poisson spiking over the response window, one millisecond bins
    spikes = sum(1 for _ in range(WINDOW_MS) if random.random() < rate / 1000.0)
    value += ALPHA * rpe                     # learn from the surprise
    label = "burst" if rate > BASE_RATE + 1 else ("pause" if rate < BASE_RATE - 1 else "tonic")
    print("trial %2d  reward %.0f  predicted %.2f  error %+.2f  %5.1f Hz  %d spikes  %s"
          % (trial, reward, prediction, rpe, rate, spikes, label))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only cell whose death you can see with the naked eye at autopsy, because the black stripe in the midbrain fades to grey.

If a neuron is pigmented with neuromelanin, paces itself at a few hertz using calcium channels, grows a four-and-a-half-metre unmyelinated axon into the striatum, releases transmitter from its dendrites as well as its axon, and encodes the difference between what happened and what was expected, it is a substantia nigra pars compacta dopamine neuron.

CHAPTER 79. VENTRAL TEGMENTAL AREA NEURONS (Reward-Pursuit Driving Neuron) (COG)

Every drug that humans have ever become addicted to, from opium to nicotine to cocaine, ends up doing the same thing to the same few hundred thousand cells sitting in the middle of your midbrain.

Ventral tegmental area is an address, naming a patch of midbrain floor and nothing about the work done there.

Reward-Pursuit names the class of events these cells report on and narrows it correctly, because their signal governs the effort spent chasing a prize far more than the pleasure felt on receiving it, the distinction experimenters draw between wanting and liking.

Driving names the action, since a brief burst of firing when a cue predicts something better than expected sets alight the approach behaviour that follows, while a pause when the outcome is worse than expected damps it.

Neuron marks it as one cell among about six hundred and ninety thousand in the human ventral tegmental area, of which fewer than one in five are dopaminergic, the rest being inhibitory and glutamate-releasing cells that shape the same output.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The ventral tegmental area is a small, loosely bordered patch of the midbrain that sits just inside and above the substantia nigra.

It is the origin of two famous pathways: the mesolimbic pathway, which runs to the nucleus accumbens and the amygdala and hippocampus, and the mesocortical pathway, which runs to the prefrontal cortex.

Its dopamine cells do for motivation and emotion roughly what their nigral cousins do for movement.

They signal that something is better or worse than expected, they signal salience, meaning how much an event matters whether it is good or bad, and they set how hard an animal is willing to work.

But the ventral tegmental area is not simply a dopamine nucleus, and this is the single most common mistake made about it.

Only around 60 percent of its cells make dopamine; roughly a third make gamma-aminobutyric acid, the brain's main inhibitory chemical, and a small but powerful minority make glutamate, the main excitatory chemical.

The interplay among these three, inside one nucleus, is what makes the region a decision point for reward rather than a simple transmitter tap.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Dopamine cells here are medium-sized, fusiform or multipolar, with three to six moderately branched dendrites and, unlike their nigral cousins, comparatively little neuromelanin pigment when young.

Local gamma-aminobutyric acid cells are smaller and rounder with denser, shorter dendritic trees, and they fire much faster.

Glutamate cells are small and are concentrated in the medial and rostral parts of the nucleus.

There is no crisp cytoarchitectural boundary anywhere around the ventral tegmental area; it fades into the substantia nigra laterally and into the periaqueductal grey and raphe medially.

Many cells carry more than one transmitter machine, with vesicles for glutamate and for gamma-aminobutyric acid kept in separate pools inside a single axon terminal.

SIZE:

Dopamine cell bodies are 15 to 30 micrometres across, slightly smaller on average than nigral dopamine cells.

Gamma-aminobutyric acid cell bodies are 10 to 20 micrometres.

Dendritic fields span 300 to 800 micrometres.

The axonal arbour of a ventral tegmental dopamine neuron is large but smaller than the nigral record holder, with total lengths estimated in the tens of centimetres to a metre or more, and a mesocortical cell contacts a far sparser field than a mesolimbic one.

The whole human ventral tegmental area is only a few tens of cubic millimetres per side.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Dopamine cells projecting to the nucleus accumbens medial shell, which respond most strongly to reward and to drugs of abuse.

• Dopamine cells projecting to the nucleus accumbens lateral shell and core, which respond to reward-predicting cues and drive approach.

• Dopamine cells projecting to the prefrontal cortex, which are less sensitive to autoreceptor feedback, fire faster, and respond strongly to stress and to aversive events.

• Dopamine cells projecting to the basolateral amygdala and to the ventral hippocampus, involved in emotional memory.

• Local and projecting gamma-aminobutyric acid neurons, which inhibit dopamine cells and also project all the way to the nucleus accumbens themselves.

• Glutamate neurons expressing the vesicular glutamate transporter VGLUT2, which co-release gamma-aminobutyric acid and promote positive reinforcement in their own right.

Anatomically the nucleus is also divided into named subnuclei: the parabrachial pigmented nucleus, the paranigral nucleus, the interfascicular nucleus, and the rostral and caudal linear nuclei.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It occupies the ventromedial midbrain tegmentum, corresponding to cell group A10, bounded laterally by the substantia nigra, ventrally by the interpeduncular nucleus, dorsally by the red nucleus, and medially by the midline raphe.

The lateral border with the substantia nigra pars compacta is a true interface with no anatomical wall, and cells straddle it.

Just caudal to it lies the rostromedial tegmental nucleus, a small inhibitory structure that acts as the ventral tegmental area's off switch for disappointment.

Its axons leave in the medial forebrain bundle, passing through the lateral hypothalamus on the way to the ventral striatum and cortex.

NUMBER:

This book budgets about 450 thousand ventral tegmental area neurons in total, both sides combined.

Applying the standard composition gives roughly 270 thousand dopamine cells, about 150 thousand gamma-aminobutyric acid cells, and perhaps 20 thousand glutamate cells, with heavy overlap because many cells carry two transmitters.

Confidence is low to moderate: unbiased counts in humans are scarce, and the best primate anchor is a macaque study reporting about 110 thousand dopamine neurons in cell group A10 per hemisphere, which scales to a similar human total.

That is about 0.23 percent of the roughly 200 million neurons of the basal ganglia and about 0.0005 percent of the 86 billion neurons of the whole nervous system.

Fewer cells than live in a small town run the entire reward economy of a human life.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Dopamine cells are slow, broad-spiking pacemakers firing at 1 to 8 hertz in a regular or irregular single-spike pattern.

They switch into burst mode, firing 2 to 6 spikes at 15 to 30 hertz, when glutamate activates N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors, and bursts release far more dopamine per spike than tonic firing does.

They show the hyperpolarisation-activated current sag, though less consistently than nigral cells, so sag alone is an unreliable identifier here.

Gamma-aminobutyric acid cells are fast, narrow-spiking, and fire tonically at 10 to 40 hertz, providing the constant inhibitory tone that dopamine cells must overcome.

Glutamate cells are electrically heterogeneous and do not fall into a single class.

The critical circuit fact is disinhibition: reduce the gamma-aminobutyric acid cells' firing and the dopamine cells burst, and this is exactly how opioids work.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory input arrives from the prefrontal cortex, the laterodorsal tegmental nucleus, the pedunculopontine nucleus, the lateral hypothalamus including orexin neurons, the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis and the subthalamic nucleus.

Inhibitory input arrives from the nucleus accumbens, the ventral pallidum, the lateral habenula by way of the rostromedial tegmental nucleus, and from local gamma-aminobutyric acid cells inside the nucleus itself.

Outputs go to the nucleus accumbens core and shell, the olfactory tubercle, the prefrontal and cingulate cortex, the amygdala, the hippocampus and the lateral habenula.

Local gamma-aminobutyric acid cells synapse directly on dopamine cell bodies and proximal dendrites, the position of maximum authority.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Dopamine axons are thin, roughly 0.1 to 0.5 micrometres, mostly unmyelinated, and slow, conducting at well under 1 metre per second.

Mesolimbic axons branch densely within the nucleus accumbens; mesocortical axons run much further and branch far more sparsely in cortex.

Most release sites are not classical synapses, so dopamine escapes into the extracellular space and acts by volume transmission.

Co-releasing terminals keep glutamate vesicles and gamma-aminobutyric acid vesicles in physically separate pools inside the same bouton, so one terminal can send two opposite messages.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Dopamine, made by tyrosine hydroxylase and packaged by the vesicular monoamine transporter VMAT2, acting on D1-family and D2-family receptors.

Gamma-aminobutyric acid, from both local interneurons and long-range projection cells.

Glutamate, packaged by VGLUT2, released by a distinct population and also co-released by some dopamine cells.

Neurotensin and cholecystokinin are common peptide co-transmitters in the dopamine cells here, which distinguishes them chemically from their nigral cousins.

Mu-opioid receptors sit on the gamma-aminobutyric acid cells, and nicotinic acetylcholine receptors sit on the dopamine cells, which is why morphine and nicotine both raise accumbens dopamine by different routes.

MYELINATION:

Ventral tegmental dopamine axons are largely unmyelinated, like their nigral counterparts and for the same reason: enormous branching is incompatible with a myelin sheath.

Some of the long mesocortical fibres running in the medial forebrain bundle carry thin myelin over their unbranched stretch.

Slow, uninsulated conduction is acceptable because dopamine's message is a concentration change lasting hundreds of milliseconds to seconds, not a millisecond-precise pulse.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High, for the same reasons as the nigral cells: sustained pacemaking, a large unmyelinated arbour, and the oxidative chemistry of dopamine itself.

The burden is measurably lighter than in the substantia nigra pars compacta, because these cells rely less on Cav1.3 calcium channels for pacemaking, they express more of the calcium-buffering protein calbindin, and their arbours are smaller.

That difference in metabolic style is the best current explanation for a striking clinical fact: in Parkinson's disease the nigral cells die while the ventral tegmental cells largely survive.

PLASTICITY:

A single exposure to cocaine, morphine, nicotine, alcohol or amphetamine strengthens the glutamate synapses onto these dopamine cells for days, a phenomenon that is one of the foundations of addiction research.

The mechanism involves the insertion of calcium-permeable AMPA receptors into the synapse, which changes the rules of subsequent learning.

Stress produces the opposite pattern in mesocortical cells, and chronic stress reshapes their firing into a burst-heavy pattern implicated in depression.

Endocannabinoids, retrograde messengers made by the dopamine cell and sent backwards across the synapse, suppress incoming inhibition and are a major local plasticity mechanism here.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here express high levels of the enzyme that degrades dopamine and help set the size of dopamine transients in the target regions.

Microglial density in the ventral tegmental area is lower than in the substantia nigra, and microglial activation here is more strongly driven by systemic inflammation than by local debris, which is one route by which illness produces the loss of motivation called sickness behaviour.

Astrocytes in this nucleus release adenosine triphosphate and adenosine that modulate dopamine cell firing, and alcohol acts partly through this glial pathway.

Oligodendrocyte involvement is limited because most of the arbour is unmyelinated.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and the ventral striatum still receives all its cortical and limbic information but has no way to know whether any of it matters.

Reward stops teaching, cues stop attracting, and effort stops being worth spending, which in a person looks like profound apathy and anhedonia, the inability to feel pleasure.

Delete only the gamma-aminobutyric acid cells and the opposite happens: dopamine release becomes unrestrained and every stimulus acquires exaggerated significance, which is the leading circuit account of psychosis.

The flavour the region would lose is wanting.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Co-transmission is the defining feature: one terminal can release dopamine and glutamate, or glutamate and gamma-aminobutyric acid, from separate vesicle pools, so the sign of the output depends on which receptors the target expresses.

Volume transmission means dopamine acts as a slowly changing chemical field rather than a wire signal.

Somatodendritic dopamine release feeds back onto D2 autoreceptors, giving local self-inhibition.

Retrograde endocannabinoid signalling lets the cell turn down its own inhibitory inputs on demand.

Gap-junction coupling between dopamine cells has been reported and may synchronise small clusters.

Local disinhibition, in which drugs silence gamma-aminobutyric acid cells and thereby release dopamine cells, is a circuit-level computation that cannot be captured by modelling the dopamine cell alone, and any simulation must include at least the two-cell motif.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Prefrontal cortex glutamate, 0 to 40 hertz, driving bursts through N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors.

◦ Laterodorsal tegmental and pedunculopontine acetylcholine and glutamate, gating the switch from tonic to burst mode.

◦ Rostromedial tegmental and accumbens gamma-aminobutyric acid, producing pauses of 100 to 400 milliseconds.

◦ Local gamma-aminobutyric acid interneuron tone, 10 to 40 hertz, perisomatic.

◦ Orexin from the lateral hypothalamus, linking arousal and appetite to reward.

◦ Pharmacological inputs: mu-opioid agonists acting on the gamma-aminobutyric acid cells, nicotine acting on the dopamine cells.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Dopamine into the nucleus accumbens core and shell, tonic 5 to 50 nanomolar, phasic transients up to several hundred nanomolar.

◦ Dopamine into prefrontal cortex, amygdala and hippocampus.

◦ Gamma-aminobutyric acid and glutamate from the non-dopamine populations to the same targets.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage of the dopamine cell and of the local gamma-aminobutyric acid cell.

◦ N-methyl-D-aspartate receptor activation level, setting burst probability.

◦ Vesicular dopamine stock, 0 to 1.

◦ Extracellular dopamine concentration in each target.

◦ Endocannabinoid level, controlling incoming inhibition.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Dopamine cell tonic rate 1 to 8 hertz, burst rate 15 to 30 hertz.

◦ Gamma-aminobutyric acid cell tonic rate 10 to 40 hertz.

◦ Population composition about 60 percent dopamine, about 30 to 35 percent gamma-aminobutyric acid, about 2 to 5 percent glutamate.

◦ Dopamine uptake in the accumbens is slower than in the dorsal striatum, so transients last longer.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Interspike interval 125 to 1,000 milliseconds.

◦ Burst duration 100 to 200 milliseconds.

◦ Accumbens dopamine transient decay about 200 to 600 milliseconds.

◦ Drug-induced synaptic strengthening lasts 5 to 10 days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Chronic drug exposure locks the glutamate inputs into a strengthened state, producing craving and relapse long after the drug is gone.

◦ Excess mesolimbic dopamine signalling is the leading circuit account of the hallucinations and delusions of schizophrenia, and every effective antipsychotic blocks D2 receptors in this pathway.

◦ Reduced activity in the mesolimbic pathway produces anhedonia and lack of motivation in depression.

◦ Unlike the nigral cells, these largely survive Parkinson's disease, so late non-motor symptoms depend on their relative sparing.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a ventral tegmental area dopamine neuron that is held down by a local inhibitory neuron and bursts when an opioid drug silences that inhibitory neuron.
Create a number variable called dopamine rate with initial value 4.
Create a number variable called inhibitory rate with initial value 25.
Create a number variable called accumbens dopamine with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called peak dopamine with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
While step is less than 4000, repeat the following steps.
    Create a number variable called opioid present with initial value 0.
    Check if both step is greater than or equal to 1000 and step is less than 3000 is true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set opioid present to 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Create a number variable called inhibitory target with initial value 25.
    Check if opioid present is equal to 1.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set inhibitory target to 2.5.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of inhibitory target and inhibitory rate.
    Set inhibitory rate to the sum of inhibitory rate and the quotient of that result divided by 30.
    Calculate the difference of 25 and inhibitory rate.
    Create a number variable called disinhibition with initial value 0.
    Set disinhibition to the product of that result and 0.35.
    Create a number variable called dopamine target with initial value 0.
    Set dopamine target to the sum of 4 and disinhibition.
    Calculate the difference of dopamine target and dopamine rate.
    Set dopamine rate to the sum of dopamine rate and the quotient of that result divided by 50.
    Calculate the product of dopamine rate and 0.05.
    Create a number variable called release with initial value 0.
    Set release to that result.
    Calculate the quotient of accumbens dopamine divided by 200.
    Set accumbens dopamine to the sum of accumbens dopamine and the difference of release and that result.
    Check if accumbens dopamine is greater than peak dopamine.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set peak dopamine to accumbens dopamine.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of step and 1.
    Set step to that result.
End the loop.
Display dopamine rate to the console.
Display peak dopamine to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

JavaScript is chosen because the essential lesson here is a two-cell circuit rather than a single membrane, and JavaScript runs in any browser with no build step, so a simulation team can put disinhibition on screen as a live demonstration.

// Ventral tegmental area: a dopamine cell held down by a local GABA cell.
// An opioid silences the GABA cell, and the dopamine cell is released.

const dt = 1.0;          // milliseconds per step
const STEPS = 4000;      // 4 seconds of simulated time
const TAU_DA = 50.0;     // ms, how fast the dopamine cell changes rate
const TAU_GABA = 30.0;   // ms, how fast the GABA cell changes rate

let daRate = 4.0;        // Hz, tonic pacemaker rate of the dopamine cell
let gabaRate = 25.0;     // Hz, local inhibitory tone
let dopamine = 40.0;     // nanomolar in the nucleus accumbens
let peak = dopamine;

for (let i = 0; i < STEPS; i++) {
  const t = i * dt;
  const opioid = (t >= 1000.0 && t < 3000.0) ? 1.0 : 0.0;   // morphine on board
  const gabaTarget = 25.0 * (1.0 - 0.9 * opioid);           // mu receptors hush it
  gabaRate += dt * (gabaTarget - gabaRate) / TAU_GABA;

  // less inhibition means more dopamine firing: this is disinhibition
  let daTarget = 4.0 + 0.35 * (25.0 - gabaRate);
  if (daTarget < 0.0) daTarget = 0.0;
  daRate += dt * (daTarget - daRate) / TAU_DA;

  // release scales with firing rate; the transporter clears with tau 200 ms
  dopamine += dt * (0.05 * daRate - dopamine / 200.0) * 10.0;
  if (dopamine > peak) peak = dopamine;
}

console.log("GABA cell rate at the end:", gabaRate.toFixed(1), "Hz");
console.log("dopamine cell rate at the end:", daRate.toFixed(1), "Hz");
console.log("peak accumbens dopamine:", peak.toFixed(1), "nM");

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only place in the brain where a single nucleus contains dopamine, gamma-aminobutyric acid and glutamate neurons in roughly a sixty, thirty-five and five percent mix, with individual terminals holding two different transmitters in separate vesicle pools.

If a midbrain cell is a slow broad-spiking pacemaker that bursts when its local inhibitory neighbour is silenced by an opioid, projects to the nucleus accumbens and prefrontal cortex, carries neurotensin or cholecystokinin, and survives Parkinson's disease while its lateral neighbour dies, it is a ventral tegmental area neuron.

CHAPTER 80. NUCLEUS ACCUMBENS MEDIUM SPINY NEURONS (Motivation-To-Action Converting Neuron) (COG)

This is the cell that decides whether something is worth wanting, and it spends most of its life refusing to fire.

The old name is an address joined to a shape description, naming the nucleus and the spines but not the transformation performed.

Motivation-To-Action names the transformation itself, which is the classic description of the accumbens as the place where limbic drive becomes motor output.

Converting names the work in between, since value-laden input from the amygdala, hippocampus and prefrontal cortex arrives on the dense dendritic spines together with dopamine from the midbrain, and what leaves is an inhibitory command to the ventral pallidum and midbrain that biases whether an approach is actually made.

Neuron marks it as the cell making up more than ninety-five percent of all neurons in the nucleus accumbens, so that naming it names the structure.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The nucleus accumbens is the ventral part of the striatum, sitting where the caudate nucleus and putamen merge underneath the front of the brain.

Its main cell is the medium spiny neuron, so called because it is medium-sized and its dendrites are carpeted with spines, tiny mushroom-shaped knobs that each receive one excitatory contact.

Roughly nine out of ten accumbens neurons are of this type.

Its job is to sit at the exact point where information about the world, about memory, about emotion and about value all arrive at once, and to fire only when enough of them agree.

Emotion arrives from the amygdala, context from the hippocampus, plans from the prefrontal cortex, alerting signals from the thalamus, and the price tag from dopamine cells of the ventral tegmental area.

The output is a decision about whether to approach, avoid, or expend effort, delivered as inhibition to the ventral pallidum and the midbrain.

This is the cell on which addictive drugs leave their longest-lasting structural marks.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is round or ovoid with a thin rim of cytoplasm, and it looks almost featureless until the dendrites are stained.

Seven to ten primary dendrites radiate outward, branching two or three times to produce a roughly spherical tree.

Beyond the first 20 to 30 micrometres the dendrites are densely covered with spines, at a density of one to two spines per micrometre, giving a single cell somewhere between 5,000 and 15,000 spines.

Each spine is a separate chemical compartment and can be strengthened or weakened independently, which is what makes this cell a learning machine rather than a switch.

Accumbens spiny neurons have slightly smaller and less densely spined dendritic trees than their dorsal striatal counterparts, and the shell subdivision has the smallest of all.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 12 to 20 micrometres across.

Dendritic trees span 300 to 500 micrometres, so the cell listens within a sphere about a third of a millimetre wide.

The local axon collateral field roughly matches the dendritic field.

The main axon travels 1 to 3 centimetres to reach the ventral pallidum or the midbrain.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• D1 receptor-expressing medium spiny neurons, which carry the excitatory dopamine receptor, also carry the peptides substance P and dynorphin, and project to the ventral pallidum, the ventral tegmental area and the substantia nigra, forming the direct-like pathway.

• D2 receptor-expressing medium spiny neurons, which carry the inhibitory dopamine receptor, also carry enkephalin and the adenosine A2A receptor, and project only to the ventral pallidum, forming the indirect-like pathway.

• Core neurons, in the region wrapped around the anterior commissure, resembling dorsal striatal cells and driving cue-triggered approach and effort.

• Shell neurons, in the surrounding rim, resembling extended amygdala cells, controlling hedonic reactions and the reinstatement of drug seeking.

A minority of accumbens spiny neurons carry both D1 and D2 receptors, so the two-pathway picture is a strong simplification rather than a law.

Older simple accounts assign reward to D1 cells and aversion to D2 cells, but careful stimulation experiments show both populations can drive both, depending on the pattern and duration of activity.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The nucleus accumbens lies in the basal forebrain, ventral to the caudate nucleus and medial to the putamen, immediately below and around the anterior commissure, which is the landmark separating core from shell.

It is continuous above with the caudate and putamen and continuous below with the olfactory tubercle, so it is best understood as one end of a gradient rather than a separate organ.

The medial shell borders the septum and the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis, and this shell-to-extended-amygdala interface is where reward circuitry meets stress circuitry.

Laterally the shell abuts the ventral pallidum, its principal target.

NUMBER:

The human nucleus accumbens has a volume of roughly 470 cubic millimetres per side, which is about 5 percent of the striatum.

Applying that fraction to the striatum's roughly 110 million neurons gives about 5 million accumbens neurons in total across both sides, of which about 90 to 95 percent are medium spiny neurons, so roughly 4.5 to 4.7 million cells of this type.

Confidence is low, because this is a volume-scaled estimate rather than a direct human count, and the defensible range is 3 million to 8 million.

That is about 2.4 percent of the roughly 200 million neurons of the basal ganglia and about 0.005 percent of the 86 billion neurons of the whole nervous system.

The split between D1-type and D2-type cells is close to even, with a modest excess of mixed and D1 cells in the shell.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The defining feature is the down state, a deeply hyperpolarised resting voltage near minus 80 to minus 90 millivolts, held there by inward-rectifying potassium channels of the Kir2 family that clamp the membrane hard.

In this state the cell has a low input resistance, so ordinary inputs barely move it, and it is effectively deaf.

When many excitatory inputs arrive together the cell jumps into the up state, a plateau near minus 55 millivolts, from which spiking becomes possible.

Even in the up state, firing is sparse, typically under 1 to 5 hertz, and the cell never fires spontaneously.

A slowly inactivating A-type potassium current adds a long delay between the arrival of input and the first spike, of the order of tens to hundreds of milliseconds, which further enforces the need for sustained agreement among inputs.

Accumbens cells sit slightly more depolarised and are somewhat more excitable than dorsal striatal cells, and D2 cells are more excitable than D1 cells.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory glutamate inputs arrive from the prefrontal cortex, especially prelimbic and infralimbic regions, the basolateral amygdala, the ventral hippocampus by way of the subiculum, and the paraventricular and midline thalamic nuclei.

These sources target the two cell types unevenly, and inputs to the core are broadly scattered while inputs to the shell are concentrated.

Dopamine arrives from the ventral tegmental area, mostly at non-synaptic release sites near the necks of spines, so it modulates rather than drives.

Local inhibition arrives from fast-spiking parvalbumin interneurons, from low-threshold-spiking interneurons, and from the axon collaterals of neighbouring spiny neurons.

Acetylcholine arrives from local giant cholinergic interneurons whose pauses are timed to reward events.

Outputs are inhibitory synapses on the ventral pallidum, and for D1 cells also on the ventral tegmental area, the substantia nigra, the lateral hypothalamus and the pedunculopontine nucleus.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thin, 0.3 to 1 micrometre, and emits a rich local collateral network before leaving the nucleus.

Those local collaterals provide lateral inhibition, letting an active cell suppress its neighbours, although the effect is weaker than classical winner-take-all models assumed.

The main axon runs in the medial forebrain bundle to the ventral pallidum and midbrain, a distance of one to three centimetres.

Terminals are numerous and form conventional inhibitory synapses, with peptide co-release requiring higher firing rates than gamma-aminobutyric acid release.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Gamma-aminobutyric acid is the fast transmitter, acting on gamma-aminobutyric acid type A receptors in the targets.

D1 cells co-release substance P, which acts on neurokinin receptors, and dynorphin, which acts on kappa-opioid receptors and produces dysphoria.

D2 cells co-release enkephalin, which acts on delta and mu-opioid receptors and is broadly pleasurable.

So the two cell types deliver not only opposite circuit signs but opposite chemical moods, and both effects only appear during vigorous firing.

MYELINATION:

The long axons to the ventral pallidum and midbrain are thinly myelinated by oligodendrocytes.

Local axon collaterals inside the nucleus are unmyelinated.

Myelination here is functionally modest, since conduction over one to three centimetres at a few metres per second takes only a few milliseconds, which is fast enough for a system whose decisions unfold over hundreds of milliseconds.

Notably, chronic social stress and chronic cocaine both change myelin gene expression in this region, so the insulation itself is part of the disease process.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Baseline cost is low, because the cell rests deeply hyperpolarised and fires rarely, and it spends most of the day silent.

The expensive part is the spine population: 5,000 to 15,000 spines each maintaining receptors, scaffolding proteins and local protein synthesis machinery.

Costs spike during learning, when spines are built, and after drug exposure, when spine density rises measurably within days.

PLASTICITY:

This is one of the most plastic cells in the adult brain, and its plasticity is where addiction physically lives.

Cocaine increases dendritic spine density on both D1 and D2 accumbens spiny neurons, a structural change visible under a microscope and lasting weeks.

The transcription factor delta-FosB accumulates in D1 cells with repeated drug exposure and stays elevated for weeks, reprogramming gene expression and increasing the reward value of the drug.

Long-term depression at prefrontal-to-accumbens synapses is lost after chronic cocaine, and restoring it reduces relapse in animals.

Calcium-permeable AMPA receptors are inserted into these synapses during prolonged withdrawal and drive craving that grows rather than fades with time.

Scaffolding proteins including glutamate receptor-interacting protein 1 and A-kinase anchoring protein 150 control these changes differently in D1 and D2 cells.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here do something with unusual clinical importance: they supply the glutamate that occupies extrasynaptic receptors, through the cystine-glutamate exchanger, and chronic cocaine collapses this system.

Astrocytes in the accumbens also physically retract from synapses after drug exposure, so glutamate spills further and reaches receptors it should not.

Restoring the cystine-glutamate exchanger with the drug N-acetylcysteine reduces relapse in animals and has been tested in people, making this one of the few places where a glial fix is a candidate therapy.

Microglia in the accumbens respond to alcohol and opioids and release cytokines that alter these neurons' excitability.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell and the ventral forebrain still receives every emotional, contextual and cognitive signal but has nothing that combines them into a verdict.

The limbic system would be all input and no output, capable of feeling but not of translating feeling into effort.

Because the cell only fires on convergence, its removal specifically destroys the brain's ability to require agreement before acting, so the region would lose its scepticism.

Concretely, motivation to pursue a distant reward would disappear, cues would stop triggering approach, and the transition from liking something to working for it would be severed.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The up and down state transition is a genuine two-state nonlinearity, not a threshold, and no leaky integrator reproduces it.

Individual spines perform local computation, with their own calcium dynamics, their own protein synthesis and their own strength.

Dendrites generate local calcium spikes and plateau potentials that can be triggered by clustered inputs without the cell body knowing.

Back-propagating action potentials from the cell body sweep into the dendrites and mark recently active spines for change.

Gene transcription is a first-class part of the cell's behaviour here, with delta-FosB acting on a timescale of weeks, so a serious simulation of addiction must model transcription as a state variable.

Local protein synthesis inside dendrites supports lasting spine changes without instruction from the cell body.

Retrograde endocannabinoid signalling from this cell suppresses its own incoming excitation and is required for long-term depression.

Metabolic coupling to astrocytes through the cystine-glutamate exchanger makes the surrounding glutamate tone part of the cell's own computation.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Prefrontal cortex glutamate, 0 to 20 hertz per fibre, thousands of fibres, AMPA and N-methyl-D-aspartate receptors.

◦ Basolateral amygdala glutamate, 0 to 20 hertz, carrying emotional value.

◦ Ventral hippocampal glutamate, 0 to 15 hertz, carrying context and driving the up state most effectively.

◦ Paraventricular thalamic glutamate, 0 to 15 hertz, carrying salience and arousal.

◦ Dopamine, 5 to 50 nanomolar tonic and up to several hundred nanomolar phasic, acting on D1 or D2 receptors.

◦ Local gamma-aminobutyric acid from fast-spiking interneurons and neighbouring spiny neurons.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Gamma-aminobutyric acid onto the ventral pallidum from both cell types.

◦ Gamma-aminobutyric acid onto the ventral tegmental area, substantia nigra, lateral hypothalamus and pedunculopontine nucleus from D1 cells.

◦ Substance P and dynorphin from D1 cells, enkephalin from D2 cells, released only above about 10 hertz.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage in millivolts, with an explicit up or down state flag.

◦ Total excitatory conductance in nanosiemens.

◦ A-type potassium current inactivation, 0 to 1.

◦ Per-spine weight vector, 5,000 to 15,000 entries.

◦ Delta-FosB level, a slow variable with a timescale of weeks.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Down state voltage minus 80 to minus 90 millivolts; up state plateau about minus 55 millivolts; threshold about minus 45 millivolts.

◦ Input resistance 40 to 100 megaohms in the down state, higher in the up state.

◦ Spine count 5,000 to 15,000; spine density 1 to 2 per micrometre.

◦ Firing rate 0 hertz at rest, under 5 hertz when active.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane time constant 10 to 20 milliseconds.

◦ Up state duration 200 to 1,000 milliseconds.

◦ Delay to first spike 50 to 300 milliseconds because of the A-type potassium current.

◦ Dopamine transient decay 200 to 600 milliseconds.

◦ Delta-FosB accumulation and decay, weeks.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Chronic drugs of abuse add spines, load D1 cells with delta-FosB, abolish long-term depression and insert calcium-permeable AMPA receptors, producing craving that intensifies during abstinence.

◦ Astrocytic cystine-glutamate exchange collapses, extrasynaptic glutamate falls and relapse becomes easier to trigger.

◦ Reduced activity in this cell population is associated with anhedonia in depression, and deep brain stimulation of the nucleus accumbens has been trialled for treatment-resistant depression and for obsessive-compulsive disorder.

◦ Excess D2-pathway activity contributes to aversion and avoidance.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to simulate a nucleus accumbens medium spiny neuron that stays in a deep down state until several named glutamate inputs arrive together, then enters an up state and fires sparsely.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value -85.
Create a number variable called excitation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called up state steps with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called step with initial value 0.
While step is less than 4000, repeat the following steps.
    Create a number variable called active sources with initial value 0.
    Check if both step is greater than or equal to 1000 and step is less than 3000 is true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set active sources to 4.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set active sources to 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of active sources and 0.4.
    Set excitation to the sum of excitation and that result.
    Calculate the quotient of excitation divided by 20.
    Set excitation to the difference of excitation and that result.
    Create a number variable called rectifier with initial value 1.
    Check if voltage is less than -70.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set rectifier to 2.5.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the difference of voltage and -85.
    Create a number variable called leak with initial value 0.
    Set leak to the product of that result and rectifier.
    Calculate the product of excitation and 12.
    Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
    Set drive to the difference of that result and leak.
    Set voltage to the sum of voltage and the quotient of drive divided by 24.
    Check if voltage is greater than -70.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of up state steps and 1.
        Set up state steps to that result.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if voltage is greater than or equal to -45.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set spike count to that result.
        Set voltage to -70.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of step and 1.
    Set step to that result.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
Display up state steps to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Go is chosen because a realistic ventral striatum needs millions of these cells stepped together, and Go gives compiled speed and easy parallelism with a standard library that needs no external packages.

package main

import (
	"fmt"
	"math/rand"
)

func main() {
	const dt = 0.5      // ms per step
	const steps = 4000  // 2000 ms of simulated time
	const vDown = -85.0 // mV, the down state the rectifier clamps to
	const vTh = -45.0   // mV, spike threshold
	const tau = 12.0    // ms, membrane time constant
	const tauSyn = 10.0 // ms, glutamate conductance decay
	const weight = 32.0 // mV of drive per unit of conductance

	rng := rand.New(rand.NewSource(3))
	v, gExc := vDown, 0.0
	spikes, upSteps := 0, 0

	for i := 0; i < steps; i++ {
		t := float64(i) * dt
		p := 0.002 // quiet background rate per source per step
		if t >= 500.0 && t < 1500.0 {
			p = 0.020 // cortex, amygdala, hippocampus and thalamus all fire
		}
		for s := 0; s < 4; s++ { // four named glutamate sources
			if rng.Float64() < p {
				gExc += 1.0
			}
		}
		gExc -= dt * gExc / tauSyn
		g := 1.0
		if v < -70.0 {
			g = 2.5 // the inward rectifier grips hard in the down state
		}
		v += dt * (-g*(v-vDown) + weight*gExc) / tau
		if v > -70.0 {
			upSteps++
		}
		if v >= vTh {
			spikes++
			v = -70.0
		}
	}
	fmt.Printf("spikes: %d\n", spikes)
	fmt.Printf("time spent in the up state: %.0f ms\n", float64(upSteps)*dt)
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is a spine-covered cell that sits at minus 85 millivolts refusing to answer, that needs the cortex, the amygdala, the hippocampus and the thalamus to agree before it will speak, and that is physically remodelled by every addictive drug.

If a neuron in the basal forebrain shows a two-state membrane with a long delay to its first spike, carries either substance P and dynorphin or enkephalin and the adenosine A2A receptor, projects to the ventral pallidum, and grows new dendritic spines after cocaine, it is a nucleus accumbens medium spiny neuron.

CHAPTER 81. BOTH-PATHWAY QUIETING NEURON (COG) (PUC)

You reach for a mug, see the steam, and your hand freezes in mid air.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

It is an inhibitory cell of the external globus pallidus (GPe), a pale wedge deep in the brain.

Its axon runs backwards into the striatum, the movement system's input station, where a go team competes with a no go team.

This construct takes no side and hushes both teams at once.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The spindle shaped cell body carries three to five smooth dendrites, the antennae that gather signals, splayed into a flat disc.

Almost its whole axon lies in the striatum as a net.

SIZE:

The cell body is 15 to 25 micrometres long, a micrometre being one thousandth of a millimetre.

Rat tracing gives tens of millimetres of striatal axon.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Territorial variants matter: dorsolateral for limbs, dorsomedial for rules, ventral for motivation.

A molecular split separates cells carrying the FoxP2 gene switch from a fringe carrying Npas1 alone.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies sit in the external globus pallidus, enriched along its edges.

Name the interfaces: the pallidostriatal border with putamen and caudate, the internal capsule edge, the lamina between external and internal pallidum, and the ventral pallidum seam.

Terminals fill both striatal compartments, striosome and matrix.

NUMBER:

Arkypallidal cells are a quarter of GPe neurons in rodents, so against a human pallidal budget of 1.5 million, halved for those ignoring pathway identity, the estimate is 150,000 to 220,000.

Confidence is low, since older human counts of the whole pallidum run nearer 700,000.

That is 0.09 percent of the 200 million basal ganglia neurons and 0.0002 percent of all 86 billion.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

In the rat it fires under 2 spikes per second and irregularly, while its neighbours fire above 10 regularly.

A deep afterhyperpolarisation, the voltage dip after each spike, restrains it until a stop cue drives a burst of 50 to 100 spikes per second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

It receives gamma aminobutyric acid (GABA) from indirect pathway striatal cells, glutamate from the subthalamic nucleus, and sparse cortical input.

It contacts both pathways and striatal interneurons with only a few boutons each.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thin, near half a micrometre, and its oddity is direction, since pallidal axons normally run onward while this one turns back.

Travel takes 1 to 3 milliseconds in a rat.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The fast transmitter is GABA, read by GABA type A receptors, chloride channels that open within a millisecond.

It also makes preproenkephalin, parent of the opioid peptide enkephalin, which damps glutamate release for seconds.

MYELINATION:

Myelin is the fatty sheath that speeds conduction, and this axon carries only a thin one, since a centimetre of travel needs no speed.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Nearly silent at rest, its baseline bill is modest.

The cost sits in thousands of bare terminal branches that leak sodium and must be pumped clean using adenosine triphosphate, the cell's energy currency.

PLASTICITY:

Striatal inputs onto it strengthen when dopamine is lost, which is why this brake grows overbearing in Parkinson's disease.

Its terminals fade within a burst, so the cancel signal is brief and not repeatable.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing exotic is documented for the glia here, and saying so plainly is honest.

The neighbourhood differs: the pallidum is among the most iron rich grey matter in the body, so its astrocytes, the star shaped housekeepers, carry a heavy oxidative load.

Striatal astrocytes clear the released GABA and set how far it spreads.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete it and the striatum keeps its go and no go contest but loses its emergency brake.

An action already committed could never be aborted, so the region loses last moment reversibility.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

GABA spilling from its scattered synapses reaches receptors outside the synapse, a volume transmission in which the messenger diffuses instead of being aimed.

The enkephalin it co releases lasts seconds, and endocannabinoids sent backwards by the receiving cell tune its own inputs.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.
