The purpose of this procedure is to compute, trial by trial, how disappointed a lateral habenular neuron is and whether that disappointment is large enough to make it burst.
Create a number variable called expectation with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called learning rate with initial value 0.25.
Create a number variable called baseline rate with initial value 4.
Create a number variable called gain with initial value 22.
Create a number variable called error with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called the cell is bursting with initial value false.
Create a list variable called rewards with initial value empty list.
For each reward in rewards, perform the following operations.
    Set error to the difference of expectation and reward.
    If error is less than 0 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set error to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set firing rate to the sum of baseline rate and the product of gain and error.
    Check if error is greater than 0.6.
    Set the cell is bursting to the result of that check.
    If the cell is bursting is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to the product of firing rate and 2.5.
    End the conditional block.
    Display firing rate to the console.
    Set expectation to the sum of expectation and the product of learning rate and the difference of reward and expectation.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python chosen because the heart of this cell is a running expectation and a
# subtraction, and plain arithmetic keeps the psychology visible in the code.
ALPHA = 0.25           # how fast the expectation tracks recent reward
BASELINE_HZ = 4.0      # tonic firing when nothing surprising happens
GAIN_HZ = 22.0         # extra spikes per second per unit of disappointment
BURST_THRESHOLD = 0.6  # disappointment big enough to trigger a calcium burst
NMDA_BLOCK = 0.0       # set to 1.0 to simulate a single dose of ketamine

expectation = 0.0
trials = [1.0, 1.0, 1.0, 1.0, 0.0, 1.0, 0.0, 0.0, 1.0, 1.0]

for n, reward in enumerate(trials):
    error = expectation - reward             # positive means worse than expected
    rate = BASELINE_HZ + GAIN_HZ * max(0.0, error)
    burst = (error > BURST_THRESHOLD) and (NMDA_BLOCK < 0.5)
    if burst:
        rate = rate * 2.5                    # a burst counts far more downstream
    dopamine_cut = min(1.0, rate / 40.0)     # fraction of dopamine cells silenced
    expectation = expectation + ALPHA * (reward - expectation)
    print("trial %2d  reward %.1f  expect %.2f  habenula %5.1f Hz  burst %-5s"
          "  dopamine cut %.0f%%"
          % (n + 1, reward, expectation, rate, str(burst), 100 * dopamine_cut))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only population in the brain whose firing rate goes up when something good fails to happen, and whose axons leave in a single dense vertical cable aimed at the machinery of reward.

Nowhere else does an astrocyte's potassium channel decide whether a neuron bursts, and nowhere else does blocking one receptor type in a pea-sized structure lift a person's mood within hours.

CHAPTER 89. ZONA INCERTA NEURONS (Action-Releasing Gate Neuron) (COG)

In 1877 Auguste Forel found a thin grey sheet under the thalamus, could say nothing certain of it, and named it the zone of uncertainty.

The old name is Latin for uncertain zone, a nineteenth-century confession of ignorance that still tells a student nothing.

Action-Releasing names the mechanism, because the cells are overwhelmingly inhibitory, so their effect is to lift restraint rather than to command.

Gate names the breadth of what gets released, since switching these cells on drives immediate binge-like eating, producing about thirty-five percent of a normal day's food intake in ten minutes, and simultaneously cuts short defensive freezing.

Neuron marks it as a thin sheet of cells carrying a broad, non-specific movement signal to an unusually wide list of targets, from cortex and thalamus to midbrain, of which around three-quarters are most active during sleep, so the honest reading is a general permission switch for whole behaviours rather than a dedicated feeding or fear circuit.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

Zona incerta neurons are a flat sheet of mostly inhibitory cells wedged under the thalamus.

Inhibitory means they make targets less likely to fire; the sheet gates incoming touch and sets the intensity of hunting, feeding, fear, pain and sleep.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Most are multipolar: thick dendrites, the branches that collect input, radiate from the cell body like a starburst.

Dendrites run 300 to 600 micrometres, and axon side branches, called collaterals, run 400 to 500 micrometres sideways.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are small to medium, though one rodent sector is named magnocellularis, Latin for large-celled.

The dendritic field exceeds a millimetre, and the human rostral zona incerta measures 54.6 cubic millimetres a side.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Sectors are rostral, dorsal, ventral and caudal, plus magnocellularis and retropolaris in rodents.

About 79 percent of neurons release gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the main inhibitory messenger, and 19 percent glutamate; among GABA cells 53 percent carry calretinin, 37 percent somatostatin and 2 percent parvalbumin, with a dopamine group named A13 rostromedially.

WHERE IT LIVES:

It lies in the subthalamus, below the thalamus and above the subthalamic nucleus, between the white matter fields of Forel, H1 above and H2 below.

Laterally it abuts the internal capsule and cerebellothalamic fasciculus, medially the hypothalamus; its key interface is the caudal zona incerta, posteromedial to the subthalamic nucleus where the posterior subthalamic area and medial lemniscus meet.

NUMBER:

No unbiased human count exists, so this is an estimate.

Roughly 200 cubic millimetres a side, at the subthalamic nucleus density of 2,200 to 3,300 neurons per cubic millimetre, gives half a million a side and a million in all, some 2 percent of the 60 million diencephalic neurons.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Firing is slow, 1.53 spikes per second in slow-wave states and 6.33 when activated, with rebound bursts on release from inhibition through low-threshold T-type calcium channels.

Rheobase, the smallest current that triggers a spike, is 23.1 picoamps in calretinin cells, 29.2 in glutamate cells, 41.3 in somatostatin cells.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from cortical layer 5, 65 to 75 percent of human cortico-incertal tracts being frontal, plus amygdala somatostatin cells onto local parvalbumin cells, superior colliculus, ventral tegmental area and brainstem cholinergic nuclei.

Outputs reach posterior medial and paraventricular thalamus, periaqueductal grey, superior colliculus, lateral habenula, lateral hypothalamus, inferior olive, pontine nuclei and neocortex directly.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are thin, long and branched, so one soma sends an ascending branch to thalamus and a descending branch to midbrain at once.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA dominates, glutamate is a large minority, dopamine is confined to A13, and somatostatin, calretinin, parvalbumin, calbindin and vasopressin tune the signal.

In mouse medial zona incerta 43 percent of cells show tyrosine hydroxylase, the dopamine-making enzyme, against 4 percent laterally.

MYELINATION:

Its own axons carry thin myelin, the fatty insulation that speeds conduction.

The region is defined instead by the heavy myelin around it, seen only by windowing scans to a T1 relaxation time of 1,000 to 2,000 milliseconds.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Demand is modest at a few spikes a second, though fast-spiking parvalbumin cells are costly and iron handling here is abnormal in Parkinson's disease models.

PLASTICITY:

Amygdala synapses onto parvalbumin cells strengthen with fear conditioning and are needed to recall that fear weeks later.

Sleep pressure recruits a subgroup marked by the gene Lhx6, whose deletion in development permanently shortens sleep.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Nothing uniquely incertal has been shown for its glia, the brain's support cells.

Only position differs: sitting inside white matter, these neurons are crowded by myelin-making oligodendrocytes rather than the astrocyte carpet of cortex.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove it and the posterior thalamus loses its brake, so fingertip signals that should arrive late, small and filtered arrive early, large and raw.

That is central pain syndrome, where ordinary touch becomes agony.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Its millimetre-wide dendritic field averages input across the whole sheet, and T-type calcium channels store recent inhibition to turn a pause into a rebound burst.

Brainstem acetylcholine suppresses the population, so arousal sets output, while A13 cells release dopamine by volume transmission, drifting through tissue fluid to distant receptors.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: cortical layer 5 excitation 0 to 40 picoamps, amygdala inhibition, collicular and tegmental drive, acetylcholine 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: GABA onto posterior medial thalamus, periaqueductal grey, superior colliculus and lateral hypothalamus; glutamate from 19 percent of cells; dopamine from A13.

• STATE: voltage, T-type readiness, output conductance, recent spikes.

• PARAMETERS: rest minus 70 millivolts, threshold minus 50, rheobase 23 to 41 picoamps, peak 42 to 105 hertz.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 milliseconds, T-type recovery 100 milliseconds, synapse 8 milliseconds, behaviour seconds to minutes.

• FAILURE MODE: too little output gives central pain and sensory flooding; disordered output feeds tremor, and stimulating the caudal zona incerta cuts contralateral tremor about 88 percent.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide when one zona incerta neuron fires, including its rebound after a pause in cortical drive.
Create a number variable called voltage with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called readiness with initial value 0.
For each moment in the drive list, perform the following operations.
    Calculate the sum of moment and readiness.
    Calculate the sum of voltage and the result.
    Set voltage to the result.
    If voltage is less than -20 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set voltage to -20.
        Set readiness to 6.
    End the conditional block.
    If voltage is greater than or equal to 20 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set voltage to 0.
        Set readiness to 0.
        Display moment to the console.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python: no libraries, and it reads almost like English.
DT, TAU_M, V_REST, V_TH = 0.5, 20.0, -70.0, -50.0  # ms and mV
TAU_H, H_HALF, G_T = 100.0, -75.0, 2.5             # T-type calcium channel

def run(drive):
    v, h, spikes = V_REST, 0.0, []
    for i, x in enumerate(drive):
        h += DT * ((1.0 if v < H_HALF else 0.0) - h) / TAU_H  # arms when hyperpolarised
        v += DT * (-(v - V_REST) + x + G_T * h * max(0.0, v - H_HALF)) / TAU_M
        if v >= V_TH:
            v, h = V_REST, h * 0.5                 # a spike spends the stored charge
            spikes.append(i * DT)                  # spike time, milliseconds
    return spikes

n = 400                                            # tonic, pause, release
s = run([22.0] * n + [-25.0] * n + [22.0] * n)
print(round(min(t for t in s if t >= 400.0) - 400.0, 1), "ms to rebound spike")

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other cell is a paper-thin sheet of inhibition slipped between two white matter bundles, with dendrites spanning its own nucleus and axons reaching thalamus, midbrain and cortex.

It alone makes a gentle touch feel like fire by falling silent, and it alone, stimulated, stills a trembling hand.


PART SEVEN: HYPOTHALAMUS - HORMONES, DRIVES, AND BODY STATE

CHAPTER 90. MAGNOCELLULAR NEUROSECRETORY CELLS (Large-Bodied Blood-Releasing Neuron) (NON-COG)

Behind your eyes sits a small crowd of giant cells that are half nerve and half gland, and every time your blood turns salty they squirt hormone straight into your bloodstream.

Magnocellular simply means large celled, so the old label carried size and nothing else.

Large-Bodied preserves that one fact, since these are among the biggest neurons in the human brain.

Blood-Releasing names the astonishing thing they do, which is to skip every synapse and empty their cargo straight into the bloodstream at the posterior pituitary.

Neuron marks it as a cell sending a single long axon with almost no branches to the posterior lobe, where it swells into roughly two thousand secretory terminals and about four hundred large storage swellings.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A magnocellular neurosecretory cell is a nerve cell that has taken a second job as a gland, and magnocellular simply means large-celled.

It fires action potentials, which are brief voltage spikes, but instead of handing them to a partner neuron it uses them to blow hormone-filled bubbles open into blood.

Its task is to convert two body facts, how concentrated the blood is and whether a baby is suckling, into a hormone level in the circulation minutes later.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A plump oval cell body crammed with rough endoplasmic reticulum, the ribbon-shaped protein factory of a cell, carrying only one to three thick dendrites, which are the branch-like input arms of a neuron.

Those dendrites are not passive listeners but are packed with hormone vesicles they release locally, while one long axon dives through the pituitary stalk and ends in a spray of terminals on blood capillaries, swelling on the way into hormone depots called Herring bodies.

SIZE:

The cell body is about 20 to 35 micrometres across, and a micrometre is one thousandth of a millimetre, so three of these would span a human hair.

Dendrites run 100 to 1000 micrometres, the axon travels 2 to 4 centimetres and ends in an estimated 10,000 terminals, and Herring bodies swell to 5 to 20 micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two families, the oxytocin cells and the vasopressin cells, each described as a separate type in the entries that follow.

In human tissue the vasopressin cells have slightly larger cell bodies, and roughly one to three cells in a hundred contain both peptides.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The main colony is the supraoptic nucleus, a dense cap on the optic tract just above where the optic nerves cross, with a second in the magnocellular division of the paraventricular nucleus against the third ventricle wall, and accessory groups such as the nucleus circularis between them.

The decisive interface is the pituitary stalk and posterior pituitary, where the axon leaves the protected brain and meets fenestrated capillaries, meaning vessels with windows in their walls, and a second interface is the border with the organum vasculosum of the lamina terminalis, a gap in the blood-brain barrier where the brain samples blood directly.

NUMBER:

Both sides combined, the supraoptic nuclei hold about 75 thousand of these cells, the magnocellular paraventricular about 30 thousand, and accessory clusters perhaps 15 thousand more.

That gives roughly 100 to 130 thousand in a human, a moderate-confidence figure because post-mortem counts vary with method, making about 1 percent of the hypothalamus and one neuron in every 700 thousand in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

At rest the cell idles at 1 to 3 spikes per second, slow enough to count by ear.

A transient potassium current delays the first spike after a quiet period, while a slow depolarising afterpotential, a lingering voltage bump left by each spike, summates so that firing makes further firing easier.

Output climbs about 0.4 to 0.5 picograms of hormone per millilitre for every milliosmole per kilogram the blood rises above a threshold near 280.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Glutamate, the brain's main accelerator chemical, arrives from the organum vasculosum, subfornical organ and nucleus tractus solitarius, while gamma-aminobutyric acid, shortened to GABA and the brain's main brake, arrives from the perinuclear zone.

Noradrenaline from the brainstem A1 group drives the cell hard during blood loss, and it also receives messages that are not synapses at all, in the form of peptide released from neighbouring dendrites.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin at 0.5 to 1 micrometre and conducting slowly at 0.5 to 1 metre per second, which costs nothing when the readout is a blood hormone level.

Each axon branches into thousands of endings pressed against windowed capillaries rather than onto another cell.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The products are peptide hormones, oxytocin or vasopressin, each packaged with a carrier protein called a neurophysin.

Co-stored partners include dynorphin, an opioid self-brake, plus galanin, cholecystokinin, nitric oxide and adenosine triphosphate, abbreviated ATP, the cell's energy currency doubling as a signal.

MYELINATION:

Unmyelinated throughout, meaning no fatty insulating sleeve, with the stalk axons wrapped instead by specialised glia called pituicytes.

This is no design failure, because slow conduction is invisible when the message is a hormone that circulates for minutes.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Protein output dominates the bill, not spiking, since peptide must be made, packaged and shipped along centimetres of axon.

Under dehydration these neurons hypertrophy, meaning they physically swell, raising messenger ribonucleic acid, shortened to mRNA, for the hormone gene many-fold, and they lean heavily on lactate supplied by astrocytes.

PLASTICITY:

During dehydration, birth and breastfeeding the tissue rewires within hours, as astrocyte sheets withdraw, neighbouring neurons touch and new synapses form on the exposed membrane.

The whole arrangement reverses within days, making this the best-documented structural plasticity in the adult mammalian brain.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

These astrocytes are exceptional because they retract and re-extend on demand, which astrocytes elsewhere do not do on this scale, and by leaving they stop mopping up glutamate and let neurons synchronise.

They also release taurine when swollen, quietening the neurons when blood is dilute, while pituicytes unwrap the nerve endings during demand as a mechanical tap, and microglia, the brain's immune cells, activate here in dehydration.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete these cells and the hypothalamus loses its plumbing department entirely.

The person would pour out fifteen to twenty litres of dilute urine a day, could not give birth normally or breastfeed, and the region would lose its most spectacular trick, remodelling its own architecture in response to body state.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Dendritic release is the headline: dendrites secrete hormone into the local space as a chemical broadcast, a mode called volume transmission, triggered by back-propagating action potentials that travel backwards from cell body into dendrite.

Dendritic dynorphin feeds back onto the same cell to shut off its own afterpotential, an elegant self-timer.

The cells couple through gap junctions, direct pores between neighbours, and through ephaptic effects, where one cell's electrical field nudges the next without contact.

Nitric oxide diffuses backwards to inhibit their own inputs and dilates nearby vessels, and sustained demand switches on gene transcription and dendritic protein synthesis, so the cell rebuilds itself while working.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Plasma osmolality from organum vasculosum and subfornical organ, 270 to 320 milliosmoles per kilogram.

◦ Blood volume and pressure from nucleus tractus solitarius and A1 group, percent change from normal.

◦ Suckling and cervical stretch afferents, 0 to 20 events per second.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Peptide hormone into posterior pituitary capillaries, plasma 0.5 to 20 picograms per millilitre.

◦ Dendritic peptide into the nucleus itself, 0 to 100 nanomolar.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, afterpotential amplitude, internal calcium, releasable vesicle pool, axonal store, dendritic autoinhibition, cell volume.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Osmotic threshold about 280 milliosmoles per kilogram, gain about 0.5 spikes per second per milliosmole, maximum rate 13 spikes per second, resting 1 to 3, about 10,000 terminals per axon.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 10 milliseconds, afterpotential 3 to 6 seconds, autoinhibition 20 to 60 seconds, plasma half-life 1 to 20 minutes, remodelling hours to days.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss of 80 to 90 percent of the vasopressin cells gives arginine vasopressin deficiency, formerly central diabetes insipidus, with torrential dilute urine, while inappropriate excess gives the syndrome of inappropriate antidiuresis, dangerously diluting blood sodium.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert blood saltiness into a quantity of hormone released into the bloodstream by one magnocellular neurosecretory cell.
Create a number variable called osmolality with initial value 300.
Create a number variable called excess with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called pool with initial value 100.
Create a number variable called released with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called quantum with initial value 0.
Set excess to the difference of osmolality and 280.
If excess is less than 0, then perform the following actions.
    Set excess to 0.
End the conditional block.
Set firing rate to the sum of 1.5 and the product of 0.55 and excess.
If firing rate is greater than 13, then perform the following actions.
    Set firing rate to 13.
End the conditional block.
Repeat the following steps exactly 200 times.
    Set quantum to the product of 0.0012 and the product of firing rate and pool.
    Set pool to the sum of 0.04 and the difference of pool and quantum.
    Set released to the sum of released and quantum.
End the loop.
Display firing rate to the console.
Display released to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the model is arithmetic on a handful of numbers and keeps the physiology visible instead of burying it in boilerplate.

# Stimulus to secretion coupling in a magnocellular neurosecretory cell.
DT = 0.05            # seconds per step
OSM_SET = 280.0      # osmotic threshold, milliosmoles per kilogram
GAIN = 0.55          # spikes per second per milliosmole above threshold
MAX_RATE = 13.0      # ceiling on sustained firing
POOL_MAX = 100.0     # releasable hormone at the terminals, arbitrary units
REFILL = 0.8         # units resupplied by axonal transport per second
FUSION = 0.012       # fraction of pool released per spike at low rates
def firing_rate(osmolality):
    return max(0.0, min(MAX_RATE, 1.5 + GAIN * (osmolality - OSM_SET)))
def facilitation(rate):
    return 1.0 + 2.5 * (rate / (rate + 4.0))   # release per spike saturates
def run(osmolality, seconds):
    pool, released = POOL_MAX, 0.0
    rate = firing_rate(osmolality)
    for _ in range(int(seconds / DT)):
        out = min(pool, FUSION * facilitation(rate) * pool * rate * DT)
        pool = min(POOL_MAX, pool - out + REFILL * DT)
        released += out
    return rate, released, pool
for osm in (280.0, 290.0, 300.0):
    r, total, left = run(osm, 10.0)
    print("osmolality %.0f: %.2f Hz, released %.1f, pool %.1f" % (osm, r, total, left))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Nothing else in the human brain is a neuron at one end and an endocrine gland at the other, with a single unmyelinated axon running centimetres out of the brain to squirt peptide into blood.

A huge cell body, one or two fat unbranched dendrites, hormone beads along the axon, and astrocytes that move out of the way when the body is thirsty: that is this cell.

CHAPTER 91. OXYTOCIN NEURONS (Birthing-And-Nursing Burst Neuron) (NON-COG)

Every few minutes during breastfeeding a few thousand scattered cells fall silent, then fire together in a single three-second thunderclap, and milk appears.

Oxytocin is a peptide barcode, naming the molecule rather than the cell or the moment it serves.

Birthing-And-Nursing names the two occasions this cell owns, since its hormone drives the contractions of labour and the ejection of milk during suckling.

Burst corrects the common picture of a steady drip, because these cells are silent for minutes at a time and then fire a brief synchronised volley lasting only a few seconds, which is what forces a pulse of hormone into the blood.

Neuron marks it as a magnocellular secretory cell, and naming the firing pattern rather than the peptide tells a student why the milk arrives in waves every five minutes or so during a feed rather than continuously.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

An oxytocin neuron is a magnocellular neurosecretory cell making oxytocin, a nine-amino-acid peptide hormone whose name means swift birth.

Its headline job is mechanical, squeezing the muscle-like cells around the milk glands so milk is ejected and squeezing the womb during labour, and its second job is chemical influence inside the brain over social recognition, trust, pain thresholds and stress buffering.

The hard part is timing, because the cells must convert a continuous stimulus, suckling, into rare synchronised pulses.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Slightly smaller and rounder than its vasopressin sibling, with one to three long, sparsely branched dendrites that bundle into a shared zone at the ventral edge of the nucleus where cells lie membrane to membrane with no astrocyte between them.

The cytoplasm holds dense-core vesicles, small bags with a dark centre under the electron microscope, each carrying oxytocin bound to a protein called neurophysin I.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are roughly 18 to 30 micrometres across, measurably smaller in human tissue than a neighbouring vasopressin cell.

Dendrites reach several hundred micrometres and shrink by about 40 percent in total length during lactation, while the axon runs 2 to 4 centimetres to a gland holding roughly 28 micrograms of oxytocin, about 14 international units.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Magnocellular oxytocin cells make hormone for the body through the posterior pituitary, while a much smaller parvocellular group projects into brain and spinal cord instead and carries the pain and social functions.

A third group sends collaterals to both, so one cell doses blood and brain in the same moment.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Cell bodies sit in the supraoptic nucleus, mainly its dorsal part, in the magnocellular paraventricular nucleus and in accessory nuclei between them, with the parvocellular oxytocin cells in the medial parvocellular paraventricular subdivision.

Their axons reach the nucleus accumbens, central amygdala, hippocampal region CA2, nucleus tractus solitarius, dorsal vagal complex and spinal cord dorsal horn.

The key interface is the posterior pituitary, where brain tissue meets windowed capillaries, and a second is the glia-free dendritic zone at the edge of the supraoptic nucleus.

NUMBER:

There are roughly 50 thousand magnocellular oxytocin neurons in a human, both sides combined, with a defensible range of 40 to 60 thousand and moderate confidence, plus perhaps a few thousand brain-projecting parvocellular cells.

That is about 0.5 percent of the hypothalamus and one neuron in every 1.6 million in the nervous system, a startlingly small committee for birth, feeding and attachment.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Between bursts the cell fires slowly and rather regularly at 1 to 3 spikes per second, kept evenly spaced by a transient potassium current, and it does not show the long phasic bursts of a vasopressin cell.

During the milk-ejection reflex it does something unique: an explosive burst of 2 to 4 seconds at 40 to 80 spikes per second, with every oxytocin cell in both hemispheres joining within about a second.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Suckling information climbs the spinal cord and brainstem, but no fast pathway is strong enough on its own to trigger the burst.

Glutamate from the organum vasculosum and noradrenaline from the A2 group set the background rate, while GABA input from the perinuclear zone switches from inhibitory to excitatory in late pregnancy because the chloride balance inside the cell changes.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Thin, unmyelinated, roughly 0.5 to 1 micrometre across, conducting at 0.5 to 1 metre per second and ending in thousands of terminals.

Release shows strong frequency facilitation, so a 40 hertz burst delivers far more than forty times what one second at 1 hertz would.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Oxytocin with neurophysin I is the main product, acting on a single oxytocin receptor that raises internal calcium and cross-reacting weakly with vasopressin receptors.

Co-released partners include cholecystokinin, dynorphin, corticotropin-releasing hormone in some cells, and glutamate at brain-projecting terminals.

MYELINATION:

Unmyelinated throughout, both the pituitary-bound axons and most brain-projecting collaterals.

Myelin would be pointless for hormone release and unnecessary for slow peptide signalling inside the brain.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate at rest and enormous during lactation, when oxytocin mRNA rises several-fold and an emptied terminal store must be refilled every few minutes.

The cells rely on astrocyte-supplied lactate and dense local capillaries, and their mitochondria cluster in the dendritic release zones.

PLASTICITY:

This is the plasticity champion of the mammalian brain, as astrocyte processes withdraw, cell bodies touch, new GABA and glutamate synapses form on exposed membrane, the dendritic tree shrinks by about 40 percent and gap junctions multiply.

Every change reverses within days of weaning, and oxytocin itself is the signal that drives them.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The astrocytes here behave like retractable curtains rather than fixed insulation, and their withdrawal is the enabling step for synchronised bursting, because when they leave they stop clearing glutamate and transmitter spills between cells.

Pituicytes retract from the terminals during suckling to expose more surface to the capillary wall, and astrocytes carry oxytocin receptors themselves, so neuron and glia talk both ways.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove oxytocin cells and the supraoptic nucleus becomes a pure salt thermostat with no social or reproductive dimension.

Labour would stall, milk would be made but never released, the circuit would lose synchrony altogether because vasopressin cells alone never fire together, and the wider brain would lose a diffuse calming signal that slows the heart and raises pain thresholds.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Dendritic release is the burst mechanism: suckling slowly builds oxytocin in the dendritic zone, priming the vesicles of every neighbour, and once primed the population fires together.

This is positive feedback written in chemistry rather than wiring, so the cells are coupled without being connected, and because oxytocin mobilises calcium from internal stores the priming outlasts the activity that caused it.

Gap junctions add direct electrical coupling, back-propagating spikes drive dendritic calcium entry, nitric oxide gives retrograde inhibition of incoming synapses, and oxytocin acting on astrocytes changes glutamate handling in a genuine three-way neuron, glia and hormone loop.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Suckling afferent rate from spinal cord, 0 to 20 events per second.

◦ Cervical and vaginal stretch during labour, 0 to 1 normalised.

◦ Local dendritic oxytocin, 0 to 100 nanomolar.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Plasma oxytocin, baseline 1 to 5 picograms per millilitre, pulse 20 to 100.

◦ Dendritic oxytocin priming neighbours in the nucleus.

◦ Axonal oxytocin to amygdala, accumbens, CA2, brainstem and spinal cord.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, dendritic pool, primed fraction, burst timer, refractory timer, internal calcium, astrocyte coverage.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Background 1 to 3 spikes per second, burst 40 to 80, burst length 2 to 4 seconds, inter-burst interval 3 to 10 minutes.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 10 milliseconds, priming build-up 10 to 60 seconds, dendritic clearance about 15 seconds, plasma half-life 1 to 6 minutes, glial retraction hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Failure of the burst causes failed milk ejection despite normal milk production and weak or prolonged labour, while reduced central oxytocin signalling is implicated in autism spectrum conditions and Prader-Willi syndrome, on correlational human evidence.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to reproduce the milk-ejection burst of a population of oxytocin neurons coupled only by hormone released from their dendrites.
Create a number variable called dendritic oxytocin with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called suckling input with initial value 2.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called burst timer with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called refractory timer with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called ejection times with initial value empty list.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
While clock is less than 900, repeat the following steps.
    If burst timer is greater than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to 55.
        Set burst timer to the difference of burst timer and 0.05.
        If burst timer is less than or equal to 0, then perform the following actions.
            Set refractory timer to 240.
        End the conditional block.
    Else if refractory timer is greater than 0, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to the sum of 1.5 and suckling input.
        Set refractory timer to the difference of refractory timer and 0.05.
    Else if dendritic oxytocin is greater than or equal to 0.62, then perform the following actions.
        Set burst timer to 3.
        Add clock to the end of ejection times.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to the sum of 1.5 and suckling input.
    End the conditional block.
    Set dendritic oxytocin to the sum of dendritic oxytocin and the product of 0.05 and the difference of the product of 0.018 and firing rate and the product of 0.06 and dendritic oxytocin.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 0.05.
End the loop.
Display ejection times to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the burst is a slow feedback loop rather than a solver problem, and a plain loop makes the priming mechanism obvious.

# Milk-ejection bursting in a dendritically coupled oxytocin population.
DT = 0.05          # seconds per step
N_CELLS = 20       # cells sharing one dendritic pool
BASE = 1.5         # background spikes per second
BURST_RATE = 55.0  # spikes per second inside a burst
BURST_LEN = 3.0    # seconds
REFRACT = 240.0    # seconds of reduced excitability after a burst
THRESH = 0.62      # dendritic oxytocin needed to trigger a burst
CLEAR = 0.06       # fraction of dendritic pool cleared per second
PROD = 0.0009      # dendritic oxytocin released per spike per cell
def simulate(suckling, seconds):
    dend, burst_left, block, t = 0.0, 0.0, 0.0, 0.0
    ejections = []
    while t < seconds:
        if burst_left > 0.0:
            rate = BURST_RATE
            burst_left -= DT
            if burst_left <= 0.0:
                block = REFRACT          # cells cannot re-burst at once
        else:
            rate = BASE + suckling
            if block > 0.0:
                block -= DT
            elif dend >= THRESH:
                burst_left = BURST_LEN   # the population fires together
                ejections.append(round(t, 1))
        dend += (PROD * rate * N_CELLS - CLEAR * dend) * DT
        t += DT
    return ejections
print("milk ejections at seconds:", simulate(suckling=2.0, seconds=900.0))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other neuron coordinates with its neighbours by bathing them in its own hormone and then firing with them in a three-second thunderclap every few minutes.

A large hypothalamic cell whose astrocytes move aside during breastfeeding, whose dendrites secrete the peptide its axon delivers to blood, and which alternates long quiet stretches with brief 50 hertz explosions, is an oxytocin neuron.

CHAPTER 92. VASOPRESSIN NEURONS, ALSO CALLED ANTIDIURETIC HORMONE NEURONS (Water-Sparing Kidney-Signal Neuron) (NON-COG)

This cell is the reason you can walk across a desert for a day and still make urine, and it decides how much of your body water you are allowed to throw away.

Vasopressin and antidiuretic hormone are two chemical labels for one molecule, and both describe the substance rather than the cell.

Water-Sparing names the purpose, which is to hold water inside the body rather than lose it in urine.

Kidney-Signal names the target, because the hormone these cells release acts on the collecting ducts of the kidney to reabsorb water.

Neuron marks it as a superb sensor in its own right, swelling or shrinking with the saltiness of the blood and changing its firing rate to match, so that a rise of only one to two percent in blood concentration is enough to raise hormone output.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A vasopressin neuron is a magnocellular neurosecretory cell making vasopressin, also called antidiuretic hormone, a nine-amino-acid peptide differing from oxytocin by just two building blocks.

Antidiuretic means urine-reducing, and that is the job, since the hormone tells the kidney to pull water back out of the urine and return it to the blood.

It is a live sensor as well as a gland, because its own membrane detects how concentrated the blood is without any other cell telling it, and it also tightens blood vessels, which is why it is called vaso-pressin.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Large, angular cell body with one or two thick dendrites running hundreds of micrometres with very few branches.

The membrane carries mechanically gated channels, pores that open when the cell physically shrinks, so water loss becomes a voltage change, and the interior is dominated by hormone factories including an oversized Golgi apparatus, the packaging depot of a cell, and dense-core vesicles holding vasopressin bound to neurophysin II.

SIZE:

The cell body is about 20 to 35 micrometres across and averages larger than a neighbouring oxytocin cell, a difference measurable in human post-mortem tissue.

Dendrites run 200 to 1000 micrometres, the axon 2 to 4 centimetres, and the hormone beads called Herring bodies along it swell to 5 to 20 micrometres.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Magnocellular vasopressin cells serve body water balance and are the type described here.

Parvocellular vasopressin cells in the paraventricular nucleus are small, send vasopressin to pituitary portal blood to amplify the stress signal, and often also contain corticotropin-releasing hormone, while separate populations in the suprachiasmatic nucleus, bed nucleus of the stria terminalis and medial amygdala are counted with their own regions.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The densest colony is the ventral supraoptic nucleus, sitting on the optic tract just lateral to the optic chiasm, with a second in the magnocellular paraventricular nucleus and accessory groups such as the nucleus circularis between them.

Two interfaces are decisive: the posterior pituitary, where the axon crosses out of the blood-brain barrier into windowed capillaries, and the border with the organum vasculosum of the lamina terminalis and subfornical organ, circumventricular organs where the brain tastes blood directly.

NUMBER:

There are roughly 70 thousand magnocellular vasopressin neurons in a human, both sides combined, within a defensible range of 60 to 80 thousand and with moderate confidence, making up 55 to 60 percent of the magnocellular population.

That is about 0.7 percent of the hypothalamus and roughly one neuron in every 1.2 million in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

At rest the cell fires slowly and irregularly at 1 to 3 spikes per second, but raise the blood concentration and it switches into phasic firing, its signature pattern of bursts lasting 20 to 60 seconds at 5 to 13 spikes per second separated by silences of 15 to 40 seconds.

The burst is built by a slow depolarising afterpotential that summates into a plateau and ended by dynorphin released from the cell's own dendrites, which switches that afterpotential off.

Osmotic sensing depends on a shortened form of the TRPV1 channel, a pore pulled open by the shrinking cell's own skeleton, so shrinking equals depolarising equals firing.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Glutamate arrives from the organum vasculosum and subfornical organ carrying concentration and angiotensin signals, and noradrenaline from the A1 group of the ventrolateral medulla is the blood loss signal, powerfully excitatory here unlike in most of the brain.

GABA from the perinuclear zone provides the brake and is driven by baroreceptors, the pressure sensors in the great arteries, so rising blood pressure quietens the cell.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Unmyelinated, roughly 0.5 to 1 micrometre across, conducting at 0.5 to 1 metre per second and ending in thousands of terminals on capillaries.

Release shows strong frequency facilitation then fatigue, which is exactly why phasic firing is efficient, because a burst releases far more hormone per spike and the silence lets terminals recover.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Vasopressin with neurophysin II is the main product and dynorphin the co-stored burst-terminating self-brake, alongside galanin, apelin, nitric oxide and ATP.

Vasopressin acts on three receptors: V1a on blood vessels and in brain, V1b on pituitary corticotrophs, and V2 on kidney collecting duct cells, where it inserts water channels called aquaporin-2 into the membrane.

MYELINATION:

Unmyelinated everywhere.

The readout is a hormone concentration that takes minutes to act on the kidney, so a few tens of milliseconds of conduction delay are irrelevant.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Very high in dehydration, when the cell hypertrophies and the tail of its own hormone messenger RNA is lengthened to make the message more stable, a rare form of regulation.

Synthesis, packaging and centimetres of axonal transport dominate the energy budget rather than spiking, and misfolded mutant precursors cause an inherited form of the disease.

PLASTICITY:

Dehydration produces the same dramatic remodelling seen with oxytocin cells, as astrocytes retract, neurons touch, new synapses form and the balance of GABA and glutamate shifts within hours.

Chronic salt loading also changes which channels the cell expresses, raising intrinsic excitability, and all of it reverses on rehydration over several days.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The astrocytes here release taurine through volume-sensitive channels when blood is dilute, and taurine directly inhibits the neuron, so the glia act as a second, independent osmometer feeding the same cell.

Pituicytes physically unwrap the nerve endings during dehydration and rewrap them afterwards, forming a mechanical gate on hormone output, while microglia activate in chronic dehydration and heart failure and alter excitatory drive.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete this one cell type and the supraoptic nucleus stops being a water regulator and becomes only a milk and labour circuit.

The person would produce fifteen to twenty litres of dilute urine a day, drink constantly, risk death from dehydration during any illness that stopped them drinking, and the region would lose its metronome, since phasic bursting is the slow pulse of the nucleus.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The cell is its own sensor, because mechanical gating of the shortened TRPV1 channel converts cell volume directly into membrane voltage with no synapse required.

Dendritic dynorphin release is an autocrine self-timer, a signal a cell sends to itself to end its own burst, while dendritic vasopressin gives slower autoinhibition and coordinates neighbours.

Back-propagating spikes drive the dendritic calcium entry that makes this possible, and gap junctions plus ephaptic field effects synchronise small clusters.

Nitric oxide and carbon monoxide act as retrograde messengers onto its own inputs, vasopressin adjusts local microvessels, and sustained demand rewrites transcription and physically enlarges the cell.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Plasma osmolality sensed intrinsically and via organum vasculosum, 270 to 320 milliosmoles per kilogram.

◦ Blood volume via A1 noradrenergic input, minus 30 to plus 30 percent of normal.

◦ Arterial pressure via baroreceptor-driven GABA, 60 to 160 millimetres of mercury.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Plasma vasopressin, 0.5 to 20 picograms per millilitre normally, hundreds after haemorrhage.

◦ Dendritic vasopressin and dynorphin into the nucleus.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, afterpotential amplitude, dendritic dynorphin, internal calcium, terminal store, cell volume, burst flag.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Osmotic threshold 280 to 285 milliosmoles per kilogram, sensitivity 0.4 to 0.5 picograms per millilitre per milliosmole, burst rate 5 to 13 spikes per second, burst 20 to 60 seconds, silence 15 to 40 seconds.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 10 milliseconds, afterpotential 3 to 6 seconds, dynorphin autoinhibition 25 to 60 seconds, plasma half-life 5 to 20 minutes, kidney response 10 to 30 minutes.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Destruction of 80 to 90 percent of these cells causes arginine vasopressin deficiency with 15 to 20 litres of dilute urine daily, while unregulated excess causes the syndrome of inappropriate antidiuresis, diluting blood sodium and causing confusion and seizures.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to reproduce the phasic bursting of a vasopressin neuron, in which a fast self-excitation builds a burst and a slow self-inhibition ends it.
Create a number variable called osmolality with initial value 295.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called afterpotential with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called dynorphin with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called release contribution with initial value 0.
Create a boolean variable called bursting with initial value false.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
Set drive to the product of 0.45 and the difference of osmolality and 281.
While clock is less than 600, repeat the following steps.
    If bursting is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to the sum of 9 and the product of 0.3 and drive.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to drive.
        If firing rate is greater than 3, then perform the following actions.
            Set firing rate to 3.
        End the conditional block.
    End the conditional block.
    Set afterpotential to the sum of afterpotential and the product of 0.05 and the difference of the product of 0.03 and firing rate and the quotient of afterpotential divided by 6.
    Set release contribution to 0.
    If firing rate is greater than 5, then perform the following actions.
        Set release contribution to the product of 0.0022 and the difference of firing rate and 5.
    End the conditional block.
    Set dynorphin to the sum of dynorphin and the product of 0.05 and the difference of release contribution and the quotient of dynorphin divided by 25.
    If bursting is true, then perform the following actions.
        If dynorphin is greater than or equal to 0.25, then perform the following actions.
            Set bursting to false.
            Set afterpotential to 0.
            Display clock to the console.
        End the conditional block.
    Else if both afterpotential is greater than or equal to 0.4 and dynorphin is less than or equal to 0.08 are true, then perform the following actions.
        Set bursting to true.
        Display clock to the console.
    End the conditional block.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 0.05.
End the loop.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the phasic burst is a relaxation oscillator built from two feedback variables on different clocks, and Python shows both clocks side by side.

# Phasic bursting in a vasopressin neuron: fast plateau versus slow opioid brake.
DT = 0.05        # seconds per step
DAP_ON = 0.40    # plateau needed to launch a burst
DYN_OFF = 0.25   # dendritic dynorphin that kills the plateau
DYN_ON = 0.08    # dynorphin low enough to permit the next burst
def phasic(osmolality, seconds):
    dap, dyn = 0.0, 0.0        # afterpotential and dynorphin, arbitrary units
    bursting = False
    bursts, start, t = [], 0.0, 0.0
    drive = max(0.0, 0.45 * (osmolality - 281.0))   # spikes per second
    while t < seconds:
        rate = (9.0 + 0.3 * drive) if bursting else min(3.0, drive)
        dap += (0.030 * rate - dap / 6.0) * DT        # builds over seconds
        release = 0.0022 * max(0.0, rate - 5.0)       # needs high firing
        dyn += (release - dyn / 25.0) * DT            # builds over tens of seconds
        if bursting and dyn >= DYN_OFF:
            bursting = False
            bursts.append((round(start, 1), round(t - start, 1)))
            dap = 0.0
        elif not bursting and dap >= DAP_ON and dyn <= DYN_ON:
            bursting, start = True, t
        t += DT
    return bursts
for osm in (285.0, 295.0):
    print("osmolality %.0f -> (start s, length s):" % osm, phasic(osm, 600.0)[:5])

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only cell in the body that measures its own volume, turns that measurement into a hormone level in blood, and paces itself with an opioid released from its own dendrites.

On a recording the signature is unmissable: half a minute of steady firing, half a minute of silence, over and over, faster the thirstier you are.

CHAPTER 93. PARVOCELLULAR NEUROSECRETORY CELLS (Pituitary-Steering Small Neuron) (NON-COG)

These tiny cells hold the leash on every hormone gland in your body, and they do it by dripping picograms of peptide into a private vein the width of a pencil lead.

Parvocellular means small celled and distinguishes these cells from their giant neighbours by size alone.

Pituitary-Steering names the work, because instead of releasing hormone into the general circulation they drip tiny quantities into a private set of portal veins at the median eminence, six to ten small vessels that carry the signal a few millimetres down the stalk to the anterior pituitary.

Small preserves the only content the old term carried.

Neuron marks it as a secretory cell that biases the gland's output up or down rather than firing it directly, which is why steering is the honest verb.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A parvocellular neurosecretory cell is a small neuron that secretes a releasing or inhibiting hormone into a dedicated set of blood vessels leading to the anterior pituitary gland, and parvocellular means small-celled.

They never touch the pituitary, releasing instead into the hypophysial portal system, a private circuit that carries their message a few centimetres down the stalk without diluting it in the rest of the body.

Because of them a handful of brain cells sets cortisol, thyroid hormone, growth, fertility and milk production for the whole organism.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Small, spindle-shaped or triangular cell bodies with two to five slender dendrites forming a modest tree.

They carry fewer and smaller dense-core vesicles than magnocellular cells because they deliver nanograms into a tiny volume rather than micrograms into the whole circulation, and their fine unmyelinated axons end as clubs pressed against the basement membrane of portal capillaries.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are roughly 8 to 15 micrometres across, less than half the diameter and under a tenth of the volume of a magnocellular cell.

Dendrites reach 100 to 300 micrometres and axons only 1 to 5 millimetres, and the portal blood they secrete into has a volume on the order of a hundredth of a millilitre, which is why tiny amounts make large concentrations.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

They are named by their product: corticotropin-releasing hormone cells, thyrotropin-releasing hormone cells, growth hormone-releasing hormone cells, somatostatin cells that inhibit growth hormone, gonadotropin-releasing hormone cells, and tuberoinfundibular dopamine cells that hold back the milk hormone prolactin.

A look-alike group, the preautonomic parvocellular neurons, project to brainstem and spinal cord instead and are not neurosecretory at all.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The parvocellular divisions of the paraventricular nucleus hold the corticotropin-releasing and thyrotropin-releasing hormone cells across its medial, dorsal, ventral and periventricular parts.

The arcuate nucleus at the floor of the hypothalamus holds growth hormone-releasing hormone and dopamine cells, the periventricular nucleus holds most somatostatin cells, and gonadotropin-releasing hormone cells are scattered through the medial preoptic area and diagonal band, having migrated in from the nose during development.

The defining interface is the median eminence, the funnel at the base of the hypothalamus where the blood-brain barrier is absent, and specifically the boundary between its internal zone, where axons pass, and its external zone, where they release.

NUMBER:

The parvocellular divisions of the paraventricular nuclei hold roughly 55 thousand neurons in a human, both sides combined, of which only some are neurosecretory rather than preautonomic.

Adding arcuate, periventricular and preoptic groups, the total is on the order of 150 to 250 thousand, a low to moderate confidence range because human counts for most subtypes have never been made, giving roughly 1.5 to 2.5 percent of the hypothalamus and about one neuron in every 400 thousand in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These cells fire faster and more irregularly than magnocellular cells, typically 2 to 10 spikes per second, and many carry a low-threshold calcium spike allowing brief doublets and triplets, plus a transient potassium current that sharpens the onset of a hormone pulse when inhibition lifts.

Their most important property is at population level, because the group synchronises into pulses lasting minutes and the pituitary reads the frequency and amplitude of those pulses rather than the mean firing rate.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Dense GABA input from the peri-paraventricular zone, the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis and the preoptic area holds them under constant restraint, while glutamate from brainstem, arcuate and other hypothalamic nuclei supplies the drive.

Noradrenaline and serotonin modulate, and the cells are directly sensitive to blood-borne cortisol, thyroid hormone and sex steroids feeding back on their own output.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Very fine at 0.2 to 0.5 micrometres, unmyelinated, conducting well under one metre per second, travelling in the tuberoinfundibular tract and ending in the external zone of the median eminence separated from portal blood by only two basement membranes.

Tanycytes, specialised glia lining the third ventricle, wrap those terminals and can block or expose them, so the axon's access to blood is gated by glia.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The defining products are peptide releasing hormones, or for the dopamine cells a classical monoamine used as a hormone, and most also release glutamate or GABA at brain-directed collaterals.

Common co-stored peptides include vasopressin, which amplifies the corticotropin-releasing hormone signal, plus neurotensin, enkephalin, galanin and cocaine- and amphetamine-regulated transcript.

MYELINATION:

Not applicable in the usual sense, because these axons are entirely unmyelinated and no myelinated variant exists.

The message is a hormone concentration built over seconds to minutes, so conduction speed contributes nothing and myelin would only obstruct the terminals' access to blood.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest compared with magnocellular cells, since the quantities are nanograms, but sustained during chronic stress or thyroid failure when synthesis climbs.

The median eminence has one of the highest blood flows in the brain, so substrate is generous, but because it lies outside the blood-brain barrier these terminals are also exposed to circulating toxins and immune signals.

PLASTICITY:

The terminals remodel constantly as tanycyte processes advance and retreat over hours, opening and closing the door to portal blood under the influence of oestrogen, thyroid hormone and nutrition.

Synaptic input onto the cell bodies is rebuilt by stress, with glutamate synapses strengthening within an hour of a severe stressor, and the cells switch peptide phenotype, adding vasopressin to their output during chronic stress.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Tanycytes are the specialised glia here and they do what no other glia do, acting as a physical shutter and a chemical filter between nerve terminal and blood.

They convert thyroid hormone from inactive to active form, carry the enzyme that destroys thyrotropin-releasing hormone outside the terminal, and transport leptin and glucose signals in from blood, while median eminence microglia sample blood directly and are the first brain cells to learn of an infection.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and the hypothalamus stops being the master gland controller and becomes merely a behavioural and autonomic centre.

The anterior pituitary would fall silent within days, with no cortisol response to stress, no thyroid hormone, no growth, no puberty, no fertility, and paradoxically a flood of prolactin because the dopamine brake would be gone, and the region would lose the hourly and daily hormone cadence that organises the body's day.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

These cells are pulse generators, not relays, because their essential computation is to synchronise into episodic bursts whose frequency carries the message, and pituitary cells decode frequency rather than level.

Release into blood with no postsynaptic partner is the purest volume transmission in the brain, and it is gated mechanically by tanycytes, so output can change without a single spike changing.

They read their own feedback directly, since steroid and thyroid hormones enter the cell nucleus and alter transcription over hours, while faster membrane feedback silences their own excitatory inputs within minutes through retrograde endocannabinoid messengers.

Lying outside the blood-brain barrier, they also perform immune surveillance, responding to circulating cytokines, which are inflammatory messenger proteins, and they run rapid gene transcription with immediate early genes switching on minutes after a stressor.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ GABA drive from peri-paraventricular zone and bed nucleus of the stria terminalis, 0 to 40 events per second.

◦ Glutamate drive from brainstem and hypothalamic nuclei, 0 to 40 events per second.

◦ Feedback hormones: cortisol 1 to 25 micrograms per decilitre, free thyroxine 10 to 25 picomoles per litre, sex steroids in physiological range.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Releasing or inhibiting hormone into portal blood, picomolar to nanomolar there and undetectable in the general circulation.

◦ Fast transmitter at collateral branches inside the brain.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, internal calcium, releasable peptide pool, peptide messenger RNA, tanycyte gate openness, pulse phase.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Cell body 8 to 15 micrometres, firing 2 to 10 spikes per second, axon 1 to 5 millimetres, portal volume about 0.05 millilitres, portal flow about 0.5 millilitres per minute.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 15 milliseconds, portal washout 5 to 10 seconds, secretory pulses 5 to 120 minutes apart, transcriptional feedback 1 to 24 hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss of the population, as in stalk section or tumour, causes panhypopituitarism, meaning failure of all anterior pituitary hormones, with high prolactin as the tell-tale sign, while loss of pulsing alone is itself a disease, since continuous rather than pulsatile gonadotropin-releasing hormone shuts fertility down completely.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to turn a pulse of firing in a parvocellular neurosecretory cell into a concentration of releasing hormone in the small volume of portal blood reaching the pituitary.
Create a number variable called portal volume with initial value 0.05.
Create a number variable called portal flow with initial value 0.008.
Create a number variable called concentration with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called secretion with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called peak with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called phase with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called clock with initial value 0.
While clock is less than 10800, repeat the following steps.
    Set phase to the remainder of clock divided by 3600.
    If phase is less than 300, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to 12.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set firing rate to 0.2.
    End the conditional block.
    Set secretion to the product of 0.004 and firing rate.
    Set concentration to the sum of concentration and the product of 0.5 and the quotient of the difference of secretion and the product of portal flow and concentration divided by portal volume.
    If concentration is greater than peak, then perform the following actions.
        Set peak to concentration.
    End the conditional block.
    Set clock to the sum of clock and 0.5.
End the loop.
Display peak to the console.
Display concentration to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the whole computation is a one-line mass balance on a small compartment, and Python lets that single equation stay the centre of attention.

# Firing pulse to portal hormone concentration for a parvocellular cell.
DT = 0.5              # seconds per step
PORTAL_VOLUME = 0.05  # millilitres of hypophysial portal blood
PORTAL_FLOW = 0.008   # millilitres per second washing through it
PER_SPIKE = 0.004     # picomoles secreted per spike
def portal_profile(period, width, pulse_rate, basal_rate, seconds):
    conc = 0.0                       # picomoles per millilitre
    peak, trough, t = 0.0, float("inf"), 0.0
    while t < seconds:
        rate = pulse_rate if (t % period) < width else basal_rate
        secretion = PER_SPIKE * rate                    # picomoles per second
        conc += (secretion - PORTAL_FLOW * conc) / PORTAL_VOLUME * DT
        if t > 3600.0:               # ignore the first hour while it settles
            peak = max(peak, conc)
            trough = min(trough, conc)
        t += DT
    return peak, trough
p, tr = portal_profile(3600.0, 300.0, 12.0, 0.2, 10800.0)
print("portal peak %.3f, trough %.3f pmol/mL, ratio %.0f" % (p, tr, p / tr))
# Washout time constant is volume over flow, about 6 seconds, so the pituitary
# sees a sharp square pulse rather than a smeared average.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is a neuron whose synapse is a blood vessel, whose postsynaptic cell is a gland centimetres away, and whose message is written in pulses per hour rather than spikes per second.

Look for a small cell with a very short unmyelinated axon ending in the median eminence, outside the blood-brain barrier, with a tanycyte wrapped around the terminal like a shutter.

CHAPTER 94. CORTICOTROPIN-RELEASING HORMONE (CRH) NEURONS (Stress-Cascade Starting Neuron) (NON-COG)

When a car horn blares behind you, a few thousand cells the size of dust motes fire, and within fifteen minutes your blood is carrying stress hormone to every cell you own.

The old label is a hormone barcode compressed to an acronym, naming the peptide and not the cell.

Stress names the trigger, since these cells are recruited by threats of every kind, from a predator odour to low blood sugar.

Cascade names the chain they open, in which their peptide reaches the pituitary, the pituitary releases adrenocorticotropic hormone into the blood, and the adrenal cortex then makes cortisol.

Starting is the precise verb, because they are the first switch in that three-step chain, and the chain never fully rests, running instead as roughly hourly pulses across the whole twenty-four hour day even with no threat present.

Neuron marks it as a parvocellular secretory cell.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A corticotropin-releasing hormone neuron, shortened to CRH neuron, is the switch at the top of the body's stress system.

Corticotropin-releasing hormone is a chain of 41 amino acids dripped into the portal blood going to the anterior pituitary, where it makes pituitary cells release adrenocorticotropic hormone, abbreviated ACTH, which travels to the adrenal glands above the kidneys and releases cortisol.

That three-step chain is the hypothalamic-pituitary-adrenal axis, and this neuron is its first step, its rate setter and its feedback receiver, while branches staying inside the brain drive freezing, arousal and loss of appetite directly.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

A small oval or spindle-shaped cell body with three to five modestly branched dendrites spanning a few hundred micrometres.

Those dendrites are blanketed with GABA-releasing terminals more densely than almost any other hypothalamic cell, because the default state of this neuron is to be held down, and in chronically stressed people its hormone vesicles also carry vasopressin.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are roughly 10 to 15 micrometres across, small enough that eight would fit inside one magnocellular cell.

Dendritic trees span 150 to 300 micrometres and the axon to the median eminence is only 1 to 3 millimetres, one of the shortest hormone-delivery axons in the body.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The main type is the hypophysiotropic CRH neuron of the paraventricular nucleus, which alone controls the stress axis, and a subset of these co-expresses vasopressin in a fraction that rises with age and chronic stress.

Separate CRH populations in the central amygdala, bed nucleus of the stria terminalis, Barrington's nucleus and cortex are counted with their own regions.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The hypophysiotropic cells sit almost entirely in the dorsal medial parvocellular subdivision of the paraventricular nucleus, a compact wing close to the third ventricle, and their axons end in the external zone of the median eminence on portal capillaries.

The critical interface is that median eminence, where the axon leaves the protected brain, and a second is the peri-paraventricular zone, a shell of GABA neurons around the nucleus through which almost all inhibitory control passes.

NUMBER:

The best available human estimates put CRH-expressing neurons in the paraventricular nuclei at roughly 5 to 20 thousand in total across both sides, and confidence is low.

The uncertainty is worth stating plainly, because counts come from staining tissue for the peptide, so a quiet neuron holding little peptide is invisible and the number measured tracks activity as well as anatomy, which is why the counted number rises about fourfold in people who died with major depression.

Taking the middle of the range, these are about 0.1 percent of the hypothalamus and one neuron in every 6 million in the nervous system.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

At rest the cell fires slowly at 0.5 to 5 spikes per second, much of the time in brief rhythmic bursts that cap the average rate, and a stressor flips it to continuous single spiking, which removes that cap and lets the rate climb steeply.

Across the day the population shows an ultradian rhythm, meaning a rhythm faster than daily, with a period close to one hour, and each upstate produces one pulse of cortisol.

A low-threshold calcium current and a transient potassium current together give a burst of spikes the moment inhibition lifts, a property called rebound firing.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Powerful GABA inhibition arrives from the peri-paraventricular shell, the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis, the medial preoptic area and the hippocampus by way of relay neurons.

Glutamate excitation arrives from the nucleus tractus solitarius carrying pain and blood pressure alarms and from the arcuate and dorsomedial nuclei carrying metabolic news, and a local loop exists in which CRH neurons excite nearby interneurons carrying the CRH receptor, which inhibit them back.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Fine at 0.2 to 0.5 micrometres, unmyelinated, conducting far below one metre per second, with a main branch only millimetres long ending on portal capillaries.

Collaterals reach the brainstem locus coeruleus and autonomic centres, so the same spike that orders a cortisol pulse also raises arousal and heart rate.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Corticotropin-releasing hormone is the defining product, acting on the CRH receptor type 1 on pituitary corticotroph cells.

Vasopressin is co-released by a large subset and acts on the V1b receptor, where it does little alone but multiplies the effect of CRH severalfold, while glutamate serves the brain-directed collaterals alongside neurotensin, enkephalin and cholecystokinin.

MYELINATION:

Not applicable in the usual sense, since these axons are unmyelinated and no myelinated form of this cell exists.

The output is a hormone concentration built over tens of seconds, so conduction speed is irrelevant and myelin would only impede release.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low at rest and steeply higher under chronic stress, when peptide synthesis rises and the gene is transcribed within minutes of a stressor.

Terminals sit in the median eminence, among the best perfused tissue in the brain, so substrate is generous, though chronic overdrive is costly and animal studies show raised mitochondrial density and oxidative stress.

PLASTICITY:

Acute stress rewires these cells within an hour, as glutamate synapses onto them strengthen lastingly, GABA synapses weaken and the neuron becomes easier to excite next time.

Chronic stress goes further, adding vasopressin to the peptide mix so the same output becomes more potent at the pituitary, while endocannabinoids released backwards onto their own inputs let cortisol switch off excitatory drive within minutes.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes around these cells are unusual in carrying glucocorticoid receptors and changing their glutamate transporter levels in response to cortisol, so the glia sit inside the feedback loop.

Microglia matter here in a way untrue of most regions, because the median eminence lies outside the blood-brain barrier, so microglia detect circulating inflammatory signals and relay them to these neurons, which is how an infection raises cortisol.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the paraventricular nucleus keeps water balance, thyroid and autonomic control but loses the stress axis entirely.

The person would mount no cortisol response to injury, infection or surgery and would risk circulatory collapse during any serious illness, and the nucleus would lose its hourly pulse and its daily cortisol rhythm, including the morning surge that organises alertness, appetite and immune tone.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The essential computation is delayed negative feedback, because the neuron commands cortisol, cortisol takes about fifteen minutes to appear, and the neuron then reads that cortisol back, which is exactly the recipe for an oscillator.

That is why cortisol arrives in roughly hourly pulses rather than a steady stream, and the pulse pattern itself is the signal target tissues read.

Feedback runs on three timescales at once: seconds to minutes through a membrane receptor and retrograde endocannabinoid release, tens of minutes through changed excitability, and hours through gene transcription.

The cell switches on immediate early genes within five minutes of a stressor, responds directly to cytokines, releases CRH diffusely inside the brain as volume transmission, and runs a genuine microcircuit through CRH-receptor interneurons.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ GABA drive from peri-paraventricular zone and bed nucleus of the stria terminalis, 0 to 50 events per second.

◦ Glutamate drive from nucleus tractus solitarius and hypothalamic nuclei, 0 to 50 events per second.

◦ Plasma cortisol feedback, 1 to 25 micrograms per decilitre, fast membrane path plus slow genomic path.

◦ Circulating cytokines such as interleukin-1 beta and interleukin-6, picograms per millilitre.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ CRH into portal blood, high picomolar to low nanomolar there, with vasopressin co-released.

◦ Glutamate and CRH to locus coeruleus and autonomic centres.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane potential, burst or single-spike mode, internal calcium, releasable CRH pool, CRH messenger RNA, vasopressin co-expression fraction, endocannabinoid tone.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting 0.5 to 5 spikes per second, peak stress 15 to 25, ultradian period 50 to 90 minutes, cell body 10 to 15 micrometres, axon 1 to 3 millimetres.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 15 milliseconds, portal washout 6 seconds, ACTH rise 2 to 5 minutes, cortisol delay 12 to 20 minutes, fast feedback 1 to 10 minutes, genomic feedback 1 to 24 hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Persistent overactivity is the best-documented biological finding in major depression, where counted CRH neuron numbers are about four times higher than in controls, and it also appears in primary hypertension, while underactivity gives secondary adrenal insufficiency after long steroid treatment.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to reproduce the roughly hourly cortisol pulse that arises when a CRH neuron commands a hormone which returns fifteen minutes later to switch it off.
Create a number variable called acth with initial value 2.
Create a number variable called cortisol with initial value 5.
Create a number variable called previous cortisol with initial value 5.
Create a number variable called ratio with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called feedback with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called crh with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called delayed acth with initial value 2.
Create a list variable called delay line with initial value empty list.
Create a list variable called peak times with initial value empty list.
Create a boolean variable called rising with initial value true.
Create a number variable called minute with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 15 times.
    Add 2 to the end of delay line.
End the loop.
While minute is less than 600, repeat the following steps.
    Set ratio to the quotient of cortisol divided by 6.
    Set feedback to the quotient of 1 divided by the sum of 1 and the product of ratio and the product of ratio and ratio.
    Set crh to feedback.
    Set acth to the sum of acth and the difference of the product of 3 and crh and the product of 0.2 and acth.
    Set delayed acth to the item at position 0 in delay line.
    Remove the item at position 0 from delay line.
    Add acth to the end of delay line.
    Set cortisol to the sum of cortisol and the difference of the product of 0.3 and delayed acth and the product of 0.12 and cortisol.
    If both rising is true and cortisol is less than previous cortisol are true, then perform the following actions.
        Add minute to the end of peak times.
        Set rising to false.
    Else if cortisol is greater than previous cortisol, then perform the following actions.
        Set rising to true.
    End the conditional block.
    Set previous cortisol to cortisol.
    Set minute to the sum of minute and 1.
End the loop.
Display peak times to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

Python is chosen because the pulse depends on a fifteen minute transport delay, and Python's deque gives an exact delay line in one line of code.

# Ultradian cortisol pulsing from delayed feedback onto a CRH neuron.
from collections import deque
DELAY = 15          # minutes from ACTH rise to cortisol appearing
def hpa_axis(crh_drive, minutes):
    acth, cort = 2.0, 5.0                        # pg/mL and micrograms/dL
    pipe = deque([acth] * DELAY, maxlen=DELAY)   # the adrenal transport delay
    peaks, prev, rising = [], cort, True
    for t in range(minutes):
        feedback = 1.0 / (1.0 + (cort / 6.0) ** 3)   # cortisol brakes the cell
        crh = crh_drive * feedback                   # portal CRH, arbitrary units
        acth += 3.0 * crh - 0.20 * acth              # pituitary corticotrophs
        delayed = pipe[0]                            # ACTH from 15 minutes ago
        pipe.append(acth)
        cort += 0.30 * delayed - 0.12 * cort         # adrenal cortex output
        if rising and cort < prev:
            peaks.append((t, round(prev, 2)))        # one cortisol pulse peak
            rising = False
        elif cort > prev:
            rising = True
        prev = cort
    return peaks
pulses = hpa_axis(crh_drive=1.0, minutes=600)
print("pulse peaks (minute, micrograms per decilitre):", pulses[:8])
if len(pulses) > 1:
    gaps = [pulses[i + 1][0] - pulses[i][0] for i in range(len(pulses) - 1)]
    print("mean pulse interval %.1f minutes" % (sum(gaps) / len(gaps)))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron whose output loops through two glands and the bloodstream before returning to its own nucleus as a steroid that rewrites its genes.

Look for a small, densely GABA-blanketed cell in the dorsal medial parvocellular paraventricular nucleus that switches from rhythmic mini-bursts to continuous firing the instant something frightening happens.

CHAPTER 95. THYROTROPIN-RELEASING HORMONE (TRH) NEURONS (Chemical-Pace Setting Neuron) (NON-COG)

Three amino acids long, the smallest hormone in your body is made by a cell that decides how fast every mitochondrion in you will burn.

The old label is a hormone barcode compressed to an acronym, and it names the released peptide rather than the quantity being defended.
