Neuron marks it as one of only a few tens of thousands of such cells, all packed into one small strip of the lateral hypothalamus.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These neurons make a pair of peptides discovered twice in the same year by two teams, which is why they have two names: orexin-A and orexin-B to one group, hypocretin-1 and hypocretin-2 to the other.

Both peptides are cut from a single parent protein called prepro-orexin, and orexin-A is 33 amino acids long while orexin-B is 28.

The cells do not generate wakefulness by themselves; they stabilise it, by holding every other arousal system in the brain in the "on" position at once.

They are simultaneously a metabolic sensor, quietening when blood sugar rises after a meal and firing harder when the body is hungry, which is why a heavy lunch makes you drowsy.

They are also the switch that keeps the muscle paralysis of dream sleep from leaking into waking life.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is medium-sized and irregularly shaped, often triangular or many-sided, with three to six thick dendrites that radiate without an obvious preferred direction.

The dendritic tree is untidy and open rather than neatly layered, which suits a cell that must sample whatever is drifting through the tissue.

Vesicles containing the peptide are large and dense-cored, meaning they look black in an electron microscope, and they are found not only at nerve terminals but along the dendrites.

SIZE:

The cell body is 20 to 30 micrometres across in the human brain, noticeably larger than a typical cortical neuron.

The dendritic field is 300 to 500 micrometres wide.

The axon is extraordinary for the size of the cell: a single orexin neuron sends branches to almost the entire brain, with total axonal length plausibly tens of centimetres, reaching from the front of the cortex to the lower spinal cord.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

There is no clean anatomical division, but three distinctions matter.

• A medial group near the dorsomedial hypothalamus, biased towards arousal and stress, and a lateral group biased towards reward and feeding.

• Cells that co-express the peptide dynorphin and cells that do not, which changes whether the target is excited or inhibited.

• Cells that project to the locus coeruleus versus cells that project to the ventral tegmental area, separable by their firing during reward anticipation.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The perifornical area, the lateral hypothalamic area and the posterior hypothalamic area, forming a band wrapped around the fornix, which is the arching white matter bundle carrying fibres from the hippocampus.

In humans the population extends into the dorsomedial hypothalamic nucleus and the subthalamic region, and a few scattered cells lie in the zona incerta.

The critical interface is with melanin-concentrating hormone neurons, which are intermingled cell by cell in the very same tissue while doing the opposite job.

NUMBER:

Approximately 70 thousand orexin neurons in the human brain, from careful counts of post-mortem tissue, with a refined range of 60 thousand to 80 thousand.

Confidence is moderate rather than high, because a second team using different staining reported about 20 thousand, and the discrepancy is thought to reflect staining sensitivity.

That is about 0.7 percent of the roughly 10 million hypothalamic neurons, and about one neuron in 1.2 million in the nervous system as a whole.

In narcolepsy with cataplexy, 85 to 95 percent of them are gone.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage is about minus 55 to minus 60 millivolts, unusually depolarised, meaning the cell sits close to its firing threshold and needs little persuasion.

Firing is 3 to 7 spikes per second during active waking, slower in quiet waking, sparse in deep sleep, and almost completely silent in rapid eye movement sleep.

They possess a low-threshold calcium spike and a strong after-hyperpolarisation, which together make them fire in irregular bursts rather than metronomically.

Glucose inhibits them directly by opening a background potassium channel of the two-pore family, and the sensitivity covers the physiological range, so the difference between a fasting and a post-meal brain is enough to change firing.

They are also acid-sensitive, firing faster when carbon dioxide rises, which links breathing to arousal and explains part of the arousal from sleep apnoea.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from the lateral septum, the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis, the amygdala, the preoptic sleep-promoting neurons, the suprachiasmatic clock by way of the subparaventricular zone, and from arcuate AgRP neurons.

Outputs are close to global: the densest projection of all goes to the locus coeruleus, the noradrenaline source, and heavy projections reach the tuberomammillary histamine neurons, the dorsal raphe, the laterodorsal and pedunculopontine tegmentum, the basal forebrain, the ventral tegmental area, the thalamus, the whole cortex and the spinal cord.

Orexin acts on two receptors: orexin receptor 1, which prefers orexin-A, and orexin receptor 2, which takes both and is the one that matters most for holding sleep at bay.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are fine, 0.2 to 0.8 micrometres, and conduct at roughly 0.5 to 3 metres per second, which is slow, but the system is not built for speed.

Branching is enormous, with one cell contributing terminals in a dozen or more distant nuclei.

Terminals are heavily varicose, releasing peptide into the tissue at large as well as at conventional synapses.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Orexin-A and orexin-B, both excitatory at their receptors and both acting slowly, over seconds.

Glutamate is co-released as the fast excitatory signal, and most of these cells also carry dynorphin, an opioid peptide that is inhibitory, so a single terminal can excite and inhibit the same target on different timescales.

Many also carry galanin and neuronal activity-regulated pentraxin, the latter being useful because it survives in post-mortem tissue and lets pathologists count the cells that used to be there.

MYELINATION:

Most orexin axons are unmyelinated or very thinly myelinated.

Slow conduction is acceptable because the job is to set a background state that must last for hours, not to deliver a millisecond-accurate message.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate in absolute terms, but with a peculiar dependence: these neurons rely on lactate supplied by neighbouring astrocytes, and blocking astrocyte lactate release silences them.

This makes their electrical output a direct readout of local glial metabolism, and it is one reason arousal tracks whole-body energy state so tightly.

PLASTICITY:

Their synaptic inputs are remodelled by food restriction, by chronic stress and by repeated exposure to drugs of abuse, with increased excitatory contacts after cocaine or morphine.

They also change their own peptide production with the circadian clock, and they show striking developmental plasticity, forming their vast projection map in the first weeks of postnatal life.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes surrounding orexin neurons are their fuel supply, exporting lactate through monocarboxylate transporters, without which the cells stop firing.

The same astrocytes release adenosine that acts on A1 receptors to inhibit these neurons as waking hours accumulate, so the glia are part of the sleep-pressure mechanism rather than mere scaffolding.

Microglia are the villains of narcolepsy's endgame: the loss is an immune attack, associated with a tissue type variant called human leucocyte antigen DQB1 06:02, in some cases triggered by influenza infection or by a particular 2009 pandemic influenza vaccine.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Take these cells out and the lateral hypothalamus still regulates feeding, still signals reward, still drives arousal in bursts.

What it loses is continuity.

Wakefulness becomes a state the brain keeps falling out of, dream-sleep paralysis intrudes into laughter and surprise as cataplexy, and the boundary between sleeping and waking, which the rest of the brain treats as absolute, becomes porous.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Orexin peptide is released from dendrites as well as terminals, and orexin neurons excite each other through this local release, so the population behaves as a self-igniting unit rather than a set of independent cells.

They are directly chemosensitive to glucose, to carbon dioxide and to the hormones ghrelin and leptin, which makes them a genuine sensor organ inside the brain.

They exert control over local blood flow and over the sympathetic nervous system, raising blood pressure and heart rate as part of the waking package.

Their dynorphin co-release provides a built-in brake that opposes their own orexin signal with a different time course, a form of self-limiting output.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Extracellular glucose, 0.5 to 5 millimoles per litre in brain tissue, inhibitory.

◦ Carbon dioxide or pH, excitatory as carbon dioxide rises.

◦ Ghrelin, excitatory, and leptin, inhibitory, both from blood.

◦ Gamma-aminobutyric acid from the ventrolateral preoptic sleep-promoting neurons, inhibitory.

◦ Circadian drive from the suprachiasmatic nucleus by way of the subparaventricular zone and dorsomedial hypothalamus.

◦ Astrocytic lactate availability, 0 to 1, a hard requirement for firing.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Orexin-A and orexin-B to locus coeruleus, tuberomammillary nucleus, dorsal raphe, basal forebrain, ventral tegmental area, thalamus, cortex, spinal cord.

◦ Glutamate as a fast excitatory co-transmitter, dynorphin as a slow inhibitory one.

◦ A scalar arousal-stabilising signal, 0 to 7 spikes per second.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage in millivolts.

◦ Homeostatic sleep pressure, accumulated adenosine, 0 to 1.

◦ Peptide store in vesicles, 0 to 1.

◦ Calcium concentration governing the after-hyperpolarisation.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting voltage minus 58 millivolts, threshold minus 45 millivolts, reset minus 65 millivolts.

◦ Membrane time constant about 100 milliseconds.

◦ Half-maximal glucose inhibition near 5 millimoles per litre.

◦ Population size 70 thousand cells.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Spike generation, milliseconds.

◦ Peptide action at targets, 1 to 60 seconds.

◦ Glucose and hormone modulation, minutes.

◦ Sleep pressure accumulation, 12 to 16 hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Immune destruction of 85 to 95 percent of the population produces narcolepsy type 1, defined clinically by a cerebrospinal fluid orexin-A level below 110 picograms per millilitre, with cataplexy, sleep paralysis and hallucinations at sleep onset.

◦ Overactivity contributes to insomnia and to panic, which is why orexin receptor blockers such as suvorexant, lemborexant and daridorexant are licensed sleeping medicines.

◦ Replacing the missing signal is now feasible: the selective orexin receptor 2 agonist oveporexton met all endpoints in two phase 3 trials in narcolepsy type 1 reported in 2025.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether an orexin neuron of the lateral hypothalamus is still holding the brain awake as sleep pressure builds.
Create a number variable called membrane_potential with initial value -58.
Create a number variable called glucose with initial value 4.
Create a number variable called sleep_pressure with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called brake with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike_count with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 600 times.
    Calculate the difference of glucose and 5.
    Set brake to the result.
    Calculate the product of brake and 3.
    Set brake to the result.
    Calculate the difference of 16 and brake.
    Set drive to the result.
    Calculate the difference of drive and sleep_pressure.
    Set drive to the result.
    Calculate the sum of -58 and drive.
    Set membrane_potential to the result.
    Check if membrane_potential is greater than -45.
    If the result is true, then perform the following actions.
        Calculate the sum of spike_count and 1.
        Set spike_count to the result.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the sum of sleep_pressure and 0.03.
    Set sleep_pressure to the result.
End the loop.
Display spike_count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C is chosen because a whole-brain simulator runs this inner loop millions of
   times per simulated second, and C compiles to a tight deterministic loop.
   Build with:  cc -std=c99 orexin.c -lm -o orexin                          */
#include <stdio.h>
#include <math.h>

static double sigmoid(double x) { return 1.0 / (1.0 + exp(-x)); }

int main(void) {
    const double dt = 0.001;        /* seconds per step */
    const double tau = 0.100;       /* membrane time constant, 100 ms */
    const double v_rest = -58.0;    /* mV */
    const double v_thresh = -45.0;  /* mV */
    const double v_reset = -65.0;   /* mV */
    const double wake_drive = 18.0; /* mV of depolarising arousal input */
    double v = v_rest;
    double glucose = 4.0;           /* mmol per litre, fasting brain tissue */
    int spikes_before = 0, spikes_after = 0;

    for (int i = 0; i < 10000; i++) {          /* ten seconds of simulated time */
        if (i == 5000) glucose = 8.0;          /* a meal is absorbed */
        /* two-pore potassium current: glucose opens it and quietens the cell */
        double glucose_brake = 14.0 * sigmoid((glucose - 5.0) * 1.5);
        double input = wake_drive - glucose_brake;
        v += (dt / tau) * (-(v - v_rest) + input);
        if (v >= v_thresh) {
            v = v_reset;
            if (i < 5000) spikes_before++; else spikes_after++;
        }
    }
    printf("spikes in 5 s of low glucose: %d\n", spikes_before);
    printf("spikes in 5 s of high glucose: %d\n", spikes_after);
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other cell in the body is a peptide-releasing glucose sensor that projects to nearly every arousal nucleus at once, is silent in dream sleep, and can be identified beyond doubt by a single laboratory test on spinal fluid, because losing it drops the orexin-A concentration below 110 picograms per millilitre and turns an ordinary life into narcolepsy.

CHAPTER 104. MELANIN-CONCENTRATING HORMONE (MCH) NEURONS OF THE LATERAL HYPOTHALAMUS (Dream-Sleep Promoting Neuron) (COG)

They are mixed cell by cell among the orexin neurons in the same slab of tissue, they use a peptide first found in fish, where it makes skin go pale, and in you they fall silent all day and then blaze into life during your dreams.

Melanin-concentrating hormone is a peptide barcode named for a fish skin-colour effect that has nothing to do with its job in the human brain.

Dream-Sleep names the one sleep stage this cell selectively serves, because switching these cells on drives that stage specifically rather than raising sleep in general, and it does so even when its glutamate release is genetically removed.

Promoting is deliberately modest and honest, since the cell also carries cocaine- and amphetamine-regulated transcript and influences feeding, so promotion of one sleep stage is the best-established claim rather than the whole story.

Neuron marks it as a lateral hypothalamic cell sitting interleaved with the sleep-boundary cells of the same region and firing in near-opposition to them.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These neurons make melanin-concentrating hormone (MCH), a ring-shaped peptide 19 amino acids long that got its name from its job in fish skin and kept it after it turned out to do something entirely different in mammals.

In the human brain MCH is a sleep and energy-conservation signal.

These cells are nearly silent during waking, tick over slowly during deep sleep, and fire fastest of all during rapid eye movement sleep, the phase in which most vivid dreaming happens.

They are the mirror image of their neighbours the orexin neurons in almost every respect: orexin promotes waking, MCH promotes sleep, glucose inhibits orexin neurons and excites MCH neurons, and losing orexin neurons causes narcolepsy while MCH neurons survive untouched in the same brains.

They also promote feeding and fat storage, and they suppress the neurons that form new memories in the hippocampus, which is one current explanation for why dreams are so rarely remembered.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is fusiform or triangular, with two to four long, sparsely branched dendrites that tend to run parallel to the passing fibre bundles.

The whole cell is less bushy than an orexin neuron, giving a spare, elongated silhouette.

Peptide is stored in large dense-cored vesicles distributed all along the axon, which is thick with varicosities.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 15 to 25 micrometres across, slightly smaller on average than the orexin neurons they sit among.

The dendritic field is elongated, roughly 400 by 150 micrometres.

The axonal arbour is again vast for the size of the cell, reaching from the olfactory bulb at the front of the brain to the spinal cord.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Cells that also contain the inhibitory transmitter gamma-aminobutyric acid, which is the large majority, since about 85 percent of MCH neurons carry the enzyme that makes it.

• A glutamate-releasing minority, defined by vesicular glutamate transporter 2.

• Cells co-expressing cocaine- and amphetamine-regulated transcript or nesfatin-1, biased towards feeding control.

• A separate group in the zona incerta and incerto-hypothalamic area, anatomically continuous with the main population but with different targets.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The lateral hypothalamic area, the perifornical region, the posterior hypothalamic area, the zona incerta and the incerto-hypothalamic area, in a broad sheet that in humans spreads further laterally and posteriorly than the orexin field.

The defining interface is with the orexin population: the two cell types are interdigitated in the same cubic millimetres of tissue, share the same astrocytes, and yet keep opposite schedules.

A second interface is with the fornix and the medial forebrain bundle, the fibre highways that these axons join to reach the rest of the brain.

NUMBER:

There is no agreed unbiased stereological count of MCH neurons in the human brain, and it is honest to say so.

In every species measured they outnumber orexin neurons by roughly one and a half to three times, which for a human orexin population of 70 thousand gives a working estimate of 80 thousand cells, with a defensible bound of 50 thousand to 200 thousand and low confidence.

That is around 0.8 percent of the roughly 10 million hypothalamic neurons, and about one neuron in a million in the nervous system as a whole.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Resting voltage is about minus 60 millivolts and these cells are quieter than orexin neurons at rest.

In freely behaving animals they are essentially silent in waking, fire at low rates in slow wave sleep, and reach their maximum, often several spikes per second, in rapid eye movement sleep.

They show a prominent A-type potassium current, a fast-inactivating outward current that delays the first spike after an input arrives, so the cell responds late and gradually rather than instantly.

Glucose excites them, over the same physiological range that inhibits orexin neurons, which makes the pair a push-pull glucose detector built out of two intermingled cell types.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs come from the ventrolateral preoptic sleep-promoting neurons, the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis, the lateral septum, the amygdala, the arcuate nucleus, the raphe and the locus coeruleus, and from orexin neurons themselves acting through orexin receptor 2.

Outputs are brain-wide and heavily biased towards the cortex, the hippocampus, the septum, the thalamic reticular nucleus, the dorsal and median raphe, the tuberomammillary nucleus, the locus coeruleus and the spinal cord.

In the human brain they also make direct contacts onto gonadotropin-releasing hormone neurons, linking energy state to fertility.

MCH acts on MCH receptor 1, and humans, unlike mice and rats, additionally possess a functional MCH receptor 2.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are 0.3 to 1 micrometre in diameter, conducting at about 0.5 to 2 metres per second.

They branch profusely and are studded with varicosities, and a striking feature is that MCH-containing fibres reach the ependymal lining of the ventricles, so peptide can be released into cerebrospinal fluid and carried far from its source.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

MCH itself, which is inhibitory at MCH receptor 1 because that receptor works through the inhibitory G protein Gi.

Gamma-aminobutyric acid in the large majority of cells, glutamate in a minority, plus cocaine- and amphetamine-regulated transcript and nesfatin-1 in subsets.

Notably, MCH neurons can promote rapid eye movement sleep even when their glutamate release is genetically deleted, which shows the peptide and the inhibitory transmitter carry the essential message.

MYELINATION:

Largely unmyelinated, with thin myelin on the longest descending fibres.

As with orexin, the currency is a slowly changing brain state, so conduction speed is not the limiting factor.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low while awake, since the cells are near-silent, rising during sleep, which is an unusual inversion of the normal brain pattern where most cells are cheapest at night.

Their excitation by glucose means their energy supply and their signal are the same molecule, so they are intrinsically well matched to their fuel.

PLASTICITY:

Fasting increases MCH gene expression sharply, and the peptide level tracks body energy stores over days.

Their inputs are remodelled by chronic high-fat feeding and by sleep deprivation, and their activity shapes plasticity elsewhere: by inhibiting hippocampal circuits during rapid eye movement sleep they influence which memories are consolidated and which are allowed to fade.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The astrocytes here are shared with orexin neurons and supply the same lactate, but MCH neurons are far less dependent on it because they are usually quiet when the brain is most active.

The most remarkable glial and immune fact about these cells is negative: in narcolepsy, where the immune system destroys almost every orexin neuron in the immediate neighbourhood, MCH neurons in the very same tissue are spared in normal numbers, which tells us the attack is exquisitely molecularly targeted rather than a general injury to the region.

In Alzheimer's disease, by contrast, MCH neurons show their own specific pattern of degeneration.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the lateral hypothalamus keeps its hunger and its arousal, but it loses its off-switch and its dream engine.

The region would still shout "eat" and "wake up" and would have no voice saying "stop, conserve, sleep, and let this experience go unrecorded".

Rapid eye movement sleep would become shorter and more fragile, body weight would fall, and the nightly wave of hippocampal quietening that normally accompanies dreaming would not arrive.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

MCH is released into the cerebrospinal fluid from fibres near the ventricle wall, a genuine example of volume transmission over centimetres rather than micrometres.

The peptide acts on a receptor that inhibits its targets by a slow second-messenger route, so a burst of firing during one dream episode changes the excitability of distant cortex for minutes afterwards.

These neurons are direct chemical sensors of glucose, and they are also modulated by their own neighbours' peptide, since orexin excites them through orexin receptor 2, creating a delayed negative feedback that helps terminate waking.

Their inhibition of hippocampal circuits during rapid eye movement sleep is an active instruction to forget, one of the clearest cases in the brain of a cell type whose function is subtraction rather than addition.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Extracellular glucose, 0.5 to 5 millimoles per litre, excitatory.

◦ Orexin from neighbouring cells acting at orexin receptor 2, excitatory and delayed.

◦ Gamma-aminobutyric acid from preoptic sleep neurons and noradrenaline from locus coeruleus, inhibitory during waking.

◦ Rapid eye movement sleep drive from the pontine sublaterodorsal nucleus, 0 to 1.

◦ Leptin and body fat signals, setting slow gain over days.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ MCH peptide to cortex, hippocampus, septum, thalamic reticular nucleus, raphe, locus coeruleus, tuberomammillary nucleus, spinal cord and cerebrospinal fluid.

◦ Gamma-aminobutyric acid at conventional synapses in most cells.

◦ A scalar sleep-and-conserve signal, 0 to 6 spikes per second.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage in millivolts.

◦ A-type potassium current inactivation, 0 to 1.

◦ Peptide store, 0 to 1.

◦ Accumulated rapid eye movement sleep debt, 0 to 1.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting voltage about minus 60 millivolts, threshold about minus 45 millivolts.

◦ Peak firing in rapid eye movement sleep, 3 to 6 spikes per second.

◦ Fraction of population that is gamma-aminobutyric acid positive, 0.85.

◦ Population size, working estimate 80 thousand cells.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane time constant, 30 to 80 milliseconds.

◦ A-type current inactivation, 20 to 100 milliseconds.

◦ Peptide action at targets, 10 seconds to several minutes.

◦ State switching between waking and rapid eye movement sleep, 1 to 10 minutes.

◦ Gene expression response to fasting, 12 to 48 hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss or silencing shortens and fragments rapid eye movement sleep and produces leanness with resistance to diet-induced obesity.

◦ Overactivity is associated with excessive rapid eye movement sleep, weight gain and, in animal models, depression-like behaviour, and MCH receptor 1 blockers were developed as anti-obesity drugs before cardiac side effects halted them.

◦ Specific degeneration of these neurons occurs in Alzheimer's disease and may contribute to its disrupted sleep.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to find the moment at which a melanin concentrating hormone neuron overcomes the waking system and lets rapid eye movement sleep begin.
Create a number variable called wake_activity with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called mch_activity with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called rem_pressure with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called mch_drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called glucose with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called switch_minute with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called minute with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 120 times.
    Calculate the sum of minute and 1.
    Set minute to the result.
    Calculate the sum of rem_pressure and 0.01.
    Set rem_pressure to the result.
    Calculate the product of rem_pressure and 3.
    Set mch_drive to the result.
    Calculate the sum of mch_drive and glucose.
    Set mch_drive to the result.
    Calculate the difference of mch_drive and wake_activity.
    Set mch_drive to the result.
    Set mch_activity to mch_drive.
    Check if mch_activity is greater than 0.5.
    If the result is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set wake_activity to 0.
        Set switch_minute to minute.
        Break out of the current loop immediately.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display switch_minute to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the essential idea is two populations inhibiting each
# other, which reads most clearly as four lines of arithmetic inside one loop.
import math

def sigmoid(x):
    return 1.0 / (1.0 + math.exp(-x))

dt = 0.5                 # seconds per step
tau_wake, tau_mch = 60.0, 90.0     # seconds, slow state variables
wake, mch = 1.0, 0.0     # normalised population activity, 0 to 1
rem_pressure = 0.0       # builds up across a sleep episode
glucose = 1.0            # normalised; glucose excites MCH neurons
log = []

for i in range(4800):                     # forty minutes of simulated sleep
    rem_pressure += dt / 1800.0
    drive_wake = 3.0 - 6.0 * mch          # MCH neurons inhibit the wake side
    drive_mch = 6.0 * rem_pressure + 2.0 * glucose - 6.0 * wake - 3.0
    wake += dt / tau_wake * (sigmoid(drive_wake) - wake)
    mch += dt / tau_mch * (sigmoid(drive_mch) - mch)
    if mch > 0.5:                         # entering REM discharges the pressure
        rem_pressure = max(0.0, rem_pressure - dt / 300.0)
    log.append((i * dt, wake, mch))

crossing = next((t for t, w, m in log if m > 0.5), None)
print("MCH activity crossed 0.5 at t =", crossing, "seconds")
print("final wake activity %.3f, final MCH activity %.3f" % (log[-1][1], log[-1][2]))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the cell that sits shoulder to shoulder with the orexin neurons and does everything backwards: excited by the sugar that silences them, loudest when they are mute, spared by the immune attack that annihilates them, and named after a job it does only in fish.

CHAPTER 105. HISTAMINERGIC NEURONS OF THE TUBEROMAMMILLARY NUCLEUS (Wake-Holding Ceiling Neuron) (COG)

Every drop of histamine your brain has ever used came from about sixty-four thousand cells at the very base of the hypothalamus, which is why an old-fashioned hay-fever tablet can put a grown adult to sleep in twenty minutes.

The old name is a transmitter label joined to a Latin address.

Wake-Holding names the effect, because these cells switch on just after waking, fire at about five impulses per second, and fall silent in sleep.

Ceiling names the reach of a tiny population, since roughly sixty-four thousand cells in the human brain, all clustered in one small nucleus, send axons to the cerebral cortex, basal ganglia, cerebellum, brainstem and spinal cord, and they are the sole source of histamine for the entire nervous system.

Neuron marks it as the cell whose receptors, when blocked, are precisely why older antihistamine tablets for hay fever make people drowsy.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These are the only neurons in the entire nervous system that make histamine, a small molecule better known for causing itching and swelling in an allergic reaction.

They manufacture it from the dietary amino acid histidine using a single enzyme, histidine decarboxylase, and that enzyme is their unmistakable chemical signature.

Their job is wakefulness of a particular flavour: not the raw ability to be awake, but alertness, attention and the suppression of drowsiness.

They fire steadily whenever you are awake and attentive, slow down when you are drowsy, and stop completely during rapid eye movement sleep.

Because they broadcast to nearly the whole brain, blocking their receptor with a first-generation antihistamine that crosses into the brain produces the familiar heavy sedation.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

These are large, plump neurons with a big pale nucleus and two to five thick, long, sparsely branched dendrites.

The dendrites are remarkable for where they go: many of them run out of the nucleus altogether and lie along the surface of the brain, directly beneath the pia mater, dipping into the subarachnoid space that holds cerebrospinal fluid.

A neuron with its antennae in the spinal fluid is sampling the whole body's chemistry, not just its neighbours' opinions.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 20 to 35 micrometres across in the human brain, among the larger neurons of the hypothalamus.

Dendrites extend 400 to 800 micrometres, sometimes further along the brain surface.

Total axonal length per cell is very large, since a single neuron can send branches to cortex, thalamus, hippocampus and brainstem at once.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The human tuberomammillary nucleus is conventionally divided into five groups, often labelled E1 to E5, and functionally at least three subtypes matter.

• A ventral group projecting mainly to the cortex and the basal forebrain, serving cortical arousal.

• A medial and diffuse group projecting to the thalamus, hypothalamus and brainstem, serving autonomic and feeding control.

• A caudal group projecting to the spinal cord and to the trigeminal system.

Subtypes also differ in whether they co-express galanin, gamma-aminobutyric acid, enkephalin, substance P or thyrotropin-releasing hormone.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The tuberomammillary nucleus of the posterior hypothalamus, wrapped around and behind the mammillary bodies, spreading forward along the ventral surface as far as the supraoptic region.

The cells are scattered rather than tightly packed, forming a loose shell rather than a dense ball.

The interfaces that matter are three: the boundary with the mammillary bodies just behind, the boundary with the lateral hypothalamic orexin field just in front and above, and the boundary between the nucleus and the pial surface of the brain, across which the dendrites reach into cerebrospinal fluid.

NUMBER:

About 64 thousand histamine-producing neurons in the human brain, a figure that is reasonably well established and repeatedly cited, with a plausible range of 50 thousand to 80 thousand.

Confidence is moderate: the counts come from a small number of post-mortem series.

That is about 0.6 percent of the roughly 10 million hypothalamic neurons, and about one neuron in every 1.3 million in the nervous system as a whole.

Every histamine synapse in the brain, in a structure of 86 billion neurons, is supplied by this handful of cells.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

They are slow, regular pacemakers: even removed from the brain and placed in a dish, they keep firing rhythmically at about 1 to 4 spikes per second with no input at all.

Resting voltage is about minus 50 to minus 55 millivolts, and the pacemaking depends on a persistent sodium current and a hyperpolarisation-activated current that together drag the voltage steadily up to threshold after each spike.

The action potential is broad, 1.2 to 2 milliseconds, with a large calcium component and a long after-hyperpolarisation lasting tens of milliseconds, which enforces the slow rate.

Firing is highest in active waking, roughly halved in quiet waking, near zero in slow wave sleep and completely absent in rapid eye movement sleep.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The strongest excitatory input is from orexin neurons, which is how the two arousal systems reinforce one another.

Powerful inhibition arrives from the ventrolateral preoptic nucleus, using gamma-aminobutyric acid and the peptide galanin, and this is the direct pathway by which the sleep switch turns histamine off.

Other inputs come from the infralimbic cortex, the septum, the amygdala and the brainstem.

Outputs reach essentially the whole brain: all cortical layers, hippocampus, amygdala, thalamus, striatum, hypothalamus, brainstem nuclei, cerebellum and spinal cord.

Histamine acts on four receptors, three of which matter here: H1 and H2 excite the target, and H3 sits on the histamine neuron's own terminals as an autoreceptor, a brake that reports how much histamine has already been released.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are very fine, 0.1 to 0.5 micrometres, and conduct slowly, around 0.5 to 1.5 metres per second.

Branching is exuberant, and most of the fibre length is varicose: the swellings frequently have no postsynaptic partner facing them at all.

Histamine is therefore released mainly into the extracellular space rather than into sealed synaptic clefts, and diffuses to receptors tens of micrometres away.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Histamine, made by histidine decarboxylase from histidine and packaged by the vesicular monoamine transporter 2.

Gamma-aminobutyric acid is co-released by most of these cells, along with galanin, enkephalin, substance P and thyrotropin-releasing hormone in various subsets.

A defining oddity: unlike every other monoamine, histamine has no reuptake transporter on the releasing neuron, so once released it must be chemically inactivated by the enzyme histamine N-methyltransferase, which makes the signal slower to clear and inherently broadcast in character.

Human tuberomammillary neurons are also unusually rich in adenosine deaminase, which is a useful marker for them in human tissue.

MYELINATION:

Essentially unmyelinated throughout.

This is entirely consistent with the cell's role: it is a chemical broadcaster setting a background level over minutes, and a fast insulated cable would be wasted on it.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest and steady, but with a strict daily rhythm, because the cell is on for sixteen hours and off for eight.

The histamine it makes is expensive in a different currency: histidine must be imported from the diet across the blood-brain barrier, so the size of the brain's histamine signal is partly set by what was eaten.

PLASTICITY:

Histidine decarboxylase levels rise with prolonged wakefulness, so the system upregulates its own supply under pressure.

H3 autoreceptor sensitivity adapts over days, which is why tolerance develops to some antihistamines.

The projection is also remodelled by chronic stress and by long-term antihistamine or antipsychotic exposure, and the histamine system instructs plasticity elsewhere by gating long-term potentiation in the hippocampus and cortex.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes around these neurons carry an unusually high load of histamine N-methyltransferase, so the glia, not the neuron, are the main off-switch for the transmitter, a division of labour found nowhere else in monoamine signalling.

Because the dendrites lie against the pia, these neurons are wrapped by meningeal cells and bathed in cerebrospinal fluid rather than being fully enclosed by astrocytic endfeet.

The neighbourhood also contains mast cells, immune cells that themselves store histamine, so this is one of very few brain regions where a neuronal transmitter has a non-neuronal source sitting beside it.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the posterior hypothalamus still remembers routes through space in the mammillary bodies and still runs autonomic reflexes, but the whole brain loses its tone control.

The flavour lost is vigilance: the fine adjustment between merely being awake and being sharp.

Animals without histamine are awake for a normal number of hours yet fail to wake up properly when something new happens, and they cannot stay alert at the wrong time of day.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

These neurons release transmitter from dendrites as well as axons and they self-inhibit through H3 autoreceptors, so the population computes its own output level by negative feedback rather than being commanded from outside.

H3 receptors also sit on other neurons' terminals as heteroreceptors, controlling the release of noradrenaline, acetylcholine, dopamine and serotonin, so this cell type is a master gain control over the other arousal chemicals.

The dendrites in cerebrospinal fluid make the cell a whole-body chemosensor, and the axon terminals influence the calibre of local blood vessels, coupling arousal to brain blood flow.

They also signal to the immune system and are themselves modulated by immune mediators, which is part of why infection makes you drowsy.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Orexin from lateral hypothalamus, excitatory, 0 to 1 normalised.

◦ Gamma-aminobutyric acid and galanin from the ventrolateral preoptic nucleus, inhibitory, the sleep switch.

◦ Extracellular histamine acting on H3 autoreceptors, inhibitory feedback, 0 to 1.

◦ Histidine availability from the blood, setting maximum synthesis rate.

◦ Circadian drive from the suprachiasmatic nucleus by way of the subparaventricular zone.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Histamine, released by volume transmission across cortex, thalamus, hippocampus, striatum, brainstem, cerebellum and spinal cord.

◦ Gamma-aminobutyric acid and peptide co-transmitters at conventional synapses.

◦ A scalar vigilance signal, 0 to 4 spikes per second.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage in millivolts.

◦ Pacemaker current activation, 0 to 1.

◦ Extracellular histamine concentration in the target field.

◦ H3 autoreceptor occupancy, 0 to 1.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Intrinsic pacemaker rate with no input, 1 to 4 spikes per second.

◦ Action potential width, 1.2 to 2 milliseconds.

◦ Maximum autoreceptor suppression of release, about 50 to 80 percent.

◦ Population size, 64 thousand cells.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Interspike interval, 250 to 1000 milliseconds.

◦ After-hyperpolarisation, 30 to 100 milliseconds.

◦ Histamine clearance by histamine N-methyltransferase, tens of seconds to minutes.

◦ Sleep and wake state transitions, seconds to minutes.

◦ Histidine decarboxylase upregulation, hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss of these neurons occurs in multiple system atrophy and dementia with Lewy bodies and contributes to the daytime sleepiness in those diseases.

◦ Blocking H1 receptors, as first-generation antihistamines and many antipsychotics do, causes sedation, cognitive slowing and weight gain.

◦ Blocking the H3 autoreceptor with the inverse agonist pitolisant releases the brake and raises histamine output, and is a licensed treatment for the sleepiness of narcolepsy.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to follow the histamine level around a tuberomammillary neuron as its own autoreceptor brake is applied and then lifted by a drug.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 2.
Create a number variable called histamine with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called brake with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called release with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called change with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drug with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called tick with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 1200 times.
    Calculate the sum of tick and 1.
    Set tick to the result.
    Check if tick is equal to 600.
    If the result is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set drug to 2.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of histamine and 0.05.
    Set brake to the result.
    Calculate the difference of brake and drug.
    Set brake to the result.
    Calculate the difference of firing_rate and brake.
    Set release to the result.
    Calculate the quotient of histamine divided by 60.
    Set change to the result.
    Calculate the difference of release and change.
    Set change to the result.
    Calculate the sum of histamine and change.
    Set histamine to the result.
End the loop.
Display histamine to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the whole point is a negative feedback loop, and it is
# easiest to trust when it is four lines of arithmetic you can read at a glance.
FIRING_RATE = 2.0     # spikes per second, quiet waking
AUTO_GAIN = 0.05      # strength of the H3 autoreceptor brake per unit histamine
CLEARANCE_TAU = 60.0  # steps for histamine N-methyltransferase to clear the pool
DT = 1.0              # one step, arbitrary time units

histamine = 0.0       # extracellular histamine, arbitrary units
drug = 0.0            # pitolisant-like H3 inverse agonist, 0 or 2
trace = []

for tick in range(1200):
    if tick == 600:
        drug = 2.0                                  # the drug arrives
    brake = AUTO_GAIN * histamine - drug            # self-inhibition, minus the block
    release = FIRING_RATE - brake                   # release per step
    clearance = histamine / CLEARANCE_TAU
    histamine += DT * (release - clearance)
    trace.append(histamine)

print("steady histamine before the drug: %.2f" % trace[599])
print("steady histamine after the drug:  %.2f" % trace[-1])
print("fold increase: %.2f" % (trace[-1] / trace[599]))
# Analytic check: before the drug 2 = 0.05h + h/60 gives h = 30.
# After the drug 4 = 0.05h + h/60 gives h = 60, so histamine doubles.

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the sole source of a transmitter for an entire brain, it has no reuptake pump so its glia must destroy the signal for it, it dangles its dendrites in cerebrospinal fluid, and it is the cell you switch off by accident every time you take the wrong hay-fever tablet.

CHAPTER 106. KISSPEPTIN, NEUROKININ B, AND DYNORPHIN (KNDY) NEURONS (Hormone-Pulse Generator Neuron) (NON-COG)

Once an hour, for your entire reproductive life, a small knot of cells at the floor of the brain fires all at once for a few minutes and then falls silent, and that heartbeat is the metronome of human fertility.

The old name is three peptide barcodes compressed into an unspeakable acronym.

Hormone-Pulse names the stake, since without this rhythm the ovaries and testes are never driven and puberty never arrives, which is why humans lacking kisspeptin signalling fail to enter puberty.

Generator names the machinery, because these cells are the pulse generator itself, neurokinin B exciting the network into a synchronised burst, kisspeptin carrying that burst to the gonadotropin-releasing hormone cells, and dynorphin then shutting the burst off after a short delay.

Neuron marks it as the cell that swells and overproduces its peptides after menopause, which is why drugs blocking the neurokinin B receptor now treat hot flushes.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

KNDy is an acronym built from the three chemicals every one of these cells makes at the same time: kisspeptin, neurokinin B (NKB), and dynorphin, with the y borrowed from the chemical shorthand Dyn.

Kisspeptin is the output, the message sent onward.

Neurokinin B is the accelerator the cells use on each other, and dynorphin is the brake they use on each other.

Together those three make a relaxation oscillator, an engineering term for a circuit that charges up, discharges in a burst, and then has to recharge before it can burst again.

Every burst releases kisspeptin onto gonadotropin-releasing hormone (GnRH) neurons, which then release their own hormone into the blood vessels feeding the pituitary gland, which then releases luteinising hormone into the general circulation, which then tells the ovary or testis what to do.

If this clock beats too slowly, puberty never happens and periods stop; if it beats correctly, the whole reproductive system follows.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Medium-sized, round to oval cell bodies with three to five dendrites, distinguished by an unusual habit: their axons and dendrites wrap around each other's cell bodies.

Each KNDy neuron is contacted by many other KNDy neurons, forming a densely reciprocal mesh, which is exactly the architecture needed for a population that must ignite together.

In humans the cells also send fibres into the median eminence, the specialised zone where brain and pituitary blood supply meet, and there they lie next to GnRH terminals.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are 15 to 25 micrometres across in premenopausal women.

They enlarge dramatically after the menopause: measured cell body areas roughly triple, from about 175 square micrometres before the menopause to about 565 square micrometres afterwards, because the oestrogen that normally restrains them has gone.

Dendritic fields are compact, 150 to 300 micrometres, since most of the wiring is local.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• The infundibular or arcuate population, which is the KNDy population proper and generates the pulses.

• A separate preoptic and rostral periventricular kisspeptin population, which does not make neurokinin B or dynorphin and instead generates the single large surge of luteinising hormone that triggers ovulation.

• Within the infundibular group, cells differ in whether they also contain substance P, which is common in humans and rare in rodents.

• The population is sexually dimorphic, with more cells in women than in men.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The infundibular nucleus of the human hypothalamus, which is the human name for the arcuate nucleus, forming a curved sheet along the wall of the third ventricle just above the pituitary stalk.

Fibres run down into the median eminence and up into the preoptic area.

The interface that matters most is the one with the median eminence, because that is where the blood-brain barrier is deliberately incomplete: these neurons are positioned to be bathed in circulating sex hormones, which is the whole point of their location.

A second interface is with the tanycytes lining the ventricle, which physically move to open and close access to the portal blood.

NUMBER:

No consensus human count exists, and it would be wrong to pretend otherwise.

Studies counting kisspeptin-labelled cell bodies in human post-mortem tissue report thousands per nucleus, and a reasonable working estimate for the combined infundibular KNDy population is about 10 thousand cells, with a defensible bound of 3 thousand to 30 thousand and low confidence.

That is roughly 0.1 percent of the approximately 10 million neurons of the hypothalamus, and about one neuron in every 8 million in the nervous system as a whole.

For a population that decides whether a species reproduces, it is a startlingly small number of cells.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Between pulses they are quiet, firing at well under 1 spike per second.

During a pulse the whole population synchronises and fires in a burst lasting one to several minutes, with individual rates of 5 to 20 spikes per second.

The synchrony is built from mutual excitation: neurokinin B acting on the NK3 receptor slowly depolarises neighbouring KNDy cells, and glutamate acting at AMPA receptors provides the fast component.

Termination is built from dynorphin acting on the kappa opioid receptor, which arrives with a delay of tens of seconds to minutes and shuts the burst down; the slow recovery from that inhibition sets the interval to the next pulse.

In humans and other primates the resulting rhythm is roughly one pulse per hour in the follicular phase of the menstrual cycle, slowing to one pulse every few hours in the luteal phase.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs include one another, which is the defining feature, plus proopiomelanocortin and AgRP neurons reporting energy state, orexin and MCH fibres, noradrenergic brainstem inputs, and inputs carrying circadian time from the suprachiasmatic nucleus.

Outputs go to GnRH cell bodies in the preoptic area and to GnRH terminals in the median eminence, and also to the preoptic thermoregulatory area, which is why the same cells that time fertility also help set body temperature.

Oestrogen and testosterone act directly on these cells through oestrogen receptor alpha and the androgen receptor, and this is the principal site of the negative feedback that keeps sex hormone levels stable.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are thin, 0.2 to 0.6 micrometres, unmyelinated, conducting at under 1 metre per second.

Local collaterals are short and dense, while the projection to the median eminence runs a few millimetres.

Terminals in the median eminence are the classic neurosecretory type, releasing peptide into a blood plexus rather than onto another neuron.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Kisspeptin, the product of the KISS1 gene, acting on the kisspeptin receptor KISS1R.

Neurokinin B, the product of the TAC3 gene, acting on the NK3 receptor encoded by TACR3.

Dynorphin, the product of the PDYN gene, acting on the kappa opioid receptor.

Glutamate is co-released as the fast transmitter, and in humans substance P is frequently present as well.

The genetics is the proof: loss-of-function mutations in KISS1, KISS1R, TAC3 or TACR3 each cause normosmic idiopathic hypogonadotropic hypogonadism, in which puberty simply never arrives in an otherwise healthy person.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable in any meaningful sense, because these axons are unmyelinated.

The reason is worth stating so it is not mistaken for an omission: the signal here is a hormone pulse measured in minutes, travelling at most a few millimetres, so there is nothing for myelin to improve.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low on average, since the cells are silent most of the time, with sharp metabolic spikes during each burst.

Peptide synthesis is the main ongoing cost, and it is strongly regulated: after the menopause, kisspeptin and neurokinin B gene expression in these cells rises steeply.

They are also energy sensors, and they shut the reproductive clock down when the body is starved, which is the mechanism behind the loss of periods in anorexia nervosa and in extreme athletic training.

PLASTICITY:

These cells rewire in response to sex hormones across the menstrual cycle, across puberty and across the menopause.

Oestrogen withdrawal causes hypertrophy, increased kisspeptin and neurokinin B expression and decreased dynorphin expression, all of which speed and strengthen the pulse.

Puberty itself is largely a story of these neurons increasing their coupling and escaping childhood restraint.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Tanycytes are the specialised glia that matter here: they ensheath the terminals in the median eminence and physically retract or extend their processes across the oestrous cycle, opening and closing the neurons' access to the portal blood in a way glia do nowhere else.

Astrocytes in the infundibular nucleus express oestrogen receptors and release prostaglandin E2 and growth factors that modulate the pulse generator directly.

Because the barrier is porous here, microglia in this nucleus are chronically exposed to circulating signals and respond to diet and inflammation with unusual speed.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove this cell type and the infundibular nucleus still senses hunger and still controls growth hormone and thyroid pathways, but it loses its clock.

The region would become a metabolic sensor with no rhythm and no reproductive voice: GnRH neurons would sit intact and silent, puberty would not begin, and the ovary and testis would never receive an instruction.

The nucleus would also lose the thermostat contribution that makes falling oestrogen produce hot flushes.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

This is one of the clearest examples in the brain of computation performed by a network's chemistry rather than by any single cell: neither the accelerator nor the brake would generate a rhythm alone, and the period of the oscillation is set by the decay rate of a peptide, not by any membrane property.

The cells release peptide onto each other by volume transmission, so the burst spreads through the tissue as a chemical wave.

They read steroid hormones directly by nuclear receptors and change their gene transcription accordingly, so the same cell operates on timescales from seconds to decades.

They release their product into blood rather than onto a neuron, making them neurosecretory in the strict sense, and their projection to the preoptic thermoregulatory neurons means they also directly command skin blood flow.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Neurokinin B from other KNDy neurons, excitatory, slow, 0 to 1 normalised.

◦ Dynorphin from other KNDy neurons, inhibitory, slower still, 0 to 1.

◦ Glutamate from other KNDy neurons, fast excitatory.

◦ Circulating oestradiol, 20 to 400 picograms per millilitre, or testosterone, acting through nuclear receptors as negative feedback.

◦ Energy-state signals: leptin, insulin and input from AgRP and proopiomelanocortin neurons.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Kisspeptin onto GnRH cell bodies and terminals, driving one pulse of gonadotropin-releasing hormone per burst.

◦ Projections to preoptic thermoregulatory neurons setting the heat-loss threshold.

◦ A pulse train, typically one pulse every 30 to 120 minutes.

• STATE.

◦ Population activity, 0 to 1.

◦ Neurokinin B tone, 0 to 1.

◦ Dynorphin tone, 0 to 1.

◦ Peptide stores and steroid receptor occupancy.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Neurokinin B feedback gain, high enough to make the burst all or none.

◦ Dynorphin feedback gain, setting burst duration of 1 to 5 minutes.

◦ Oestradiol sensitivity, setting overall pulse frequency.

◦ Population size, working estimate 10 thousand cells.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Glutamate synchronisation, milliseconds.

◦ Neurokinin B action, 10 to 60 seconds.

◦ Dynorphin action and recovery, 10 to 60 minutes.

◦ Steroid feedback through gene transcription, hours to days.

◦ Reorganisation at puberty and menopause, months to years.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss-of-function mutations in TAC3, TACR3, KISS1 or KISS1R abolish the pulse and cause failure of puberty with normal sense of smell.

◦ Excess pulse frequency contributes to polycystic ovary syndrome.

◦ After the menopause the loss of oestrogen leaves these neurons hypertrophied and overactive, and their runaway neurokinin B signalling onto the preoptic thermostat produces hot flushes, which is why NK3 receptor blockers work: fezolinetant was approved in May 2023 and the dual NK1 and NK3 blocker elinzanetant was approved in October 2025 for menopausal hot flushes.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to generate the hourly pulse train of the kisspeptin pulse generator from a slow dynorphin brake.
Create a number variable called neurokinin with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called dynorphin with initial value 1.
Create a boolean variable called firing with initial value false.
Create a number variable called minute with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called kisspeptin_released with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called pulse_minutes with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 720 times.
    Calculate the sum of minute and 1.
    Set minute to the result.
    Check if dynorphin is less than 0.2.
    If the result is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing to true.
        Set neurokinin to 1.
        Set dynorphin to 1.
        Add minute to the end of pulse_minutes.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set firing to false.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of dynorphin and 0.97.
    Set dynorphin to the result.
    Calculate the product of neurokinin and 0.8.
    Set neurokinin to the result.
    Calculate the sum of kisspeptin_released and neurokinin.
    Set kisspeptin_released to the result.
End the loop.
Display pulse_minutes to the console.
Display kisspeptin_released to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the output of interest is a list of pulse times that a
# reader can compare directly with a real luteinising hormone assay printout.
DYN_DECAY = 0.97      # dynorphin brake decays 3 percent per simulated minute
DYN_THRESHOLD = 0.20  # below this the network escapes and fires
NKB_DECAY = 0.80      # neurokinin B burst envelope decays fast

dynorphin = 1.0
neurokinin = 0.0
kisspeptin_released = 0.0
pulse_minutes = []

for minute in range(1, 721):              # twelve hours
    if dynorphin < DYN_THRESHOLD:         # brake has worn off, the network ignites
        neurokinin = 1.0                  # neurokinin B makes the burst all or none
        dynorphin = 1.0                   # dynorphin is released and reapplies the brake
        pulse_minutes.append(minute)
    dynorphin *= DYN_DECAY
    neurokinin *= NKB_DECAY
    kisspeptin_released += neurokinin     # kisspeptin output tracks the burst envelope

gaps = [b - a for a, b in zip(pulse_minutes, pulse_minutes[1:])]
print("pulse onsets in minutes:", pulse_minutes)
print("interpulse intervals:", gaps)
if gaps:
    print("mean interval: %.1f minutes" % (sum(gaps) / len(gaps)))
print("total kisspeptin released: %.2f arbitrary units" % kisspeptin_released)

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron that carries an accelerator and a brake for its own network in the same vesicles, that triples in size when a woman's ovaries stop working, and whose single accelerator receptor can be blocked by a tablet to stop hot flushes.

CHAPTER 107. SUPRACHIASMATIC NUCLEUS NEURONS (Master Body-Clock Neuron) (COG)

Two grains of tissue the size of a pinhead, sitting just above the crossing point of your optic nerves, keep a twenty-four-hour clock that runs in a dish, in the dark, with no input at all, for weeks.

The old name is purely an address, meaning the nucleus lying above the optic chiasm, and it tells a reader only where to dig.

Master names the rank, because this cluster of roughly twenty thousand cells sets the phase of subordinate clocks in the liver, gut, heart and skin.

Body distinguishes it from any single organ rhythm.

Clock names the mechanism, because each of these cells runs a self-sustaining loop of clock gene transcription lasting close to but not exactly twenty-four hours, and a slice of the nucleus in a dish keeps ticking for weeks.

Neuron marks it as the cell reset each morning by light delivered along a direct tract from specialised retinal cells.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The suprachiasmatic nucleus (SCN) is the master circadian clock, circadian meaning "about a day".

Every neuron in it contains a molecular oscillator: a loop in which two proteins, CLOCK and BMAL1, switch on the genes Period and Cryptochrome, whose protein products then travel back into the nucleus and switch their own genes off, after which they are destroyed and the cycle restarts.

One turn of that loop takes about twenty-four hours, and it is called a transcription translation feedback loop (TTFL).

Individually these cells are sloppy timekeepers; coupled together by peptides and gap junctions they become extraordinarily precise.

Their job is to read the time of day from light arriving down a dedicated nerve pathway, correct their own clock accordingly, and then broadcast that time to every organ in the body.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

These are among the smallest neurons in the brain, with scant cytoplasm, few organelles, and short, simple, sparsely branched dendrites.

They are packed together at an unusually high density, and neighbouring cells touch one another directly, with electrical connections called gap junctions, which are protein tunnels that let ionic current pass straight from one cell into the next.

Synapses are dominated by inhibitory contacts, and dendrites overlap heavily, so the nucleus behaves less like a wiring diagram and more like a syncytium, a tissue whose cells act as one.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are only 8 to 12 micrometres across, roughly the smallest neuronal somata in the mammalian brain.

Dendritic fields extend just 50 to 150 micrometres, entirely within the nucleus.

The whole nucleus is about 0.5 to 1 cubic millimetre per side in humans, and the vasopressin-containing subnucleus occupies about 0.25 cubic millimetres per side.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Core, or ventrolateral, neurons containing vasoactive intestinal peptide (VIP) and gastrin-releasing peptide, which receive light information and act as the coupling agents that keep the population in step.

• Shell, or dorsomedial, neurons containing arginine vasopressin (AVP), which act as the primary pacemakers setting the period of the whole network and carry the output.

• Neurons containing calretinin, neurotensin, somatostatin, prokineticin 2 or angiotensin II, which subdivide the two main groups further.

• Almost every one of these cells, whatever its peptide, also uses gamma-aminobutyric acid, so the nucleus is fundamentally an inhibitory network.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The anterior hypothalamus, immediately above the optic chiasm, which is the X-shaped crossing of the two optic nerves, and immediately beside the third ventricle, one nucleus in each hemisphere.

The interfaces that define it are unusually crisp: below and in front lies the optic chiasm, the source of its light input; medially lies the third ventricle and its ependymal lining; dorsally and caudally lies the subparaventricular zone, through which almost all of its output passes.

NUMBER:

About 50 thousand neurons per nucleus in the human brain, so roughly 100 thousand for the pair, with moderate confidence.

The uncertainty is real: rodent nuclei contain around 10 to 20 thousand neurons each, and some human estimates are lower than 50 thousand.

The count also falls with age and falls further in Alzheimer's disease.

Taking 100 thousand, the SCN is about 1 percent of the roughly 10 million hypothalamic neurons, and about one neuron in every 860 thousand in the nervous system as a whole.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The signature property is a spontaneous circadian rhythm in firing rate: about 6 to 10 spikes per second during the subjective day, falling below 1 spike per second at night, with many cells electrically silent at night.

This rhythm persists in a slice of tissue kept alive in a dish for weeks with no light, no hormones and no input, which is the single most convincing demonstration that the clock is intrinsic.

The daily switch is produced by the clock proteins regulating potassium and sodium channels, including members of the Kv4 and Kv12 families and a persistent sodium current.

Resting voltage swings across the day too, from about minus 45 millivolts in the day to about minus 60 millivolts at night.

Action potentials are narrow and the cells fire tonically and regularly rather than in bursts.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The dominant input is the retinohypothalamic tract, a dedicated bundle of axons from a small class of retinal ganglion cells that contain the pigment melanopsin and measure overall brightness rather than form.

These fibres release glutamate and pituitary adenylate cyclase-activating polypeptide onto core neurons, and glutamate at night triggers the gene expression that shifts the clock.

Other inputs arrive from the intergeniculate leaflet of the thalamus carrying non-photic and behavioural cues, and from the median raphe carrying serotonin.

Outputs run mainly to the subparaventricular zone, and from there to the dorsomedial hypothalamus, the paraventricular nucleus, the preoptic area and the orexin field.

The route to the pineal gland runs from the paraventricular nucleus down the spinal cord to the superior cervical ganglion and back up, which is how darkness is turned into melatonin.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are extremely fine, 0.1 to 0.4 micrometres, unmyelinated, and mostly very short, because most SCN neurons contact only other SCN neurons.

Output axons travel only a few millimetres to the subparaventricular zone.

Conduction speed is under 1 metre per second, which is irrelevant to a system whose unit of time is the hour.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Gamma-aminobutyric acid is used by essentially all SCN neurons, which is surprising for a clock that must excite the body into activity, and its effect inside the nucleus can be inhibitory or excitatory depending on the time of day and the internal chloride concentration of the target cell.

VIP is the essential coupling signal; without it or its receptor VPAC2 the neurons keep ticking individually but drift out of step and behavioural rhythms collapse.

AVP carries much of the output and sets the network period, and prokineticin 2, transforming growth factor alpha and cardiotrophin-like cytokine act as diffusible output signals that suppress activity without any synapse at all.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable, because SCN axons are unmyelinated, and the reason is worth spelling out so it is not read as an oversight.

Myelin exists to make signals arrive within a millisecond of each other; this system's precision requirement is measured in minutes per day, so insulation would buy nothing.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Glucose uptake in the SCN oscillates with a clear daily rhythm, high in the subjective day and low at night, and this rhythm was one of the earliest ways the nucleus was identified in the living brain.

Total consumption is small, because the cells are tiny and slow, but it is one of the most metabolically rhythmic tissues in the body.

PLASTICITY:

The synapses inside the nucleus are structurally remodelled every single day, with astrocytic processes advancing and retreating around them on a daily cycle.

Light at night causes rapid induction of the Period genes, which is the molecular event underlying jet lag.

The network can be reprogrammed by unusual light schedules, splitting into two mutually antagonistic halves under some conditions, and its coupling strength changes with day length, which is how a mammal measures the season.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The astrocytes of the SCN are not support staff, they are timekeepers in their own right: they carry a full molecular clock, they release glutamate rhythmically at night onto neuronal receptors, and an astrocyte clock alone can drive circadian behaviour in an animal whose neuronal clock has been disabled.

Nowhere else in the brain is a glial cell an equal partner in the core computation.

Astrocytic processes also physically wrap and unwrap synapses on a daily schedule, changing how far transmitter can spread.

Microglia in the SCN show daily rhythms in shape and in inflammatory signalling.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove them and the anterior hypothalamus keeps everything else it does, sleep drive, temperature control, hormone release, but every one of those functions becomes arrhythmic.

The lost flavour is anticipation.

A body without an SCN still sleeps, eats and secretes cortisol, but it does all of these things in random bouts scattered across the day and night, always reacting and never preparing, and it can no longer use dawn to predict anything at all.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The primary computation here is not electrical, it is transcriptional: the cell's state variable is the abundance of a set of proteins, and firing rate is downstream of it, which makes this the clearest case in the nervous system of a neuron that computes with gene expression.

The cells are coupled by gap junctions and by peptides diffusing through the tissue, so the nucleus synchronises without needing precise wiring.

They release diffusible output factors that act on distant tissue without synapses at all.

They engage in genuine two-way metabolic and signalling partnership with astrocytes, they instruct daily rhythms in blood flow and in blood-brain barrier permeability, and they impose their timing on clocks in every organ of the body through hormones and the autonomic nervous system.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Retinal irradiance from melanopsin-containing retinal ganglion cells, expressed as photons per square centimetre per second or simply as light on or off, delivered as glutamate and pituitary adenylate cyclase-activating polypeptide.

◦ Serotonin from the median raphe, non-photic.

◦ Neuropeptide Y from the intergeniculate leaflet, carrying behavioural arousal.

◦ Melatonin feedback at MT1 and MT2 receptors.

◦ Astrocytic glutamate, rhythmic, peaking at night.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Gamma-aminobutyric acid and AVP to the subparaventricular zone, dorsomedial hypothalamus and paraventricular nucleus.

◦ Diffusible factors: prokineticin 2, transforming growth factor alpha, cardiotrophin-like cytokine.

◦ A phase signal, expressed as circadian time in hours from 0 to 24, and a firing rate from 0 to 10 spikes per second.

• STATE.

◦ Clock phase in circadian hours.

◦ Period and Cryptochrome messenger RNA and protein levels.

◦ Membrane voltage and potassium conductance.

◦ Coupling strength across the population, 0 to 1.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Free-running period in humans, about 24.2 hours.

◦ Daytime firing rate 6 to 10 spikes per second, night-time below 1.

◦ Maximum single-pulse phase shift, about 1 to 2 hours.

◦ Population size, about 50 thousand neurons per side.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Action potential and synaptic events, milliseconds.

◦ Immediate early gene induction by light, 15 to 60 minutes.

◦ One turn of the transcription translation feedback loop, about 24 hours.

◦ Re-entrainment after a time-zone shift, about 1 hour of shift per day.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Mutations in the clock genes cause inherited sleep timing disorders: a change in PER2 causes familial advanced sleep phase syndrome, in which people fall asleep in the early evening, and a CRY1 variant causes delayed sleep phase disorder.

◦ Age and Alzheimer's disease reduce SCN neuron number and flatten the rhythm, producing the fragmented sleep and evening agitation known as sundowning.

◦ Shift work and jet lag desynchronise the SCN from peripheral organ clocks, with measurable metabolic and cardiovascular consequences.

◦ Total destruction abolishes all circadian rhythms while leaving total sleep amount roughly normal.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to advance a suprachiasmatic clock neuron minute by minute and to shift its phase when light arrives late in the subjective night.
Create a number variable called phase_hours with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called period_ratio with initial value 1.00833.
Create a number variable called step_hours with initial value 0.0167.
Create a number variable called light with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called shift with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called advance with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called elapsed with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 1260 times.
    Calculate the sum of elapsed and step_hours.
    Set elapsed to the result.
    Set light to 0.
    Check if elapsed is greater than 20.
    If the result is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set light to 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Calculate the product of light and 1.5.
    Set shift to the result.
    Calculate the product of shift and step_hours.
    Set shift to the result.
    Calculate the quotient of step_hours divided by period_ratio.
    Set advance to the result.
    Calculate the sum of phase_hours and advance.
    Set phase_hours to the result.
    Calculate the sum of phase_hours and shift.
    Set phase_hours to the result.
End the loop.
Display phase_hours to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

// JavaScript is chosen because this phase model has no dependencies and runs
// unchanged in any browser console or under Node, so anyone can check it.
const TAU_FREE = 24.2;          // hours, the measured human free-running period
const DT = 0.005;               // hours per step
const TWO_PI = 2 * Math.PI;

// Phase 0 is subjective dawn. Light in the subjective day does nothing (the
// dead zone), light in early subjective night delays, light late advances.
function prc(phase) {
  return phase > Math.PI ? -1.5 * Math.sin(2 * (phase - Math.PI)) : 0;
}

// Run the clock until its third subjective dawn, optionally with a light pulse.
function thirdDawn(pulseStart, pulseHours) {
  let phase = 0.0, t = 0.0;
  while (phase < 3 * TWO_PI) {
    const lit = (t >= pulseStart && t < pulseStart + pulseHours) ? 1 : 0;
    const drift = TWO_PI / TAU_FREE;                       // radians per hour
    const kick = lit * prc(phase % TWO_PI) * TWO_PI / 24;  // hours to radians
    phase += DT * (drift + kick);
    t += DT;
  }
  return t;
}

const control = thirdDawn(-1, 0);                 // no pulse at all
for (let hour = 0; hour < 24; hour += 3) {
  const shift = control - thirdDawn(hour, 1);     // positive means advanced
  console.log("1 h light pulse at hour " + hour +
              ": phase shift " + shift.toFixed(2) + " h");
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron whose defining computation is a loop of gene transcription rather than a flow of current, the only one that keeps perfect twenty-four-hour time alone in a dish in total darkness, and the only one whose surrounding astrocytes are full partners in the calculation rather than support staff.

CHAPTER 108. PREAUTONOMIC NEURONS OF THE PARAVENTRICULAR NUCLEUS (Organ-Command Setting Neuron) (NON-COG)

Somewhere above the roof of your mouth sits a cluster of cells no bigger than a grain of rice that decides, second by second, how hard your heart will beat.

The old name is a technical prefix joined to an address and would mean nothing to a newcomer.

Organ-Command names what the cell reaches, because its axons run all the way down to the sympathetic outflow columns of the thoracic spinal cord and, by collaterals, to the vagal motor cells of the medulla, so a single cell can touch both the accelerating and the braking side of the involuntary nervous system.

Setting names the work rather than commanding it, because the cell biases ongoing brainstem control of blood pressure, heart rate and gastric acid rather than issuing single orders.

Neuron marks it as a descending hypothalamic cell whose overactivity is a well-documented contributor to sustained high blood pressure and to heart failure.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

A preautonomic neuron is a nerve cell that sits one step upstream of the body's automatic control wiring.

The word autonomic means the part of the nervous system that runs the body without conscious effort, such as heartbeat, sweating, blood vessel width and gut movement.

These particular cells live in the paraventricular nucleus, a nucleus being a tight cluster of nerve cell bodies with a shared job, and paraventricular meaning that it hugs the wall of a fluid-filled cavity in the brain called the third ventricle.

Their job is to take a running summary of the body's state and turn it into a command signal for the spinal cord and the brainstem.

When they fire faster, blood pressure rises, heart rate climbs, the kidney holds salt, brown fat burns energy as heat, and the liver releases sugar.

When they fall silent, the body coasts.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

Each cell has a small, roughly oval body with three to six dendrites, dendrites being the branching antennae that collect incoming signals.

The dendritic tree is sparse and long rather than bushy, so a single cell samples a wide slab of tissue instead of listening intensely to one small spot.

One thin axon, the output cable, leaves the cell and often splits, sending one branch down to the spinal cord and another to the brainstem, so a single neuron can command two relay stations at once.

Many of these cells push their dendrites right up against the wall of the third ventricle and against blood vessels, which places them where chemical messages in the cerebrospinal fluid and the blood can reach them.

SIZE:
