The cell body is small, about 10 to 15 micrometres across, which is why the population is called parvocellular, from the Latin for small cell.

A micrometre is one thousandth of a millimetre, so about a hundred of these cell bodies laid in a row would span one millimetre.

The dendritic field spans roughly 200 to 400 micrometres.

The axon is the extreme dimension: in an adult human, a branch running from the hypothalamus to the lower thoracic spinal cord travels roughly 20 to 35 centimetres, making the output cable more than ten thousand times longer than the cell body is wide.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Spinally projecting preautonomic neurons, often abbreviated as sympathetic preautonomic neurons, reach the intermediolateral cell column of the spinal cord, the strip of tissue that houses the first relay of the fight-or-flight system.

• Medulla-projecting preautonomic neurons target the rostral ventrolateral medulla, a small brainstem region that acts as the body's blood pressure amplifier.

• Vagal preautonomic neurons target the dorsal motor nucleus of the vagus and the nucleus ambiguus, which slow the heart and drive digestion.

• Dual-projecting neurons send collateral branches to both spinal cord and medulla, so one cell can act directly and indirectly on the same outflow.

• Peptide-defined subsets overlap these classes: some carry oxytocin, some vasopressin, some corticotropin-releasing hormone, some thyrotropin-releasing hormone.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They occupy the paraventricular nucleus of the hypothalamus, on both sides of the third ventricle, mainly in its parvocellular subdivisions.

Within that nucleus they concentrate in the dorsal cap, the ventromedial part and the posterior part, while the lateral magnocellular part is dominated by the large hormone-secreting cells instead.

Because their dendrites reach the ependymal lining of the third ventricle and the perivascular space around penetrating arterioles, these cells genuinely sit at an interface: the border between brain tissue, cerebrospinal fluid and blood.

Their axons cross a second interface, leaving the hypothalamus to end in the brainstem and in the thoracic and upper lumbar spinal cord.

NUMBER:

The two paraventricular nuclei together hold roughly 85 thousand neurons in the adult human.

Preautonomic cells are a minority of these, and the best available estimate places them at roughly 10 thousand to 15 thousand neurons across both sides.

Confidence is moderate at best, because the count is extrapolated from rodent tracing studies rather than from a direct human stereological census, and no unbiased human count of preautonomic subtypes exists.

Against a nervous system of about 86 billion neurons, this is on the order of 0.000015 percent, roughly one neuron in every seven million.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

At rest the inside of the cell sits at about minus 60 millivolts relative to the outside, a millivolt being one thousandth of a volt.

Input resistance is high, often 0.5 to 1.5 gigaohms, which means even a tiny injected current shifts the voltage noticeably, so these cells are easy to push.

Their signature is a low-threshold spike carried by T-type calcium channels, protein pores that open only after the cell has been held unusually negative for a while.

The practical result is rebound firing: inhibit the cell, release the inhibition, and it fires a short burst rather than simply returning to baseline.

Spontaneous firing in vivo is slow and irregular, typically 0.5 to 5 spikes per second, and this low rate is set largely by a constant background of inhibition rather than by silence of excitation.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Excitatory input arrives from the subfornical organ and the organum vasculosum, both blood-sampling sensors, and from the nucleus of the solitary tract, which relays gut, lung and blood pressure information.

Inhibitory input arrives from the surrounding hypothalamus, especially a shell of GABA-releasing cells that wraps the nucleus, GABA being gamma-aminobutyric acid, the brain's main calming chemical.

Outputs synapse onto sympathetic preganglionic neurons in the intermediolateral column, onto bulbospinal neurons of the rostral ventrolateral medulla, and onto vagal motor neurons.

A single preautonomic neuron receives on the order of one to three thousand synaptic contacts, a synapse being the junction where one neuron passes a chemical message to the next.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thin, under one micrometre in diameter for most of its length.

Antidromic latency measurements, which time how long a spike takes to travel backwards up the axon, give conduction velocities of roughly 0.4 to 1.5 metres per second.

That is walking pace, not the 60 metres per second of a large motor nerve, so a command from the hypothalamus needs a few hundred milliseconds to reach the lower spinal cord.

The axon branches sparsely along the way and forms terminal fields that are broad rather than pinpoint, so one cell influences a whole column of spinal targets.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The fast transmitter is glutamate, the brain's main excitatory chemical, packaged by the transporter VGLUT2.

Layered on top are peptides, which are short chains of amino acids that act more slowly and more diffusely: oxytocin, vasopressin, corticotropin-releasing hormone and thyrotropin-releasing hormone are all released from preautonomic terminals.

The peptide changes the meaning of the same spike train, so oxytocin released onto vagal neurons slows the heart while oxytocin released onto medullary sympathetic neurons after a heart attack speeds cardiac sympathetic drive.

Nitric oxide, a gas made inside the neuron by the enzyme neuronal nitric oxide synthase, acts as a local brake by strengthening GABA release onto the cell.

Angiotensin II, a blood-borne hormone of the salt and water system, excites these cells through the angiotensin type 1a receptor.

MYELINATION:

Most preautonomic axons are unmyelinated or only thinly myelinated, myelin being the fatty insulating wrap that speeds conduction.

This is a design choice, not a defect: autonomic commands operate on the timescale of seconds to minutes, so speed matters far less than the metabolic saving of leaving thousands of thin axons bare.

Where thin myelin does appear it is on the longest spinal branches, which is exactly where a modest speed gain is worth the cost.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Firing rates are low, so the spiking bill is small, but the peptide bill is large.

Making oxytocin, vasopressin or corticotropin-releasing hormone requires transcribing a gene, folding a precursor protein, cutting it into the active peptide inside vesicles, and shipping those vesicles up to 35 centimetres down the axon.

Axonal transport at roughly 50 to 400 millimetres per day means a vesicle made today may not arrive at the spinal terminal for one to seven days, so the cell must plan its supply chain far in advance.

Mitochondria, the cell's power plants, cluster densely at the terminals to fund transmitter release.

PLASTICITY:

These cells remodel themselves on a timescale of days when the body's demands change.

In sustained high blood pressure and in heart failure, inhibitory GABA input weakens, nitric oxide production falls, glutamate input strengthens, and the same cell fires several times faster for the same sensory input.

This is not damage, it is a gain change, and it is largely reversible if the underlying load is removed.

Chronic salt loading increases the number of glutamate synapses on preautonomic dendrites within a week.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here, astrocytes being star-shaped support cells, do something unusual: they physically retract their processes from neurons during dehydration and other sustained challenges.

Pulling back exposes the synapses to each other's spillover glutamate, so the whole nucleus becomes more excitable as a unit.

The same astrocytes carry the transporters that clear glutamate, so their retreat both raises excitation and slows its cleanup.

Microglia, the brain's resident immune cells, are unusually reactive in this nucleus during heart failure and hypertension, releasing inflammatory signals that further increase preautonomic firing.

Tanycytes, specialised ependymal cells lining the third ventricle, sit against the medial dendrites and regulate what passes from cerebrospinal fluid into the nucleus.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Delete these cells and the paraventricular nucleus becomes an endocrine gland with no nerve wiring.

The hormone-secreting magnocellular neurons would still pour oxytocin and vasopressin into the bloodstream, but the nucleus would lose its ability to act in seconds rather than minutes.

Blood pressure would drift, the fight-or-flight response would lose its hypothalamic accelerator, the reflex rise in heart rate on standing would be blunted, and stress would still raise cortisol while failing to raise sympathetic tone.

The region would keep its chemistry and lose its immediacy.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

These neurons release peptide from their dendrites, not only from their axon terminals, so a cell can chemically talk to its neighbours without firing a single spike.

Dendritically released oxytocin diffuses through the tissue and synchronises the surrounding population, a process called volume transmission because the message travels through the volume of tissue rather than along a wire.

Nitric oxide made in the cell escapes backwards across synapses as a retrograde messenger, telling the incoming GABA terminals to release more.

Back-propagating action potentials, meaning spikes that travel backwards from the cell body into the dendrites, trigger the calcium entry that drives that dendritic release.

The cells also gate local blood flow, and their sustained activation drives astrocytes into a reactive state that changes the whole nucleus.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate drive from the nucleus of the solitary tract encoding arterial baroreceptor error, 0 to 100 hertz; glutamate drive from the subfornical organ encoding plasma sodium and angiotensin II, 0 to 40 hertz; tonic GABA conductance from the perinuclear shell, 0.5 to 5 nanosiemens; circulating angiotensin II, 5 to 100 picomolar; circulating leptin and insulin as slow gain terms.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate plus peptide onto sympathetic preganglionic neurons of the intermediolateral column, onto bulbospinal neurons of the rostral ventrolateral medulla, and onto vagal motor neurons; spike output 0 to 20 hertz.

• STATE: membrane voltage; T-type calcium channel inactivation variable; intracellular calcium; releasable peptide pool; nitric oxide concentration; synaptic gain scalar.

• PARAMETERS: resting potential minus 60 millivolts; spike threshold minus 45 millivolts; input resistance 0.5 to 1.5 gigaohms; membrane capacitance 15 to 30 picofarads; conduction velocity 0.4 to 1.5 metres per second; baseline rate 0.5 to 5 hertz.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 15 to 25 milliseconds; T-type calcium de-inactivation 100 to 300 milliseconds; peptide release and clearance 10 to 120 seconds; nitric oxide signalling 1 to 10 seconds; synaptic gain remodelling 1 to 14 days.

• FAILURE MODE: in chronic heart failure and in hypertension the GABA brake and the nitric oxide brake both weaken, the gain scalar climbs, and the cells fire tonically fast, producing sustained sympathetic overdrive, salt retention and arrhythmia; loss of the vagal branch removes the heart-slowing arm and raises resting heart rate.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert baroreceptor feedback, blood-borne angiotensin signal and local nitric oxide brake into a sympathetic drive rate for one preautonomic paraventricular neuron.
Create a number variable called baseline_rate with initial value 2.
Create a number variable called gaba_brake with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called nitric_oxide with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called gain_scalar with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called drive_trace with initial value empty list.
For each sample in sensor_stream, perform the following operations.
    Set gaba_brake to the product of nitric_oxide and the baro pressure error of sample.
    Check if gaba_brake is greater than 3.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set gaba_brake to 3.
    End the conditional block.
    Set firing_rate to the product of baseline_rate and gain_scalar.
    Set firing_rate to the sum of firing_rate and the angiotensin drive of sample.
    Set firing_rate to the difference of firing_rate and gaba_brake.
    Check if firing_rate is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing_rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if both the chronic load of sample is true and gain_scalar is less than 3.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set gain_scalar to the sum of gain_scalar and 0.001.
        Set nitric_oxide to the product of nitric_oxide and 0.999.
    End the conditional block.
    Add firing_rate to the end of drive_trace.
    Display firing_rate to the console.
End the loop.
Return drive_trace to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the model is a slow-timescale control loop with
# state that drifts over days, and readability matters more than speed here.

def preautonomic_pvn(samples, dt=0.1):
    baseline_rate = 2.0      # spikes per second with no drive
    gain_scalar = 1.0        # synaptic gain, climbs under chronic load
    no_tone = 1.0            # nitric oxide brake strength, 1.0 is healthy
    trace = []
    for s in samples:
        # GABA brake is driven by baroreceptor error and scaled by NO tone.
        gaba_brake = min(no_tone * s["baro_error"], 3.0)
        rate = baseline_rate * gain_scalar
        rate += s["angiotensin"]          # blood-borne excitation, Hz
        rate -= gaba_brake                # inhibitory shell, Hz
        rate = max(0.0, min(rate, 20.0))  # cells saturate near 20 Hz
        # Chronic load (heart failure, salt) slowly raises gain, lowers NO.
        if s["chronic_load"]:
            gain_scalar = min(gain_scalar + 0.001 * dt, 3.0)
            no_tone = max(no_tone * (1.0 - 0.001 * dt), 0.2)
        trace.append(rate)
    return trace, gain_scalar, no_tone

if __name__ == "__main__":
    healthy = [{"baro_error": 1.0, "angiotensin": 0.5,
                "chronic_load": False}] * 100
    failing = [{"baro_error": 1.0, "angiotensin": 2.5,
                "chronic_load": True}] * 100000
    h, _, _ = preautonomic_pvn(healthy)
    f, g, n = preautonomic_pvn(failing)
    print("healthy steady rate: %.2f Hz" % h[-1])
    print("failing steady rate: %.2f Hz, gain %.2f, NO tone %.2f" %
          (f[-1], g, n))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only small cell in the brain that can hold a thin, slow, unmyelinated cable 30 centimetres long down to the spinal cord, release a hormone from its dendrites into its own neighbourhood while doing so, and rebound into a burst the instant you stop inhibiting it.

CHAPTER 109. OREXIGENIC AND THERMOREGULATORY NEURONS OF THE PREOPTIC AREA, INCLUDING WARM-SENSITIVE AND COLD-SENSITIVE CELLS (Body-Heat Thermostat Neuron) (NON-COG)

Warm a single cubic millimetre of this tissue by one degree and the whole body begins to sweat, even in a freezing room.

The existing label is a committee minute stringing together two functions, a region and two cell classes in one unspeakable line.

Body-Heat names the quantity regulated, which is core temperature rather than skin temperature.

Thermostat names the mechanism exactly, because the population contains a warm-sensing majority of about thirty percent that speeds up as blood temperature rises and drives sweating and skin vessel dilation, and a cold-sensing minority of about ten percent that drives shivering and brown fat heat production, the two arms working against each other around a set value.

Neuron marks it as the cell in which fever is this thermostat deliberately turned up, since prostaglandin released during infection silences the warm-sensing cells, so the body defends a higher number and the patient feels cold.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

These neurons are the body's thermostat and part of its appetite switchboard, packed into a sheet of tissue just in front of and above the crossing of the optic nerves.

Orexigenic means appetite-promoting, from the Greek word for appetite, and thermoregulatory means temperature-controlling.

A warm-sensitive neuron is a cell whose own firing rate rises when the tissue around it gets warmer, so it is a thermometer and a controller in the same object.

A cold-sensitive neuron does the opposite, firing faster as the tissue cools, although most cold-sensitive cells achieve this indirectly by receiving inhibition from a nearby warm-sensitive cell rather than by sensing temperature themselves.

Together they compare the body's actual temperature with a target, and dispatch commands that shiver you, sweat you, redirect blood to or from the skin, burn brown fat, and change how much you want to eat.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cells are small and unremarkable to look at, with an oval or triangular body and four to seven modestly branched dendrites.

Warm-sensitive cells characteristically extend their dendrites in a flattened plane parallel to the wall of the third ventricle, so they sample a sheet of tissue rather than a sphere.

The absence of dramatic morphology is itself informative: the thermometer lives in the membrane channels, not in the shape.

Axons are thin and mostly local or short-range, running caudally into the dorsomedial hypothalamus and downward into the brainstem.

SIZE:

Cell bodies measure roughly 10 to 20 micrometres across, with warm-sensitive cells clustering at the smaller end.

Dendritic fields span about 150 to 350 micrometres in the plane parallel to the ventricle, and only 50 to 100 micrometres perpendicular to it.

Membrane capacitance, the electrical measure of surface area, is about 15 to 40 picofarads.

The longest axons, those reaching the rostral raphe pallidus in the brainstem, run roughly 3 to 5 centimetres in an adult human.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Warm-sensitive neurons, defined by a firing rate that rises by at least 0.8 spikes per second for every degree Celsius of local warming; these are about a quarter to a third of preoptic neurons tested.

• Temperature-insensitive neurons, the majority, which supply steady reference input against which the warm-sensitive rate is compared.

• Cold-sensitive neurons, a small minority of roughly 5 percent, most of which are silent partners inhibited by warm-sensitive neighbours.

• Prostaglandin EP3 receptor-expressing neurons, which are GABA-releasing and hold a constant brake on the fever pathways; prostaglandin E2 switches this brake off.

• Peptide-defined warm-responsive populations carrying pituitary adenylate cyclase-activating polypeptide, brain-derived neurotrophic factor, or the peptide QRFP.

• Galanin-expressing and leptin-receptor-expressing preoptic neurons, which link temperature control to feeding and to torpor-like energy saving.

WHERE IT LIVES:

They occupy the preoptic area of the hypothalamus, specifically the median preoptic nucleus which straddles the midline at the front wall of the third ventricle, the medial preoptic area on either side of it, the ventromedial preoptic nucleus, and the neighbouring anterior hypothalamic nucleus.

The median preoptic nucleus is an interface structure in the strictest sense: it lies in the lamina terminalis, the thin front wall of the third ventricle, sandwiched between two leaky blood-sampling organs, the subfornical organ above and the organum vasculosum below.

That position lets it read blood chemistry through its neighbours while sitting safely behind the blood-brain barrier itself.

Their axons reach the dorsomedial hypothalamic nucleus and the rostral raphe pallidus of the medulla.

NUMBER:

The preoptic area of one human hypothalamus contains on the order of 200 thousand to 500 thousand neurons, and both sides together perhaps 400 thousand to 1 million.

Applying the measured fraction of thermally classified cells, warm-sensitive neurons number roughly 100 thousand to 250 thousand and cold-sensitive neurons roughly 20 thousand to 50 thousand across both sides.

Confidence is low, because these figures combine a rough human volume estimate with electrophysiological proportions measured in rodents, and no direct human count of thermosensitive neurons exists.

The honest bound is that the thermoregulatory population is certainly larger than ten thousand and certainly smaller than one million.

Against 86 billion neurons in the whole nervous system, this is about 0.0002 percent.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Warm-sensitive neurons fire spontaneously without any input, typically 2 to 10 spikes per second at normal body temperature.

Between spikes the membrane does not sit flat; it climbs steadily upward in a depolarising prepotential, and the slope of that climb sets the firing rate.

Warming speeds the climb, so the next spike arrives sooner, which is the entire mechanism of thermosensitivity in its simplest form.

The climb is shaped by a potassium current that switches off with warmth and by warmth-gated channels including TRPM2, a channel protein that opens above about 37 degrees Celsius.

The formal criterion for calling a cell warm-sensitive is a regression coefficient of at least 0.8 spikes per second per degree Celsius, and strong examples reach 2 to 5 spikes per second per degree.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Skin temperature reaches these cells by a well-mapped relay: skin thermoreceptors to the spinal dorsal horn, then to the lateral parabrachial nucleus of the pons, then to the median preoptic nucleus.

Blood-borne signals arrive from the organum vasculosum and subfornical organ next door, and from local production of prostaglandin D2 and prostaglandin E2 in the surrounding membranes.

Outputs are mostly inhibitory: preoptic neurons release GABA onto the dorsomedial hypothalamus and the rostral raphe pallidus, holding the heat-generating machinery switched off by default.

Warm-responsive glutamate-releasing populations project onward to drive heat loss actively.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are thin, generally 0.2 to 1 micrometre across, and branch heavily near their targets.

Conduction velocity is slow, roughly 0.3 to 2 metres per second.

A single warm-sensitive neuron typically contacts both the dorsomedial hypothalamus and the raphe, so one cell can release the brake on brown fat burning and on skin blood vessel constriction together.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The fever-relevant EP3 receptor population releases GABA, so its constant firing is a constant inhibition.

Warm-responsive projection neurons are largely glutamatergic, using VGLUT2.

Peptide co-transmitters include pituitary adenylate cyclase-activating polypeptide, brain-derived neurotrophic factor, QRFP, galanin, neurotensin and cholecystokinin.

The prostaglandins are not classical transmitters but local lipid messengers: prostaglandin E2, made in the membranes around brain blood vessels during infection, binds the EP3 receptor and silences the GABA brake, which is exactly how a fever starts.

Leptin, the hormone released by fat tissue, and the appetite peptides of the melanocortin system act on the orexigenic subsets, linking body fat stores to both feeding and heat production.

MYELINATION:

These axons are almost entirely unmyelinated, and the few myelinated fibres are the longest descending ones.

That is appropriate for the physics of the job: body temperature changes over minutes, so a conduction delay of tens of milliseconds is irrelevant.

The absence of myelin also means oligodendrocytes, the myelin-making cells, play little role here, and the local white matter is sparse.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Constant pacemaking is expensive because the sodium-potassium pump must run continuously to reset the ion gradients after every spike.

At 5 spikes per second, a warm-sensitive neuron fires over 400 thousand times a day and never rests.

The cell is also physically warmed by its own neighbourhood's blood flow, and it must keep sensing accurately across the narrow 35 to 41 degree Celsius range that matters, which requires stable enzyme and channel function at exactly the temperature it is measuring.

PLASTICITY:

The set point itself is adjustable, which is unusual for a sensor.

Fever is a deliberate upward reset of the target, achieved chemically rather than by rewiring.

Heat acclimatisation over one to two weeks shifts the threshold for sweating downward, and cold acclimatisation strengthens the brown fat pathway.

In pregnancy and across the menstrual cycle, progesterone raises the set point by a few tenths of a degree, which is why basal body temperature tracks ovulation.

Long-term synaptic strengthening also occurs on the skin-temperature input pathway, letting the system learn typical environmental patterns.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here do something they do almost nowhere else: they are themselves the main source of prostaglandin D2 in this region, and their production rises with temperature.

That makes the local glia part of the sensor, not merely its support staff.

The meninges and blood vessel walls overlying the preoptic area contain the enzymes cyclooxygenase-2 and microsomal prostaglandin E synthase, which manufacture the prostaglandin E2 of fever on demand when immune signals arrive from the blood.

Microglia in this region respond quickly to circulating inflammatory signals, amplifying the fever message.

Tanycytes lining the nearby third ventricle control the passage of leptin and other hormones from blood into this tissue.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and the preoptic area becomes a sleep centre and a reproductive centre with no thermostat.

The region would still initiate non-rapid-eye-movement sleep and still house the sexually dimorphic nucleus, but the body would become effectively cold-blooded, drifting with ambient temperature.

There would be no fever during infection, no sweating in heat, no shivering in cold, and no seasonal or circadian temperature rhythm.

The flavour lost is homeostatic anticipation: the ability to act on a temperature change before it becomes dangerous.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The pacemaker mechanism means these cells compute a temperature reading in the slope of a subthreshold voltage ramp, before any spike exists, so the computation is analogue and continuous rather than digital.

They are directly modulated by lipid messengers made outside the neuron by glia and by vascular cells, which is a form of chemical computation performed by the tissue rather than the cell.

Warm-sensitive neurons release GABA tonically, meaning their default output is a brake, so the system computes by disinhibition: heat generation is produced by removing inhibition, not by adding excitation.

They participate in volume transmission through prostaglandins and peptides, and their activity shapes local blood flow, which in turn changes local temperature, creating a genuine physical feedback loop between the neuron and the tissue it measures.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: local tissue temperature, 33 to 42 degrees Celsius; skin temperature signal via the lateral parabrachial nucleus, 0 to 50 hertz; prostaglandin E2 concentration, 0 to 50 nanomolar; prostaglandin D2 from astrocytes; leptin, 1 to 50 nanograms per millilitre; glutamate and GABA synaptic input.

• OUTPUTS: tonic GABA onto the dorsomedial hypothalamic nucleus and the rostral raphe pallidus; glutamate onto heat-loss effector pathways; spike rate 0 to 30 hertz.

• STATE: membrane voltage; prepotential slope; potassium conductance; TRPM2 open fraction; current set point in degrees Celsius; acclimatisation offset.

• PARAMETERS: thermosensitivity coefficient 0.8 to 5.0 spikes per second per degree Celsius; baseline rate at 37 degrees, 2 to 10 hertz; set point 36.8 degrees Celsius with a circadian swing of about 0.5 degrees; EP3 receptor sensitivity, half-maximal effect near 10 nanomolar prostaglandin E2.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 to 40 milliseconds; prepotential ramp 100 to 500 milliseconds; prostaglandin action 30 seconds to 10 minutes; whole-body thermal response 5 to 60 minutes; acclimatisation 7 to 14 days.

• FAILURE MODE: runaway loss of the brake gives fever and, in extreme form, malignant hyperthermia or heat stroke; loss of the cells gives poikilothermia, meaning body temperature drifts with the environment, as seen after hypothalamic surgery, stroke or Wernicke encephalopathy; blunted thermosensitivity in old age is a major reason elderly people die in heat waves.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the firing rate of one warm-sensitive preoptic neuron from local temperature and to release its inhibitory brake on heat generation when the body is too warm.
Create a number variable called set_point with initial value 36.8.
Create a number variable called thermosensitivity with initial value 1.6.
Create a number variable called baseline_rate with initial value 5.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called brake_strength with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called heat_production with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called temperature_log with initial value empty list.
For each sample in thermal_stream, perform the following operations.
    Set set_point to the sum of 36.8 and the prostaglandin shift of sample.
    Set firing_rate to the difference of the core temperature of sample and set_point.
    Set firing_rate to the product of firing_rate and thermosensitivity.
    Set firing_rate to the sum of firing_rate and baseline_rate.
    Set firing_rate to the sum of firing_rate and the skin warm signal of sample.
    Check if firing_rate is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing_rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set brake_strength to the quotient of firing_rate divided by 20.
    Check if brake_strength is greater than 1.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set brake_strength to 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Set heat_production to the difference of 1 and brake_strength.
    Add heat_production to the end of temperature_log.
    Display heat_production to the console.
End the loop.
Return temperature_log to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the core idea is a simple linear sensor feeding a
# disinhibition stage, and NumPy-free arithmetic keeps the logic visible.

def warm_sensitive_neuron(core_temp, skin_warm, pge2_nM,
                          set_point=36.8, coeff=1.6, base=5.0):
    """Return (firing_rate_Hz, brake_0to1, heat_drive_0to1)."""
    # Prostaglandin E2 silences the GABA brake by raising the set point.
    # Half-maximal shift near 10 nM, saturating at about +2.5 degrees C.
    shift = 2.5 * pge2_nM / (10.0 + pge2_nM)
    effective_set_point = set_point + shift
    rate = base + coeff * (core_temp - effective_set_point) + skin_warm
    rate = max(0.0, min(rate, 30.0))     # cells saturate near 30 Hz
    brake = min(rate / 20.0, 1.0)        # tonic GABA onto DMH and rRPa
    heat_drive = 1.0 - brake             # brown fat and vasoconstriction
    return rate, brake, heat_drive

if __name__ == "__main__":
    cases = [("cold room, healthy", 36.4, 0.0, 0.0),
             ("hot room, healthy",  37.6, 3.0, 0.0),
             ("infection, 37.0 C",  37.0, 0.0, 20.0)]
    for label, t, skin, pge2 in cases:
        r, b, h = warm_sensitive_neuron(t, skin, pge2)
        print("%-20s rate %5.2f Hz  brake %.2f  heat drive %.2f"
              % (label, r, b, h))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other neuron in the body reads its own temperature in the slope of the voltage ramp between two spikes, holds the furnace switched off by firing rather than by falling silent, and can have its target value moved by two degrees in twenty minutes by a fat molecule made in the wall of a nearby blood vessel.

CHAPTER 110. MAMMILLARY BODY NEURONS (Memory-Circuit Relay Neuron) (COG)

Two pea-sized bumps on the underside of the brain, so distinct that anatomists named them after breasts, are the reason a bottle of vodka a day can erase the ability to form new memories.

The old name is an address borrowed from a shape resemblance to small breasts and says nothing about function.

Memory-Circuit names the stake, since destroying these cells or cutting their single output tract produces a lasting amnesia, and their shrinkage is the classic finding in Korsakoff syndrome after thiamine deficiency.

Relay names the position, because these cells receive the output of the hippocampal formation through the fornix and pass it up the mammillothalamic tract to the anterior thalamus, forming one link of the loop that carries autobiographical memory.

Neuron marks it as a cell that is not a passive wire, since a large proportion of its spikes lock to the hippocampal theta rhythm and its firing tracks running speed and head-turning speed.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The mammillary bodies are a pair of small round nuclei at the back floor of the hypothalamus, and their neurons are relay and rhythm cells for memory.

They receive an enormous bundle of fibres from the hippocampus, the seahorse-shaped structure that builds new memories, and they pass that information onward to the thalamus, the brain's central switchboard.

Calling them a relay undersells them, because they do not simply copy their input.

They impose a rhythm on it, they sharpen the sense of which direction the head is facing, and they hold information across the seconds-long gap between seeing something and needing to act on it.

WHAT THEY DO NOT DO, despite their location, is any hormonal or autonomic work; they are a cognitive island inside an otherwise visceral hypothalamus.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The neurons are packed densely and evenly, with round or oval cell bodies and four to eight dendrites radiating outward like a starburst.

Dendrites are largely smooth, meaning they carry few of the small mushroom-shaped spines that most excitatory neurons use to receive contacts, so incoming fibres synapse directly onto the dendritic shaft.

The defining structural feature is the axon, which leaves the cell body and almost immediately splits in two: one branch turns upward as the mammillothalamic tract, and the other turns backward and downward as the mammillotegmental tract.

Both branches carry the same spike train, so every mammillary neuron speaks to the thalamus and the brainstem simultaneously and identically.

SIZE:

Cell bodies range from about 12 to 25 micrometres across, with medial mammillary neurons at the smaller end and lateral mammillary neurons somewhat larger.

Dendritic fields span 200 to 400 micrometres, and because packing density is high, neighbouring dendritic trees overlap heavily.

The mammillothalamic tract branch runs roughly 1 to 2 centimetres in an adult human to reach the anterior thalamic nuclei.

The whole mammillary body is only about 4 to 5 millimetres in diameter, small enough that its shrinkage on a magnetic resonance imaging scan is a recognised radiological sign.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Medial mammillary nucleus neurons, the large majority, which project to the anteromedial and anteroventral thalamic nuclei and carry theta-rhythm and spatial-context information.

• Lateral mammillary nucleus neurons, a small minority, which project to the anterodorsal thalamic nucleus and carry head direction information, meaning a signal that reports which compass bearing the head is pointing.

• Within the medial nucleus, calbindin-expressing neurons and parvalbumin-expressing neurons occupy complementary territories and form segregated subcircuits; work published in 2025 showed that activating the calbindin cells, but not the parvalbumin cells, produces place aversion and increased movement.

• A pars medialis and pars lateralis division of the medial nucleus maps onto the front-to-back topography of its subicular input, so position along the hippocampus is preserved as position within the mammillary body.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The mammillary bodies sit in the posterior hypothalamus, on the ventral surface of the brain, immediately behind the tuber cinereum and immediately in front of the interpeduncular fossa between the two cerebral peduncles.

The two nuclei are the medial mammillary nucleus and the lateral mammillary nucleus, with a small intercalated nucleus between them.

They are an interface structure by definition: they mark the junction of the hypothalamus with the limbic memory system, receiving the fornix, which is the great fibre bundle leaving the hippocampus, and feeding the anterior thalamus.

They also sit at the interface between forebrain and midbrain, since the mammillotegmental tract runs straight into the ventral and dorsal tegmental nuclei of Gudden in the brainstem.

NUMBER:

The two mammillary bodies together hold about 800 thousand neurons in the adult human.

The medial mammillary nucleus accounts for the great majority, roughly 600 thousand to 750 thousand, with the lateral mammillary nucleus holding perhaps 40 thousand to 80 thousand.

Confidence in the total is moderate, since the mammillary bodies are among the few hypothalamic structures with published human stereological cell counts; confidence in the medial-to-lateral split is lower and is inferred from relative volumes.

This is roughly 8 percent of all hypothalamic neurons and about 0.0009 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the whole nervous system.

In Korsakoff syndrome the medial mammillary nucleus loses a substantial fraction of these neurons and shrinks visibly.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Almost every neuron in the medial mammillary nucleus modulates its firing at theta frequency, meaning it rides a 4 to 12 hertz oscillation generated in the hippocampal system.

In an awake, moving animal, firing rates are high for a hypothalamic cell, commonly 10 to 40 spikes per second, and they climb during exploration.

The cells show a prominent hyperpolarisation-activated current, a slow inward current that switches on when the cell is pushed negative and pulls it back up, which makes them natural rhythm followers and gives them a strong rebound after inhibition.

Resting potential is around minus 60 to minus 65 millivolts and spikes are brief, under one millisecond at half height, consistent with fast, reliable relay.

Lateral mammillary neurons additionally show firing that depends on angular head velocity, meaning how fast the head is turning.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The dominant input is the postcommissural fornix, an excitatory glutamate pathway from the subiculum, which is the output gate of the hippocampus.

A second major input is inhibitory and comes from the ventral and dorsal tegmental nuclei of Gudden in the brainstem, forming a closed loop, since mammillary axons run back down to those same nuclei.

Outputs go to the anterior thalamic nuclei via the mammillothalamic tract and back to Gudden's nuclei via the mammillotegmental tract.

There are essentially no long-range outputs to anywhere else, which is remarkable: this is a structure with one great input and two destinations.

Within the nucleus, local inhibitory interneurons are sparse, so most inhibition arrives from outside.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The mammillothalamic tract, also called the bundle of Vicq d'Azyr, is a compact, heavily myelinated white matter bundle visible to the naked eye in a dissected brain.

Individual axons are 1 to 3 micrometres in diameter with myelin, giving conduction velocities of roughly 5 to 20 metres per second.

The collateral to the tegmentum is thinner and slower.

Because the branch point is close to the cell body, the two targets receive spikes within a few milliseconds of each other, effectively simultaneously.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Mammillary body neurons are glutamatergic, releasing glutamate, the brain's main excitatory chemical, at both of their targets.

They express the vesicular glutamate transporter VGLUT2.

The two main molecular subtypes are marked by calcium-binding proteins, calbindin and parvalbumin, which buffer calcium inside the cell and are used by scientists as labels rather than being transmitters themselves.

The inhibitory input from Gudden's nuclei uses GABA.

Cholinergic and histaminergic fibres, meaning fibres releasing acetylcholine and histamine, arrive from the brainstem and posterior hypothalamus and set the overall arousal gain.

MYELINATION:

This is one of the most heavily myelinated outputs in the entire hypothalamus, in sharp contrast to almost every other cell type in the region.

Oligodendrocytes, the cells that wrap axons in myelin, are abundant along the mammillothalamic tract.

The reason is timing: a head direction signal or a theta phase code is only useful if it arrives at the thalamus with millisecond precision, so the mammillary bodies pay for speed where the rest of the hypothalamus does not.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

These cells are the most metabolically vulnerable neurons in the hypothalamus and possibly one of the most vulnerable populations in the brain.

Sustained firing at 20 to 40 spikes per second requires continuous oxidative metabolism, and the key enzymes involved, including transketolase and alpha-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase, all require thiamine, which is vitamin B1, as a helper molecule.

The human body stores only about two to three weeks of thiamine.

When thiamine runs out, in chronic alcohol misuse, prolonged vomiting, bariatric surgery or starvation, the mammillary bodies are among the very first structures to develop visible haemorrhagic damage, precisely because their energy demand is so high and so constant.

PLASTICITY:

Synapses from the subiculum onto mammillary neurons can be strengthened and weakened in the ordinary way, and theta-patterned stimulation is particularly effective at strengthening them, which fits their rhythm.

The structure is not, however, a major site of experience-dependent rewiring in adulthood; it behaves more like a precisely tuned instrument than a learning surface.

Recovery after damage is poor, and the mammillothalamic tract shows little capacity to regrow, which is why Korsakoff amnesia is usually permanent.

Mammillary neurons also atrophy in a graded way after loss of their hippocampal input, so upstream damage causes downstream shrinkage over months.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes here are unusually rich in thiamine-dependent transketolase and in the thiamine transporter, and in thiamine deficiency it is the astrocytes that fail first, losing their glutamate transporters.

That failure lets glutamate accumulate outside the neurons and kill them by overexcitation, so the glia are the proximate cause of death, not merely bystanders.

The mammillary bodies also have an exceptionally dense capillary network, and it is the endothelium of those capillaries that breaks down to produce the small haemorrhages that pathologists find at autopsy in Wernicke encephalopathy.

Oligodendrocytes are abundant, as noted, and their myelin is also thiamine-sensitive.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these neurons and the hypothalamus loses its only cognitive voice.

The rest of the hypothalamus would still run temperature, hunger, thirst, hormones and sleep, and none of that would change.

What would vanish is the ability to lay down new episodic memories, meaning memories of specific events with a time and place attached, and the ability to keep a stable internal compass while navigating.

The affected person can still hold a conversation, still knows who they are, still remembers their childhood, and yet cannot remember the conversation five minutes later.

The mammillary bodies give the hypothalamus a role in the story of a life rather than only in the maintenance of a body.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

These cells do phase coding, meaning the information is carried not only in how fast they fire but in exactly when in the theta cycle they fire, so the same rate can mean different things at different phases.

Axon collateralisation is itself a computation: by broadcasting an identical spike train to the thalamus and to a brainstem nucleus that inhibits it back, the cell builds a delayed negative feedback loop that helps generate the rhythm it is riding.

Work published in 2025 shows the two molecular subtypes participate in different temporal windows of working memory maintenance, so the population divides labour in time.

They are strongly modulated by arousal transmitters, which effectively multiplies their gain according to how awake the animal is.

Their unusual dependence on thiamine also means their transcriptional and metabolic state, not just their wiring, is functionally load-bearing.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate from the subiculum via the postcommissural fornix, 0 to 60 hertz, carrying spatial and contextual information with theta phase; GABA from the ventral and dorsal tegmental nuclei of Gudden, 0 to 40 hertz; angular head velocity signal to the lateral nucleus; acetylcholine and histamine as arousal gain terms; thiamine availability as a metabolic scalar from 0 to 1.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate to the anteromedial, anteroventral and anterodorsal thalamic nuclei via the mammillothalamic tract; glutamate to Gudden's tegmental nuclei via the mammillotegmental tract; spike output 0 to 60 hertz, theta phase locked.

• STATE: membrane voltage; hyperpolarisation-activated current activation; theta phase estimate in radians; head direction estimate in degrees; working memory content held over seconds; thiamine-dependent energy reserve.

• PARAMETERS: resting potential minus 62 millivolts; spike width under 1 millisecond; baseline rate 10 to 40 hertz awake; theta frequency 4 to 12 hertz; conduction velocity 5 to 20 metres per second; population 800 thousand across both bodies.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; hyperpolarisation-activated current 100 to 400 milliseconds; theta cycle 80 to 250 milliseconds; working memory maintenance 2 to 20 seconds; thiamine depletion 14 to 21 days.

• FAILURE MODE: thiamine depletion causes Wernicke encephalopathy, with confusion, eye movement paralysis and unsteady gait, progressing to Korsakoff syndrome with dense anterograde amnesia and confabulation; fornix or mammillothalamic tract section produces the same memory failure without the metabolic crisis; lateral nucleus loss degrades the head direction compass and causes disorientation.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to relay hippocampal input to the anterior thalamus while locking the output to the theta rhythm and while tracking the thiamine reserve that keeps the cell alive.
Create a number variable called theta_phase with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called theta_step with initial value 0.4.
Create a number variable called preferred_phase with initial value 1.6.
Create a number variable called thiamine_reserve with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called phase_gate with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called thalamic_output with initial value empty list.
For each sample in subicular_stream, perform the following operations.
    Set theta_phase to the sum of theta_phase and theta_step.
    Check if theta_phase is greater than 6.28.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set theta_phase to the difference of theta_phase and 6.28.
    End the conditional block.
    Set phase_gate to the difference of theta_phase and preferred_phase.
    Check if phase_gate is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set phase_gate to the product of phase_gate and -1.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if phase_gate is less than 1.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing_rate to the product of the subicular drive of sample and thiamine_reserve.
        Set firing_rate to the difference of firing_rate and the gudden inhibition of sample.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set firing_rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if firing_rate is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing_rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set thiamine_reserve to the difference of thiamine_reserve and the thiamine drain of sample.
    Check if thiamine_reserve is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set thiamine_reserve to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Add firing_rate to the end of thalamic_output.
End the loop.
Return thalamic_output to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the essential behaviour is phase-gated relay plus a
# slowly draining metabolic reserve, both of which read clearly as plain loops.

import math

def mammillary_neuron(steps, subicular_drive, gudden_inhib,
                      thiamine_drain=0.0, theta_hz=8.0, dt=0.005):
    """Relay subicular drive to thalamus, gated by theta phase."""
    phase = 0.0
    preferred = 1.6          # radians; cell prefers a fixed theta phase
    reserve = 1.0            # thiamine-dependent energy reserve, 1.0 = full
    out = []
    for _ in range(steps):
        phase = (phase + 2 * math.pi * theta_hz * dt) % (2 * math.pi)
        # von Mises style phase gate: sharp preference for one theta phase.
        gate = math.exp(2.0 * math.cos(phase - preferred) - 2.0)
        rate = subicular_drive * reserve * gate - gudden_inhib
        rate = max(0.0, min(rate, 60.0))
        reserve = max(0.0, reserve - thiamine_drain * dt)
        out.append(rate)
    return out, reserve

if __name__ == "__main__":
    healthy, r1 = mammillary_neuron(2000, 40.0, 5.0, thiamine_drain=0.0)
    starved, r2 = mammillary_neuron(2000, 40.0, 5.0, thiamine_drain=0.09)
    print("healthy peak %.1f Hz, mean %.1f Hz, reserve %.2f"
          % (max(healthy), sum(healthy) / len(healthy), r1))
    print("starved peak %.1f Hz, mean %.1f Hz, reserve %.2f"
          % (max(starved), sum(starved) / len(starved), r2))

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron in the hypothalamus that fires in time with the hippocampal theta rhythm, sends one identical heavily myelinated message to both the thalamus and the midbrain, cares nothing for hormones or body temperature, and will die within three weeks of a vitamin running out.

CHAPTER 111. BLOOD-SAMPLING THIRST NEURON (NON-COG) (PUC)

There is a small window in the wall of your brain where the blood-brain barrier simply does not exist, and a few thousand naked neurons lean out of it and taste your blood directly.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is a Previously Unnamed Construct, meaning a functional cell type that the scientific literature describes in pieces under several different labels but has never named as one thing.

We name it here as the blood-sampling thirst neuron: an excitatory neuron that sits inside a circumventricular organ, meaning a small region of brain where the usual protective barrier between blood and brain tissue is deliberately absent, and whose job is to measure how salty and how depleted your blood is and turn that measurement into the conscious feeling of thirst.

The blood-brain barrier is a tight seal made of blood vessel walls that keeps most blood-borne molecules out of brain tissue; without it, brain function would be at the mercy of every meal.

These particular neurons trade that protection for information.

They are the reason you feel thirsty within about a minute of eating something salty, long before the salt could possibly have changed the water content of your cells.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is small and round or fusiform, meaning spindle-shaped, with two to five sparse dendrites.

The morphological signature is the relationship with blood vessels: dendrites and cell bodies press directly against a wide, leaky perivascular space, the fluid-filled sleeve around a capillary, so plasma-derived fluid bathes the membrane.

Some cells extend a short process to the ventricular surface, sampling cerebrospinal fluid as well as blood.

The cells sit in a dense, glia-rich neuropil with unusually little myelin, so the tissue looks pale and grainy under the microscope compared with surrounding brain.

SIZE:

Cell bodies are among the smallest in the hypothalamic region, roughly 8 to 15 micrometres across.

Dendritic fields are short, typically 50 to 150 micrometres, because the whole organ they live in is only about 0.5 to 1.5 millimetres across in a human.

Axons are correspondingly short, running 2 to 12 millimetres to their targets in the median preoptic nucleus, the supraoptic nucleus and the paraventricular nucleus.

Membrane capacitance is low, around 8 to 20 picofarads, which is part of why a small current changes their firing so much.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Subfornical organ excitatory neurons expressing the angiotensin type 1a receptor, which drive water drinking; these mostly also express neuronal nitric oxide synthase.

• Subfornical organ excitatory neurons of a separate population that drive salt appetite rather than water appetite; the two populations are anatomically intermingled but functionally opposite.

• Subfornical organ inhibitory neurons, which release GABA and actively suppress thirst when the body is already well hydrated.

• Organum vasculosum of the lamina terminalis neurons, which are the most strongly intrinsically osmosensitive of the group, meaning they detect saltiness in their own membrane rather than being told about it.

• Median preoptic nucleus integrator neurons, which are behind the barrier and combine the two sensor readings with signals from the mouth and gut.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The population is distributed across the lamina terminalis, the thin front wall of the third ventricle, in three linked structures.

The subfornical organ sits at the roof of the third ventricle just beneath the fornix, at the level of the interventricular foramen.

The organum vasculosum of the lamina terminalis sits at the bottom of the same wall, just above the optic chiasm.

The median preoptic nucleus lies between them.

This is an interface in the most literal anatomical sense: the subfornical organ and organum vasculosum straddle the boundary between the bloodstream, the cerebrospinal fluid of the third ventricle, and the protected brain tissue behind them, and they belong fully to none of the three.

Their axons cross into protected brain to reach the supraoptic nucleus, the paraventricular nucleus, the median preoptic nucleus, the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis and the ventral tegmental area.

NUMBER:

The human subfornical organ and organum vasculosum are tiny, together occupying only a few cubic millimetres.

The best available estimate places the combined neuron population at roughly 5 thousand to 30 thousand across both organs.

Confidence is low: this is a bound derived from human volume measurements combined with rodent packing densities, and no unbiased stereological count of human subfornical organ neurons has been published, so any single figure quoted with more precision than this should be treated with suspicion.

The defensible statement is that the population is larger than one thousand and smaller than one hundred thousand.

That places it at roughly 0.00002 percent of the nervous system's 86 billion neurons, which means the entire conscious sensation of thirst is generated by fewer neurons than sit in one cubic millimetre of cerebral cortex.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These neurons are intrinsically osmosensitive, meaning that if you shrink the cell by putting it in saltier fluid, its own membrane opens stretch-sensitive channels and it depolarises without any synaptic input at all.

The molecular osmosensor identified for this job is the channel TMEM63B.

Firing rate scales with plasma osmolality across the physiological range, climbing from roughly 1 to 3 spikes per second at 285 milliosmoles per kilogram to 10 to 20 spikes per second at 305 milliosmoles per kilogram.

Angiotensin II, acting on the angiotensin type 1a receptor, depolarises them further, adding a second, hormonal dimension to the same output.

Nitric oxide inhibits them through cyclic guanosine monophosphate, and in one classic study 96 percent of responsive subfornical organ neurons were silenced by a nitric oxide donor, making this one of the most reliable inhibitory actions recorded anywhere.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are unusual because the most important ones are not synaptic at all: angiotensin II, relaxin, sodium concentration, osmolality, and hormones such as leptin, ghrelin and amylin arrive through the blood.

Synaptic inputs do exist, arriving from the median preoptic nucleus and from ascending pathways carrying gut and oropharyngeal information, meaning signals from the mouth and throat that report swallowing.

Outputs are glutamatergic onto the median preoptic nucleus, the supraoptic and paraventricular nuclei where vasopressin is made, and onward to the bed nucleus of the stria terminalis and the ventral tegmental area, the latter connection recruiting the dopamine reward system so that drinking feels good.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are thin, 0.2 to 0.8 micrometres, unmyelinated, and short.

Conduction velocity is roughly 0.3 to 1 metre per second, so a signal reaches the supraoptic nucleus in tens of milliseconds.

They branch to contact multiple downstream nuclei, so a single sensor neuron can simultaneously trigger vasopressin release, thirst motivation and reward anticipation.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The thirst-driving neurons are glutamatergic.

Many co-express neuronal nitric oxide synthase, so they both make and are inhibited by nitric oxide, giving a built-in self-limiting brake.

The thirst-suppressing neurons are GABAergic.

Receptors that matter functionally include the angiotensin type 1a receptor, the relaxin family peptide receptor 3, the secretin receptor, the leptin receptor and the amylin receptor.

The glial protein Nax, a sodium-concentration-sensing channel expressed in the surrounding glia rather than in the neurons, sets how the tissue as a whole reads sodium, and autoantibodies against Nax in humans cause a disease of absent thirst.

MYELINATION:

Essentially none.

The circumventricular organs are among the least myelinated grey matter in the brain, which is one of the features that lets pathologists recognise them.

Because the pathway is short and the physiological event, drinking, unfolds over minutes, there is no advantage to insulation, and the absence of myelin also keeps the tissue permeable to the diffusing signals it exists to sense.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Modest in absolute terms, since the cells are small and fire slowly most of the time.

The unusual cost is chemical defence: living outside the blood-brain barrier means being continuously exposed to circulating toxins, immune cells and antibodies, so these neurons and their glia invest heavily in detoxification and in efflux transporters that pump unwanted molecules back out.

During dehydration, firing rates rise several-fold for hours or days, and local blood flow increases accordingly.

PLASTICITY:

The osmotic set point can be reset, and it is reset routinely in pregnancy, when the threshold for thirst and for vasopressin release falls by about 5 to 10 milliosmoles per kilogram, which is why pregnant women run a slightly lower blood sodium.

Chronic dehydration and chronic heart failure both shift the angiotensin sensitivity of these neurons upward.

Structural plasticity occurs too: synaptic and glial coverage of the cells changes measurably within hours of a salt load, a remodelling shared with their downstream partners in the supraoptic nucleus.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glia here are genuinely different from glia elsewhere and are part of the sensor, not merely its scaffolding.

Specialised ependymal cells and astrocytes express Nax, the sodium-level-sensing channel, and it is these glia, not the neurons, that perform the primary reading of blood sodium; they then pass the message to neurons by releasing lactate, a small metabolic molecule that changes neuronal firing.

Tanycytes form a tight barrier on the brain side of these organs, sealing the leak so that blood-borne molecules do not spread into the rest of the brain, which means the glia define the very window that makes the organ possible.

Microglia here are exposed to the circulation permanently and are constitutively more active than elsewhere.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these cells and the lamina terminalis becomes a wall with a window that nobody looks through.

The body would still make vasopressin and would still respond to a drop in blood volume through the kidney, but the conscious urge to drink would be gone, and with it the ability to correct a rising blood sodium by behaviour.

This is not hypothetical: patients with autoantibodies against Nax or with tumours destroying the subfornical organ develop adipsic hypernatremia, meaning chronically high blood sodium with no thirst at all, and they must be told to drink by a clock because they will otherwise dehydrate to the point of coma without ever feeling anything is wrong.

The flavour these neurons give the region is felt need.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The primary transduction is performed by glia and handed to neurons metabolically through lactate, so the computation is genuinely distributed across cell types.

The neurons are directly hormone-gated by molecules in the blood, which means part of their input arrives without any presynaptic partner existing.

Nitric oxide made by the neurons themselves diffuses locally and inhibits neighbours, which is volume transmission acting as automatic gain control.

Their output to the ventral tegmental area recruits phasic dopamine signalling, so the same cells that generate a deficit signal also generate the anticipation of its relief, and drinking becomes rewarding before a single molecule of water has been absorbed.

Perhaps most striking, their signal is switched off by the act of swallowing itself, within seconds and before blood sodium has changed at all, which means the system runs a predictive feedforward model of what drinking will accomplish.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: plasma osmolality, 270 to 330 milliosmoles per kilogram; plasma sodium, 130 to 160 millimoles per litre; circulating angiotensin II, 5 to 100 picomolar; relaxin, leptin, ghrelin and amylin as slower modulators; oropharyngeal swallow signal, a fast negative pulse; nitric oxide concentration as a local brake.

• OUTPUTS: glutamate to the median preoptic nucleus, supraoptic nucleus, paraventricular nucleus, bed nucleus of the stria terminalis and ventral tegmental area; spike output 0 to 25 hertz; behavioural correlate is a scalar thirst drive from 0 to 1.

• STATE: membrane voltage; TMEM63B open fraction; glial lactate signal; osmotic set point in milliosmoles per kilogram; recent-swallow trace; angiotensin sensitivity gain.

• PARAMETERS: set point 285 milliosmoles per kilogram, falling by 5 to 10 in pregnancy; osmotic gain about 0.5 to 1.0 hertz per milliosmole per kilogram; angiotensin gain about 0.1 hertz per picomolar; input resistance 1 to 3 gigaohms; capacitance 8 to 20 picofarads.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 20 to 40 milliseconds; osmosensor 1 to 10 seconds; swallow-triggered suppression 1 to 60 seconds; hormonal modulation 1 to 30 minutes; set point resetting in pregnancy, weeks.

• FAILURE MODE: destruction or autoimmune attack causes adipsic hypernatremia, with chronic dangerous elevation of blood sodium and complete absence of thirst; over-activity contributes to the excessive drinking and salt appetite of heart failure and of some psychiatric conditions; failure of the swallow-triggered brake produces overdrinking and dangerously low blood sodium.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert plasma osmolality, circulating angiotensin and recent swallowing into a thirst drive signal for one blood-sampling thirst neuron.
Create a number variable called set_point with initial value 285.
Create a number variable called osmotic_gain with initial value 0.7.
Create a number variable called angiotensin_gain with initial value 0.1.
Create a number variable called swallow_brake with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called thirst_drive with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called thirst_log with initial value empty list.
For each sample in plasma_stream, perform the following operations.
    Set firing_rate to the difference of the osmolality of sample and set_point.
    Set firing_rate to the product of firing_rate and osmotic_gain.
    Set firing_rate to the sum of firing_rate and the product of the angiotensin of sample and angiotensin_gain.
    Check if the swallow signal of sample is true.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set swallow_brake to 8.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set swallow_brake to the product of swallow_brake and 0.97.
    End the conditional block.
    Set firing_rate to the difference of firing_rate and swallow_brake.
    Set firing_rate to the difference of firing_rate and the nitric oxide of sample.
    Check if firing_rate is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing_rate to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if firing_rate is greater than 25.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set firing_rate to 25.
    End the conditional block.
    Set thirst_drive to the quotient of firing_rate divided by 25.
    Add thirst_drive to the end of thirst_log.
    Display thirst_drive to the console.
End the loop.
Return thirst_log to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the interesting part is a feedforward swallow brake
# decaying over seconds, which is clearest as an explicit stateful loop.

def thirst_neuron(samples, dt=0.1, set_point=285.0):
    """samples: dicts with osmolality (mOsm/kg), angII (pM), swallow (bool)."""
    osmotic_gain = 0.7        # Hz per mOsm/kg above set point
    ang_gain = 0.10           # Hz per pM angiotensin II
    brake = 0.0               # oropharyngeal swallow signal, Hz subtracted
    tau_brake = 5.0           # seconds; predictive brake decays over ~5 s
    log = []
    for s in samples:
        rate = osmotic_gain * (s["osmolality"] - set_point)
        rate += ang_gain * s["angII"]
        if s["swallow"]:
            brake = 8.0       # swallowing suppresses thirst before absorption
        else:
            brake *= (1.0 - dt / tau_brake)
        rate -= brake
        rate -= s.get("no_tone", 0.0)      # nitric oxide self-limiting brake
        rate = max(0.0, min(rate, 25.0))
        log.append(rate / 25.0)            # normalised thirst drive, 0 to 1
    return log

if __name__ == "__main__":
    dehydrated = [{"osmolality": 305.0, "angII": 40.0, "swallow": False}] * 20
    drinking = [{"osmolality": 305.0, "angII": 40.0, "swallow": True}] * 5
    after = [{"osmolality": 304.0, "angII": 35.0, "swallow": False}] * 100
    out = thirst_neuron(dehydrated + drinking + after)
    print("thirsty      %.2f" % out[19])
    print("mid-swallow  %.2f" % out[24])
    print("10 s later   %.2f" % out[124])

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only neuron in the human brain that sits outside the blood-brain barrier on purpose, has its primary measurement taken for it by the glial cell next door and delivered as lactate, and is switched off by the act of swallowing seconds before the water it wanted has reached the blood.

CHAPTER 112. PUSH-AND-PULL NOVELTY NEURON (COG) (PUC)

One neuron, one axon, one message, and at the far end it hits the accelerator and the brake in the same thousandth of a second.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

This is a Previously Unnamed Construct, meaning a real and well-documented cell type that the literature has never given a single working name.

We name it here as the push-and-pull novelty neuron: a cell of the supramammillary nucleus, a thin sheet of neurons draped over the top of the mammillary bodies, which releases two opposite chemicals from the very same nerve ending.

Glutamate is the brain's main excitatory chemical, the push, and GABA is the brain's main calming chemical, the pull.

Releasing both together is rare and, on the face of it, absurd, because the two effects partly cancel.

The purpose becomes clear when you see what these cells signal: novelty, arousal and the beginning of movement.

When you walk into a room you have never seen before, these cells fire, and they hand the memory-forming machinery of the hippocampus a message that says, simultaneously, pay attention now and keep the noise down.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cells are medium-sized with a triangular or multipolar cell body, meaning several processes leave it in different directions.

Three to seven dendrites radiate outward and stay mostly within the thin plate of the nucleus, so the population is wide and flat rather than globular.

The axon ascends in a long arc through the medial forebrain bundle and the fornix system to reach the hippocampal formation, branching profusely at its destination.

At the terminal, the extraordinary feature is invisible without an electron microscope: single boutons, the swellings where transmitter is released, contain both round vesicles typical of excitation and flattened vesicles typical of inhibition, and they carry both the glutamate transporter VGLUT2 and the GABA transporter VGAT.

SIZE:

Cell bodies measure roughly 15 to 25 micrometres across.

Dendritic fields span 250 to 500 micrometres within the plane of the nucleus.

The ascending axon in a human brain runs approximately 4 to 7 centimetres from the posterior hypothalamus to the dentate gyrus and area CA2 of the hippocampus.

A single axon can form several thousand terminal boutons, so one neuron addresses a wide swathe of hippocampal tissue at once.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

• Dual-transporter neurons expressing both VGLUT2 and VGAT, the true push-and-pull cells, which project to the dentate gyrus and to area CA2 of the hippocampus.

• Purely glutamatergic supramammillary neurons, which project to the medial septum and are a key node of the arousal system that wakes the cortex.

• Purely GABAergic supramammillary neurons, which project locally and to the septum and help set the theta rhythm.

• Substance P-expressing and thyrotropin-releasing hormone-expressing subsets, which overlap the above and add slow peptide modulation.

• A lateral and a medial division, with the lateral division biased toward the dentate gyrus and the medial division toward CA2 and the septum.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The supramammillary nucleus occupies the caudal, meaning rearmost, hypothalamus, forming a thin sheet immediately above and around the mammillary bodies and extending laterally on both sides of the midline.

It borders the posterior hypothalamic area above, the mammillary bodies below, and the ventral tegmental area behind.

This is a genuine interface region: the supramammillary nucleus is the anatomical seam where the hypothalamus, the midbrain arousal system and the limbic memory system meet, and it is regularly assigned to different systems by different textbooks for exactly that reason.

Its axons terminate in the dentate gyrus, area CA2, the medial septum and the diagonal band of Broca, and it receives from the ventral tegmental area, the raphe nuclei, the septum and the lateral hypothalamus.

NUMBER:

The human supramammillary nucleus is small, and the best available estimate places its neuron population at roughly 30 thousand to 80 thousand across both sides.

Of these, the dual-transporter push-and-pull subtype is probably the largest single class, perhaps 15 thousand to 40 thousand neurons.

Confidence is low, since these figures are scaled from rodent counts using human-to-rodent volume ratios, and there is no published unbiased stereological count of the human supramammillary nucleus; the honest bound is more than ten thousand and fewer than two hundred thousand.

That is roughly 0.5 percent of the hypothalamus's approximately 10 million neurons and about 0.00006 percent of the nervous system's roughly 86 billion.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

These neurons fire spontaneously and fast for hypothalamic cells, often 5 to 25 spikes per second at rest, rising sharply to 30 to 60 spikes per second on entering a novel environment.

Many are theta-rhythmic, firing in step with the 4 to 12 hertz hippocampal rhythm, and a subset fires at every theta cycle with remarkable reliability.

They show short spike durations and modest spike frequency adaptation, meaning they can sustain a high rate without fading, which suits a signal that must persist for the whole duration of an exploratory bout.

Resting potential is around minus 55 to minus 62 millivolts, and many cells sit close enough to threshold that they need no synaptic input to fire at all.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs arrive from the ventral tegmental area carrying reward and salience information, from the raphe nuclei carrying serotonin, from the medial septum, from the lateral hypothalamus and orexin neurons carrying arousal, and from the ventral hippocampus itself, closing a loop.

Outputs contact dentate granule cells, the small densely packed neurons that form the input stage of the hippocampus, and they also contact the inhibitory interneurons among them.

That double targeting is the second half of the push-and-pull trick: the cell excites the principal neuron directly while also driving the local inhibitory network, sharpening which granule cells win.

Despite the co-released GABA, the measured net effect on granule cells is excitatory, and supramammillary input can convert a subthreshold cortical input into a fired spike when the two arrive together.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

Axons are thin, roughly 0.3 to 1 micrometre, lightly myelinated at best.

Conduction velocity is estimated at 0.5 to 3 metres per second, so the hypothalamus-to-hippocampus delay is on the order of 20 to 100 milliseconds, comfortably within a single theta cycle.

The terminal arbor is very large relative to the parent axon, a broadcast architecture rather than a point-to-point one.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate and GABA, released from the same terminal, packaged by VGLUT2 and VGAT in the same or in adjacent vesicles.

The two components behave differently under repeated firing, which is the key to the design: glutamate release depresses in a frequency-dependent way while GABA release depresses steadily regardless of frequency, so the ratio of push to pull changes with how fast the cell is firing.

At low firing rates the synapse is relatively balanced, and at high firing rates it becomes net excitatory, so the cell acts as a frequency-dependent filter that only lets strong novelty signals through as excitation.

Peptide co-transmitters in subsets include substance P and thyrotropin-releasing hormone.

Serotonin and dopamine from the brainstem modulate the cells rather than being released by them.

MYELINATION:

Light and patchy.

Some ascending fibres pick up thin myelin as they run through the medial forebrain bundle, and the terminal branches are bare.

The timing requirement here is only that the message land within the right theta cycle, a window of roughly 100 milliseconds, which thin myelin comfortably achieves without the metabolic expense of heavy insulation.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

High for a hypothalamic neuron, because of sustained fast firing combined with a very large terminal field.

Maintaining two separate vesicle-filling machineries in the same terminal doubles part of the housekeeping cost, since the cell must run both the glutamate transporter and the GABA transporter, each with its own proton gradient requirement, and must synthesise GABA using the enzyme glutamic acid decarboxylase from the very glutamate it also packages as an excitatory signal.

Mitochondria are densely packed in the terminal boutons.

PLASTICITY:

The push-and-pull ratio is itself plastic and is a likely substrate for state-dependent learning.

Repetitive activation shifts the balance toward inhibition within seconds through short-term depression, and this is reversible within a second of rest.

Longer-term changes follow chronic stress and chronic arousal states, and the pathway is strongly engaged during the enhancement of memory by novelty, the well-documented effect in which encountering something new improves memory for unrelated things learned around the same time.

These cells also drive adult-born granule cells in the dentate gyrus preferentially, linking them to the brain's ongoing production of new neurons.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

Astrocytes at the supramammillary terminal field face an unusual problem, because they must clear two opposite transmitters from the same synaptic cleft at the same instant.

They therefore express both the glutamate transporters and the GABA transporters at high density at these specific contacts, a combination less commonly required elsewhere, and the balance of those two transporters helps set the push-to-pull ratio the postsynaptic cell actually experiences.

In the cell body region, the supramammillary nucleus sits within a dense vascular bed shared with the mammillary bodies and inherits some of their thiamine vulnerability, though less severely.

Otherwise the glia here are unremarkable, and the myelinating cells do comparatively little.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove these neurons and the caudal hypothalamus loses its ability to tell the rest of the brain that something is new.
