The mammillary bodies would still relay memories and the posterior hypothalamus would still support wakefulness, but the hippocampus would lose the signal that flags a moment as worth recording, and the theta rhythm would lose one of its principal drivers.

Behaviourally, the loss shows as flattened exploration: an animal enters a new environment and does not increase its activity, does not sample the space, and forms weaker memories of it.

The flavour lost is the physiological equivalent of curiosity, the spontaneous upward gear change that novelty produces in an intact brain.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Co-transmission of an excitatory and an inhibitory chemical from one bouton is a computation performed at the synapse rather than in the cell body, since the meaning of a spike depends on the recent firing history of that same axon.

The frequency-dependent divergence between glutamate and GABA release makes the terminal a genuine high-pass filter implemented in vesicle biophysics, with different calcium channel coupling for the two transmitters.

The cells simultaneously excite principal neurons and feedforward interneurons, implementing gain control and contrast enhancement in one stroke.

They also entrain the theta oscillation, so their effect is temporal as well as amplitude-based, positioning granule cell spikes at a particular phase where those spikes are most likely to strengthen synapses.

Volume transmission by substance P adds a slow arousal-linked layer on top, and the pathway preferentially targets newly born neurons, coupling an electrical signal to a structural, developmental process.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS: glutamate and dopamine-linked salience input from the ventral tegmental area, 0 to 40 hertz; serotonin from the raphe as a slow negative gain term; orexin from the lateral hypothalamus as an arousal gain term, 0 to 20 hertz; feedback from the ventral hippocampus; a novelty scalar from 0 to 1 derived from mismatch between expectation and input.

• OUTPUTS: co-released glutamate and GABA onto dentate granule cells, dentate interneurons and area CA2; glutamate onto the medial septum; spike output 0 to 60 hertz, theta phase locked.

• STATE: membrane voltage; theta phase; readily releasable glutamate pool; readily releasable GABA pool; short-term depression variable for each transmitter separately; novelty adaptation trace.

• PARAMETERS: resting potential minus 58 millivolts; baseline rate 5 to 25 hertz; novelty-evoked rate 30 to 60 hertz; glutamate-to-GABA release ratio at rest near 1.0, rising above 2.0 at high rates; conduction velocity 0.5 to 3 metres per second; population 30 thousand to 80 thousand.

• TIME CONSTANTS: membrane 10 to 20 milliseconds; glutamate short-term depression 200 to 800 milliseconds; GABA short-term depression 500 to 2000 milliseconds; recovery from depression 1 to 3 seconds; novelty adaptation 30 seconds to 10 minutes; theta cycle 80 to 250 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE: loss of these neurons blunts novelty-driven memory enhancement and flattens exploratory behaviour; excessive drive is implicated in the hyperarousal and intrusive memory formation of post-traumatic states; degradation of the push-and-pull balance toward pure excitation is a plausible contributor to seizure spread from the dentate gyrus, since the GABA component normally caps how much the hippocampal input stage can be driven.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to compute the net effect of one push-and-pull novelty neuron on a dentate granule cell, given that its glutamate and its GABA components depress at different rates during repeated firing.
Create a number variable called glutamate_pool with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called gaba_pool with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called push with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called pull with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called net_effect with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called firing_rate with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called output_trace with initial value empty list.
For each sample in novelty_stream, perform the following operations.
    Set firing_rate to the sum of 8 and the product of the novelty of sample and 45.
    Set firing_rate to the sum of firing_rate and the orexin gain of sample.
    Set push to the product of glutamate_pool and 1.2.
    Set pull to the product of gaba_pool and 1.0.
    Set net_effect to the difference of push and pull.
    Check if net_effect is less than 0.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set net_effect to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set glutamate_pool to the difference of glutamate_pool and the product of firing_rate and 0.004.
    Set gaba_pool to the difference of gaba_pool and the product of firing_rate and 0.001.
    Check if glutamate_pool is less than 0.05.
    If that condition is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set glutamate_pool to 0.05.
    End the conditional block.
    Set glutamate_pool to the sum of glutamate_pool and 0.02.
    Set gaba_pool to the sum of gaba_pool and 0.02.
    Add net_effect to the end of output_trace.
    Display net_effect to the console.
End the loop.
Return output_trace to the calling function.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python is chosen because the point is two independent depressing pools with
# different rate constants, which is easiest to read as an explicit loop.

def push_pull_novelty(novelty_series, dt=0.01):
    """Return per-step net drive onto a dentate granule cell."""
    glu, gab = 1.0, 1.0        # normalised releasable pools
    w_glu, w_gab = 1.2, 1.0    # postsynaptic weights of push and pull
    d_glu, d_gab = 0.004, 0.001  # depletion per spike (glutamate depresses more)
    recover = 1.0              # pool recovery rate, per second
    out = []
    for nov in novelty_series:
        rate = 8.0 + 45.0 * nov            # Hz: baseline plus novelty drive
        spikes = rate * dt                 # expected spikes this step
        push = w_glu * glu
        pull = w_gab * gab
        out.append(max(0.0, push - pull))  # net excitation onto granule cell
        glu = max(0.05, glu - d_glu * spikes) + recover * (1 - glu) * dt
        gab = max(0.05, gab - d_gab * spikes) + recover * (1 - gab) * dt
    return out

if __name__ == "__main__":
    familiar = [0.05] * 200                # quiet, well-known environment
    novel = [1.00] * 200                   # brand new environment
    seq = push_pull_novelty(familiar + novel)
    print("familiar steady net drive %.3f" % seq[199])
    print("novel onset net drive     %.3f" % seq[205])
    print("novel sustained net drive %.3f" % seq[399])

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

No other cell in the hypothalamus ships excitation and inhibition down the same axon into the same bouton, lets the two decay at different speeds so that the message flips from balanced to excitatory purely as a function of how excited the sender is, and uses that trick to tell the memory system that the world just changed.


PART EIGHT: CEREBELLUM - COORDINATION AND TIMING

CHAPTER 113. PURKINJE CELLS (COG)

Flatten a great oak tree until it is as thin as a sheet of paper, stand it upright, and you have the shape of the only cell allowed to speak for the cerebellum.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The Purkinje cell is the sole output neuron of the cerebellar cortex, which means every calculation the cerebellar surface performs must leave through these cells or not leave at all.

It is an inhibitory neuron, so its message is always a form of "less", never "more".

It fires continuously, tens of times a second, all day and all night, and it carries information by speeding up and slowing down around that steady beat.

Its job is to shape the timing and the size of movements, and in the human brain also to shape the timing of thought, by releasing and reapplying a brake on the deep cerebellar nuclei beneath it.

Jan Evangelista Purkyne described the cell in 1837, which makes it one of the first neurons ever drawn.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell body is shaped like a small flask, and from its top rises one thick primary dendrite, or in humans often two or three, which then divides again and again into a fan.

A dendrite is the receiving branch of a neuron, the part that collects incoming signals.

The extraordinary thing is that the fan is almost perfectly flat, like a leaf pressed in a book, and every Purkinje cell in the cerebellum is pressed in the same plane.

The parallel fibres, which are the axons of granule cells, run at right angles through that plane, so each Purkinje cell fan is a comb that the fibres must pass through.

The thin outer branches are called spiny branchlets and they are studded with tens of thousands of tiny knobs called spines, each of which is one synapse.

A single climbing fibre from the inferior olive in the brainstem winds up the thick inner branches like ivy on a trunk.

SIZE:

In humans the cell body is about 33 micrometres across, measured as 32.8 plus or minus 3.07 micrometres, which is roughly twice the mouse value of 16.5 plus or minus 0.78 micrometres.

A micrometre is one millionth of a metre, so this soma is about one third the width of a human hair.

The human primary dendrite is about 6.5 micrometres thick, roughly 2.1 times thicker than in mouse.

The total length of dendrite in one human Purkinje cell is 6 to 7 centimetres, which is the largest dendritic arbor of any neuron in the human brain.

Multiply that by 15 million cells and the human cerebellum contains roughly 900 to 1,050 kilometres of Purkinje dendrite.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The population is split into parasagittal stripes by a protein called zebrin II, which is the enzyme aldolase C, and the stripes run front to back like the stripes on a zebra.

Zebrin-positive Purkinje cells fire slower simple spikes, near 60 hertz, and zebrin-negative cells fire faster, near 90 hertz, so the stripes are also speed zones.

Human Purkinje cells add a further split by dendrite architecture, with single-primary, split and poly-primary forms, the multi-branched forms being far more common in humans than in rodents.

Purkinje cells of the flocculonodular lobe are a functional subtype because they bypass the deep nuclei and project straight to the vestibular nuclei in the brainstem.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The cell bodies form the Purkinje cell layer, a sheet exactly one cell thick that lies at the interface between the molecular layer above and the granular layer below.

That single-cell interface is the most sharply defined boundary in the whole nervous system, and it exists everywhere the cerebellar cortex exists.

That means all ten lobules, numbered I to X, across the vermis in the midline, the paravermis beside it, the great lateral hemispheres, and the flocculonodular lobe underneath.

Their axons run down through the granular layer and the white matter to reach the fastigial nucleus, the interposed nuclei called globose and emboliform, and the dentate nucleus, with the flocculonodular group reaching the vestibular nuclei instead.

NUMBER:

About 15 million per human cerebellum, from a careful stereological count that found 15.4 million Purkinje cell nucleoli.

Confidence is high, because this is one of the few neuron types counted by an unbiased sampling method rather than estimated.

That is about 0.022 percent of the 69.03 billion neurons in the cerebellum, and about 0.017 percent of the roughly 86 billion neurons in the whole brain.

So the cell that carries every cerebellar answer is fewer than one neuron in four thousand of the cerebellum.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The Purkinje cell is a pacemaker, which means that if you cut away every input it still fires, at 20 to 100 hertz, forever.

Its two spike types are utterly different and this is its signature.

Simple spikes are ordinary fast spikes at tens to over 100 hertz, and up to about 200 hertz when driven.

Complex spikes happen only 1 to 2 times a second, are triggered by the single climbing fibre, and consist of one big depolarisation carrying a burst of small spikelets at around 600 hertz.

The pacemaking depends on a resurgent sodium current carried by the Nav1.6 channel, and on large-conductance and small-conductance calcium-activated potassium channels.

Input resistance is unusually low, in the tens of megaohms, because the vast dendritic surface leaks current.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are parallel fibres, about 175,000 in rodent and up to roughly 1 million in humans against 30,000 to 40,000 in mouse.

One climbing fibre from the inferior olive makes several hundred release sites on the proximal dendrites, an all-or-nothing input of overwhelming strength.

Basket cells wrap the soma and axon initial segment, stellate cells contact the dendritic shafts, Lugaro and candelabrum cells act indirectly, and recurrent Purkinje axon collaterals inhibit neighbours.

Diffuse inputs arrive from noradrenaline neurons in the locus coeruleus and serotonin neurons in the raphe nuclei.

Outputs go to deep cerebellar nuclear neurons, with roughly 40 Purkinje cells converging onto one large nuclear neuron.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon leaves the base of the soma, is about 1 micrometre thick, and is the only myelinated axon carrying information out of the cerebellar cortex.

Its initial segment is packed with Nav1.6 sodium channels and is enclosed by a dense basket cell structure called the pinceau, French for paintbrush.

Recurrent collaterals branch back before the axon leaves, spreading inhibition to neighbouring Purkinje cells and to Lugaro, globular and candelabrum interneurons.

Conduction velocity is a few metres per second, so a signal crosses the human cerebellum in a handful of milliseconds.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

The transmitter is gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the main inhibitory chemical messenger of the brain.

It is made by the enzymes glutamic acid decarboxylase 65 and 67 and loaded into vesicles by the vesicular GABA transporter.

On the receiving side the cell carries alpha-amino-3-hydroxy-5-methyl-4-isoxazolepropionic acid (AMPA) receptors containing the GluA2 subunit, the unusual delta-2 receptor, and metabotropic glutamate receptor type 1, which is essential for learning.

Purkinje cells are famously rich in the calcium-binding protein calbindin D-28k, which is the standard stain used to find them.

MYELINATION:

Yes, and heavily.

Oligodendrocytes, the cells that make myelin in the brain, wrap the axon in a fatty sheath from just below the granular layer onward.

Myelin is electrical insulation, and it lets the signal jump between gaps called nodes of Ranvier instead of crawling along.

Purkinje axons are among the earliest cerebellar axons myelinated, which matters because an infant cannot sit upright until this wiring is insulated.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Enormous, and this is why the Purkinje cell dies first in so many diseases.

It must run sodium and potassium pumps continuously to support 50 to 100 spikes a second, and it must maintain up to a million synapses on a hundredth of a kilometre of dendrite.

Each pump cycle costs adenosine triphosphate (ATP), the energy currency of the cell, so mitochondria crowd the soma and the dendritic trunks.

Purkinje cells are the classic casualty of oxygen starvation, thiamine deficiency, chronic alcohol exposure and mitochondrial failure.

PLASTICITY:

Long-term depression (LTD) at the parallel fibre synapse is the most studied learning rule in the cerebellum, and it requires parallel fibre activity and a climbing fibre complex spike within a narrow time window.

Depression means the synapse gets permanently weaker, and it works through metabotropic glutamate receptor type 1, protein kinase C and the physical removal of AMPA receptors from the membrane.

The same synapses can also undergo long-term potentiation (LTP), which strengthens them, so the cell can learn in both directions.

Inhibitory synapses show rebound potentiation, and the cell also changes its own excitability by adjusting potassium channels.

During development each Purkinje cell starts with many climbing fibres and prunes them down to exactly one, a famous example of winner-takes-all wiring.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The specialised glial partner is the Bergmann glial cell, a radial astrocyte whose body sits in the Purkinje cell layer and whose fibres rise to the surface.

Bergmann glia wrap about 87 percent of climbing fibre synapses and about 65 percent of parallel fibre synapses, a level of intimacy no other brain region matches.

They express very high levels of the glutamate aspartate transporter (GLAST), which vacuums up escaping glutamate so that neighbouring synapses stay private.

Unusually for astrocytes, they carry calcium-permeable AMPA receptors lacking the GluA2 subunit, so they respond to the Purkinje cell's own inputs with calcium signals.

Their fibres are also the rails along which granule cells migrate, and damage to Bergmann glia alone, as in spinocerebellar ataxia type 7, kills Purkinje cells indirectly.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the Purkinje cell and the cerebellar cortex becomes a magnificent calculation with no way to publish its answer.

The molecular layer would still be a dense weave of parallel fibres, the granular layer would still expand mossy fibre input into billions of sparse codes, and none of it would reach a muscle or a thought.

The deep nuclei would fire unbraked and unshaped, and every movement would become a coarse, overshooting lunge.

Concretely, the region would lose its sense of when to stop.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The dendrite computes on its own, generating local calcium spikes through P and Q type calcium channels that stay confined to single branches, so one cell can hold many semi-independent computations at once.

Back-propagation of the spike into the dendrite is weak and decremental here, unlike in cortical pyramidal cells, so the dendrite is not simply told what the axon did.

The climbing fibre synapse inhibits neighbouring Purkinje cells electrically, without any transmitter, by dragging current through the shared extracellular space, an effect called ephaptic coupling.

The basket cell pinceau does the same trick at the axon initial segment, silencing the cell by pure electrical field.

Purkinje cells release endocannabinoids backwards across the synapse to tell their own inputs to quieten, make nitric oxide, synthesise proteins locally inside dendrites during learning, and are wrapped in perineuronal nets that lock their connections in place in adulthood.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Parallel fibres, count 175,000 in rodent to about 1,000,000 in human, each firing 0 to 200 hertz, excitatory.

◦ Climbing fibre, exactly 1 from the inferior olive, firing 0.5 to 2 hertz, treated as an all-or-nothing event flag.

◦ Molecular layer interneurons, basket and stellate, inhibitory, 0 to 50 hertz.

◦ Neuromodulators, noradrenaline from locus coeruleus and serotonin from raphe, as slow scalar gains.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Inhibitory spike train, 20 to 200 hertz, to deep cerebellar nuclear neurons and, from the flocculonodular lobe, to vestibular nuclei.

◦ Recurrent inhibition to neighbouring Purkinje cells and to Lugaro, globular and candelabrum cells.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, dendritic calcium concentration, per-branch calcium, parallel fibre synaptic weight vector, recent-input eligibility trace, complex spike pause timer.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential about minus 63 millivolts, threshold about minus 50 millivolts, pacemaker drive equivalent to about 18 millivolts, zebrin identity as a boolean setting baseline rate to 60 or 90 hertz.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane about 20 milliseconds, dendritic calcium decay about 200 milliseconds, complex spike pause 10 to 30 milliseconds, LTD induction window about 100 to 200 milliseconds, consolidation of learning minutes to hours.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ In spinocerebellar ataxias the cell first loses its dendrites and its regular firing, and only later dies, so ataxia begins as dysfunction rather than loss.

◦ In essential tremor Purkinje cell density falls and axonal swellings called torpedoes appear.

◦ Autoantibodies against Purkinje cells cause paraneoplastic cerebellar degeneration, and loss of the P and Q type calcium channel causes episodic ataxia type 2 and familial hemiplegic migraine.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert steady parallel fibre input and a rare climbing fibre event into simple spikes, complex spikes, and a lasting reduction in parallel fibre gain.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -63.
Create a number variable called parallel fibre gain with initial value 1.
Create a number variable called dendritic calcium with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called recent input trace with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called synaptic drive with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called spike record with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 30000 times.
    Set synaptic drive to the product of parallel fibre gain and the count of active parallel fibres.
    Set recent input trace to the sum of the product of recent input trace and 0.99 and the count of active parallel fibres.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the quotient of the sum of synaptic drive and the pacemaker drive divided by 200.
    Check if a climbing fibre event has arrived on this step.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add the text complex spike to the end of spike record.
        Set dendritic calcium to 1.
        Set membrane voltage to -58.
    Else if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -50 is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add the text simple spike to the end of spike record.
        Set membrane voltage to -58.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if both dendritic calcium is greater than 0.2 and recent input trace is greater than 0.2 are true.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set parallel fibre gain to the difference of parallel fibre gain and 0.00002.
    End the conditional block.
    Set dendritic calcium to the product of dendritic calcium and 0.9995.
End the loop.
Get the total number of items in spike record.
Display parallel fibre gain to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python was chosen because no libraries are needed, the units stay visible, and the learning rule reads like the biology.
DT = 0.1              # milliseconds per step
TAU_M = 20.0          # membrane time constant, milliseconds
V_REST = -63.0        # millivolts
V_THRESH = -50.0      # millivolts, simple spike threshold
V_RESET = -58.0       # millivolts
PACEMAKER = 18.0      # standing intrinsic drive, millivolts
PF_GAIN = 0.0013      # millivolts per parallel fibre per hertz
LTD_RATE = 0.00002    # depression per step when calcium and input coincide
CF_EVERY = 10000      # one climbing fibre event every 1000 ms
def run(steps=30000, n_pf=100, pf_rate_hz=60.0):
    v, weight, ca, pf_trace = V_REST, 1.0, 0.0, 0.0
    simple_spikes = complex_spikes = 0
    for i in range(steps):
        pf_drive = weight * n_pf * pf_rate_hz * PF_GAIN
        pf_trace = pf_trace * 0.99 + pf_rate_hz * DT * 0.001
        v += DT * ((V_REST - v) + PACEMAKER + pf_drive) / TAU_M
        if i > 0 and i % CF_EVERY == 0:      # climbing fibre, all or nothing
            complex_spikes += 1
            ca = 1.0                         # global dendritic calcium transient
            v = V_RESET                      # the complex spike ends in a pause
        elif v >= V_THRESH:
            simple_spikes += 1
            v = V_RESET
        if ca > 0.2 and pf_trace > 0.2:      # the conjunction rule of long term depression
            weight = max(0.2, weight - LTD_RATE * ca * pf_trace)
        ca *= 0.9995                         # calcium decays with a 200 ms time constant
    seconds = steps * DT / 1000.0
    print("simple spikes per second:", round(simple_spikes / seconds, 1))
    print("complex spikes per second:", round(complex_spikes / seconds, 1))
    print("parallel fibre gain after learning:", round(weight, 4))
run()

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

Nothing else in the body is a flat fan.

Nothing else fires two completely different kinds of spike, one a hundred times a second and the other once a second.

Nothing else receives exactly one input that can never be ignored.

If you see a giant flask-shaped cell in a single-cell-thick row, stained brown for calbindin, with a leaf of dendrite rising above it, you are looking at a Purkinje cell.

CHAPTER 114. CEREBELLAR GRANULE CELLS (COG)

Four out of every five neurons you own are this one cell, and it is so small that a hundred of them would fit inside a single Purkinje cell body.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The cerebellar granule cell is the smallest and by far the most numerous neuron in the human nervous system.

It is excitatory, meaning it pushes its targets towards firing, and its transmitter is glutamate.

Its job is expansion recoding: it takes a few thousand incoming mossy fibre channels and re-expresses them as billions of sparse, highly specific combinations.

Think of it as turning a short word into an enormous alphabet, so that the Purkinje cell above can learn to respond to one exact situation and ignore everything else.

Because each granule cell needs several of its inputs to be active at the same moment, it is also a coincidence detector, and coincidence in the cerebellum means timing.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The cell is almost nothing but a nucleus with a thin rim of cytoplasm, and four or five short dendrites splay out from it like the claws of a bird's foot.

Each dendrite ends in a small tuft called a dendritic digit, and each tuft plunges into a structure called a glomerulus.

A glomerulus is a shared synaptic ball, wrapped in glia, containing one mossy fibre ending, about fifty granule cell dendrites, and the inhibitory endings of Golgi cells.

The axon rises straight up through the Purkinje cell layer into the molecular layer, then splits into a perfect T, and the two arms of the T become the parallel fibre.

SIZE:

The cell body is 5 to 8 micrometres across, making it one of the smallest neurons in the vertebrate body.

The dendrites are only 10 to 30 micrometres long, so short that the whole cell behaves as a single electrical point, a property called electrotonic compactness.

The parallel fibre, by contrast, is enormous relative to its owner: 4.2 to 4.7 millimetres long in the human cerebellum.

Multiply that length by 68.5 billion cells and the human cerebellum holds roughly 308,000 kilometres of parallel fibre, which is about four fifths of the distance to the Moon, an arithmetic product of the two measured figures rather than a directly measured value.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Granule cells are strikingly uniform, which is part of why there are so many of them.

The main structural distinction is depth: cells whose bodies sit deep in the granular layer send their parallel fibres to the bottom of the molecular layer, while superficial cells send theirs to the top, so the molecular layer is layered by granule cell birth date.

A second distinction is functional rather than anatomical, between the ascending segment of the axon, which contacts the Purkinje cell directly above, and the parallel fibre segment, which contacts hundreds of Purkinje cells sideways.

Molecular profiling now separates granule cells by lobule and by the mossy fibre systems they serve, but no classical named subtypes exist.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The granular layer of the cerebellar cortex, the deep dark layer beneath the Purkinje cell row, throughout every lobule from I to X, in the vermis, the paravermis, the hemispheres and the flocculonodular lobe.

The cell body sits in the granular layer but the axon lives in the molecular layer, so this single cell physically spans the interface between the two layers and stitches them together.

Its dendrites sit inside cerebellar glomeruli, which are themselves interfaces where incoming mossy fibres from the pons, spinal cord and vestibular nuclei meet local circuitry.

NUMBER:

About 68.5 billion in the human cerebellum, derived from the isotropic fractionator count of roughly 69 billion cerebellar neurons, of which granule cells are the overwhelming majority.

Confidence in the total cerebellar figure is high; confidence in the exact granule share is moderate, because the count does not separate granule cells from their few neighbours directly.

That is about 99.2 percent of all cerebellar neurons and about 80 percent of all the neurons in the human brain.

If you picked a neuron at random from your own head, four times out of five you would be holding a cerebellar granule cell.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The granule cell is quiet at rest and has a very high input resistance, so a small current makes a large voltage change.

It needs roughly two to four of its four dendrites to receive input at nearly the same time before it fires, and that threshold is not fixed.

The threshold is set from outside by a standing curtain of inhibition called tonic inhibition, produced by GABA leaking out of Golgi cell synapses and activating extrasynaptic GABA type A receptors that contain the alpha-6 subunit.

An ambient GABA concentration of only 0.1 to 0.2 micromolar is enough to switch this curtain on.

When it fires it can fire in short high-frequency bursts, up to several hundred hertz, because its tiny capacitance lets the voltage move fast.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs are four or five mossy fibre synapses, one per dendrite, from the pontine nuclei carrying cortical commands, from the spinal cord carrying limb position, from the vestibular nuclei carrying head motion, and from unipolar brush cells.

Inhibitory input comes from Golgi cells, both as fast synaptic events and as the tonic curtain, and each granule cell dendrite typically receives contact from two or three Golgi cells.

Outputs go to Purkinje cells, Golgi cells, molecular layer interneurons, Lugaro cells and candelabrum cells, with each parallel fibre making a few hundred synapses along its length.

A single Purkinje cell receives up to a million of these, and a single Golgi cell is contacted by thousands.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is one of the thinnest in the nervous system, about 0.2 micrometres in diameter, which is close to the physical limit for reliable signalling.

It ascends vertically, then bifurcates into two arms that run in the transverse plane, at right angles to every Purkinje cell fan.

Conduction is slow, around 0.3 to 0.5 metres per second, so a signal takes several milliseconds to reach the far end of a parallel fibre, and that delay is thought to be used as a timing resource.

The ascending segment makes stronger, more reliable synapses on the Purkinje cell directly above it than the parallel fibre segment makes sideways.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Glutamate, the main excitatory transmitter of the brain, packaged by vesicular glutamate transporter 1.

On the receiving side the granule cell carries AMPA receptors and N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptors containing the developmentally distinctive GluN2C subunit.

It also carries the alpha-6 containing GABA type A receptors mentioned above, which exist almost nowhere else in the brain and are the reason certain drugs act selectively on the cerebellum.

Metabotropic glutamate receptor type 4 sits on the presynaptic mossy fibre terminal and turns the input down when activity is high.

MYELINATION:

Almost none, and this is a deliberate design choice by evolution.

Parallel fibres are unmyelinated, which is why they are slow, but it is also why 300,000 kilometres of them can be packed into an organ the size of a fist.

Myelin would multiply the volume of the molecular layer many times over and the cerebellum would not fit inside the skull.

A small amount of myelination occurs on the initial ascending portion of some granule cell axons, but the parallel fibre proper is bare.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low per cell and gigantic in total.

Each granule cell is tiny, quiet most of the time, and cheap to run, which is exactly what allows there to be 68.5 billion of them.

But because there are so many, the granular layer as a whole is one of the densest capillary beds in the brain, and cerebellar glucose consumption tracks granule cell activity closely.

Granule cells depend on trophic support from mossy fibres and on brain-derived neurotrophic factor during development, and they are exquisitely sensitive to loss of potassium gradient.

PLASTICITY:

The mossy fibre to granule cell synapse shows both long-term potentiation and long-term depression, so the very first stage of cerebellar processing is itself adjustable.

The granule cell to Purkinje cell synapse is the classic site of cerebellar learning, and the granule cell is the presynaptic partner in that famous rule.

Presynaptic long-term potentiation at the parallel fibre terminal increases the probability of transmitter release, which is a change made on the granule cell side of the synapse.

Granule cells also change their intrinsic excitability by adjusting potassium and calcium-activated potassium channels after strong mossy fibre input.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The glomerulus is sealed by a specialised astrocyte process, sometimes called a velate astrocyte because it is thin and veil-like, and its job is genuinely unusual: it does not seal tightly.

By allowing GABA to escape the synaptic cleft it creates the ambient GABA that produces tonic inhibition, so the glial cell here is an active part of the computation rather than a mere housekeeper.

Bergmann glia guide granule cells during their long developmental migration from the surface of the cerebellum down past the Purkinje cells to their adult position.

Microglia prune excess granule cells during development, and the granular layer is one of the most heavily pruned tissues in the body.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove granule cells and the cerebellar cortex loses all of its detail and all of its capacity to learn specifics.

Purkinje cells would still receive climbing fibres, so they would still be told that an error occurred, but they would have no rich context to attach that error to.

Concretely, the region would lose the ability to distinguish one moment from the next.

The cerebellum could still register "something went wrong" and could never learn "something goes wrong at this exact instant of this exact movement".

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Because the cell is electrotonically compact, it does no dendritic computation at all, and this is itself a computational choice: it is a pure, honest coincidence detector with no hidden internal logic.

Its parallel fibre performs computation by geometry rather than biochemistry, using pure conduction delay along millimetres of thin axon to convert space into time.

Parallel fibre terminals release glutamate that spills over onto neighbouring molecular layer interneurons and Bergmann glia, a form of volume transmission.

Granule cell bursts trigger local increases in blood flow through nitric oxide, and their glutamate release drives astrocyte calcium waves that in turn regulate capillary diameter.

Granule cell activity instructs oligodendrocyte precursor cells in the underlying white matter, and granule cells are the origin of two of the four molecular subgroups of medulloblastoma, the commonest malignant brain tumour of childhood.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Mossy fibre excitation, 4 to 5 channels per cell, each 0 to 500 hertz in bursts, from pontine, spinocerebellar, vestibular and reticular sources.

◦ Golgi cell phasic inhibition, 2 to 3 sources per dendrite, 0 to 50 hertz.

◦ Golgi cell tonic inhibition, a scalar equivalent to ambient GABA of 0.1 to 0.2 micromolar.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ One glutamatergic axon, spikes at 0 to 300 hertz, delivered to Purkinje cells, Golgi cells, molecular layer interneurons, Lugaro cells and candelabrum cells.

◦ Delivery is delayed by 0 to about 10 milliseconds depending on distance along the parallel fibre.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, refractory countdown, per-synapse short-term depression factor.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential about minus 70 millivolts, threshold about minus 50 millivolts, drive per active mossy fibre about 11 millivolts, tonic inhibition 0 to 6 millivolts, refractory period about 1 millisecond.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 5 to 10 milliseconds, synaptic AMPA current about 1 millisecond, NMDA current about 50 milliseconds, tonic inhibition effectively constant, parallel fibre conduction delay 0 to 10 milliseconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Loss of granule cells produces cerebellar hypoplasia and severe ataxia, seen in congenital disorders of glycosylation and in some forms of ataxia with cerebellar atrophy.

◦ Uncontrolled proliferation of granule cell precursors in the external germinal zone produces sonic hedgehog subgroup medulloblastoma.

◦ Alcohol and benzodiazepine-like drugs act strongly on the alpha-6 GABA type A receptor, which is one reason intoxication looks like cerebellar disease.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to decide whether a granule cell fires, given which of its four mossy fibre inputs are active and how strong the standing tonic inhibition is.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -70.
Create a number variable called refractory countdown with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called active input count with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called synaptic drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spike count with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called mossy fibre states with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 1000 times.
    Set active input count to 0.
    For each state in mossy fibre states, perform the following operations.
        Set active input count to the sum of active input count and state.
    End the loop.
    Set synaptic drive to the difference of the product of active input count and 11 and the tonic inhibition level.
    Check if refractory countdown is greater than 0.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set refractory countdown to the difference of refractory countdown and 1.
        Skip to the next iteration of the loop.
    End the conditional block.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the quotient of the sum of the difference of -70 and membrane voltage and synaptic drive divided by 80.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -50.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set spike count to the sum of spike count and 1.
        Set membrane voltage to -70.
        Set refractory countdown to 10.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display spike count to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

/* C was chosen because there are 68.5 billion of these cells and the bytes per cell decide whether a whole cerebellum fits in memory. */
#include <stdio.h>
#define NDEND 4
#define DT 0.1f
#define TAU 8.0f
#define VREST -70.0f
#define VTH -50.0f
#define WSYN 11.0f
#define TONIC 4.5f
typedef struct { float v; unsigned char refr; } Granule;   /* 8 bytes per granule cell */
static int step(Granule *g, const unsigned char act[NDEND], float tonic)
{
    int i, n = 0;
    float drive;
    for (i = 0; i < NDEND; i++) n += act[i];
    drive = WSYN * (float)n - tonic;          /* mossy fibre drive minus the standing curtain */
    if (g->refr) { g->refr--; return 0; }
    g->v += DT * ((VREST - g->v) + drive) / TAU;
    if (g->v >= VTH) { g->v = VREST; g->refr = 10; return 1; }   /* reset plus 1 ms refractory */
    return 0;
}
int main(void)
{
    Granule g;
    unsigned char act[NDEND];
    float tonic[2];
    int t, pattern, i, spikes;
    tonic[0] = TONIC; tonic[1] = 0.0f;        /* Golgi cell inhibition on, then off */
    for (t = 0; t < 2; t++) {
        printf("tonic inhibition %.1f mV\n", tonic[t]);
        for (pattern = 0; pattern <= NDEND; pattern++) {
            g.v = VREST; g.refr = 0; spikes = 0;
            for (i = 0; i < NDEND; i++) act[i] = (unsigned char)(i < pattern);
            for (i = 0; i < 1000; i++) spikes += step(&g, act, tonic[t]);   /* 100 ms */
            printf("  %d of 4 mossy fibres active gives %d spikes in 100 ms\n", pattern, spikes);
        }
    }
    return 0;
}

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the smallest neuron you will ever see, packed so densely that the granular layer looks under a microscope like coarse sand or caviar.

It has four claw-like dendrites and no more.

Its axon makes a perfect capital T, and no other neuron in the human body does that.

And it is the only cell of which you personally own tens of billions.

CHAPTER 115. GOLGI CELLS (COG)

One Golgi cell can quieten two thousand granule cells at once, which makes it the volume knob for four fifths of the neurons in your brain.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The Golgi cell is a large inhibitory interneuron of the granular layer, and it is the gatekeeper of the cerebellum's front door.

An interneuron is a neuron whose axon stays inside its own region rather than leaving to talk to distant places.

Its job is to control how many granule cells are allowed to respond to any given mossy fibre input, which keeps the granule cell code sparse and therefore useful.

Without it, a strong input would light up the whole granular layer, every pattern would look the same, and the Purkinje cell above would have nothing specific to learn.

It also imposes rhythm, because Golgi cells fire as pacemakers and are electrically coupled to each other, so the granular layer oscillates.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The soma is large and roughly polygonal, sitting in the upper part of the granular layer.

Two dendritic systems emerge, and this two-headed shape is its signature.

Apical dendrites rise up through the Purkinje cell layer into the molecular layer, where they are combed by parallel fibres, sampling the output of the granular layer.

Basal dendrites stay down in the granular layer and reach into glomeruli, sampling the input to the granular layer directly from mossy fibres.

The axon breaks up immediately into a dense, bushy plexus that spreads horizontally through the granular layer and terminates inside glomeruli.

SIZE:

The cell body is 15 to 25 micrometres across, so it is two to three times the width of a granule cell but only about half the width of a human Purkinje cell.

Recorded membrane capacitance splits the population into small cells around 19.8 plus or minus 1.4 picofarads and large cells around 32.8 plus or minus 3.8 picofarads.

The axonal plexus is very wide for an interneuron, spreading 300 to 600 micrometres horizontally, which is how one cell reaches so many targets.

The apical dendrites reach the full thickness of the molecular layer, so the cell is several hundred micrometres tall.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

The clearest split is chemical: some Golgi cells release only GABA while others release GABA and glycine together, and the mixed type is the more common in the granular layer.

Glycine is a second inhibitory transmitter, simpler than GABA, and its presence is what distinguishes cerebellar inhibition from inhibition almost everywhere else in the forebrain.

Golgi cells are also split by the marker metabotropic glutamate receptor type 2, which most Golgi cells carry and which their look-alike neighbours the Lugaro and globular cells do not.

A further marker split uses neurogranin, present in Golgi cells and absent from Lugaro and globular cells, and this pair of markers is the standard way to tell these confusable cells apart.

WHERE IT LIVES:

Throughout the granular layer of the cerebellar cortex, in every lobule I to X, in the vermis, paravermis, hemispheres and flocculonodular lobe.

Most somata sit in the outer third of the granular layer, close to the interface with the Purkinje cell layer.

The cell straddles two interfaces at once: its apical dendrites live in the molecular layer while its soma and axon live in the granular layer, so it physically bridges the input stage and the output stage of the cortex.

Its axon terminals live inside cerebellar glomeruli, the shared synaptic balls where mossy fibres meet granule cell dendrites.

NUMBER:

On the order of 10 million in the human cerebellum, which is a scaled estimate rather than a direct count, so confidence is moderate at best.

The scaling rests on the well-measured rodent ratio of roughly one Golgi cell for every 430 granule cells and on the roughly 1:1 relationship between Golgi cells and Purkinje cells in rodent tissue.

Ten million is about 0.014 percent of the 69.03 billion neurons in the cerebellum, and about 0.012 percent of all brain neurons.

The honest bound is somewhere between 5 and 20 million, and no unbiased human stereological count of Golgi cells has been published.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

Golgi cells are spontaneous pacemakers, firing without any input at all, typically at 1 to 10 hertz at rest and rising to 20 to 50 hertz when driven.

They possess a hyperpolarisation-activated current, called the h current, which pulls the membrane back up after every dip and makes the rhythm regular.

A remarkable property is that hyperpolarising a Golgi cell, either by injecting current or by activating metabotropic glutamate receptor type 2, produces a long-lasting increase in its spontaneous rate, so inhibition paradoxically makes the cell faster in the long run.

Strong granule cell input can also silence a Golgi cell completely for hundreds of milliseconds, a pause that opens a window of granule cell freedom.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

Inputs on the basal dendrites come directly from mossy fibres, giving fast feedforward information about what is arriving.

Inputs on the apical dendrites come from parallel fibres, and inputs on the basal dendrites also come from granule cell ascending axons, giving feedback information about what the granular layer just did.

Inhibitory inputs come from Lugaro cells and globular cells, from other Golgi cells, and from molecular layer interneurons, although only a minority of the inhibition a Golgi cell receives comes from local GABAergic cells.

Outputs go exclusively to granule cell dendrites inside glomeruli, and to a lesser extent to unipolar brush cells, with a single Golgi cell reaching about 40 glomeruli and roughly 2,000 granule cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is thin, unmyelinated, and branches immediately and profusely without ever leaving the granular layer.

Its terminals are the inhibitory component of every glomerulus, positioned so that transmitter released there bathes dozens of granule cell dendrites at once.

Because the glomerulus is not a tight seal, transmitter escapes and produces the tonic inhibition that sets the granule cell firing threshold, so this axon works by leak as well as by aim.

Conduction distances are short, under a millimetre, so delays are well under a millisecond.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

GABA in all Golgi cells and glycine in addition in most of them, released together from the same vesicles.

Both are loaded by the vesicular inhibitory amino acid transporter, and the mix determines whether the granule cell or the unipolar brush cell responds, because granule cells have few glycine receptors while unipolar brush cells have many.

Golgi cells are the only granular layer cells that reliably express metabotropic glutamate receptor type 2 and neurogranin.

They also carry nicotinic acetylcholine receptors, so cholinergic input from the brainstem can raise their rate directly.

MYELINATION:

Not Applicable in any meaningful sense, and the reason is worth stating so it is not mistaken for an omission.

The Golgi cell axon never leaves the granular layer, its longest branch is well under a millimetre, and its function depends on slow, diffuse, spatially broad release rather than on fast point-to-point delivery.

Myelin would speed conduction the cell does not need and would physically block the transmitter spillover the cell depends on.

Golgi cell axons are therefore entirely unmyelinated, by design rather than by accident.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Moderate and steady, dominated by the cost of never stopping.

A cell that pacemakes at 5 to 30 hertz for a lifetime must run its sodium and potassium pumps continuously, and the h current adds a further standing ion flux that must be undone.

The very wide axonal plexus means a large membrane area to maintain relative to a small soma, so mitochondria are concentrated in the axonal branches.

Golgi cells are relatively resistant to the insults that kill Purkinje cells, which is why cerebellar disease often leaves the granular layer circuitry structurally intact.

PLASTICITY:

The mossy fibre to Golgi cell synapse and the granule cell ascending axon to Golgi cell synapse both show activity-dependent strengthening.

The best-known plasticity here is intrinsic rather than synaptic: a period of hyperpolarisation produces a long-lasting increase in the spontaneous firing rate, so the cell remembers having been inhibited by becoming more active.

Golgi cell inhibition onto granule cells also shows a form of disinhibition, because GABA type B receptors on the Golgi terminal itself reduce further GABA release when activity is high.

Gap junction coupling strength between Golgi cells is itself modifiable, changing how tightly the network oscillates.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The relevant glia are the velate astrocytes that wrap each glomerulus, and their behaviour here is genuinely unlike astrocytes elsewhere.

Instead of sealing the synapse and clearing transmitter as fast as possible, the glomerular sheath deliberately allows GABA to accumulate and escape, which is what creates tonic inhibition of granule cells.

The astrocytes also express GABA transporters whose activity level directly sets the ambient GABA concentration, so a glial cell here is effectively turning the granule cell threshold up and down.

Beyond the glomerulus there is nothing unusual about the oligodendrocytes or microglia around Golgi cells.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove Golgi cells and the granular layer loses its sparseness and its rhythm on the same day.

Every mossy fibre burst would recruit far more granule cells than it should, so different inputs would produce overlapping, blurry patterns instead of crisp distinct ones.

Concretely, the region would lose contrast, in exactly the sense a photograph loses contrast when it is overexposed.

The theta-band oscillation of the granular layer, which is thought to time the arrival of information, would also vanish.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

Golgi cells are coupled to each other by gap junctions, which are direct pores between cells that let electrical current flow without any transmitter, and this coupling makes an isolated group of Golgi cells oscillate spontaneously.

They exert volume transmission, releasing GABA that spreads beyond the synapse to activate extrasynaptic receptors on many granule cells at once.

They receive non-synaptic signals from climbing fibres, responding to glutamate spilling out of climbing fibre terminals metres away in circuit terms and micrometres away in physical terms.

They show intrinsic plasticity, they autoregulate through presynaptic GABA type B receptors, and their apical dendrites in the molecular layer perform genuine dendritic integration of parallel fibre input separately from what the basal dendrites are doing.

The two dendritic domains are electrically distant enough that one cell effectively computes a feedforward signal and a feedback signal in parallel and compares them at the soma.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Mossy fibre excitation onto basal dendrites, 0 to 500 hertz in bursts, feedforward.

◦ Parallel fibre and ascending axon excitation from granule cells, 0 to 300 hertz, feedback.

◦ Inhibition from Lugaro cells, globular cells, molecular layer interneurons and other Golgi cells, 0 to 30 hertz.

◦ Gap junction current from neighbouring Golgi cells, proportional to voltage difference.

◦ Acetylcholine, serotonin and noradrenaline as slow scalar modulators.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ Mixed GABA and glycine release into about 40 glomeruli, reaching about 2,000 granule cells and some unipolar brush cells.

◦ A phasic inhibitory conductance plus a tonic ambient GABA level of 0.1 to 0.2 micromolar.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, h current activation, spontaneous rate set point that drifts after hyperpolarisation, ambient GABA pool.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential about minus 60 millivolts, threshold about minus 45 millivolts, pacemaker drive equivalent to about 12 millivolts, gap junction coupling coefficient 0.02 to 0.1, divergence about 2,000 granule cells.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane 20 to 30 milliseconds, h current activation 100 to 500 milliseconds, inhibitory synaptic decay 10 to 20 milliseconds, granule-driven pause 100 to 500 milliseconds, intrinsic rate plasticity minutes.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Selective ablation of Golgi cells in animals causes severe ataxia within days, dominated by an inability to keep granule cell activity sparse.

◦ Ethanol blocks the granule-cell-driven silencing of Golgi cells, over-inhibiting the granular layer, which contributes to the ataxia of intoxication.

◦ Loss of glycine co-release disrupts unipolar brush cell control and degrades vestibular processing.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to run two gap-junction-coupled Golgi cells that pacemake, are driven by granule cells, and in turn suppress those granule cells.
Create a number variable called first voltage with initial value -60.
Create a number variable called second voltage with initial value -52.
Create a number variable called inhibition with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called granule rate with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called coupling with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spikes this step with initial value 0.
Repeat the following steps exactly 8000 times.
    Set granule rate to the difference of 40 and inhibition.
    Set coupling to the product of 0.05 and the difference of second voltage and first voltage.
    Set drive to the sum of 12 and the product of granule rate and 0.12.
    Set spikes this step to 0.
    Set first voltage to the sum of first voltage and the quotient of the sum of the sum of drive and coupling and the difference of -60 and first voltage divided by 50.
    Set second voltage to the sum of second voltage and the quotient of the difference of the sum of drive and the difference of -60 and second voltage and coupling divided by 50.
    Check if first voltage is greater than or equal to -45.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set first voltage to -62.
        Set spikes this step to the sum of spikes this step and 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if second voltage is greater than or equal to -45.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set second voltage to -62.
        Set spikes this step to the sum of spikes this step and 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Set inhibition to the product of the sum of inhibition and the product of spikes this step and 6 and 0.97.
End the loop.
Display granule rate to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python was chosen because two coupled cells plus a feedback loop stay readable with no libraries at all.
DT = 0.5                 # milliseconds per step
TAU = 25.0               # membrane time constant, milliseconds
V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET = -60.0, -45.0, -62.0     # millivolts
PACE = 12.0              # intrinsic pacemaker drive, millivolts
FEEDBACK = 0.12          # millivolts of drive per hertz of granule cell firing
INHIB_PER_SPIKE = 6.0    # hertz of granule layer suppression added by one Golgi spike
def run(gj, steps=8000, mossy_rate=40.0):
    v = [-60.0, -52.0]                          # start the two cells out of phase
    inhibition, gap_sum = 0.0, 0.0
    spikes = [0, 0]
    for _ in range(steps):
        granule_rate = max(0.0, mossy_rate - inhibition)
        gap = [gj * (v[1] - v[0]), gj * (v[0] - v[1])]   # computed before either cell moves
        gap_sum += abs(v[0] - v[1])             # how far apart the two cells are held
        for c in (0, 1):
            drive = PACE + FEEDBACK * granule_rate + gap[c]
            v[c] += DT * ((V_REST - v[c]) + drive) / TAU
            if v[c] >= V_TH:
                v[c] = V_RESET
                spikes[c] += 1
                inhibition += INHIB_PER_SPIKE   # feedback onto the granule layer
        inhibition *= 0.97                      # decays with about a 17 ms time constant
    seconds = steps * DT / 1000.0
    print("gap junction", gj,
          "rates in hertz", [round(s / seconds, 1) for s in spikes],
          "mean voltage gap in mV", round(gap_sum / steps, 2),
          "granule rate left in hertz", round(granule_rate, 1))
run(0.0)     # uncoupled
run(0.05)    # electrically coupled

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is the only cerebellar neuron with one set of dendrites in the molecular layer and another set in the granular layer, reading the output and the input of the same circuit at the same time.

It is the only cell whose axon terminals fill the glomeruli.

Stain a slice for metabotropic glutamate receptor type 2 and neurogranin together and the Golgi cells light up while their look-alike neighbours stay dark.

And it is the cell that decides how many of your 68.5 billion granule cells get to speak.

CHAPTER 116. LUGARO CELLS (COG)

This cell sleeps until serotonin arrives, and then it wakes up and silences an entire strip of the cerebellar cortex at once.

WHAT IT IS AND WHAT IT DOES:

The Lugaro cell is an inhibitory interneuron lying immediately beneath the Purkinje cell layer, described by Ernesto Lugaro in 1894 and then largely ignored for a century.

It is a chemical switch rather than a message carrier.

At rest it is essentially silent, firing about 0.06 hertz, which is roughly one spike every sixteen seconds.

When serotonin floods the cortex from the raphe nuclei in the brainstem, the same cell begins firing steadily at about 15 hertz, a jump of more than two hundred fold.

Because its long axon runs the length of a parasagittal strip and inhibits Golgi cells, basket cells and stellate cells, that switch changes the operating mode of a whole slab of cerebellum.

STRUCTURE AND MORPHOLOGY:

The soma is fusiform, meaning spindle-shaped, and it lies horizontally, flattened along the underside of the Purkinje cell layer.

Two thick dendrites emerge from opposite ends of the spindle and run horizontally in the parasagittal plane, in the same plane as the Purkinje cell fans, for several hundred micrometres.

Those dendrites are the sampling apparatus: they run directly through the field of Purkinje cell axon collaterals.

The axon is the striking part: it runs transversely for one to two millimetres, at right angles to the dendrites, and sends a dense plexus of beaded branches up into the molecular layer.

SIZE:

The soma is roughly 10 to 20 micrometres in its long axis and considerably narrower across, so it is comparable in bulk to a small Golgi cell.

Membrane capacitance is about 18.1 plus or minus 0.8 picofarads for typical Lugaro cells sitting in the middle of the granular layer, and about 13.7 plus or minus 3.1 picofarads for the small fusiform subtype just under the Purkinje layer.

The dendrites span 300 to 600 micrometres front to back.

The axon covers one to two millimetres side to side, so a single Lugaro cell influences a strip of cortex far larger than itself.

STRUCTURAL SUBTYPES:

Two forms are distinguished by position and by size.

The classical Lugaro cell sits in the upper granular layer with a large fusiform soma and higher capacitance, around 18 picofarads.

The small fusiform Lugaro cell sits pressed against the underside of the Purkinje cell layer, has a lower capacitance around 14 picofarads, and is the form most dramatically switched on by serotonin.

A third form, sometimes called the globular cell, was historically grouped with Lugaro cells and is now treated as a separate type, described in its own entry.

WHERE IT LIVES:

The upper granular layer of the cerebellar cortex, with many somata directly at the interface between the granular layer and the Purkinje cell layer.

That interface position is functionally the point: the cell sits exactly where Purkinje cell axons and their recurrent collaterals pass on their way down, so it is the first thing the cortex's own output meets.

Lugaro cells are found in all lobules I to X, in the vermis, paravermis and hemispheres, and in the flocculonodular lobe.

Their axons project into the molecular layer of the same and neighbouring folia, and their influence is organised in parasagittal strips aligned with the zebrin stripes.

NUMBER:

On the order of 1 million in the human cerebellum, estimated by scaling the rodent observation that Lugaro cells number roughly one for every fifteen Purkinje cells.

With 15 million human Purkinje cells that gives about 1 million, and confidence is low because no direct human count exists.

One million is about 0.0014 percent of the 69.03 billion cerebellar neurons and about 0.001 percent of all brain neurons.

A defensible bound is 0.5 to 3 million, and anyone building a simulation should treat the figure as an order of magnitude rather than a measurement.

ELECTRICAL PROPERTIES:

The defining property is bistability driven by chemistry rather than by voltage.

Without serotonin the cell sits below threshold and fires at 0.06 plus or minus 0.06 hertz, which is functionally silence.

With serotonin applied, small fusiform Lugaro cells fire at 15.2 plus or minus 1.7 hertz, and the firing is remarkably regular, more like a metronome than a neuron responding to events.

Noradrenaline, which excites many other cerebellar interneurons, produces no spike discharge in small fusiform Lugaro cells, so the switch is specific to serotonin.

Once switched on, the cell tends to fire at its own rate rather than following its synaptic inputs, so it broadcasts a state, not a message.

SYNAPTIC CONNECTIONS:

The principal excitatory input is not a classical synapse at all: it is serotonin arriving by volume transmission from raphe fibres, acting on 5-hydroxytryptamine type 2 receptors.

Purkinje cell axon collaterals provide powerful inhibitory input, so the cortex's own output holds the Lugaro cell down.

Mossy fibres and parallel fibres provide weaker excitatory input, and other inhibitory interneurons contribute.

Outputs are the important part: Lugaro cell axonal varicosities form multiple symmetrical synapses onto the somata and proximal dendrites of basket cells and stellate cells, and onto Golgi cells.

AXON CHARACTERISTICS:

The axon is unmyelinated, thin, and unusually long for an interneuron, running one to two millimetres transversely through the granular layer before its branches ascend.

It runs in the same direction as the parallel fibres, which means one Lugaro cell shares a target field with an enormous number of granule cells and can veto them collectively.

Its terminals are beaded varicosities rather than discrete boutons, consistent with broad, low-precision, high-coverage release.

Conduction is slow, a fraction of a metre per second, which is acceptable because the signal being sent is a slow state change.

NEUROTRANSMITTERS:

Lugaro cells release GABA, and they are positive for both glutamic acid decarboxylase 67 and glycine transporter 2, meaning they also handle glycine.

At the Lugaro cell to Purkinje cell synapse the effect is purely through GABA type A receptors, whereas the Lugaro cell to Golgi cell synapse has a glycinergic component as well, so the same axon speaks two languages depending on who is listening.

They are negative for metabotropic glutamate receptor type 2 and for neurogranin, which is precisely how they are told apart from Golgi cells.

Their key receptor is the serotonin type 2 receptor, which is what makes the whole cell a serotonin-gated switch.

MYELINATION:

None.

Lugaro cell axons are unmyelinated along their entire one to two millimetre course.

The cell does not need speed because it is signalling a slow neuromodulatory state, and an unmyelinated axon also allows the beaded varicosities to release transmitter along its whole length rather than only at the tip.

This is not an omission in the data; it is a consistent finding of every ultrastructural study of the cell.

METABOLIC DEMANDS:

Low at baseline and moderate when switched on.

A cell that fires once every sixteen seconds costs almost nothing to maintain, which is why the Lugaro cell can afford to be a standing reserve.

When serotonin arrives, the cell must support 15 hertz firing plus transmitter release across a long, densely varicose axon, so demand rises sharply and then falls again when the serotonin clears.

This duty cycle makes the Lugaro cell one of the most energy-efficient designs in the cerebellum: it is expensive only when it is useful.

PLASTICITY:

Poorly characterised, and it is important to say so rather than to invent a mechanism.

What is established is that the serotonin response itself can be desensitised by prolonged exposure, so the switch has a form of adaptation.

The Purkinje cell collateral synapses onto Lugaro cells show short-term dynamics that alter the strength of the brake over hundreds of milliseconds.

No long-term potentiation or depression rule has been demonstrated at any Lugaro cell synapse, and a simulation should model plasticity here as absent unless new data appear.

SUPPORT SYSTEM:

The astrocytes around Lugaro cells do something unusual and directly relevant: they express serotonin transporters and monoamine oxidase, so they set how long serotonin lingers in the tissue.

Because the Lugaro cell is driven by ambient serotonin rather than by a synapse, the glial clearance rate is effectively part of the cell's input pathway.

Bergmann glial fibres pass directly through the Lugaro cell's dendritic field and their end-feet contact its axonal plexus in the molecular layer.

Otherwise the oligodendrocytes and microglia here behave as they do elsewhere in the granular layer.

HOW IT COLOURS ITS REGION:

Remove the Lugaro cell and the cerebellar cortex loses its ability to change gear.

Serotonin release from the brainstem, which rises with arousal, locomotion and behavioural state, would still bathe the tissue but would no longer be converted into a coordinated, strip-wide suppression of inhibitory interneurons.

Concretely, the region would lose its state control, the mechanism that says "we are now running, so process input this way" as opposed to "we are now still, so process it that way".

The cerebellum would compute correctly but would compute the same way whether you were asleep or sprinting.

BEYOND INTEGRATE AND FIRE:

The Lugaro cell is the cerebellum's clearest example of a neuron whose principal input is not a synapse: it reads a chemical concentration in the extracellular space, which is volume transmission in its purest form.

It performs a nonlinear thresholded conversion of a slow chemical variable into a regular spike rate, effectively acting as a chemical-to-frequency converter.

Its output is disinhibitory as well as inhibitory, because by silencing basket and stellate cells it releases Purkinje cells from inhibition, so one cell can both suppress and liberate depending on the path traced.

It is embedded in a closed loop with the Purkinje cell, receiving inhibition from Purkinje axon collaterals and indirectly modulating Purkinje output, which makes it a feedback controller rather than a feedforward element.

Its transverse axon crossing the parasagittal dendrites of Purkinje cells means it operates on the orthogonal axis to the parallel fibre system, giving the cortex a second, slower coordinate.

SYSTEMS INTERFACE:

• INPUTS.

◦ Ambient serotonin concentration, 0 to about 2 micromolar, acting on serotonin type 2 receptors, treated as a slow scalar rather than as spikes.

◦ Purkinje cell axon collateral inhibition, 20 to 100 hertz, powerful.

◦ Weak mossy fibre and parallel fibre excitation, 0 to 100 hertz.

◦ Noradrenaline, explicitly ineffective on the small fusiform subtype and modelled as a null input.

• OUTPUTS.

◦ GABA onto basket cells and stellate cells, and GABA plus glycine onto Golgi cells, at 0 to 20 hertz.

◦ Spatial coverage of a strip 1 to 2 millimetres wide transversely by 300 to 600 micrometres sagittally.

• STATE.

◦ Membrane voltage, bound serotonin receptor fraction, receptor desensitisation level, output firing rate.

• PARAMETERS.

◦ Resting potential about minus 65 millivolts, threshold about minus 45 millivolts, baseline rate 0.06 hertz, serotonin-driven rate 15.2 hertz, half-maximal serotonin concentration about 0.5 micromolar.

• TIME CONSTANTS.

◦ Membrane about 30 milliseconds, serotonin rise and fall in tissue about 400 milliseconds, switch-on to steady rate about 1 to 2 seconds, receptor desensitisation tens of seconds.

• FAILURE MODE.

◦ Drugs that raise brain serotonin, including selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors, will chronically hold this switch on, and cerebellar side effects such as tremor and gait unsteadiness are consistent with that.

◦ Serotonin syndrome produces exactly the clinical picture of over-driven cerebellar interneurons, with tremor, clonus and incoordination.

◦ Loss of Lugaro cells is not a recognised human disease on its own, which is itself worth recording as a known unknown.

ENGLISH READABLE CODE:

The purpose of this procedure is to convert a slowly changing serotonin concentration into the switch between a silent Lugaro cell and a steadily firing one.
Create a number variable called membrane voltage with initial value -65.
Create a number variable called serotonin level with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called target serotonin with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called drive with initial value 0.
Create a number variable called spikes this second with initial value 0.
Create a list variable called rate report with initial value empty list.
Repeat the following steps exactly 20000 times.
    Check if the current time in milliseconds is greater than or equal to 5000.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set target serotonin to 2.
    Otherwise, perform the following actions.
        Set target serotonin to 0.
    End the conditional block.
    Set serotonin level to the sum of serotonin level and the quotient of the difference of target serotonin and serotonin level divided by 400.
    Set drive to the quotient of the product of 28 and serotonin level divided by the sum of 0.5 and serotonin level.
    Set membrane voltage to the sum of membrane voltage and the quotient of the sum of drive and the difference of -65 and membrane voltage divided by 30.
    Check if membrane voltage is greater than or equal to -45.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Set membrane voltage to -68.
        Set spikes this second to the sum of spikes this second and 1.
    End the conditional block.
    Check if a whole second has just elapsed.
    If that check is true, then perform the following actions.
        Add spikes this second to the end of rate report.
        Set spikes this second to 0.
    End the conditional block.
End the loop.
Display rate report to the console.
The procedure is complete.

CODE SAMPLE:

# Python was chosen because the whole point is a slow chemical switch and Python makes the two states obvious.
DT = 1.0                 # milliseconds per step
TAU_M = 30.0             # membrane time constant, milliseconds
V_REST, V_TH, V_RESET = -65.0, -45.0, -68.0     # millivolts
KD_5HT = 0.5             # micromolar serotonin giving half the maximum drive
MAX_DRIVE = 28.0         # millivolts of depolarisation at saturating serotonin
TAU_5HT = 400.0          # serotonin rises and falls slowly in tissue, milliseconds
def run(seconds=20.0):
    v, serotonin = V_REST, 0.0
    spikes_this_second, second, report = 0, 0, []
    for step in range(int(seconds * 1000 / DT)):
        t_ms = step * DT
        target = 2.0 if 5000 <= t_ms < 12000 else 0.0   # a raphe burst floods the cortex
        serotonin += DT * (target - serotonin) / TAU_5HT
        drive = MAX_DRIVE * serotonin / (KD_5HT + serotonin)   # simple binding curve
        v += DT * ((V_REST - v) + drive) / TAU_M
        if v >= V_TH:
            v = V_RESET
            spikes_this_second += 1
        if int(t_ms) // 1000 != second:
            report.append(spikes_this_second)
            spikes_this_second, second = 0, int(t_ms) // 1000
    report.append(spikes_this_second)
    print("Lugaro cell firing rate in hertz, one value per second:")
    print(report)
run()

WHAT MAKES IT UNMISTAKABLE:

It is a spindle lying on its side directly under the Purkinje cells, with dendrites running front to back and an axon running side to side, a perfect cross.

It is the only cerebellar neuron that is silent until you add serotonin and then fires like a metronome.

It stains positive for glycine transporter 2 and negative for metabotropic glutamate receptor type 2, the exact opposite of the Golgi cell it superficially resembles.

And it is the only cell in the cerebellum whose main input is a chemical in the water rather than a wire.

APPENDIX 1. THE THREE-STAGE PROVING SYSTEM OF PREVIOUSLY UNNAMED CONSTRUCTS (PUC)


A1.1 Why the Names Must Be Proved

Eleven times in this work, a construct has been marked with the tag (PUC), meaning Previously Unnamed Construct, and given a name.

That is a strong act, and it carries a burden.

To claim that a thing is real, is used, and yet has never been named is to make three assertions at once, and each of them can be wrong.

The thing might not be real.

Clustering algorithms produce clusters whether or not nature put a boundary there, and a great deal of what looks like a cell type is a cut through a continuum, drawn where the analyst set a parameter.

It might already have a name that the author overlooked.

This is the commonest and most embarrassing failure, and it is the reason the second stage below carries the heaviest burden of the three.

Or the proposed name might be a poor one that the field is right to reject.

A responsible naming, therefore, is not a christening but a proof.

This appendix supplies that proof system, and the appendices that follow it apply the system to each Previously Unnamed Construct named in this volume.

No construct earns its name by assertion.

Each must survive the three stages.

The naming is never a claim of discovery.

Every construct named in this work was already real, already relied upon, already published in parts.

What was missing was the word, and a science cannot reason efficiently about a thing it cannot name.

The proving system is the discipline that ensures a proposed word deserves to exist.


A1.2 The Three Stages

Every construct is evaluated against three stages, in order.

A construct that fails any stage does not earn its name.

STAGE 1: CONCEPTUAL PRECISION.

The construct must be demonstrably real and currently relied upon in the neuroscience literature.

It must be physically or functionally distinct from every adjacent named construct, not merely a paraphrase of something already named.

And it must be functionally necessary, playing a specific causal role whose existence is documented, rather than being a convenience of description.

In short: the thing must exist, must be distinct, and must matter.

Applied to a cell type rather than to a brain region, this stage is sharpened into three specific tests.

• The convergence test, for reality.

Do independent methods agree that this population exists?

A cluster that appears only in gene-expression data, and that no anatomist, physiologist or connectivity study has ever separated from its neighbours, fails.

A population that appears as a distinct cluster in sequencing, a distinct shape under a microscope, a distinct firing pattern under an electrode, and a distinct projection target in a tracing study has passed the strongest test the field can apply.

• The boundary test, for distinctness.

Which named cell type is this one most likely to be confused with, and what specifically separates them?

The answer must be stated, not assumed.

• The ablation test, for necessity.

If this population is silenced, deleted, or lost to disease, what specifically fails?

Evidence that a cell is merely active during a behaviour is not enough.

Something must break.

STAGE 2: IDENTIFICATION OF A GAP, AND PROOF OF NAMELESSNESS.

The construct must lack an accepted, widely used proper name in the existing literature.

The burden here is high, and it is placed on the person claiming the gap rather than on the reader.

Informal descriptive phrases, pedagogical metaphors, and passing usages in single papers do not count as proper names.

An established proper name is one used consistently across the literature, appearing in standard textbooks, understood without further definition by the relevant community.

If any such name exists, even an imperfect or contested one, the construct does not qualify, and it is cited as a named concept instead.

For cell types one further clarification is needed, and it is the clarification that makes this exercise possible.

A label is not a name.

None of the following counts as an accepted proper name, however consistently it is used.

• A gene symbol, or a combination of gene symbols, such as Sst Chodl or Lamp5 Lhx6.

A gene symbol names a gene, designates the cell by a correlate, and changes when a better marker is found.

• An alphanumeric cluster identifier from any atlas or taxonomy, such as subcluster 0417 or MLI2.
