• An anatomical coordinate, such as layer 6b or lamina II inner.

• A description of the method by which the cell was found, or of how it looks on a plot.

• A phrase that varies from paper to paper, so that two papers about the same cell cannot be retrieved by the same search.

An eponym, meaning a scientist's surname, does count as a proper name, and disqualifies a candidate.

A long-established functional term that appears in textbooks and is understood without definition, such as silent nociceptor or intrinsic primary afferent neuron, also counts and also disqualifies.

Each construct must state, by name, the strongest counter-candidate term and precisely why that term does not clear the bar.

STAGE 3: PEDAGOGICAL CLARITY.

The proposed name must be self-explanatory to an informed reader, must accurately describe the construct's identity and function, and must be more useful for teaching, research and clinical application than the current unnamed state.

Names that are opaque, misleading, excessively long, or redundant with existing terminology do not pass.

The name must earn its place.

Every name proposed in this work obeys six rules.

• Plain English, with no Latin, no Greek, no eponym, no gene symbol, no acronym and no alphanumeric string.

• The name tells the story of the cell's purpose, function and faculty, so that a first-year student meeting the name alone learns something true.

• Two to five words, ending in the word neuron or cell.

• Dignified enough to print in a journal and plain enough for a curious teenager.

• Not redundant with, and not confusable with, any existing neuron name, nor with the other names proposed alongside it.

• Compound modifiers hyphenated, so that the reader parses the name correctly on first reading.


A1.3 The Additional Considerations: Ten Properties of a Sound Construct

The three stages are the minimum.

To hold each construct to the maximum, each is also examined against ten properties, drawn from the qualities that distinguish a genuine construct from a mere label.

These are stated once here and then reported briefly for each construct.

REALITY: the construct denotes something that physically or functionally exists, not a modelling artefact.

NECESSITY: the construct is required to reason about the domain; remove it and something true becomes unsayable.

CONCEPTUAL PRECISION AND SPECIFICITY: the construct picks out one thing exactly, with sharp boundaries, not a fuzzy region.

IDENTIFICATION OF A GAP AND PROOF OF NAMELESSNESS: there is a demonstrable hole in the field's vocabulary that this construct fills, and no accepted proper name already occupies it.

PEDAGOGICAL CLARITY: the name teaches; a student meeting it learns something true about the construct from the name alone.

DIRECTIONALITY: what the cell takes in, and where it sends its output, is definite and stated, not ambiguous.

HIERARCHICAL POSITION: the construct sits at a definite layer of the fifteen-layer framework, and its relation to the layers above and below it is specified.

Every construct named in this work sits at Layer 6, the Cellular Layer, and each entry states the Layer 8 microcircuit motif it participates in and the Layer 9 region or pathway it helps to build.

UNIVERSALITY WITHIN ITS CLASS: the construct is not a one-off; it is an instance of a recognisable class, and the class is populated.

THEORETICAL COMPLETENESS IN HIERARCHICAL MODELS: naming the construct closes a gap that a complete hierarchical model of the nervous system would otherwise leave open.

CLINICAL CONSEQUENCE: a person is different because of it, whether through its loss, its excess, or a drug in current use that acts upon it.

For brevity, where a property is fully satisfied and needs no elaboration, it is marked as satisfied; where it requires comment, the comment is given.

A construct for which a property genuinely does not apply is marked (NA), meaning Not Applicable, and is always accompanied by the reason for its non-applicability, so that a reader never mistakes a property that does not apply for one that was merely omitted.


A1.4 How These Eleven Were Selected

The eleven Previously Unnamed Constructs named in this work were not chosen.

They are what survived.

Two sweeps were run across the whole of neuroscience, the first across twenty territories and the second across eighteen more, and together they returned four hundred and sixty-three candidate cell types, which merged into two hundred and twenty-nine distinct constructs.

Forty-six of those were carried into the proving system.

Each of the forty-six was then attacked by two independent skeptics working separately, neither of whom saw the other's report.

The first was instructed to refute the claim of namelessness, to search the standard textbooks, the review literature, the Petilla terminology, the Cell Ontology and the major brain atlases, and to reject the construct if it found any qualifying proper name, or if it was merely uncertain.

The second was instructed to refute the claim of reality, distinctness and necessity, to look for clustering artefacts, arbitrary cuts through continua, single-laboratory findings that failed to replicate, and rebrandings of things already known, and to demand causal evidence rather than correlation.

A construct passed only if both skeptics tried and failed.

Eleven survived.

Thirty-five did not.

Twenty-one of the thirty-five failed because a proper name already existed and the proposer had not found it, which is the single most important practical lesson of the exercise: the hard part of claiming that something is unnamed is not the claiming, it is the looking.

The appendices that follow apply the three stages, and the ten additional considerations, to each Previously Unnamed Construct that appears in this volume.


APPENDIX 2. Harm-Converging Gatekeeper Neuron, proved. (From CHAPTER 65.)

WHAT IT NAMES.

This cell lives in the outer shell of the central nucleus of the amygdala, the almond-shaped structure in each temporal lobe that gives events their emotional weight.

Small and densely branched, it is 48 percent of the neurons in the lateral part of that shell.

Its messenger is gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main quietening signal, so every message it sends silences.

It silences the amygdala's own output neurons next door, and sends long axons, the output cables of a nerve cell, to the zona incerta, parabrachial nucleus and periaqueductal gray, which govern pain.

Its work is to take in news that something is wrong, whether injury, toxin, fullness or an opioid wearing off, and turn it all into one verdict: stop eating, get away, hurt more.

WHAT THE LITERATURE CALLS IT NOW.

The commonest labels, "PKC-delta-positive neurons" and "Prkcd neurons", are a protein and gene-symbol barcode welded to an anatomical coordinate.

The legacy label "CeL-Off cells" is an unstable descriptive phrase taken from how a firing rate looks in one fear-conditioning plot.

The atlases offer "supertype 368, CeA Prkcd-Ezr" and "MC-6 (Prkcd/Cartpt)", which are cluster identifiers.

STAGE 1, CONCEPTUAL PRECISION.

The cell is real, not an artefact of one laboratory or one clustering algorithm.

Antibody staining, ribonucleic acid (RNA) sequencing, paired recording, viral tracing and activity imaging converge on it in at least six laboratories, and matching populations appear in rat, macaque and human.

It is distinct, and it is most easily confused with its neighbour, the somatostatin-positive cell of the same shell.

Hunt and colleagues found 48 percent of these neurons carrying protein kinase C delta and 38 percent carrying somatostatin, with only 1.5 percent carrying both, and the two pull in opposite directions.

It is functionally necessary, and two experiments show what fails without it.

Cai and colleagues silenced it, and three unrelated "stop eating" signals lost their power to suppress appetite.

Singh and colleagues switched off its endings in the zona incerta and reversed nerve-injury hypersensitivity, while switching them on produced it in uninjured mice.

STAGE 2, IDENTIFICATION OF A GAP, AND PROOF OF NAMELESSNESS.

What is named is the neighbourhood, not the resident: the amygdala, its central nucleus, and its inhibitory messenger all have names.

What is not named is the cell itself, the one inhibitory type where pain, nausea, fullness, learned threat and drug withdrawal converge and become a single aversive state.

The strongest counter-candidate is "CeL-Off cell", and it fails on three grounds.

It is not co-extensive, since reviews say those cells only partly overlap this population.

It is paradigm-bound, vanishing from the feeding, pain and withdrawal literature on the same cell, so two papers about one cell are not found by one search.

It appears in no standard textbook, and it collides with the established ON-cell and OFF-cell terms of the rostral ventromedial medulla.

The gap is genuine.

STAGE 3, PEDAGOGICAL CLARITY.

"Bad" teaches valence, the direction of feeling, and says this cell deals in what a body wants less of.

"News" teaches that these are incoming reports arriving in many forms rather than one.

"Gate" teaches control of passage, because a gate can be held shut or swung open, and this cell does both: a warning cue opens it and freezing follows, while a harmful signal shuts it on appetite.

"Neuron" says nerve cell.

The one insight a student gains from the name alone is that many different miseries share a single switch.

That insight unlocks chronic pain, appetite loss in illness, drug withdrawal and anorexia nervosa, which stop looking like separate problems.

ADDITIONAL CONSIDERATIONS.

• Reality.

◦ Satisfied: convergent staining, sequencing, recording, tracing and imaging in many laboratories and four species.

• Necessity.

◦ Satisfied: silencing it abolishes appetite suppression by three unrelated satiety signals.

• Conceptual precision and specificity.

◦ Satisfied: only 1.5 percent overlap with the somatostatin cell, though the marker also labels a caudal variant.

• Identification of a gap and proof of namelessness.

◦ Satisfied: no textbook, eponym, ontology entry or encyclopaedia names this cell.

• Pedagogical clarity.

◦ Satisfied: the name alone teaches what the cell handles and that it controls passage.

• Directionality.

◦ It takes in harm signals from the parabrachial nucleus and sends inhibition to the neighbouring output neurons, the zona incerta and periaqueductal gray.

• Hierarchical position.

◦ Layer 6, the Cellular Layer, of the fifteen-layer framework.

◦ At Layer 8 it joins a lateral-inhibition motif with the somatostatin cell, at Layer 9 it builds the central extended amygdala, and at Layer 10 it sets a defensive state seen at Layer 12 as freezing and pain.

• Universality within its class.

◦ Satisfied: half the lateral shell in mouse, reproduced in rat, matched by clusters in macaque and human.

• Theoretical completeness in hierarchical models.

◦ Satisfied: no model explains how unrelated harms yield one shared response without a named gate.

• Clinical consequence.

◦ Knocking the marker down suppresses compulsive alcohol drinking and destroying these cells protects mice from self-starvation, but no human disease has yet been traced to it.

SOURCES FOR THIS CONSTRUCT.

• Haubensak and colleagues, Genetic dissection of an amygdala microcircuit that gates conditioned fear, Nature, 2010.

• Cai and colleagues, Central amygdala PKC-delta neurons mediate the influence of multiple anorexigenic signals, Nature Neuroscience, 2014.

• Hunt and colleagues, Intrinsic circuits in the lateral central amygdala, eNeuro, 2017.

• Singh and colleagues, An inhibitory circuit from central amygdala to zona incerta drives pain-related behaviors in mice, eLife, 2022.

• Toivainen and colleagues, Generation and characterization of a novel Prkcd-Cre rat model, Journal of Neuroscience, 2024.

• Wooldridge and colleagues, Central amygdalar PKC-delta neurons mediate fentanyl withdrawal, Neuropsychopharmacology, 2025.


APPENDIX 3. Both-Pathway Quieting Neuron, proved. (From CHAPTER 81.)

WHAT IT NAMES.

This cell lives in the striatum, the forebrain hub that turns wanting into doing, across the caudate, putamen and nucleus accumbens.

It is a small to medium cell whose output wire never leaves the striatum, its branches nearly bare of the knobs called spines.

Its messenger is gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's main quieting signal.

It listens to an alerting relay from the parafascicular thalamus, to acetylcholine and to dopamine, and answers by inhibiting the striatum's output cells, the spiny projection neurons, on both opposed routes.

In primates it is one of the two most numerous inhibitory cells of the striatum.

WHAT THE LITERATURE CALLS IT NOW.

In primates the labels are "TAC3 interneurons", "TAC3+ interneurons" and "the TAC3 class", after the gene symbol for tachykinin precursor 3, so each is a gene-symbol barcode.

In rodents the same lineage is "tyrosine hydroxylase interneurons", "TH interneurons" or "THINs" after an enzyme, again a gene-symbol barcode, with variants "Type I" through "Type IV", which are bare cluster identifiers.

The database entry, Cell Ontology CL:4042001 "TAC3-positive striatal interneuron", welds a gene-symbol barcode to an atlas cluster identifier.

None says what the cell does.

STAGE 1, CONCEPTUAL PRECISION.

The cell is real, and was found by methods sharing no assumptions.

Whole-cell recording and light-triggered activation in mouse slices established a reproducible inhibitory cell with four electrical variants (Ibanez-Sandoval and colleagues, 2010; Xenias and colleagues, 2015).

Single-nucleus ribonucleic acid (RNA) sequencing, which reads one cell's gene activity at a time, recovered a discrete population at 30 percent of marmoset and 34 percent of human striatal interneurons (Krienen and colleagues, 2020).

An independent study of twenty-eight human donors found the same class at 28.6 percent of interneurons (Garma and colleagues, 2024).

It is distinct from the fast-spiking parvalbumin interneuron, which it resembles in shape in the macaque: imaging shows they never overlap, and this cell spikes briefly and cannot sustain firing.

It is functionally necessary: destroying these cells in mice with a targeted toxin gene impairs prepulse inhibition, the damping of a startle by a quiet warning just before the bang, while habituation and walking survive (Assous and colleagues, 2017).

The same lesion leaves mice unable to change action after the reward is made worthless (Kaminer and colleagues, 2019); no causal test has yet been run in a primate.

STAGE 2, IDENTIFICATION OF A GAP, AND PROOF OF NAMELESSNESS.

Already named, and used without definition, are the striatum's other local cells: cholinergic, fast-spiking and low-threshold-spike interneurons.

Unnamed is the population supplying roughly a third of every inhibitory cell in the primate striatum.

The strongest counter-candidate, "tyrosine hydroxylase interneuron", fails twice: it is an enzyme name, and it misleads, because these cells lack the rest of the dopamine machinery and release GABA.

The older phrase "dopaminergic neuron intrinsic to the striatum" fails likewise, having been refuted rather than adopted.

The gap is genuine.

STAGE 3, PEDAGOGICAL CLARITY.

"Even-Handed" teaches that the cell plays no favourites.

The striatum issues its orders along two opposed routes, one promoting a movement and one suppressing it; this cell inhibits both.

"Brake" teaches that it slows rather than drives, and is applied by an arriving signal.

"Neuron" marks it as one nerve cell, not a region and not a pathway.

The insight from the name alone is that the brain holds cells whose job is to slow a whole decision, not tip it one way.

That insight unlocks sensory gating, the ability of a warning to blunt a reaction, and the flexibility to drop an action whose reward has gone.

ADDITIONAL CONSIDERATIONS.

• Reality.

Satisfied by slice electrophysiology, primate single-nucleus sequencing, tissue imaging and macaque patch recording.

• Necessity.

Satisfied in rodent only: targeted destruction impairs prepulse inhibition and abolishes action switching after devaluation.

• Conceptual precision and specificity.

Satisfied: the class is defined by several genes together, not the shared TAC3 signal, and parts cleanly from parvalbumin cells.

• Identification of a gap and proof of namelessness.

Satisfied: every label in use is a gene symbol, an atlas string or an ordinal.

• Pedagogical clarity.

Satisfied: the name gives the effect's direction and its impartiality.

• Directionality.

In comes glutamate from the parafascicular thalamus, with acetylcholine and dopamine; out goes GABA to spiny projection neurons of both routes.

• Hierarchical position.

Layer 6, the Cellular Layer, of the fifteen-layer framework.

At Layer 8 it forms a feed-forward inhibition motif; at Layer 9, the striatum and its loop through the basal ganglia.

The Layer 10 consequence is impartial damping of action selection, the Layer 12 consequence sensory gating.

• Universality within its class.

Satisfied: the founding developmental class occurs in primates, pig, ferret and mouse, though far scarcer in mouse.

• Theoretical completeness in hierarchical models.

Satisfied: any account listing only cholinergic, fast-spiking and low-threshold-spike cells omits a third of primate striatal inhibition.

• Clinical consequence.

Not yet known: no human disease has been tied to this cell, and no drug or genetic tool yet reaches it.

SOURCES FOR THIS CONSTRUCT.

• Krienen and colleagues, Innovations present in the primate interneuron repertoire, Nature, 2020.

• Corrigan and colleagues, Conservation and alteration of mammalian striatal interneurons, Nature, 2025.

• Garma and colleagues, Interneuron diversity in the human dorsal striatum, Nature Communications, 2024.

• Xenias and colleagues, Are striatal tyrosine hydroxylase interneurons dopaminergic?, Journal of Neuroscience, 2015.

• Ibanez-Sandoval and colleagues, Electrophysiological and morphological characteristics and synaptic connectivity of tyrosine hydroxylase-expressing neurons in adult mouse striatum, Journal of Neuroscience, 2010.

• Assous and colleagues, Differential processing of thalamic information via distinct striatal interneuron circuits, Nature Communications, 2017.

• Kaminer and colleagues, Loss of striatal tyrosine-hydroxylase interneurons impairs instrumental goal-directed behavior, European Journal of Neuroscience, 2019.


APPENDIX 4. Dashed-Hope Damping Neuron, proved. (From CHAPTER 82.)

WHAT IT NAMES.

This cell sits in a dense cluster in the midbrain, just behind the region making most of the brain's dopamine, the messenger of better-than-predicted news.

It is a projection neuron, one whose fibre reaches far beyond its neighbourhood, carrying gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), the brain's quietening signal, to the dopamine-making cells of the ventral tegmental area and substantia nigra.

It is studded with the receptors through which morphine acts.

It fires in bursts to shock, to cues warning of shock, and to rewards that fail to arrive, and falls silent when reward comes, mirroring the dopamine cells it commands.

WHAT THE LITERATURE CALLS IT NOW.

The labels in use, verbatim, are "RMTg neurons", "rostromedial tegmental nucleus neurons", "tVTA neurons", "FoxP1+ neurons" and "Pnoc neurons".

"Rostromedial tegmental" and "tail of the ventral tegmental area" are anatomical coordinates, a direction word bolted onto a region, or a position relative to a neighbour.

"FoxP1" and "Pnoc", short for prepronociceptin, are gene-symbol barcodes, while "LatC", applied in 2026 to the adjacent human cluster, is a cluster identifier.

STAGE 1, CONCEPTUAL PRECISION.

The cell is real, found twice over in 2009 by two laboratories working separately, under Jhou and under Barrot.

Methods sharing no assumptions converge: fibre tracing from the dopamine regions, staining for the enzyme that builds GABA, single-unit recording in rats and macaques, and staining of ten human midbrains in 2026.

It is most easily confused with the local GABA interneurons of the ventral tegmental area, which also inhibit dopamine cells but are midbrain-born and wire only to neighbours.

It is functionally necessary: destroying the cells left rats freezing 76 percent less to a danger-predicting tone and abolished avoidance of an elevated maze's exposed arms.

A second study showed the chemical half: the share of dopamine cells falling silent to footshock, siren and bright light collapsed to 19, 16 and 18 percent.

STAGE 2, IDENTIFICATION OF A GAP, AND PROOF OF NAMELESSNESS.

Named already are the ventral tegmental area, the lateral habenula, GABA, the genes FoxP1 and Pnoc, and the nucleus itself, which holds an atlas entry.

What is not named is the cell, since every phrase in use means neuron at address X, or neuron carrying gene Y.

The strongest counter-candidate, "rostromedial tegmental nucleus neuron", is a coordinate, grammatically identical to laterodorsal tegmental and pedunculopontine tegmental, and coordinates are excluded however consistent.

It is also not understood without definition: papers still explain on first mention that this nucleus is also called the tail of the ventral tegmental area.

It appears in no standard textbook, returns zero matches in the Cell Ontology and Uberon, and the field's "brake" shorthand stays in scare quotes.

The gap is genuine.

STAGE 3, PEDAGOGICAL CLARITY.

"Worse" teaches that this cell cares how badly an event turned out, not merely that it happened.

"Than-Expected" teaches that the brain runs a standing prediction and that this cell reports the shortfall rather than reality itself.

"Restraint" teaches what the report is for, since the output is not an alarm but a holding back, applied to dopamine and thence to pursuit.

"Neuron" teaches that one cell type, not a region, does the whole computation.

The insight from the name alone is that disappointment is a measurement, not a mood, and that the brain has hardware turning it into restraint.

That insight unlocks punishment resistance, the failure to stop a rewarding action that has begun to hurt, which is the core of addiction.

ADDITIONAL CONSIDERATIONS.

• Reality.

Satisfied by two independent 2009 discoveries and convergent tracing, recording and sequencing.

• Necessity.

Satisfied: destroying the cells removes most of the dopamine pause and most learned avoidance.

• Conceptual precision and specificity.

Satisfied with one limit: only 30 to 40 percent of neurons there show the full pattern, chiefly those projecting to the dopamine cells.

• Identification of a gap and proof of namelessness.

Satisfied: every label is an address or a gene symbol, and no eponym exists.

• Pedagogical clarity.

Satisfied: the name teaches prediction, shortfall and inhibition in plain words.

• Directionality.

It takes excitation from the lateral habenula, frontal cortex and parabrachial nucleus, and sends inhibition to dopamine cell bodies.

• Hierarchical position.

Layer 6, the Cellular Layer, of the fifteen-layer framework.

◦ Layer 8 motif: the sign-inverting feed-forward inhibitory relay, which opioids exploit in reverse by silencing it.

◦ Layer 9 pathway: the lateral habenula to midbrain route, by which bad outcomes reach dopamine.

◦ Layer 10: it sets the sign of dopamine's teaching signal to the striatum; Layer 12: punishment learning.

• Universality within its class.

Satisfied across rat, mouse and macaque, the human cluster mapped in 2026.

• Theoretical completeness in hierarchical models.

Satisfied: learning models need a source for the negative half of prediction error.

• Clinical consequence.

Silencing it makes rats endure more footshock for cocaine, and opioids act partly here, but no human disease is yet tied to it.

SOURCES FOR THIS CONSTRUCT.

• Jhou and colleagues, The rostromedial tegmental nucleus (RMTg), a GABAergic afferent to midbrain dopamine neurons, encodes aversive stimuli and inhibits motor responses, Neuron, 2009.

• Hong and colleagues, Negative reward signals from the lateral habenula to dopamine neurons are mediated by rostromedial tegmental nucleus in primates, Journal of Neuroscience, 2011.

• Smith and colleagues, Gene expression and neurochemical characterization of the rostromedial tegmental nucleus (RMTg) in rats and mice, Brain Structure and Function, 2019.

• Li and colleagues, Generality and opponency of rostromedial tegmental (RMTg) roles in valence processing, eLife, 2019.

• Filimontseva, Fu and Halliday, Location and mapping of the human rostromedial tegmental nucleus and associated midbrain inhibitory nuclei regulating dopamine neurons, Brain Structure and Function, 2026.


APPENDIX 5. Blood-Sampling Thirst Neuron, proved. (From CHAPTER 111.)

WHAT IT NAMES.

This neuron lives in the subfornical organ, a lentil-sized button of tissue on the roof of the third ventricle, a fluid-filled chamber in the brain.

Its vessels are deliberately leaky, so this cell is bathed in whole blood rather than shielded behind the blood-brain barrier, the tight vessel lining that keeps circulating chemicals out of brain tissue.

It is a small excitatory neuron, a nerve cell that urges its targets to fire, and it speaks with glutamate, the brain's main excitatory messenger.

It reads the blood directly: concentration, through a stretch-gated pore called transmembrane protein 63B (TMEM63B), and the hormone angiotensin, released when blood volume falls.

Its output excites the median preoptic nucleus, the hub commanding drinking.

When it fires, an animal that has just drunk its fill will cross a cage and drink again, so its firing is not a measurement of thirst but the thirst itself.

WHAT THE LITERATURE CALLS IT NOW.

The leading current review calls it "SFOVglut2 neurons" and "thirst-promoting glutamatergic neurons", with markers "Slc17a6, Etv1, Nos1, Agtr1a, Pdyn, and Nxph4", a gene-symbol barcode bolted to an anatomical coordinate.

Laboratories also write "SFO-nNOS neurons", "SFO-GLUT" and "SFO-ETV1", the same pattern, and profiling adds cluster identifiers like "Glut5-Rxfp3".

The rest are unstable descriptive phrases: "thirst neurons" is used for subfornical, preoptic and even fly cells, and "water neurons" is re-explained in the text each time it appears.

STAGE 1, CONCEPTUAL PRECISION.

The cell is real, and independent laboratories converge on it.

Switching on genetically marked cells with light evoked immediate, water-specific drinking in fully water-satiated mice (Oka and colleagues, 2015).

Profiling of 2,642 subfornical neurons resolved five excitatory and three inhibitory types (Pool and colleagues, 2020), and the relay to the preoptic area was mapped by ablation and silencing (Augustine and colleagues, 2018).

It is distinct from the cell most easily confused with it, the inhibitory thirst-suppressing neuron of the same organ.

The two differ in messenger and marker genes, and most plainly in behaviour: switching one on makes a sated mouse drink, switching the other on makes a parched mouse stop.

It is functionally necessary: mice lacking transmembrane protein 63B drank about half as much as normal when dehydrated, and restoring the gene inside this organ alone returned drinking to about three quarters of normal (Yang and colleagues, 2024, confirmed by Zou and colleagues, 2025).

STAGE 2, IDENTIFICATION OF A GAP, AND PROOF OF NAMELESSNESS.

What is named is generous: the organ, the hormone angiotensin, the sensation thirst, the channel, and the illness.

What is not named is the cell that does the work.

The strongest counter-candidate is "osmoreceptor", which is textbook-grade and needs no definition, and it fails three ways.

It is used predicatively: these cells function as osmoreceptors rather than being them; its classical anchor is the organum vasculosum and the supraoptic nucleus; and it names half the job, saying nothing of angiotensin or of the urge it produces.

The gap is genuine.

STAGE 3, PEDAGOGICAL CLARITY.

"Blood" teaches that this cell reads the bloodstream itself, not a report relayed from other neurons.

"Sampling" teaches that the reading is direct and continuous, possible only because it sits outside the blood-brain barrier.

"Thirst" teaches that its output is not a number but a want the animal works to switch off.

"Neuron" teaches that an ordinary nerve cell does this, not a gland.

The insight from the name alone is that thirst is manufactured, by a few nerve cells permitted to touch the blood.

That unlocks adipsic hypernatremia, in which the blood turns dangerously salty while the person feels no thirst, because that same exposure lets antibodies reach these cells.

ADDITIONAL CONSIDERATIONS.

• Reality.

Satisfied, by light activation, single-cell profiling and gene deletion across independent laboratories.

• Necessity.

Satisfied, by deleting the sensing channel in this organ and rescuing it there.

• Conceptual precision and specificity.

Satisfied, though the name denotes a class of at least five excitatory types.

• Identification of a gap and proof of namelessness.

Satisfied, no textbook or ontology entry supplies a proper name.

• Pedagogical clarity.

Satisfied, the name states input, privilege and output in three words.

• Directionality.

It takes in blood concentration, circulating angiotensin and fast reports from mouth and gut, and excites the median preoptic nucleus and wider lamina terminalis.

• Hierarchical position.

Layer 6, the Cellular Layer, of the fifteen-layer framework.

Its Layer 8 motif is a first-order sensory drive node with recurrent inhibitory braking.

Its Layer 9 structure is the lamina terminalis thirst pathway.

The Layer 10 consequence is lifelong fluid balance, and the Layer 12 consequence is conscious motivation to seek water.

• Universality within its class.

Satisfied for mammals in principle, shown in mouse and rat, though the species match is unsettled.

• Theoretical completeness in hierarchical models.

Satisfied, it is the entry point without which the fluid-balance hierarchy has no first term.

• Clinical consequence.

In autoimmune adipsic hypernatremia, antibodies against this organ leave children salt-loaded and without thirst.

SOURCES FOR THIS CONSTRUCT.

• Oka and colleagues, Thirst driving and suppressing signals encoded by distinct neural populations in the brain, Nature, 2015.

• Hiyama and colleagues, Adipsic hypernatremia without hypothalamic lesions accompanied by autoantibodies to subfornical organ, Brain Pathology, 2017.

• Augustine and colleagues, Hierarchical neural architecture underlying thirst regulation, Nature, 2018.

• Pool and colleagues, The cellular basis of distinct thirst modalities, Nature, 2020.

• Yang and colleagues, TMEM63B channel is the osmosensor required for thirst drive of interoceptive neurons, Cell Discovery, 2024.

• Grove and Knight, The neurobiology of thirst and salt appetite, Neuron, 2024.

• Zou and colleagues, TMEM63B functions as a mammalian hyperosmolar sensor for thirst, Neuron, 2025.


APPENDIX 6. Push-And-Pull Novelty Neuron, proved. (From CHAPTER 112.)

WHAT IT NAMES.

This small nerve cell sits in the lateral supramammillary nucleus, a thumbprint of tissue in the hypothalamus at the base of the brain.

It sends one long fibre, an axon, into the dentate gyrus, the entrance hall of the hippocampus, where memories of places are laid down.

Its defining property is that each ending releases two opposite messengers at once: glutamate, the brain's main go signal, and gamma-aminobutyric acid (GABA), its main stop signal.

It contacts granule cells, the output cells of the dentate gyrus, and the inhibitory cells that restrain them, but not the neighbouring hilar mossy cells.

It fires when the animal meets a place it has not seen before.

Alone it cannot fire a granule cell; it raises readiness, so a cortical message arriving at the same moment now succeeds.

WHAT THE LITERATURE CALLS IT NOW.

The labels in use, verbatim, are "SuM neurons", "SuML neurons", "SuM-DG neurons", "SuML-Vgat neurons", "VGluT2+/VGAT+ neurons" and "GABA-glutamate supramammillary neurons".

The first three are anatomical coordinates or wiring addresses, naming tissue that holds several unrelated cell types rather than the cell.

The next two are gene-symbol barcodes, and the last is an unstable phrase used for unrelated cells elsewhere.

STAGE 1, CONCEPTUAL PRECISION.

The cell is real, found by tracing fibres under a microscope rather than by clustering, so it is no statistical artefact.

Soussi and colleagues found both loading proteins in 96 percent of endings traced into the rat dentate gyrus, and Billwiller and colleagues in 378 of 380 mouse neurons.

Three further laboratories confirmed the double message by direct recording, and a molecular survey found it again.

It is most easily confused with its neighbour in the medial supramammillary nucleus, which reaches area CA2 and the lower dentate gyrus and speaks with one voice only.

Just 4 percent of that neighbour's endings carry the inhibitory marker.

It is functionally necessary: when Li and colleagues stripped the glutamate loader from these neurons, mice still learned a new place but could not retrieve it.

STAGE 2, IDENTIFICATION OF A GAP, AND PROOF OF NAMELESSNESS.

What is named is everything around the cell: the tissue, the target granule cell, and both messengers.

What is not named is the cell itself, the one thing on that list that does the work.

No textbook names it, the Cell Ontology holds no entry, and the atlas offers only region, gene and number.

The strongest counter-candidate is "SuML GABA-glutamate neuron", stable across three decades of anatomy.

It fails three times: the first half is a coordinate, the second a transmitter description used elsewhere, and no textbook carries it.

It also fails retrieval: six papers on this cell share no search string.

The gap is genuine.

STAGE 3, PEDAGOGICAL CLARITY.

"Two-Voiced" teaches what makes this cell extraordinary, that one nerve ending can say go and stop in the same breath.

"Novelty" teaches what makes it speak, the arrival of surroundings the animal has not met before.

"Neuron" teaches that this is one cell, not a region and not a pathway.

The insight from the name alone is that the brain runs a dedicated line from its housekeeping floor into its memory gate to say the world has changed, using push and brake together.

That unlocks why a new place is remembered better than a familiar one, and points at Alzheimer's disease, where this line runs abnormally in mice.

ADDITIONAL CONSIDERATIONS.

• Reality.

Satisfied, by fibre tracing in two species, recording in four laboratories, and a molecular survey.

• Necessity.

Satisfied, because removing the glutamate loader abolishes memory retrieval while leaving learning intact.

• Conceptual precision and specificity.

Satisfied, because two-voicedness separates this cell from its single-voiced medial neighbour.

• Identification of a gap and proof of namelessness.

Satisfied, because every label in use is a coordinate, barcode or wiring address.

• Pedagogical clarity.

Satisfied, because the name teaches co-release and novelty reporting in three words.

• Directionality.

Satisfied.

It takes in arousal and state signals from the brainstem and nearby hypothalamus.

Its output goes to the upper dentate gyrus, onto granule cells and inhibitory cells, never hilar mossy cells.

• Hierarchical position.

Satisfied.

It sits at Layer 6, the Cellular Layer, of the fifteen-layer framework.

Its Layer 8 motif is long-range gain setting, one fibre exciting a principal cell and recruiting its inhibitory partners.

Its Layer 9 structure is the supramammillary-to-dentate pathway.

At Layer 10 it helps generate dentate theta and gamma rhythms during rapid eye movement (REM) sleep, and at Layer 12 it makes new places memorable.

• Universality within its class.

Satisfied for rodents, though never shown in a monkey or a human.

• Theoretical completeness in hierarchical models.

Satisfied, because it fills the empty slot for a subcortical cell reporting world state to the memory gate.

• Clinical consequence.

Satisfied in animals only.

In Alzheimer's disease model mice these neurons fire abnormally, and driving them restores memory.

No human evidence exists, and no drug acts on this cell.

• Li and colleagues, Supramammillary nucleus synchronizes with dentate gyrus to regulate spatial memory retrieval through glutamate release, eLife, 2020.

• Luo and colleagues, Segregated supramammillary-dentate gyrus circuits modulate cognitive and affective function in healthy and Alzheimer's disease model mice, Neuron, 2025.
